Crustless Quiche
This crustless quiche recipe offers the tender texture that you love in a regular quiche. It's an easy, healthy, high-protein egg dish!
Posted by Kathryne Taylor on November 13, 2026
Craving quiche, but don’t want to fuss with a crust? This eggy wonder is perfectly tender inside, like a regular quiche! You’d never guess it, but there’s cottage cheese inside, which makes it nice and soft, similar to egg bites. This protein-rich quiche recipe is the perfect way to balance morning muffins and breads.
If you’re particular about your eggs like I am, you’ll appreciate this recipe. It’s made differently from a baked frittata. While I love a well-made frittata (you can find all my tips here), this recipe is a safer bet. Even if you cook it a couple of minutes too long, it won’t turn brown on the bottom or spongy like a frittata can. Try it, and you’ll see!
How to Make a Quiche Without Crust
You’ll find the full recipe at the bottom of the post, but here’s why this recipe works so well.
1. Cottage cheese instead of milk or cream.
It’s the secret ingredient that offers a very subtle tang and extra protein. Once blended, the lumpy texture disappears, which explains why I’d never guess it was there. Quiche typically has a high milk-to-eggs ratio, and the cottage cheese helps the eggs firm up just right without spending a long time in the oven.
2. Baked in a water bath.
I promise, the water bath is easy to manage, and it makes all the difference. We’ll bake the egg dish on a rimmed baking sheet filled with a small amount of hot water. The steam from the water bath gives these bites a luscious, smooth texture by gently cooking the eggs, preventing them from rising so quickly that they develop big air bubbles. Without the water bath, the eggs would be firmer and brown at the bottom.
Watch how to make this recipe
Flavor Variations that Work
The ingredients shown here are inspired by traditional French quiche Florentine, which typically includes sautéed spinach with onion and Gruyère or Swiss cheese. You can’t go wrong with this classic combination. That said, you can easily change up the greens, onion and cheese:
- Instead of spinach, you could sauté chopped kale, chard or collard greens until tender (remove the tough ribs before cooking).
- Instead of yellow onion, you could use 1/4 cup chopped green onion.
- As for the cheese, anything goes. Cheddar, Parmesan or goat cheese would be lovely.
You can also add up to 1 cup tender or cooked vegetables, cut into bite-sized pieces. Try cooking bell pepper with your onions, or adding quartered cherry tomatoes.
Cheese Tip
Buy your own block of cheese and grate it before using. Pre-shredded and pre-crumbled cheeses are coated in cellulose powder, so they don’t melt as nicely.
Quiche Serving Suggestions
This easy, protein-rich quiche makes a great breakfast or brunch, but you could also make it for lunch or dinner. Here are my suggestions to build a full meal.
- For a simple occasion, round out your meal with crusty sourdough (toasted if you’d like) and a little green salad.
- For a full-blown spread, consider serving quiche with crispy hash browns or any of my pancake recipes, which you can cook on the stove while the quiche bakes in the oven. Or instead of pancakes, make muffins the day before.
- For a fun summer salad, try my Easy Tomato Salad or Summertime Fruit Salad. In the cooler months, try my French Carrot Salad or Favorite Green Salad with Apples, Cranberries and Pepitas.
Please let me know how your quiche turns out in the comments. I love hearing from you.
Crustless Quiche
This crustless quiche recipe offers the tender texture that you love in a regular quiche. It’s an easy, healthy, high-protein egg dish! This spinach, onion and cheese version was inspired by traditional quiche Florentine. Check the post for variations. Recipe yields 6 medium slices; you could likely bake two quiches in the oven at once.
Ingredients
- 8 large eggs
- 1 cup (8 ounces) cottage cheese (4% milkfat or higher, made with whole milk)
- ¼ teaspoon fine salt
- 10 twists of freshly ground black pepper
- 2 teaspoons unsalted butter, plus more for greasing the pan
- ½ cup chopped yellow onion
- 2 cups (3.5 ounces) finely chopped spinach
- 1 packed cup (4 ounces) freshly grated Gruyère or Swiss cheese
Instructions
- Preheat the oven to 325 degrees Fahrenheit, and grease a glass pie plate with butter. In a kettle or medium saucepan, bring 3 cups of water to a boil and maintain a simmer until it’s needed.
- In a blender, combine the eggs, cottage cheese, and the salt and pepper. Blend on high for 8 to 10 seconds, just until the mixture is smooth and frothy (no longer!). Set it aside.
- Melt the butter in a large skillet over medium heat. Add the onion and a pinch of salt, and stir to combine. Cook until the onion is tender and translucent, about 3 to 5 minutes. Add the spinach, and cook until the spinach is wilted and reduced in volume, about 1 to 2 minutes. Remove the mixture from the heat and let it cool for a couple of minutes, then stir it into the blender mixture. Stir in half of the cheese.
- Place the prepared pie plate on a large, rimmed baking sheet. Carefully pour the egg mixture into the pie plate. Sprinkle the remaining cheese on top, then transfer the stacked pans to the oven. Carefully pour the hot water into the sheet pan, enough to submerge the bottom of the pie pan by at least ⅛ inch.
- Bake for 34 to 39 minutes, until the eggs are puffed and appear cooked, and the center of the quiche jiggles just a bit when you give it a gentle shimmy.
- Remove the pie plate from the sheet pan of water in the oven, and let it cool for at least 10 minutes on a cooling rack before serving. Leftover quiche will keep in the fridge, covered, for up to 4 days, or in the freezer for up to 3 months. Reheat slices in a microwave or on a rimmed baking sheet in a 300-degree oven until warmed through (they will release some moisture while reheating, which you can discard).
Nutrition
The information shown is an estimate provided by an online nutrition calculator. It should not be considered a substitute for a professional nutritionist’s advice. See our full nutrition disclosure here.

You can adjust the position of the trackball on Keychron’s new wireless mouse
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
18:10:41 UTC
03:10:41 KST

The Nape Pro was Keychron’s first trackball device that was introduced at CES 2026 with a slim and compact design allowing it to be positioned right up against a keyboard. The company’s new T1 HE instead looks more like other trackball mice already available from companies like Logitech and Kensington with a 34mm ball positioned on the side to improve ergonomics. But since hands and fingers come in various sizes, the T1 HE allows the position of its trackball to be fine-tuned with minor positional adjustments so it rests squarely under your thumb without any reaching.
You can adjust the position of the trackball on Keychron’s new wireless mouse
A pair of dials underneath the T1 HE let you fine-tune where its trackball is positioned under your thumb.
The T1 HE is now available for preorder through Keychron’s online store for $59.99 in black, white, or translucent color options. Shipping is expected to start in early December. A pair of dials on the underside of the mouse are used to adjust the vertical and horizontal position of the trackball, which can also be extended or retracted. The adjustments are limited to just a few millimeters in either direction, but those minor improvements to ergonomics could go a long way to making the T1 HE more comfortable to use on a regular basis.
The sensitivity of the T1 HE’s optical sensor can be adjusted from 100 DPI to 2,400 DPI using a button that cycles through presets or the Keychron Launcher software that also allows for other customizations including button remapping and creating macros. The mouse features six customizable buttons in total, and a 600mAh battery good for up to 106 hours of use in Bluetooth mode, or 98 hours when using an included 2.4GHz wireless receiver. If you don’t want to worry about charging, the T1 HE Wireless Adjustable Trackball Mouse can also be used with a USB-C cable.

Advocacy Groups Sue AI Giants to Freeze New Models, Warning of 'Catastrophic' Risks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Advocacy Groups Sue AI Giants to Freeze New Models, Warning of 'Catastrophic' Risks 9
			
		 	
				A coalition of online safety and consumer advocacy groups has sued five of the world's leading artificial intelligence companies, asking a California judge to halt the development and deployment of new AI models until the companies can address what the plaintiffs describe as potentially "catastrophic" risks. The lawsuit alleges the companies are violating California unfair competition law by endangering public health and computer systems, arguing that increasingly autonomous AI could ultimately escape human control. The Associated Press reports:  The filing, first reported on by The Associated Press, asks a judge to stop Anthropic, Google, Meta, OpenAI and SpaceXSI from developing or deploying new AI models, pointing to documented examples of cyberattacks and other harmful actions by AI systems and the companies' own alarming acknowledgments that their technology has the potential to someday destroy humanity. "No other industry is allowed to treat its own development of unsafe products -- much less ones that could kill us all -- as inevitable," reads the complaint from the Irreplaceable Education Fund, Public Citizen, Heat Initiative and the Alliance for Secure AI. "Plaintiffs bring this suit to stop Defendants from continuing their unlawful path to disaster."
[...] The plaintiffs, all nonprofits, allege that the companies named in the lawsuit have violated a California unfair competition law by endangering public health and interfering with the use of property such as computer systems. They claim they've had to divert resources away from other planned work to address the acute harms threatened by each of the companies' advancing AI models. They also warn that the companies' goals of creating AI systems that are broadly as smart as humans and can increasingly manage tasks and learn things autonomously could result in a permanent loss of control of the technology and heighten existential risks to humanity.

After 600 experiments, here's what I discovered about JEPA world models
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:55:28 UTC
02:55:28 KST

World models · JEPA · Planning
After 600 experiments, here's what I discovered about JEPA world models
There's a big debate in AI right now about what a world model should be. Most of the attention goes to generative world models, which predict the future by generating it, usually in pixels. Many scientists, including Yann LeCun, argue that this is wasted effort. Most of the pixels don't matter for deciding what to do, and many of them can't be predicted at all. [1]
The proposed alternative is JEPA, the joint-embedding predictive architecture. It has three parts: an encoder, a predictor and a planner. The encoder turns each frame of observation into a learned embedding, a compact vector that describes the scene. The predictor takes that embedding plus an action and predicts the embedding of the next frame. The planner then tries hundreds of action sequences inside the predictor and picks the one whose predicted end state lands closest to the embedding of the goal. The whole loop runs in embedding space, so the model only has to learn the parts of the scene that are important for deciding actions.
This year a team including LeCun released LeWM [2], a JEPA world model whose main benchmark is Push-T [3], a simulation where a robot pushes a gray T-shaped block across a table until the T sits in the green target position shown in a goal image. I've spent the last few months running 600 experiments on it.
The first thing I wanted to know was how much LeWM is held back by its predictions, so I started by swapping out the predictor for the simulator's perfect physics, while keeping everything else the same. I then gave both versions long-horizon tasks, with the goal 75 steps away, along with the same subgoals: frames from an expert demonstration, one every 25 steps. Vanilla LeWM succeeded 78% of the time. The version with true physics succeeded 77%. Along the kinds of paths it was trained on, LeWM's predictions are already as good as perfect physics for planning.
That means the next gains for a JEPA world model come from how the trained architecture is used, and that's where most of my 600 experiments went.
Without retraining the model once, I found four new planning methods. The first two together took LeWM's success rate on long-horizon tasks from 22% to 85%, about the same as a planner that is handed the ideal subgoals from an expert demonstration. The other two methods extend LeWM to harder goals, and one of them, a single change to how the goal is scored, raised the success rate by 60 points, three times as much as swapping in perfect physics did.
1. Plan through subgoals 25 steps apart
Used exactly as published, LeWM succeeds on 22% of long-horizon tasks, where the goal is 75 steps away. [2] When the goal is 25 steps away, it reaches it 89.7% of the time, within a point of the exact-physics version at 90.5%.
So the model is accurate over 25 steps and struggles over 75. The usual explanation is that small prediction errors compound over a long plan, which would call for a better model. I tested that against the alternative on the same 250 long-horizon tasks.
Exact physics added 20 points. Splitting the task into 25-step segments, each the length LeWM already handles almost perfectly, added nearly 60. LeWM's useful planning horizon is about 25 steps, so it was clear that the way to go further is to chain segments of that length together.
This is hierarchical planning: a high level picks subgoals and a low level reaches them. It's widely considered one of the main open problems for world models, and on Push-T it's worth three times as much as swapping in perfect physics.
Extra search doesn't substitute for it. Running the planner four times and keeping the lowest-cost plan took four times the compute and added less than one point, because the four runs almost always succeeded or failed together.
The subgoals in this experiment came from an expert demonstration, which a deployed robot won't have. The second method removes that dependence.
2. A latent memory graph with a cost on every jump
LeWM was trained on expert demonstrations of the task, and its encoder can embed every frame of them, giving about 460,000 embedded states that I connected into a graph. Each state links to the states that follow it in its own demonstration, and to states in other demonstrations that are close in embedding space. To solve a long-horizon task, the planner searches this graph for a path from the current state to the goal and uses the states along that path as subgoals.
This only works if distance in embedding space means something physical, and it does. Take one subgoal the robot can reach within 25 steps and one it can't, and LeWM's embedding distance ranks the reachable one as closer 87% of the time. So the embedding space doubles as a map of what the robot can reach.
The first version had a flaw. A jump between nearby states in two different demonstrations cost the same as a real step, so the search strung jumps together into paths the block could never follow (318 of 1,015 plans tried to use six or more jumps). The fix was adding a cost on every jump between demonstrations in the graph. Using nothing but LeWM's own embeddings of past demonstrations, success on the same 250 long-horizon tasks (goal 75 steps away) went from 62% to 85%, which is the same score received by the planner using expert subgoals. Can't get much better than that, right?

Jensen Huang was reckless with the environment, too
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:44:22 UTC
02:44:22 KST

Under cofounder Jensen Huang, Nvidia has become the engine for the AI boom — and one of the tech industry’s biggest environmental liabilities. Its sought-after chips have triggered a new wave of water- and chemical-intensive factories, drained power grids from inside massive data centers, and set the stage for a looming e-waste problem.
Jensen Huang was reckless with the environment, too
Nvidia’s environmental footprint extends way beyond its own offices.
Under Huang’s leadership, Nvidia clashed with environmental groups seeking to hold the company accountable for its so-called “hidden” emissions. Huang has talked up the idea of using AI to fight climate change while his company’s supply chain emissions ballooned. Nvidia’s investments, meanwhile, have supported the buildout of data centers that have sparked intense backlash for both their local and global impact. Now, after the CEO’s shocking resignation amid a sprawling sex-and-espionage scandal, advocates are urging Nvidia to turn the page.
He compared AI to a surgeon who has “to cut you open to save you.”
“Jensen Huang has failed to put climate responsibility at the heart of the company’s growth strategy, to slow down the unchecked expansion of data centres or to prevent its chips from being deployed in unethical applications like automated warfare,” Avex Li, Greenpeace East Asia’s project lead for AI supply chain campaign, said in a press release Thursday. The organization is now pushing interim CEO Colette Kress to commit Nvidia to decarbonizing by investing in renewable energy, as well as ensuring its technology is used in a way that “respects human rights.” Nvidia didn’t immediately respond to requests for comment from The Verge.
Huang has projected optimism about how AI can boost the adoption of renewable energy, even as the planet-heating emissions of tech companies — including his own — have grown in the generative AI era. “If you want to turn a corner on climate change, if you want a future that’s sustainable, lean into A.I. It is the best opportunity we have to get there,” Huang said in a September episode of The Ezra Klein Show, arguing that electricity demand for AI is leading companies to buy more sustainable energy.
In the same interview, however, he admitted data centers will rely on more fossil fuels in the near term, and that building up the infrastructure for AI will likely result in “an enormous amount of pain and suffering.” He compared AI to a surgeon who has “to cut you open to save you.”
Nvidia’s supply-chain emissions have grown 725 percent since 2020, according to an analysis of the company’s public disclosures by research analyst Ketan Joshi and several environmental organizations, including Greenpeace and Climate Action Against Disinformation, published in September. It’s an increase of about 9.4 million metric tons of planet-heating pollution, roughly equivalent to the emissions from nearly 25 gas plants over a year. Supply chain emissions the company disclosed for fiscal year 2026 reached around 10.7 million metric tons of CO2, according to Nvidia’s latest sustainability report.
Joshi and his co-authors approximated how much greenhouse gas pollution comes from the use of chips the company sells, a category Nvidia doesn’t include in its sustainability reports. Those emissions could have reached 21 million metric tons in 2025, according to the report, based on estimated chip power consumption. That level of pollution puts Nvidia on par with Russia’s state-owned coal company, according to Joshi.
Those “indirect” emissions — from its supply chain and the use of its products — make up the bulk of Nvidia’s climate impact. The carbon footprint of facilities Nvidia actually owns and operates looks much smaller in comparison because Nvidia designs chips that other companies like TSMC then manufacture. Greenhouse gas emissions coming directly from Nvidia-operated facilities and vehicles only reached 9,822 metric tons in fiscal year 2026, the company’s sustainability report shows. And in 2025, the company reached a goal of matching 100 percent of its facilities’ electricity use with clean energy purchases.
Greenpeace wants Nvidia to extend that goal to its entire manufacturing supply chain. When Greenpeace activists disrupted Huang’s speech at Nvidia GTC in Berlin last month, he responded onstage by saying, “I’ll talk to you guys later … All important issues, but let me get through my keynote.” In the Thursday press release, Greenpeace says Nvidia has yet to meet with Greenpeace despite the organization’s attempts to “seek dialogue” with the company over the past two years.
“One of Huang’s defining legacies is inflating a speculative AI bubble, pouring billions of dollars into infrastructure with little regard for the real-world consequences inflicted on local communities and the climate,” Katrin Wu, Big Tech campaigner at Greenpeace International, said in an email to The Verge. “Huang may be gone, but the ‘tech colonialism’ Nvidia perpetuates continues.”
“Huang may be gone, but the ‘tech colonialism’ Nvidia perpetuates continues.”
Beyond Nvidia’s day-to-day operations, Huang threw his weight around as the CEO of one of the world’s most valuable companies — chumming it up with President Donald Trump and lobbying for the US to allow sales of its powerful chips to China. The company’s influence on the global economy extended beyond chips as it invested in data centers stocking its GPUs.
And with electricity supply often the thorniest bottleneck to building more and bigger data centers, Nvidia’s long tendrils reached into the energy sector as well. NVentures, the company’s venture capital arm, has funded battery recycling company Redwood Materials and startups developing AI tools to optimize power grids and make data centers more responsive to fluctuations in energy demand.
Nvidia joined one of those startups, Emerald AI, along with Google to cofound the AI Energy Management Alliance (AEMA) in September. The alliance advocates for strategies that tech companies promote as ways to keep data centers from overwhelming power grids or raising utility bills for other customers, including generating power on-site and making “flexible” facilities that can pause operations during times of peak energy demand. Notably, some environmental groups argue that those measures might just help data centers come online faster without fully insulating households from the impact that rising electricity demand has on their air quality or utility bills.
Whether Nvidia’s environmental footprint shrinks or expands from here will depend on whoever steps in to fill Huang’s shoes. Over the years, Greenpeace and other environmental groups have pointed out that despite having an outsize impact on the environment, Nvidia doesn’t necessarily come under the same public scrutiny as more consumer-facing brands like Apple or Google. Huang’s exploits have thrust the company into a new kind of spotlight, however, just as data centers increasingly face backlash from nearby communities. Nvidia won’t be able to hide from the glare.

====================================================================================================

AI unlocks Gödel's notebooks, said to contain hundreds of proved theorems
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:30:10 UTC
02:30:10 KST

The Austrian logician Kurt Gödel was one of the most original thinkers of the 20th century. His two incompleteness theorems, published in 1931, transformed mathematics and paved the way for the digital age.
After his seminal results of the 1930s, Gödel published only a few papers. His unpublished work, however, runs to tens of thousands of pages, and is of great interest to historians of science.
Since 2017, the Finnish logician Jan von Plato has led a project at Helsinki University to understand the contents of 16 notebooks that Gödel wrote in the early 1940s. This material – the “workbooks” – is 1,009 pages of densely written text about maths and logic. Much of it is written in a German shorthand, Gabelsberger, that was obsolete even when Gödel was using it.
Progress in the mathematical understanding of the text was painfully slow – about a page a week – until September, when von Plato enlisted the help of the AI model Claude. Within a couple of days, Claude had provided a comprehensive digest of the entire material, revealing Gödel had proved hundreds of theorems that no one knew about.
The most famous logician of modern times had, in fact, discovered significantly more than anyone had thought.
“I was flabbergasted,” said von Plato. “The workbooks not only contain dozens of anticipations of results not known until the 70s and 80s, but they also contain 14 themes in logic that have not been pursued. Gödel was far ahead of his time.”
Gödel changed the course of logic because he revealed the limits of mathematical knowledge. His incompleteness theorems show that in all mathematical systems there are true statements that can neither be proved or disproved. Yet while this result was a blow to previous generations of logicians, who had hoped that all truths were verifiable, Gödel’s work pushed the field in new directions, inspiring Alan Turing to write his 1936 paper that began the theory of modern computation.
In 1940, Gödel fled Austria under the Nazis. He settled in Princeton, New Jersey, and became a close friend of Albert Einstein at the Institute of Advanced Study. Increasingly paranoid in his later years, he refused to eat food that was not cooked by his wife, and died of starvation in 1978.
Von Plato said he had started his Gödel project in 2017 because he was facing mandatory retirement from Helsinki University. “What should I do to make myself indispensable? Oh yes, I’ll start reading Gödel’s enigmatic shorthand pages!” He received a €2.5m (£2.1m) grant from the European Research Council, taught himself Gabelsberger shorthand – “I am one of only six people who can read it in the world” – and assembled a small team.
In August, at a conference in France about AI and theorem proving, von Plato met computer scientists who suggested that he co-opt the help of Claude in trying to make sense of the workbooks. On his return to Helsinki he began the process, showing Claude his transcriptions of the workbooks, as well as six books he had previously written on Gödel and other secondary scientific literature.
“In a couple of days, I got to see a 650-page explanation of the workbooks. It had to be controlled page for page, but it’s basically a work that is impossible to do for human beings,” he said. “Humans read one page at a time and we tend to forget things. It takes us a year to read about 50 pages. We just are not able to keep in mind all the different parts.
“The AI can connect all the pieces in a thousand pages. It can see all of Gödel’s writings as one piece.”
Gödel’s incompleteness theorems revealed the existence of statements whose truths were independent of the system they were written in: that is, they could not be proved or disproved within the system. In 1966, the American mathematician Paul Cohen won the Fields medal, the highest honour in maths, for showing the independence of two well-known mathematical statements: the continuum hypothesis and the axiom of choice.
Gödel reacted to Cohen’s Fields medal by saying that he had already proved the result in the 1940s. Yet when pushed for a proof by his peers, Gödel was unable to reconstruct one from his notes.
The first task von Plato set Claude was to discover if Gödel’s workbooks had a proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, which would reveal that Gödel had been telling the truth in 1966. The answer came back: yes, almost. “He was just one step away,” said von Plato. “There’s one definition that Gödel got wrong, but he had the right definition two years earlier, and so the thought is that he would have arrived at the right definition anyway if he had just continued for a few months. For me, the architecture of his proof is just wonderful. It makes all of these results of set theory follow a uniform pattern.
“What strikes me especially is that he starts from ideas. He’s not like someone who plays with formulas and hopes to arrive somewhere. He has very, very clear ideas, and then he’s methodical. Step after step he goes ahead and he manages to fulfil the aims that he has in this formal work.”
Von Plato said that when Claude presented him with the reconstructed proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, he ran it through the proof assistant Coq, a program that verifies the validity of mathematical proofs, and it passed. Von Plato believes that had Gödel published his work on the axiom of choice, he would have won the 1940 Fields medal. “Maybe he felt that when Cohen got the medal, that that was something he sort of missed.”
In further discussions with Claude, von Plato asked the AI model to extract all theorems in the workbooks for which Gödel either had a proof, had sketched a proof or had an idea for a proof. Claude found 663. When these results were put into Coq: “About 400 were verified as stated, about 200 were repaired, meaning that perhaps the statement of the theorem had to be made more precise, or some assumption had to be added. About 50 were false, and this is no wonder. Gödel himself, when rereading his notes, would occasionally add a marginal remark: falsch! [incorrect] or schlecht! [bad].”
Von Plato added: “I am sure that the continued study of Gödel’s manuscripts will reveal further treasures. The workbooks contain, Claude says, more than a dozen themes in logic and foundations of mathematics that have not been found in the 80-plus years since they were written down. So this is one thing to explore. But there are many others. His physics and cosmology notebooks are an unknown territory I plan to start exploring once I am finished with my present logic projects.”
Joel David Hamkins, a professor of logic at the University of Notre Dame, Indiana, has not seen Claude’s reconstruction of Gödel’s proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, but said that if it stood up “it is remarkable and historically interesting. Although how much is Claude and how much is Gödel is a job for the historians.”
Hamkins said: “The fact that there are things in there that resemble [Cohen’s proof] does not mean he had it. It shows he was on the path. You don’t get credit for work you don’t tell anyone about. But Gödel had so many ground-breaking ideas that it is maybe not surprising that his notebooks are full of exciting things.”

Von Plato said he had started his Gödel project in 2017 because he was facing mandatory retirement from Helsinki University. “What should I do to make myself indispensable? Oh yes, I’ll start reading Gödel’s enigmatic shorthand pages!” He received a €2.5m (£2.1m) grant from the European Research Council, taught himself Gabelsberger shorthand – “I am one of only six people who can read it in the world” – and assembled a small team.
In August, at a conference in France about AI and theorem proving, von Plato met computer scientists who suggested that he co-opt the help of Claude in trying to make sense of the workbooks. On his return to Helsinki he began the process, showing Claude his transcriptions of the workbooks, as well as six books he had previously written on Gödel and other secondary scientific literature.
“In a couple of days, I got to see a 650-page explanation of the workbooks. It had to be controlled page for page, but it’s basically a work that is impossible to do for human beings,” he said. “Humans read one page at a time and we tend to forget things. It takes us a year to read about 50 pages. We just are not able to keep in mind all the different parts.
“The AI can connect all the pieces in a thousand pages. It can see all of Gödel’s writings as one piece.”
Gödel’s incompleteness theorems revealed the existence of statements whose truths were independent of the system they were written in: that is, they could not be proved or disproved within the system. In 1966, the American mathematician Paul Cohen won the Fields medal, the highest honour in maths, for showing the independence of two well-known mathematical statements: the continuum hypothesis and the axiom of choice.
Gödel reacted to Cohen’s Fields medal by saying that he had already proved the result in the 1940s. Yet when pushed for a proof by his peers, Gödel was unable to reconstruct one from his notes.
The first task von Plato set Claude was to discover if Gödel’s workbooks had a proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, which would reveal that Gödel had been telling the truth in 1966. The answer came back: yes, almost. “He was just one step away,” said von Plato. “There’s one definition that Gödel got wrong, but he had the right definition two years earlier, and so the thought is that he would have arrived at the right definition anyway if he had just continued for a few months. For me, the architecture of his proof is just wonderful. It makes all of these results of set theory follow a uniform pattern.
“What strikes me especially is that he starts from ideas. He’s not like someone who plays with formulas and hopes to arrive somewhere. He has very, very clear ideas, and then he’s methodical. Step after step he goes ahead and he manages to fulfil the aims that he has in this formal work.”
Von Plato said that when Claude presented him with the reconstructed proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, he ran it through the proof assistant Coq, a program that verifies the validity of mathematical proofs, and it passed. Von Plato believes that had Gödel published his work on the axiom of choice, he would have won the 1940 Fields medal. “Maybe he felt that when Cohen got the medal, that that was something he sort of missed.”
In further discussions with Claude, von Plato asked the AI model to extract all theorems in the workbooks for which Gödel either had a proof, had sketched a proof or had an idea for a proof. Claude found 663. When these results were put into Coq: “About 400 were verified as stated, about 200 were repaired, meaning that perhaps the statement of the theorem had to be made more precise, or some assumption had to be added. About 50 were false, and this is no wonder. Gödel himself, when rereading his notes, would occasionally add a marginal remark: falsch! [incorrect] or schlecht! [bad].”
Von Plato added: “I am sure that the continued study of Gödel’s manuscripts will reveal further treasures. The workbooks contain, Claude says, more than a dozen themes in logic and foundations of mathematics that have not been found in the 80-plus years since they were written down. So this is one thing to explore. But there are many others. His physics and cosmology notebooks are an unknown territory I plan to start exploring once I am finished with my present logic projects.”
Joel David Hamkins, a professor of logic at the University of Notre Dame, Indiana, has not seen Claude’s reconstruction of Gödel’s proof of the independence of the axiom of choice, but said that if it stood up “it is remarkable and historically interesting. Although how much is Claude and how much is Gödel is a job for the historians.”
Hamkins said: “The fact that there are things in there that resemble [Cohen’s proof] does not mean he had it. It shows he was on the path. You don’t get credit for work you don’t tell anyone about. But Gödel had so many ground-breaking ideas that it is maybe not surprising that his notebooks are full of exciting things.”

‘The Sacred Ordinary’ Shines a Light on Heritage in Birmingham’s Civil Rights District
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:13:57 UTC
02:13:57 KST

‘The Sacred Ordinary’ Shines a Light on Heritage in Birmingham’s Civil Rights District
In the second stanza of “How to Be a Poet,” Wendell Berry wrote: “There are no unsacred places; / there are only sacred places / and desecrated places.” When it comes to preserving important historical locations, such as monuments, parks, and buildings, a new interactive public art installation asks, “what happens when preservation itself is endangered?”
The Sacred Ordinary is an initiative organized by 1504, a production studio based in Birmingham that emphasizes in-depth storytelling to connect people with meaningful, human-focused services and experiences. The participatory artwork is presented in collaboration with the National Parks Conservation Association, which aims to protect U.S. national parks for future generations.
In early 2025, President Trump issued an executive order that mandated the removal of some National Park Service signage relating to slavery, the LGBTQ+ community, climate change, and more, a move that erases histories inextricable from the nation’s identity. (The current administration has also blocked millions in funding for numerous parks and disrupted services.)
In Birmingham, the Civil Rights District is formally designated a national monument, comprising neighborhoods that Black people historically called home and where Jim Crow laws authorized segregation until the 1960s. The Civil Rights Movement reached a nexus here when activists Fred Shuttlesworth, Martin Luther King, Jr., and Ralph Abernathy, among many others, organized nonviolent marches in 1963, known today as the Birmingham Campaign. Police turned their dogs on demonstrators and used fire hoses on children. A bombing at a Baptist church killed four young Black girls.
The Sacred Ordinary venerates access to things most of us take for granted: the ability to shop at any store, attend the nearest school, walk around any neighborhood, and sit at the front of the bus. The exhibition is installed around-the-clock, but by day, elements of the installation comprise vintage yet mundane objects that one typically walks by without giving a second thought.
A huge midcentury television rests on an open lawn, and a red newspaper vending box for The Birmingham News, which ceased publishing in 2012, advertises 25 cents per copy. Additional sites include a characteristically blue U.S. Postal Service mailbox and a walk-in telephone booth. Sheets of paper with tear-off tabs posted around town invite participants to learn more by texting a phone number. If you text “HERE” to the number, you’ll be directed to a starting point and an otherwise nondescript yellow fire hydrant.
In scavenger-hunt style, visitors are prompted to enter the year stamped on the unit, and if it’s between 5 and 8 p.m., the sequence of texted prompts doubles as a multimedia self-guided tour of Birmingham’s Civil Rights District like you’ve never seen it: the television screen, mailbox, phone booth, and more begin to glow once the sun has set.
“Birmingham, Alabama, is known around the world for such places—sites of civil rights history that many regard as sacred and that connect us to something larger than ourselves. But today, that memory is endangered,” 1504 says in a statement. “Aging infrastructure, the passing of a generation of foot soldiers, and federal efforts to erase chapters of American history raise an urgent question: if institutions alone cannot sustain history, what role can the public play in caring for sacred sites?”
Rather than center primarily on signage, The Sacred Ordinary focuses on objects associated with civic life in the mid-20th century, evoking collective memories and providing touchpoints for younger generations for whom boxy televisions, printed newspapers, and telephone booths feel distant from today’s experiences. Emblematic of the way news and information spread before the internet and smartphones, the objects confront visitors with not only the realities of how people organized and communicated during that time but also how those memories are preserved today. Even without didactics, objects and places carry meaning that no one can simply delete.
The tour opens with a narration by actor André Holland, who played a fundamental role in preserving a historic theater in nearby Bessemer, Alabama, and 1504 has dedicated the installation to Birmingham-based artist Joe Minter. The Sacred Ordinary continues nightly through December 13, with accompanying guided walks and other programs scheduled in the meantime. Learn more on the project’s website, and explore the studio’s work on Instagram. You might also be interested in “The Praise House,” directed by 1504’s Tyler Jones.

The smallest version of Camp Snap’s retro digital camcorder fits on your keychain

02:13:43 KST

Last week, Camp Snap launched a miniaturized version of its screen-free CS-8 video camera that was half the size of the original with a smaller grip designed to be easier for kids to hold. But it turns out the company wasn’t done shrinking its hardware. Camp Snap’s new CS-8 Micro is even smaller than the CS-8 Mini. It’s around three inches long and weighs just 1.5 ounces so you can hang it from your keys and, like its larger predecessors, it can capture video using one of five built-in filters.
The smallest version of Camp Snap’s retro digital camcorder fits on your keychain
The Camp Snap CS-8 Micro is just three inches long and adds a tiny LCD screen that’s only used as a viewfinder.
The CS-8 Micro is now available through Camp Snap’s online store and Urban Outfitters for $39.99, and similar to the collectible Kodak Charmera, the Micro is being sold in sealed mystery boxes. Colors like hot pink, lilac, lime, and orange will be easier to find, while vintage classic (black) and glow in the dark will be much rarer.
Unlike the original CS-8 and CS-8 Mini, the new Micro does include an LCD display, but it’s only used as a viewfinder. You can’t watch, edit, or delete anything you’ve captured on the camera itself. You need to transfer the videos to a phone or computer using a USB-C cable to enjoy them, or move its included 4GB microSD into a card reader.
As Camp Snap has been progressively shrinking its retro camcorder, it’s also reduced its resolution. The original captured videos at 4K, while the CS-8 Mini did 2.7K. The CS-8 Micro is limited to 720p, but carries forward features like an 8x digital zoom and a selection of five filters that include vintage effects that add flicker and grain to emulate the look of 8mm film. Camp Snap estimates the Micro’s 4GB microSD card can hold 45 to 6o minutes of video, while its battery life is estimated to be 30 to 45 minutes on a full charge.

Concurrent Shell Command Queues
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:13:01 UTC
02:13:01 KST

How to run shell commands in parallel while limiting their concurrency.
I had a use case recently where I wanted to speed up some Bash processing that I was doing, but still keep concurrency constrained. So I wondered: is there a straightforward way to create thread pools in Bash? The answer is: yes, but it takes some work to be semi-resilient and portable.
You may ask: why do this in Bash at all? It seems silly to use a language with poor concurrency controls. You'd be right to ask this question, and my answer is: because I have a fondness for Bash, and I wanted the challenge. But you're still probably better off using another language, such as Python.
Here is code that creates a command queue that accepts shell commands to execute. You do not need to know all the commands that you want executed up-front, you can submit commands to it over time.
1# Start this shell's command queue.
2# @param {number} $1 Maximum number of jobs to run at once
3queue_start() {
4  # Validate that the parallelism is a valid limit for xargs
5  if [[ ! ${1:-} =~ ^[1-9][0-9]*$ ]]; then
6    echo "queue_start: size must be a positive integer, got '${1:-}'" >&2
7    return 1
8  fi
9
10  # Close this shell's copy (not the parent's) of an inherited or earlier
11  # command queue's file descriptor
12  if [[ -n ${_queue_fd:-} ]]; then
13    eval "exec ${_queue_fd}>&-"
14    unset _queue_fd _queue_pid
15  fi
16
17  # Find the first unused fd from 3 to 9 (zsh's redirect max)
18  local fd=3
19  while { : <&"${fd}"; } 2>/dev/null; do fd=$(( fd + 1 )); done
20  if [[ ${fd} -gt 9 ]]; then
21    echo "queue_start: no free fd from 3 to 9" >&2
22    return 1
23  fi
24
25  # Make a FIFO queue file in a private temporary directory
26  local dir
27  dir=$(mktemp -d "${TMPDIR:-/tmp}/queue.XXXXXX")
28  mkfifo "${dir}/queue"
29
30  # Find the shell to execute commands (the current shell)
31  local shell
32  shell=$(ps -o comm= -p "$$")
33  shell=${shell#-}
34
35  # Start xargs in the background, reading NUL-separated commands from the
36  # queue file. Job messages are hidden by turning off job control and
37  # discarding the block's stderr. xargs gets the real stderr through a copy on
38  # the queue files's fd, until the file is opened.
39  eval "exec ${fd}>&2"
40  local opts=$-
41  set +m
42  {
43    # shellcheck disable=SC2016
44    xargs -0 -n 1 -P "$1" "${shell}" -c '
45      # Output is captured rather than streamed so that command output stays separate
46      out=$(eval -- "$1" </dev/null 2>&1); rc=$?
47      [ -z "${out}" ] || printf "%s\n" "${out}"
48      # Coerce non-zero exit codes to 1, because a 255 code would stop xargs
49      [ "${rc}" -eq 0 ] || { printf "exit %d: %s\n" "${rc}" "$1" >&2; exit 1;
50    }' _ <"${dir}/queue" 2>&"${fd}" &
51    _queue_pid=$!
52    disown || :
53  } 2>/dev/null
54  if [[ ${opts} == *m* ]]; then set -m; fi
55
56  # Open the queue file for writing, which waits until xargs has opened it for reading
57  # With both ends open, the file can be safely deleted
58  eval "exec ${fd}>\"\${dir}/queue\""
59  _queue_fd=${fd}
60  rm -rf "${dir}"
61
62  # Register an exit handler to terminate this PID's children, ensuring zsh behaves like bash
63  setopt localoptions posixtraps 2>/dev/null || :
64  trap _queue_stop EXIT
65  if [[ $- != *i* ]]; then
66    trap 'exit 129' HUP
67    trap 'exit 130' INT
68    trap 'exit 143' TERM
69  fi
70}
71
72# Queue a command on this shell's command queue.
73# @param {string} $1 Command to run
74queue_submit() {
75  if [[ -z ${_queue_fd:-} ]]; then
76    echo "queue_submit: no command queue started in this shell" >&2
77    return 1
78  fi
79  # Write the command to the queue file, blocking if it is full
80  # Use a printf process so that if xargs has exited, printf will get killed and not this shell
81  if [[ $- == *i* ]]; then
82    # Write the command in the foreground, because an interactive shell won't
83    # have any TERM/HUP/INT traps that could be delayed by a full queue
84    env printf '%s\0' "$1" >&"${_queue_fd}"
85  else
86    # Write the command in the background, so that a full queue doesn't delay any TERM/HUP/INT traps
87    env printf '%s\0' "$1" >&"${_queue_fd}" &
88    wait "$!"
89  fi || {
90    echo "queue_submit: couldn't queue the command; the command queue may have stopped" >&2
91    return 1
92  }
93}
94
95# Close the queue file so xargs finishes the jobs it has, then wait for it to exit
96queue_wait() {
97  if [[ -z ${_queue_fd:-} ]]; then
98    echo "queue_wait: no command queue started in this shell" >&2
99    return 1
100  fi
101  eval "exec ${_queue_fd}>&-"
102  while kill -0 "${_queue_pid}" 2>/dev/null; do sleep 0.1; done
103  unset _queue_fd _queue_pid
104}
105
106# Stop every process below the given PID.
107# @param {number=} $1 PID whose descendants to stop, defaulting to this shell's PID
108_queue_stop() {
109  # Default to this shell's PID, found from a child's parent PID because $$ is
110  # the parent's PID in a subshell
111  local parent=${1:-$(exec sh -c 'echo "${PPID}"')}
112  local pid
113  for pid in $(exec pgrep -P "${parent}"); do
114    # Pause the process so it can't start any new children
115    kill -STOP "${pid}" 2>/dev/null || continue
116    # Terminate the process' descendants, then terminate the process
117    _queue_stop "${pid}"
118    kill -TERM "${pid}" 2>/dev/null || :
119    # Resume the process so it can respond to the termination
120    kill -CONT "${pid}" 2>/dev/null || :
121  done
122}
123
124# Stop all background processes on exit, because they ignore Ctrl-C and don't receive TERM
125if [[ $- != *i* ]]; then
126  trap _queue_stop EXIT
127  trap 'exit 129' HUP
128  trap 'exit 130' INT
129  trap 'exit 143' TERM
130fi
queue_start initializes the queue (managed by mkfifo) and starts the executor (concurrency managed by xargs -P), queue_submit submits commands to the queue, and queue_wait waits for the queue to drain before cleaning up. Calling queue_wait is optional, command processing will start as soon as you queue it with queue_submit.
You can copy and paste this code into a script, or it's also safe to put in your dotfiles, as it only sets some of the exit traps for interactive shells. It works in Bash and Zsh, and it has as much defensive programming as I know how to add, but I'm confident it has failure modes that are difficult to predict or avoid.
Example usage
My original use case had to do with making a large number of web requests, so here's an example using Postman Echo :
$ queue_start 3
$ for n in 1 2 3 4 5; do
    queue_submit "set -o pipefail; \
      curl -sS --fail-with-body --max-time 10 -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
        -d '{\"request\": ${n}}' \
        https://postman-echo.com/post | \
      jq -c .json"
  done
$ queue_wait
{"request":1}
{"request":2}
{"request":3}
{"request":4}
{"request":5}
A more deterministic scenario demonstrating that concurrency is respected, only starting command three after command two has completed:
$ queue_start 2
$ queue_submit 'sleep 1; echo first'
$ queue_submit 'echo second'
$ queue_submit 'echo third'
$ queue_wait
second
third
first
Command queues are inherited by subshells:
$ queue_start 2
$ ( queue_submit 'echo "submitted from a subshell"' )
$ queue_submit 'echo "submitted from the main shell"'
$ queue_wait
submitted from a subshell
submitted from the main shell
Different subshells can have their own command queue:
$ (
    queue_start 2
    for n in 1 2 3 4; do queue_submit "sleep 1; echo first queue, job ${n}"; done
    queue_wait
  ) &
$ first=$!
$ (
    queue_start 1
    for n in 1 2; do queue_submit "sleep 1; echo second queue, job ${n}"; done
    queue_wait
  ) &
$ second=$!
$ wait "${first}"
$ wait "${second}"
second queue, job 1
first queue, job 1
first queue, job 2
second queue, job 2
first queue, job 3
first queue, job 4
Queueing each line of a command's output as a command to execute:
$ queue_start 2
$ while IFS= read -r line; do
    queue_submit "${line}"
  done < <(printf 'echo "from command output, line %d"\n' 1 2 3)
$ queue_wait
from command output, line 1
from command output, line 2
from command output, line 3
Queueing each line of a file as a command:
$ queue_start 2
$ while IFS= read -r line; do
    queue_submit "${line}"
  done < commands.txt
$ queue_wait
Known issues
Here are some reasons why you might just want a different programming language:
- A shell can only have one queue. The amount of additional code needed to support multiple is complicated and likely isn't needed in most cases. Subshells can still have their own queues.
- Command output is intentionally not streamed. This prevents interleaving the output of most commands. Output larger than PIPE_BUF may still get interleaved.
- queue_wait doesn't return a value indicating any job failure.**
This would require a temporary file or a second file descriptor and some complicated code.
- queue_wait has to wait for running processes started after queue_start.
Processes started after queue_start will inherit the queue write file descriptor (which is what allows subshells to work), and xargs won't see the end of the queue until every copy of the write descriptor is closed.
- _queue_stop indiscriminately terminates descendants.
Any other backgrounded jobs will be terminated, including ones started before queue_start.
Why not just xargs -P?
If you know all commands that you want to execute up-front, then the solution gets far simpler:
$ printf 'echo "from command output, line %d"\0' 1 2 3 | \
    xargs -0 -n 1 -P 2 bash -c
from command output, line 1
from command output, line 2
from command output, line 3
or
$ tr '\n' '\0' < commands.txt | \
    xargs -0 -n 1 -P 2 bash -c
GNU parallel  works similarly, though macOS doesn't ship with it:
$ printf 'echo "from command output, line %d"\n' 1 2 3 | \
    parallel -j 2
from command output, line 1
from command output, line 2
from command output, line 3

Gnomon. Git for Postgres data, built for agents
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
17:09:52 UTC
02:09:52 KST

Git for Postgres data, built for agents. Give an agent its own branch of your real database, as nothing more than a connection string. See every field it changed, merge it into main when it's right, and undo it if it wasn't.
Gnomon runs inside the Postgres you already have. One extension, no copies, no second database.
create extension gnomon;
select gnomon.track('orders');                    -- history starts now, no backfill
select gnomon.create_branch('agent/refunds');
select gnomon.use('agent/refunds');
-- Every query from here on runs on the branch
update orders set status = 'refunded' where id = 4182;
select * from gnomon.diff('agent/refunds');       -- every row and field it changed
select gnomon.merge('agent/refunds');             -- three-way, type-aware, conflicts as rows
select gnomon.undo_merge(1);                      -- and back
- Documentation: https://gnomon.bitsinfra.com/
- Install: create extension gnomon on PostgreSQL 15-18, self-hosted or managed (RDS, Aurora,
Supabase). See Installation.
- New here? The tutorial takes ten minutes.
A branch reads everything from main and keeps only what it changes, so it opens instantly and copies nothing, however big your database is. Give one to every agent run, every pull request and every test.
Your application doesn't change. A branch is a connection string: point a session at it, and the queries it already runs read and write the branch instead of main.
Schema changes come along too. Run a migration on a branch to add a column, rename one or create a table, and main stays as it is until the merge, which brings the new shape and its data across together.
Every branch comes with a diff: each row and field it changed, before and after. Review it in your browser, comment on a single row and settle conflicts side by side. When an agent is done, Gnomon opens the review and sends you the link.
Add checks written in SQL, and Gnomon runs them on the merged result before the merge is allowed to land. An approval covers what you saw: if the branch changes afterwards, it comes back to you.
When main and a branch change the same value, Gnomon doesn't just pick a side. Counts add up, JSON merges key by key and text merges line by line. Anything that truly clashes becomes a conflict, for a person to settle or for a query to resolve.
Branches merge into each other too, not only into main, and a single transaction can be picked from one branch onto another. Undo works the same way: take back one transaction, everything an agent did this afternoon, or a whole merge.
Gnomon keeps the history of every table you track. Read the whole database as it was at any moment, with the queries you already have, and get the same answer every time. When an agent makes a decision, you can see exactly what it saw.
-- The open invoices at the end of October
select gnomon.use('main', '2026-10-31 23:59');
select sum(amount) from invoices where status = 'open';
The same history streams out as it happens, to Kafka, NATS or a webhook, and into dbt as snapshots that keep deleted rows. Older history can move to object storage, and Gnomon brings it back when a query reaches that far.
When a check that used to pass starts failing, gnomon bisect finds the transaction that broke it:
who made it, when and why.
One command gives an agent a database role, a branch of its own and a connection string. That's all it needs, whatever it's built with. It reads and writes its branch, and the database refuses the rest, main included, even when the agent asks for it by name.
# A database role, a branch of its own and a connection string
gnomon agent create support-bot
- It merges only what you allow. Merging is for the people you choose, or for an agent within rules you write: at most twenty rows, only these tables, every check passing. Postgres enforces them, whatever the agent is told.
- Nothing leaves before you approve. Emails, webhooks and payments an agent queues stay on its branch until the merge.
- Every change has a name on it. Each change records the agent, the run and the reason it gave.
- Replay what it saw. Gnomon records the moment of every read, so a replay returns exactly the rows the agent read.
- Try a few ways at once. Start several attempts from the same moment, compare their changes and checks, and keep the best one.
- Stop it, and take it back. One command locks an agent out, closes its branches and undoes what it merged, newest first.
- Ready for your agents. The Gnomon MCP server, on the agent's machine or over HTTP, lets it explore the schema, try a change without keeping it, and rewind its branch to a checkpoint.
The TypeScript and Python SDKs give each task your agents take on a run: a branch of its own, with limits already set. Merge it, send it for review, or let it go.
import { Gnomon } from "@bitsinfra/gnomon";
import { nodePostgres } from "@bitsinfra/gnomon/pg";
const gnomon = new Gnomon({
  app: nodePostgres(appPool),   // your application's own login
  runs: nodePostgres(runsPool), // one login shared by every agent run
});
// A branch for this task, discarded unless it's merged or submitted
await using run = await gnomon.startRun({
  task: "Refund order 4182",
  actor: "support-bot",
});
await run.sql("update orders set status = 'refunded' where id = 4182");
const { checks, changes } = await run.summary();
if (checks.passed && changes.rows <= 20) await run.merge();
else await run.submit({ title: "Refund order 4182" });
Runs can't read each other's branches, even when they share one database login. Bring the agent framework you already use: the Anthropic SDK, the Claude Agent SDK, the Vercel AI SDK, the OpenAI Agents SDK, LangChain or any MCP client gets the same few tools, bound to one run. They work through your drivers and ORMs, from node-postgres and Prisma to psycopg and Django. And in your tests, each test gets its own branch of one seeded database.
npm install @bitsinfra/gnomon      # TypeScript
pip install gnomon-postgres  # Python
Everything is at https://gnomon.bitsinfra.com/, and the same pages live in docs:
- Installation and the tutorial
- Concepts: branches, coordinates, merges
- Agents, runs and the SDKs, the MCP server
- Branches, schema changes, review, merges
- History and time travel, the change feed
- SQL reference, CLI reference, errors
- Operations, security, benchmarks
- llms.txt for coding agents
Gnomon is open source under the PostgreSQL License. Contributions are welcome: see CONTRIBUTING.md. Report security issues privately, as described in SECURITY.md.

Nvidia Employees Petition For Jensen Huang's Return (wired.com) 53

An internal "suggestion box" post submitted before Huang's resignation by a longtime engineer at Nvidia urged the company's board to take employee sentiment into consideration prior to taking any action. The submission has attracted more than 1,600 comments since then, with several asking board members to find a way to bring back Huang.
"If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss," wrote one anonymous commenter. "We are all humans and can make mistakes. Jensen did so many good things and he needs a second chance," wrote another. One employee went as far as sending a companywide email demanding transparency into Huang's exit. "I stand by Jensen," the staffer wrote. "Not because I believe any person is infallible, but because I refuse to believe that standing for fairness must mean standing at a distance from someone whose leadership has meant so much to us."
[...] It's been business as usual inside the company throughout this week, but Huang's resignation left many employees stunned. In the hours after, about two dozen employees posted on LinkedIn in support of Huang, with one describing their faith in his leadership as unwavering. Over the same period, WIRED couldn't identify any posts from Nvidia employees in support of his resignation. One employee who has been at Nvidia for a couple of years and wasn't authorized to speak publicly says they perceive the desire for Huang's reinstatement as widespread. They worry the company will eventually suffer without its founder at the helm and say that the board must bring him back. "Nvidia is Nvidia because of Jensen," the employee tells WIRED.
Many employees had expected a board investigation to conclude before any big changes would occur. For instance, the suggestion-box post called on the board to share its findings with employees and formally solicit their sentiment "about Jensen's continuing as our CEO." Instead, Huang left just as the inquiry had begun. Staffers are seeking to better understand why. "If America fails the man who helped build its future," the employee in the companywide email stated, "then this world does not deserve Jensen Huang."

Apify Was Hacked
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
16:55:59 UTC
01:55:59 KST

Update: Our investigation and response are ongoing.
What we have done since the last update - API tokens are now stored only as hashes, which limits the impact of any similar incident. - Added additional security layers to block the attack paths, including network restrictions between internal services and stronger authentication on internal systems. - We are proceeding with a comprehensive security review of our entire stack. - Continuing log analysis and monitoring for any suspicious activity. - Other work is in progress, and we will share more in upcoming updates.
What we recommend to all users - Restrict access to your storages. In Console, go to Settings → General resource access and set it to Restricted. While it’s open, anyone who knows a storage ID can read that storage. - Be cautious of phishing. Apify will never ask for your password, 2FA codes, or payment details.
Next steps - We will post the next update within 72 hours and publish a post-mortem with the root cause and remediation within a week. - If you have questions, contact Support or reply to the email you received.
Posted Nov 13, 2026 - 17:36 CET
Update
Update: We have notified customers whose personal information or stored data was accessed in this incident. Details are in the email they received. If you haven’t received an email from us, we have not identified your account as affected so far. We will contact any additional customers directly if this changes.
Next update within 24 hours.
Posted Nov 12, 2026 - 17:16 CET
Update
Update: Our investigation is ongoing.
On 9 and 10 November, an unauthorized third party used a compromised credential to obtain a number of account credentials and accessed related Apify accounts. This access path is closed, and we have detected no further activity from the attacker since 11 November.
We are notifying affected users individually. Do not hesitate to reach out to us with questions.
We have: - As a precaution, revoked some OAuth connections to external integrations. Our investigation has not found that these connections could be used as an attack vector. - As a precaution, rotated various critical internal secrets. - We continue with dozens of additional activities, about which we will share information in later updates and a post-mortem. - We notified the relevant authorities.
For you: - If you got an email from us, follow its instructions. - If you build Actors from private GitHub repos, reconnect GitHub: open an Actor built from GitHub → "Change provider" → GitHub → authorize (once per account and per organization). - If prompted, reconnect your integrations.
Next update within 6 hours, and a postmortem follows the remediation.
Posted Nov 12, 2026 - 12:08 CET
Investigating
On 9 and 10 November 2026, an unauthorized third party gained access to parts of Apify’s internal systems. We detected the activity on 11th November at 12:13 UTC, cut off access, and started an investigation that is still ongoing.
We are currently investigating the scope of the incident, including what user data may have been affected. If we determine that your account or data was affected, we will contact you directly.
We have prevented further unauthorized access and added additional monitoring to our systems.
As a precaution, we revoked some OAuth connections to external integrations, including GitHub. Affected users of integrations other than GitHub will be prompted to reconnect via a separate email notification.
If our investigation shows that your tokens were affected, we will rotate them on your behalf and notify you.
Posted Nov 11, 2026 - 20:46 CET
This incident affects: API (api.apify.com), Actors, and General.

North Korean hackers used fake video calls in $400M crypto hack, says FBI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
16:50:22 UTC
01:50:22 KST

North Korean hackers used fake video calls in $400m crypto hack, says FBI
State-sponsored hackers from North Korea have taken more than $400 million in cryptocurrency by using fake video calls to steal people's identities, US authorities say.
More than 900 victims have fallen prey to the scheme, which the FBI refers to as FaceStealer, in the last 18 months, according to the bureau.
"The North Korean regime relies on proceeds from large-scale cryptocurrency theft to fund its illicit weapons program, stealing billions of dollars in recent years," says FBI cyber director Brett Leatherman.
This comes a week after the world's largest crypto exchange Binance revealed that hackers had used fake job interviews to steal a record $6.3bn (£4.7bn) from the company.
US law enforcement agencies attributed the Binance hack, thought to be the largest crypto hack to date, to North Korea-linked actors. Pyongyang has denied this claim.
Authorities say FaceStealer, the latest scheme, "aggressively targets" those who work in the cryptocurrency and web3 sectors, particularly those who are involved in the development and maintenance of open-source software.
According to the FBI and US Justice Department, FaceStealer hackers use fake or hijacked social media accounts to invite potential victims into video calls hosted over "imposter versions" of Zoom, Microsoft Teams, and Google Meet.
Next, the hackers play pre-recorded videos of previous victims the target is acquainted with to simulate a legitimate call – except that the audio would appear to malfunction.
A message then pops up on the target's screen, asking them to "fix" the audio by clicking on a link. This "fix" would eventually download malware onto the target's device, which allows hackers to access their contact lists and apps, widening their pool of potential victims.
Hackers would secretly record these calls and use the recordings as material to scam their new victims, according to the US authorities.
The FBI has described FaceStealer as "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user".
"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships," Patrick Grandy, assistant director in charge of the bureau's Los Angeles field office, said in a statement on Thursday.
"If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough."

This Is the Only Way You Should Be Storing Bread
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
16:15:00 UTC
01:15:00 KST

We Tried 7 Methods for Storing a Loaf of Bread, and 2 Winners Outlasted Them All
Bread is fundamental. It’s delicious, and it’s perfect at any time of day: as toast for breakfast, a sandwich for lunch, or a reliable accompaniment for nearly anything you’re having for dinner. There are few instances where a nice slice of bread doesn’t improve a meal!
Given the usefulness and ubiquity of bread, it definitely hurts a little inside when a loaf starts to go stale or get moldy in your kitchen. It’s probably a safe bet that most of us store our bread in the packaging it comes in — either on the countertop or in a cabinet. But I wanted to test out those methods against a few others that are commonly used as well to find which works best for keeping a loaf fresh.
So, What Is the Best Way to Store Bread?
After testing out several different methods, we found that there were actually two different techniques that worked well: Storing the bread in its original plastic packaging, as well as storing the bread wrapped in reusable food wrap.
A Few Notes About Methodology
- The bread: I live in Brooklyn, so I’m lucky when it comes to local bakeries. Just a five-minute walk away from my apartment is a fabulous French bakery I always pop into when I’m craving a nice loaf of bread. Yes, I’m 100% that girl carrying a baguette and flowers in her tote. But I acknowledge not all of us are that lucky. So I went with something accessible to everyone for this series of tests. I knew Arnold brand country-style white bread would be easy to find basically everywhere in the country, and it’s a popular, delicious loaf that I’d want to keep going back to for slices over the course of the test.
- The tests: I experimented with seven different storage methods for this showdown. Before I began the tests, I made sure to get loaves that all had the same best-by date, so that I was able to judge accurately and fairly. I also routinely opened/took a slice or two out of the package and resealed it, just to simulate the actual experience of someone going through and eating the bread. I thought it wouldn’t be fair or realistic to test them completely untouched.
- The time: I left all the loaves to sit in their designated states for a total of two weeks. Over the course of that time, the bread started to feel varying degrees of staleness, but with every loaf there were no visible signs of molding or age (this particular brand of bread has a lot of sugar and preservatives, which definitely helps out in keeping it fresh).
- The ratings: I judged each method based on two criteria: 1) how fresh (or not) the bread felt, looked, and tasted after the two-week period, and also 2) how convenient or wasteful the packaging method was. In some cases the method of packaging was either tricky to find or it felt superfluous — why go through extra effort when it doesn’t even produce better results?
Bread Storage Method: In Paper Bag
Rating: 2/10
About this method: Paper bags are easy enough to find, and I loved the idea of trying this test because they’re recyclable and have a low environmental impact. I used a standard brown paper bag for this method.
Results: Unfortunately, storing the loaf in a paper bag was definitely the least successful of all the tests. I could feel that bread stored in this way was starting to feel stale after only 3 or 4 days. After one week it was nearly inedible, and after two weeks the entire loaf was rock hard. 
I felt like I had to give it some points for being such an affordable, recyclable option, and if you’re going to be eating your loaf of bread in a day or two, I’d definitely recommend this option! But on the whole, it’s definitely too bad that one of the most environmentally friendly options also happens to be the least successful.
Bread Storage Method: In Paper-Plastic Bread Bag
Rating: 3/10
About this method: Oftentimes, grocery store bakeries will package their bread in these types of bags, a hybrid of paper with a clear plastic film panel so you can still see what’s inside. This method also allows you to partially see the condition of the bread from the outside over time.
Results: Right off the bat, I detracted points away from this method simply for being kind of specific to source. Meaning, I could only get these bags from ordering them online. I guess it was kind of nice to have a window into what was sitting on my counter, but it was only slightly better in keeping the bread fresh than the 100% paper bag, which was terrible.
Bread Storage Method: In Freezer
Rating: 5/10
About this method: I stored the loaf on the top shelf of my freezer, safe from being squished by anything else! I stored the bread in the plastic packaging that usually comes with this brand of bread. Like with the other methods, I checked on the bread over the course of testing to see how stale (not stale) it was becoming. 
Results: I don’t think I’m revealing a groundbreaking scientific discovery when I say that the freezer will obviously last the longest, but I do think it’s worth noting that it’s the least flexible method of storage. Not to mention that a slice of bread defrosted from the freezer always tastes a little off to me. So I think in order to avoid that, you’re kind of forced to toast your bread before eating it, which I suppose is a bit limiting if you just want a sandwich without toasted bread.
Bread Storage Method: In Bread Box (out of Plastic)
Rating: 6/10
About this method: My first impression of this particular bread box is that it takes up a lot of space. It doesn’t seem necessary for shelf-stable grocery store bread that already comes in its own packaging, and because there was so much space in this bread box, that meant there was a lot of air trapped inside, too. 
But I suppose the nice thing about a bread box (that’s an actual freestanding box, as opposed to something built into your cabinets or countertop — retro!) is the fact that I can theoretically use it for other temporary storage if I wasn’t using it for a loaf of bread. 
Results: The slices didn’t get as stale as I thought they would after two weeks, but they did dry out a little bit. I do think, though, that bread boxes can serve as kitchen storage for other things when they’re not being used for bread. So it’s actually not as much of a single-use item as it sounds!
Bread Storage Method: In Refrigerator
Rating: 8/10
About this method: I kept the loaf of bread on the bottom shelf of my fridge, toward the front. Every few days I would open up the package, take out a slice, and close it up again using the plastic bread clip that came with the package.
Results: Given what we know about refrigeration preserving things, if this test lasted a month rather than 2 weeks, I think the fridge method might be the winner. I detracted one point over matters of real estate. It felt a little annoying to dedicate precious fridge space to something that could technically live on the counter.
I detracted a second point because in keeping your bread in the fridge, you obviously have to be OK with cold bread as your default bread temperature. But otherwise, this was a great option that kept the bread fresh for the whole length of the two-week test.
Bread Storage Method: In Reusable Bread Wrap
Rating: 9.5/10
About this method: This was my first experience using/storing things in beeswax wrap, and I actually really liked it. It’s definitely easier to manage than plastic wrap (particularly unwrapping and rewrapping), and it looks nicer on the counter too. I made sure to give the beeswax food wrap a rinse, and scrunched it up a bit just to encourage it to become a bit more pliable. I kept the bread slices bundled in the wrap on the kitchen counter out of the sun.
Results: I was glad to see that the bread stayed really fresh using this method and didn’t dry out even as I unwrapped/rewrapped it several times throughout the test. The downside is that managing the wrap to keep the bread protected sometimes meant squishing or deforming the bread a bit to make sure the wrap was sealed. It also felt a little silly to take the bread out of its original packaging just to put it in other packaging. But if you’re buying a fresh bakery loaf that might just come in a paper sleeve, this is definitely a good storage option.
Bread Storage Method: In Original Plastic Bag in Pantry or Cabinet
Rating: 10/10
About this method: The particular Arnold loaf I tested came wrapped in a layer of plastic that was sealed and airtight, then that little package was placed inside the plastic bag with all the printing on it, tied together with one of those standard bread clips. I opened the bread up, took out a slice, and rewrapped it as I normally would, twisting the bag closed and fastening it with the same clip. 
Results: Kind of unsurprisingly, this worked out to be the best test. It’s worth emphasizing, however, that this particular brand of bread does come with additional packaging which some other brands do not. That said, keeping the loaf of bread in a dark cabinet away from the light kept it fresh, free of moisture or humidity, and the original plastic packaging did its job well. I’m willing to bet there are scientific stress tests run on this stuff! Consider me sold.
