ChatGPT’s new Wallet lets AI agents shop and check out for you
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
16:00:12 UTC
01:00:12 KST

ChatGPT can now shop for you. On Friday, OpenAI launched a new wallet feature that lets its agents, called dots, help you decide what to buy and then complete the purchase on your behalf. The option will initially be available to a limited number of U.S. ChatGPT Pro subscribers, ages 18 and up, with a U.S.-issued Visa, Mastercard, or American Express.
OpenAI says that users are fully in control of the purchases, as they’re able to review the items, the total, and the payment card that will be used to complete the transaction before the checkout occurs. In addition, users will approve the purchases directly with a security check like Face ID or a fingerprint, which creates a record of their transaction.
The addition comes as consumer-facing AI tools are working to find the right product-market fit, in terms of how they can help people be more efficient as they complete various tasks. While companies like DoorDash and Uber have so far reported low numbers of orders placed by AI agents, Meta’s Muse, which now has over 10 million users, has seen AI-assisted shopping become a top activity on its app.
Other personal AI agents, like Instinct, also support making purchases on users’ behalf.
It’s no surprise, then, that OpenAI would want to enter this space more with an agentic shopping feature of its own.
To get started, ChatGPT subscribers add their eligible payment cards to the new ChatGPT Wallet, then ask their dot to help with a purchase. (This setup is a one-time thing; users don’t have to re-enter their payment information every time they buy.) When it’s time to transact, ChatGPT’s Wallet charges the selected card using a payment token, which is a substitute for the user’s actual card details, so that information isn’t shared with either the dot or the merchant.
The merchant will automatically receive what it needs to fulfill the order from the dot, like the shipping address and contact information, OpenAI says. The company notes that customers who shop via their dots will still be able to earn their credit card rewards and be able to take advantage of protections that typically come with cards, like being able to dispute transactions.
The limited availability at launch will allow OpenAI to test and improve the feature and its capabilities before rolling it out more broadly. In the future, the company plans to offer users more control over the feature by allowing them to set spending limits or configure other parameters around purchase categories or time periods, so they can pre-approve purchases that their agent can then handle more autonomously.
It also aims to make the agentic shopping feature an option at a broader number of supported retailers. This could be a challenge in some areas, where retailers are blocking AI agents from using their site; on the shopping front, the largest retailer to ban AI agents is Amazon, as it aims to steer users to its own AI agent, Alexa, instead.
To help retailers adapt to agentic commerce, OpenAI also published a Commerce Partner Playbook that offers tools and guidance to sites that want to support customers using agents.

Parents and Teachers Decry Google Pledge To Yank Search If Schools Block AI

Parents and Teachers Decry Google Pledge To Yank Search If Schools Block AI (theguardian.com) 31
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from The Guardian: Parents and teachers across the US are criticizing Google's announcement that schools choosing to block AI tools could also lose access to Google Search. Google announced last week a range of new AI tools for schools and classrooms, alongside options allowing teachers and administrators to restrict their use. The company said schools should have control over whether students use AI in the classroom, but that schools that choose to block Google's AI tools will also lose access to its search engine. [...] Parents and teachers across the country raised concerns to the Guardian about AI's potential to weaken students' critical-thinking skills and the growing influence of big tech in education. Some called on schools to consider alternative search engines, while others argued that students should be taught how to use AI responsibly rather than prevented from accessing it altogether.
Jen Atay, a 51-year-old teacher in Hawaii, lambasted Google's approach, saying: "When the software is free, you are the product, and it is inappropriate for Google to test or develop AI with minors. This should not be allowed at all." A 44-year-old father of two boys in Amissville, Virginia, said he worries that "AI will weaken people's ability to think critically. I use AI in my work, but it would not be effective if I did not have well over a decade of experience under my belt. Even the best models are often flat-out wrong in their responses." [...] Others questioned why schools should depend on Google at all.  An 84-year-old retiree and mother in Maryland, said: "Google decided to follow the evil path to riches. When they started, they were great. Today they are simply evil money grubbers  Schools should tell Google to go pound sand and simply have kids use DuckDuckGo or one of the other search engines  There is nothing Google offers that is not available from some other source." [...]
Not everyone believes blocking AI is the answer. Some argued that schools should focus on teaching students how to use the technology responsibly. A 67-year-old emerging technology teacher in Texas said: "I do not blame Google. AI is not going away. I've been very frustrated this year trying to teach AI literacy with AI blocked. Schools need to teach how to properly use AI, not block it." A California resident echoed that view, arguing that schools could use Google's AI tools to teach students to question the information they receive. "Google Search has existed since the 90s and it's crazy of them to completely remove it instead of just removing Gemini. That being said, I would like to see schools actually permit the use of Gemini and use it as an opportunity to show students how its sources aren't always valid and you can't trust everything an AI tells you," they said.

Comparing Jensen to Steve Jobs Is Stupid
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
15:43:09 UTC
00:43:09 KST

Comparing Jensen to Steve Jobs is Stupid
Time for a history lesson.
Many are criticizing Nvidia for kicking out Jensen, saying it is akin to Apple "firing" Steve Jobs in 1985. While the comparison is wrong—Jobs was pushed out for much less nefarious reasons—it also implies that Apple would have succeeded earlier instead of waiting for Jobs' return in 1997.
This misses the entire redemption arc Jobs goes through at NeXT Computer. He leaves Apple and takes his pride and poor management skills with him. In time, all of his NeXT co-founders leave, repelled by Steve's toxicity. He burns an absurd amount of money on hard-headed decisions, while building a beautiful workstation that ultimately few buy.
When NeXT goes nearly bankrupt and lays off a mass portion of the company, Jobs is publicly humiliated—rightly so, because there was no one else to blame. NeXT was intentionally a one-man show.
It's in NeXT's software pivot that Jobs begins to redeem himself. He becomes a stronger collaborator, working more harmoniously with his teams as they humbly keep the company alive. It eventually leads them to the Apple acquisition, and Jobs returns as a completely different person than when he was oustered.
I highly recommend Geoffrey Cain's book, Steve Jobs in Exile, which is a vivid account of this forgotten episode. NeXT is everything for Steve Jobs—there would most likely be no Apple today without his fall from grace.

====================================================================================================

Why is Google cutting off Search from AI-free schools? Here are some theories
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
15:29:10 UTC
00:29:10 KST

Why is Google cutting off Search from AI-free schools? Here are some theories
By Sequoia Carrillo
Friday, November 13, 2026 • 5:00 AM EST
Catch up on what you missed with our Education newsletter, sent weekly.
Google is a big part of the school day for millions of students. It's not only a search engine. It's a hub for sharing documents and turning in homework.
An announcement this month makes it clear: Google wants AI to be a part of that education ecosystem.
Many parents do not.
"Parents are angry," said Jodi Carreon, a mom in Southern California. "They're very angry."
Related Story: NPR
When Carreon, who also runs the advocacy group Schools Beyond Screens, first read that Google was letting schools turn off AI features for students, she was excited. Her group is part of the parent-led movement demanding limits on technology in the classroom.
But even as districts including Los Angeles Unified, Seattle Public Schools and New York City Public Schools recently agreed to bans on AI in the classroom, Google did not. If schools don't want AI Overviews to appear when students use its flagship Search tool, the company said, they will lose access to Google Search altogether. Even advocates who want less tech in schools know losing the ability to "Google" something will be an issue for teachers.
In a post on X, New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani said Google is using "its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools," and Democratic Sen. Ed Markey of Massachusetts wrote to Google's CEO demanding the company reverse its policy.
A baffling move
Tracy Pizzo worked at Google for more than a decade before leaving the company in 2022. Much of her time at the company was focused on education and ethical AI standards. Google's move this month baffled her, especially coming at a time when young people's use of AI is under such scrutiny.
She has a theory about why the tech giant is taking this stance: It could be linked to two recent court cases Google fended off in D.C.
Google was sued by big online publishers who said adding AI overview to search negatively affected their business. The publishers sued under antitrust laws, and their argument hinged on convincing the court that AI Overviews was a separate product from Google Search.
"The judge decided that Search and AI are an integrated product," Pizzo said, and subsequently dismissed the cases. "Now if Google were to provide an off switch for schools only … that's evidence that they can be separated. And that undermines the win they just had in court."
When asked for comment, Google directed NPR to its original announcement, where the company said, "Our goal is simple: provide school leaders the control and confidence to use AI products in classrooms as they see fit." The company did not respond to additional questions by NPR about any connection between its announcement and recent antitrust cases.
NPR asked antitrust experts about this theory. They weren't entirely convinced, but also didn't dismiss it altogether.
The "AI or nothing" approach
In any case, Google's AI Overviews doesn't get high marks from some watchdogs.
Common Sense Media, a nonprofit that evaluates how teens interact with technology, recently gave the product an "unacceptable risk" rating. Among other things, Common Sense dinged the tool for unsafe content and citing false information.
The day after the announcement, Google came under fire for another AI product in schools. Parents in North Carolina sued the company after Google's chatbot, Gemini, helped their 13-year-old daughter plan her suicide and funeral on her school-issued Chromebook.
Even some of Google's longtime partners are surprised by the company's current position, including superintendent Hank Thiele in Illinois.
Related Story: NPR
"It may have caused a lot of damage in the trust that Google has built in education," he said.
Thiele has worked with Google Classroom for almost two decades and says the company has always been a great partner. His district, made up entirely of high school students, is in the process of figuring out AI for its students. As an early adopter of tech in the classroom, he wants to be on top of it with AI.
He said he thinks the company will listen to people's feedback but they bungled the messaging.
"Google should have just plainly said, 'Our bad,'" Thiele said. "That would have been the appropriate response to a school."
The "AI or nothing" approach, as Thiele put it, did not land well.
Edited by Nirvi Shah and Nicole Cohen
Google is a financial supporter of NPR.

Apple announces release dates for the first Apple Home Collection smart devices
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
15:22:14 UTC
00:22:14 KST

The first few smart home devices and accessories that Apple co-developed with LG Electronics and Schneider Electric for its new Apple Home Collection are now available for preorder through the company’s online store, with delivery expected in mid-December. Apple announced the collection a month ago as part of a stronger push into the smart home space that’s led by its new Siri AI-powered HomeView smart display which is also now available starting today.
Apple announces release dates for the first Apple Home Collection smart devices
You can preorder several of the devices today, with shipping expected to start in mid-December.
Of all the Apple Home Collection devices currently listed on its online store, only a handful of LG accessories are available for preorder. These include the $299.95 LG Outdoor Cam and $199.95 Indoor Cam, plus the $249.95 LG Video Doorbell, which all show an estimated shipping timeline of December 11th to December 17th. The rest of the collection, including the $399.95 LG Floodlight Cam, $349.95 LG Smart Deadbolt Lock, $199.95 LG Smart Thermostat, $199.95 Schneider Home Smart Thermostat, and $39.95 Schneider Home Room Sensor, aren’t yet available for preorder and don’t have an estimated ship date.
The devices in the Apple Home Collection don’t include exclusive features or functionality, but through the partnership, Apple has streamlined the setup process so it’s as easy as tapping them with your iPhone to get them running. The devices in the collection also use Thread to remain connected should they lose access to the internet or your Wi-Fi network, and they can automatically update their network credentials if you make changes to your wireless network.

====================================================================================================

Palmfrets, Minnesota guitarist's invention, strikes a new chord
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
15:08:07 UTC
00:08:07 KST

FERGUS FALLS — The back room of Stella’s restaurant in Fergus Falls filled with people on Oct. 28, all there to see musician Ben Ranson play.
Ranson, who grew up in Sebeka, Minnesota, and currently lives in Sandstone, is a regular performer at venues across the region, including in Fergus Falls. But Oct. 28 was no ordinary gig. That night, Ranson revealed something he’d been working on for years.
ADVERTISEMENT
It wasn’t a new song or a clever guitar lick, it was an invention called “palmfrets” — something Ranson believes could change the way guitarists use their instrument. It allows players to play new bass lines or melodies using their palm, a special set of frets called palmfrets and a technique called palmfretting.
The invention
The idea for palmfrets came to Ranson over five years ago.
Ranson had already been doing musical experiments — even constructing organ-like foot pedals and learning the seven string guitar — all in a quest to get more notes out of the guitar, specifically more bass notes.
He said the reason behind the invention, initially, was entirely artistic. He wasn’t thinking much about developing a commercial product.
“That was not on my mind at all,” Ranson said. “And it wasn’t really until I made one that really worked that it sort of dawned on me like, oh, this is something that people are really going to want.”
Ranson made an early-stage version of the invention in his garage, and said that, at first, he didn't recognize the importance of what he had created. He set it aside and left it in a corner for awhile — years, even.
Eventually, he returned to his forgotten creation. It was then that he began to hear the music.
ADVERTISEMENT
He drafted his ideas and designs on paper, and enlisted the help of engineers. Together, they created new prototypes made from 3D printed plastic and laser-cut metal parts.
It was during that time when Ranson said something shifted.
“It hit me, like, I’ve really done it.”
Ranson knew the plastic and metal version was not practical, and only lasted a matter of weeks. But it worked, and from there, Ranson said the engineering became more serious.
Serious enough that Ranson applied for a patent, which he received on Oct. 6, 2026.
Ranson had an inkling that this was something special, and recalled the first time he started playing the guitar with a solid prototype.
“I was like, ‘I’m doing things that Chet Atkins never did,’” he joked. “I just had this sort of realization, like, other people are going to want to do this.”
ADVERTISEMENT
Fast forward to Oct. 28 in Fergus Falls, and Ranson’s invention is now in the hands of other guitar players.
How it works
Using the pisiform bone — a small, pea-shaped bone in the wrist, near the bottom of the palm — players depress a string on the palmfret, an extra fret or set of frets near the wrist of the picking hand. The player can then use the thumb of their picking hand to pluck the string fretted by the pisiform bone.
This frees up the fretting hand — the hand which frets strings on the neck of the guitar — meaning the player can move up and down the fret board more freely, and fret other strings because the pisiform bone is doing some of the hard work.
“So if you want to, you can move (the fretting hand) anywhere you want, play any other notes that you want to play,” said Ranson.
Essentially, guitarists could play a bass line with their pisiform bone and play other melodies or chords that would have otherwise been, literally, out of reach.
Learning curve
Ranson had some rules when designing palmfrets.
“One of the basic principles in inventing it has been: if it interferes with the normal way that you would play the guitar, it’s no good,” he said.
ADVERTISEMENT
“So the basic principle is you have to keep all of the function of the guitar as people know it,” he explained. “It has to add, but it can’t take away. That’s the basic principle.”
Still, adding another element to the guitar introduces a learning curve, even for players as skilled as Ranson.
He gave proof of the learning curve by showing a callus on his pisofone bone — a callus reminiscent of the ones beginning guitarists get on their fingers when first learning to play — and compared his skill-level with the palmfrets to being a teenager playing in their first band and only knowing six songs.
“That is the stage I am at now, frankly.”
During the reveal, other guitar players tested out the palmfrets, taking turns passing around acoustic guitars, electric, and even the bass.
Darren Quam, a musician from Battle Lake, who was in attendance on Oct. 28, tried his hand — or palm — at the new invention.
“At first, you know, it took me a second to kind of understand how you could use it,” Quam said. “But then — especially when Ben had us come up and try it — then, you know, trying it with my own hands, I was like, okay, I see how this could be very useful in certain situations.”
ADVERTISEMENT
Quam also commented on the learning curve.
“It’s not a whole new instrument, but it’s a whole new way of playing.”
Despite the new way of playing, during the reveal show, Quam seemed to catch on quickly, with Ranson joking “we’re going to have to take this away from you.”
From a practical standpoint as a gigging musician, Quam thought the invention would be useful for solo players.
“I think it really is probably the most useful in like a solo situation where you’re playing and you can accompany yourself, especially with those lower notes where you can fret with your palm hand and change the root note or the chords, and still be able to have the freedom to play on the higher strings as if there was someone else accompanying you, you know, like a bass player.
“So it’s kind of like you’ve got a bass player on your palm all of a sudden.”
And speaking of bass players, Ranson believes the bass guitar, as it now is, could become “obsolete.”
ADVERTISEMENT
“But for a great reason, as far as bassists are concerned,” he said.
With the palmfrets, a bass player could play a bass line beneath a bass line, or play chords while playing a bass line. Meaning a bass player could accompany themselves, or add chords when playing with a group.
“As a guitarist, you might want to play some jazz song, or whatever song you want — it’s got a clear melody, but you just need to have chords underneath it so that people can understand what that melody is.”
With palmfrets, the bass player can play the chord and the bass line, rooting the listener, while allowing the lead guitarist to focus on the melody.
Ranson explained that he expects the invention to make the bass into a very powerful instrument, “kind of like a piano.”
The future
For Ranson, thinking about how the invention will be used in the future is all speculation.
“But I think it’s going to be really interesting. I mean, I can’t wait,” said Ranson.
The true potential of the invention, according to Ranson, might not be revealed for many years.
“Let’s say 15 years from now, there’s going to be some kid who got one of these as a teenager. Then you’ll know what it can do.”

====================================================================================================

Camotes Enmielados (Mexican Candied Sweet Potatoes)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
15:00:00 UTC
00:00:00 KST

Camotes Enmielados (Mexican Candied Sweet Potatoes)
Tender Mexican sweet potatoes simmered in a rich, cinnamon-spiced piloncillo syrup.
Serves6 to 8
Prep10 minutes
Cook1 hour to 1 hour 10 minutes
Why You’ll Love Camotes Enmielados
- The syrup is everything. Rich, glossy, and full of caramel-like flavor, it’s so good you’ll want to drizzle it over everything.
- Incredibly easy to make. With just a handful of ingredients and minimal prep, this traditional Mexican dessert (often also breakfast) delivers maximum flavor with very little effort.
- It makes for the ultimate dessert. The sweet potatoes become incredibly soft and tender as they simmer in a warm, cinnamon-spiced piloncillo syrup.
When I was growing up, camotes enmielados were one of those desserts that made a regular appearance on our table — almost always with a glass of cold milk. Sometimes we even ate them for breakfast. It’s a traditional Mexican dish made with sweet potatoes simmered in a rich, cinnamon-spiced piloncillo (sugar) syrup until they’re incredibly soft and tender. It’s also a recipe that holds a special place in my heart because it was one of the many things my abuela Gutty would make and sell to help her family get ahead. Like so many other recipes I grew up with, it’s a reminder of how something made with just a handful of simple ingredients can become something truly incredible.
The key to really good camotes enmielados is patience. You want the sweet potatoes to become so soft they almost melt in your mouth, while being completely covered in a thick, sticky piloncillo syrup. As they slowly simmer with cinnamon and cloves, the syrup develops a caramel-like flavor that soaks into every bite. To this day I believe the best way to enjoy them is warm, with plenty of the delicious syrup spooned over the top and with a splash (or more) of cold milk or heavy cream (my mom Maria’s favorite) to balance out the sweetness. It’s simple, comforting, and one of those traditional Mexican desserts that never gets old.
Straight from Our Recipe Tester
“I used to say ‘I don’t feel very strongly about sweet potatoes,’ but I might have to change my tune because of this recipe. The texture is so velvety and tender, and the flavor is so cinnamon-y and rich from the piloncillo. I loved it with a drizzle of cream, but I couldn’t help daydreaming about what it might be like to serve them with a scoop of melty vanilla ice cream. Pure bliss.” —Rachel, October 2026
Why You Should Trust Me
As a Mexican American recipe developer, I grew up surrounded by traditional Mexican dishes like these camotes enmielados. With over a decade of culinary experience, I love sharing recipes that introduce people to the rich flavors and traditions of Mexican cooking.
Key Ingredients in Camotes Enmielados
- Piloncillo: An unrefined Mexican cane sugar with a caramel-like flavor that gives the syrup its signature richness. It comes in many different shapes and sizes and is often referred to as panela. The lighter colored piloncillo will melt faster than the dark (more crystalized) version. Both work for this recipe.
- Sweet potatoes: The star of the recipe; they become incredibly soft and tender as they simmer and soak up the rich, sweet syrup.
- Cinnamon sticks: Infuse the syrup with a warm, fragrant spice that perfectly complements the sweetness of the piloncillo and sweet potatoes.
How to Make Camotes Enmielados
- Make the piloncillo syrup. Combine the water, piloncillo, cinnamon stick, and cloves in a large pot. Simmer, stirring occasionally, until the piloncillo dissolves.
- Add the sweet potatoes. Arrange the sweet potatoes in an even layer in the bottom of the pot (they don’t have to be submerged in liquid). Bring mixture to a boil, then reduce the heat to a gentle simmer. Cover and cook until sweet potatoes soften.
- Simmer until tender. Flip the sweet potatoes and continue cooking, uncovered, until they’re very tender and the syrup is thickened and glossy.
- Finish the syrup. Gently move the sweet potatoes aside, stir in the baking soda, and remove the pot from the heat. Discard the cinnamon stick and cloves.
- Serve. Spoon the sweet potatoes into bowls, drizzle with the warm piloncillo syrup, and finish with a splash of milk or cream if desired.
Helpful Swaps
Swap piloncillo for dark brown sugar for a similar rich, molasses-like sweetness.
Camotes Enmielados Recipe
Tender Mexican sweet potatoes simmered in a rich, cinnamon-spiced piloncillo syrup.
Prep time 10 minutes
Cook time 1 hour to 1 hour 10 minutes
Serves 6 to 8
Nutritional Info
Ingredients
- 1 1/2 cups water
- 8 ounces piloncillo or 1 cup packed dark brown sugar
- 1 (3-inch) cinnamon stick
- 2 whole cloves
- 1 1/2 pounds medium sweet potatoes (4), ends trimmed and halved lengthwise
- 1/4 teaspoon baking soda
- Whole milk or heavy cream, for serving (optional)
Instructions
- Place 1 1/2 cups water, 8 ounces piloncillo, 1 (3-inch) cinnamon stick, and 2 whole cloves in a large pot. Bring to a simmer over medium heat, stirring occasionally to help break the piloncillo down, 5 to 6 minutes.
- Add 1 1/2 pounds halved medium sweet potatoes in an even layer (it’s OK if they’re not submerged). Bring to a boil. Reduce the heat to maintain a gentle simmer. Cover and cook, stirring occasionally, for 30 minutes.
- Flip the sweet potatoes. Cook uncovered, moving them around as needed so they cook evenly, until the sweet potatoes are very tender, and the sauce is thickened, syrupy, and coats the back of a spoon without running off, 30 to 45 minutes.
- Gently move the sweet potatoes to one side. Add 1/4 teaspoon baking soda to the syrup and stir to combine (it will bubble up). Turn off the heat. Remove and discard the cinnamon stick and cloves. Serve in a bowl, drizzled with any remaining syrup and whole milk if desired.
Recipe Notes
General tip: The sweet potatoes can be cut crosswise into 3-inch pieces instead of halves. Cook until very tender.
Storage: Refrigerate leftovers in an airtight container for up to 4 days. Serve cold, or rewarm in the microwave.

EU Digital Identity Wallet rollout: every EU state on one board

When must banks and other services accept the wallet?
Banks and other private services that must use strong customer authentication have to accept the wallet from 24 Dec 2027 (eIDAS 2, Art. 5f(2)). Micro and small enterprises are exempt.

The board
Stage is the programme's overall status. The six steps are the evidence we've confirmed, so a country can have a pilot on record and still be at an earlier stage overall.

Not an EU Member State. Not bound by eIDAS 2 deadlines.
Ukraine (Diia)
candidate (accession negotiations opened 2024-06)
What citizens use today
Diia combines a mobile app and a public-services portal, launched in 2020. The app includes an ID card, biometric passport, driving licence, vehicle registration, tax number and child's birth certificate. Its digital passports have domestic legal equivalence to paper documents under the law effective 23 August 2021. Diia.Signature supports signing documents and authenticating to services.
EUDI link
The European Commission's pilot overview, updated 22 July 2026, includes Ukraine among participants testing EUDI wallets and cross-border uses. The first four pilots began in April 2023. This programme-level evidence does not establish completed Diia interoperability tests or acceptance across the EU.

The timeline
Every dated milestone we could source, 2022 → 2029. Solid = happened. Hollow = announced target. The two vertical lines are the EU legal baselines: wallets to be offered by 24 Dec 2026; specified private services to accept them by 24 Dec 2027.
Who is building it
State agencies, awarded suppliers and shared EU pilot consortia, only where the evidence ties them to delivering or operating the wallet. For the wider market of identity-verification vendors, see the EU digital identity explorer.

How to read this
Stage
Planning → Legal basis → Procurement → In development → Pilot / testing → Public beta → Publicly available → EUDI certified. Stage is the programme's overall status. The six steps are the evidence we've confirmed, so a country can have a pilot on record and still be at an earlier stage overall. A target date is never counted as done.
Existing eID is not the wallet
The stage column shows EUDI wallet progress; Live today names an existing generally available national eID or app, not a certified EUDI wallet. Mature national logins (MyGovID, Smart-ID, ID Austria, SPID…) do not advance the wallet ladder unless the state has formally made that app the wallet vehicle.
Not publicly confirmed
We looked and could not find primary evidence. It is a gap in evidence, not a finding of failure.
Outside the EU
Non-EU comparators show relevant national wallet experience and EUDI links in a separate watch list, excluded from the 27-state board, statistics, timeline, organisation and consortium counts.
Baseline
Regulation (EU) 2024/1183 (eIDAS 2): Member States provide at least one wallet within 24 months of the implementing acts (24 Dec 2026); specified private relying parties accept wallets by 24 Dec 2027. A later national target is recorded as announced, not as a breach.

EU Digital Identity Wallet rollout: every EU state on one board

EU Digital Identity Wallet rollout: every EU state on one board
Updated 15 Oct 2026
Track EU states' wallets, their builders and the evidence behind each claim.
Europe by wallet evidence grade
Live now: Denmark, Italy · Ireland: Pilot / testing, grade D.
Choose a country to explore its score, stage and builders. Grades describe published evidence, not legal compliance.Use the grid. Grades describe published evidence, not legal compliance.
When must banks and other services accept the wallet?
Banks and other private services that must use strong customer authentication have to accept the wallet from 24 Dec 2027 (eIDAS 2, Art. 5f(2)). Micro and small enterprises are exempt.

Country evidence checked 7 Oct 2026 to 15 Oct 2026; each detail gives its own check date. "Not publicly confirmed" means we could not find primary evidence, not that it hasn't happened. The Scorecard compares published evidence across five pillars. Next quarterly review: Jan 2027.
IE
Where Ireland sits
Pilot / testing Government Digital Wallet in invitation-only beta built by OGCIO; eIDAS omnibus bill still at Heads stage; wider launch stated for 2026.
Next: 2026 Wider public launch of the Government Digital Wallet.
Banks and other private services that must use strong customer authentication have to accept the wallet from 24 Dec 2027 (eIDAS 2, Art. 5f(2)). Micro and small enterprises are exempt.
The board
Stage is the programme's overall status. The six steps are the evidence we've confirmed, so a country can have a pilot on record and still be at an earlier stage overall.
Compared with the EU’s 24 Dec 2026 deadline for national wallets.
evidenced announced target not publicly confirmed not applicableLadder: Law in force · Builder named · Pilot evidenced · Public beta · Generally available · EUDI certified
No countries match this search and filter. Clear the search or choose another stage.
Select a country for its full record: wallet, who's accountable, builders, every dated milestone and the sources.
Not an EU Member State. Not bound by eIDAS 2 deadlines.
Ukraine (Diia)
candidate (accession negotiations opened 2024-06)
What citizens use today
Diia combines a mobile app and a public-services portal, launched in 2020. The app includes an ID card, biometric passport, driving licence, vehicle registration, tax number and child's birth certificate. Its digital passports have domestic legal equivalence to paper documents under the law effective 23 August 2021. Diia.Signature supports signing documents and authenticating to services.
EUDI link
The European Commission's pilot overview, updated 22 July 2026, includes Ukraine among participants testing EUDI wallets and cross-border uses. The first four pilots began in April 2023. This programme-level evidence does not establish completed Diia interoperability tests or acceptance across the EU.
National wallet milestones
Law in force
Builder named
Pilot evidenced
Public beta
Generally available
Certification evidenced
Dots use the board legend; national availability does not establish EUDI certification.
Estonia's planned mRiik app was based on Diia in 2023; that lineage does not establish EUDI readiness.
Romania: MAI publishes RO Wallet calendar and onboarding phases
Czechia: National wallet certification scheme concept published
Germany: BfDI: January version not yet a full EUDI wallet
Estonia: Cabinet approves bill; 2028 rollout announced
Spain: Draft EUDI governance Royal Decree published for consultation
Finland: Wallet and digital identity credential acts enter into force
The timeline
Every dated milestone we could source, 2022 → 2029. Solid = happened. Hollow = announced target. The two vertical lines are the EU legal baselines: wallets to be offered by 24 Dec 2026; specified private services to accept them by 24 Dec 2027.
Who is building it
State agencies, awarded suppliers and shared EU pilot consortia, only where the evidence ties them to delivering or operating the wallet. For the wider market of identity-verification vendors, see the EU digital identity explorer.
Shared EU pilots and consortia
POTENTIAL · 19 states
Large-scale pilot: eGov, banking, telco, mobile driving licence, e-signature, e-prescription.
APTITUDE · 12 states
Second-wave pilot (2025–) continuing cross-border wallet testing.
EWC · 20 states
EU Digital Identity Wallet Consortium: travel credentials, payments, organisational identity.
WE BUILD · 22 states
Second-wave pilot (2025–) on business and travel use cases.
DC4EU · 20 states
Education and social-security credentials.
NOBID · 4 states
Nordic–Baltic pilot focused on wallet-based payments.
mDL4EU · 1 states
Mobile driving licence cross-border pilot.
EUDIW-PACT · 1 states
Organisations named in national delivery
Active in more than one state (1)
State bodies delivering their national wallet (106)Suppliers, banks and operators named in one state (40)
How to read this
Stage
Planning → Legal basis → Procurement → In development → Pilot / testing → Public beta → Publicly available → EUDI certified. Stage is the programme's overall status. The six steps are the evidence we've confirmed, so a country can have a pilot on record and still be at an earlier stage overall. A target date is never counted as done.
Existing eID is not the wallet
The stage column shows EUDI wallet progress; Live today names an existing generally available national eID or app, not a certified EUDI wallet. Mature national logins (MyGovID, Smart-ID, ID Austria, SPID…) do not advance the wallet ladder unless the state has formally made that app the wallet vehicle.
Not publicly confirmed
We looked and could not find primary evidence. It is a gap in evidence, not a finding of failure.
Outside the EU
Non-EU comparators show relevant national wallet experience and EUDI links in a separate watch list, excluded from the 27-state board, statistics, timeline, organisation and consortium counts.
Baseline
Regulation (EU) 2024/1183 (eIDAS 2): Member States provide at least one wallet within 24 months of the implementing acts (24 Dec 2026); specified private relying parties accept wallets by 24 Dec 2027. A later national target is recorded as announced, not as a breach.

Uber confirms hackers stole thousands of drivers’ license information in data breach
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:57:55 UTC
23:57:55 KST

Uber is notifying thousands of drivers that their personal and driver’s license information was stolen during an October data breach at one of the company’s tech providers.
In notices filed this week with several U.S. attorneys general, including California, the ride-hail giant said it was notified on October 9 by the provider that hackers broke in and stole internal credentials, which were used to access Uber’s data.
The company said it subsequently began notifying affected drivers that their personal information was stored with the provider.
TechCrunch has learned that the provider is Fireworks AI, which lets developers access and use open AI models. Fireworks reported a data breach last month in which hackers stole its customers’ information, including billing data and some private AI datasets.
Online productivity startup Notion confirmed its data was also stolen in the Fireworks hack.
“The notices to drivers are in relation to a security incident impacting one of our third-party vendors, Fireworks.ai, which had access to some Uber data,” an Uber spokesperson told TechCrunch, deferring our questions to Fireworks.
Uber did not say how many customers are affected by the breach, nor how many drivers it was notifying about the incident.
When reached by email, a spokesperson for Fireworks confirmed that the company identified a security issue involving unauthorized access to some of its systems, which affected customer data, but the spokesperson did not address our questions about the incident.
According to data breach notices seen by TechCrunch, the stolen data includes driver’s licenses of 2,567 individuals in Massachusetts, 3,430 people across South Carolina, and 1,873 residents of Rhode Island. A filing with Illinois showed that some state residents had their driver’s license information stolen, but that the number of affected people was pending. Uber also notified drivers who live in California, but did not say how many had their information stolen.
The number is expected to rise as more data breach notifications roll in.
Fireworks attributed its breach to a financially motivated cybercriminal group whose goals are to sell stolen tokens for accessing large language models, or LLMs.
No known cybercriminal group has taken credit for the data theft.
This is Uber’s second known data breach this year. The company reported a security incident at its Uber Freight truck shipping unit, for which an extortion gang took credit for stealing data.
Updated with a response from Fireworks.

Seán Doran’s ‘Crimson Star’ Tours the Raging Surface of the Sun
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:53:38 UTC
23:53:38 KST

Seán Doran’s ‘Crimson Star’ Tours the Raging Surface of the Sun
Celestial filmmaker Seán Doran is back with a new project that interprets stellar snapshots from NASA’s Solar Dynamics Observatory. The footage-like video tours the sun and reveals its fiery surface complete with brilliant solar flares, all depicted in a distinctive crimson.
To create the film, Doran attributed various pinks and reds to different wavelengths, creating an alluring interpretation of the fiery orb. Doran stitched together NASA’s captures, or more accurately, he says, the images were “conformed, repaired, graded, blended, rescaled and retimed” into a seamless film. “Crimson Star” is one of several astral projects he’s undertaken in recent years, and you can keep up with his latest works on YouTube. (via Kottke)

U.S. producer prices rose 6.5% YoY in October, the highest since November 2022, below expectations of 6.6% and up from 6.3% previously. PPI increased 0.3% MoM, below the expected 0.4%, while September’s reading was revised up from 1.3% to 1.4%.

U.S. October PPI Rises 6.5% YoY, Highest in Nearly Four Years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13

U.S. October PPI Rises 6.5% YoY, Highest in Nearly Four Years
156 points
by
moonypto
4 hours ago
|
hide
|
past
|
favorite
|
164 comments
U.S. producer prices rose 6.5% YoY in October, the highest since November 2022, below expectations of 6.6% and up from 6.3% previously. PPI increased 0.3% MoM, below the expected 0.4%, while September’s reading was revised up from 1.3% to 1.4%.

A Note About My 2027 Release Schedule
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:48:57 UTC
23:48:57 KST

A Note About My 2027 Release Schedule
Posted on November 13, 2026 Posted by John Scalzi 13 Comments
Which is: I won’t have a new novel in 2027 and instead will be releasing my next Tor project in early 2028.
Why? Basically, because it’s going to be a little different than what I usually do and we want to make sure we have time to prepare to market and sell it effectively.
How is it different? Well, just you wait.
No, seriously, you have to wait. I’m not telling you now. But I think you’ll like it when you see it.
Please note that “taking a year off” is not entirely unusual for me. I did it in 2024, after all, and then we released When the Moon Hits Your Eye and The Shattering Peace in 2025. It’s a thing we do from time to time.
(In 2024 it was because the only day we could have fit Moon on the schedule would have been Election Day here in the US, and to hell with that. Monsters‘ Election Day release here in 2026 was far more congenial.)
What does that mean for 2027? Well, just you wait.
No, seriously, you have to wait. I honestly don’t know yet. We’ll have to see. It’s not like me to let a whole year go by without anything out there in the world, however. I do have plans.
(Also at some point the paperback of Monsters will come out, probably in the September/October timeframe.)
Anyway, be assured that even if I don’t (currently) have a project lined up for 2027, I will not be idle. The list of projects on my development runway is long and varied. I’ll be doing a lot of furious typing, I promise, and you’ll see the results of that in 2028 and beyond.
Just you wait!
— JS

ElevenLabs’ new app combines meeting note-taking and dictation for teams

In the last few months, Fireflies, Wispr Flow, and Granola have all released competing capabilities in the dictation and meeting note-taking space. The newest entrant in that category is voice AI giant ElevenLabs, which this week released an experimental app called ElevenAI that combines meeting note-taking, dictation, and agent integration.
Launched during its 11/11 Summit in New York, ElevenAI is still in preview, and you have to sign up for early access instead of just downloading an app. ElevenLabs, valued at $22 billion, is very much positioning this as a product that lives within a team as compared to a productivity tool for an individual.
ElevenAI packs all the features typically found in other apps in the category. It can take meeting notes across your meetings and notify other participants that the conversation is being recorded. Users can query data across meetings and get live assistance during a chat, helping them recall a key detail from older conversations. ElevenLabs also allows companies to integrate ElevenAgent and take follow-up actions such as writing a draft email based on meeting notes.
Plus, there’s a dictation feature, which lets users convert speech to cleaned-up text in any app with support for over 90 languages. The company said that the app is capable of understanding industry-specific terms across dictation and meeting transcription.
ElevenAI allows the company to use its own model as a showcase and also utilize its agent platforms for better context funneling. The startup’s positioning might not be to directly compete in the productivity market, but rather to give enterprise teams an option to not buy licenses to a notetaker and use its solution instead. All meeting-related tools are rushing to become central knowledge hubs for companies while aiming to automate knowledge extraction and follow-up actions
In the last week, the company also launched its v3 series of Scribe speech-to-text models with live transcription capabilities and the ability for companies to fine-tune models using their own data, a tool to pick the right language model based on use case, and a routine creator that automates tasks based on the transcript.

A technical vision for the Haskell ecosystem
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:43:12 UTC
23:43:12 KST

Hi all,
the technical leadership committee of the Haskell Foundation has been successfully bootstrapped!
We have spent the last few weeks discussing our vision and strategy and are now fully operational.
We’re going to actively develop initiatives and proposals that we believe need funding and execution. For that we need companies, contractors and volunteers to help. Everyone can participate, whether it’s by dropping ideas informally, writing a concrete proposal themselves or finding entities that could support or fund initiatives.
We tried to keep the community proposal process as light as possible. However, we believe that fairness and participation are vital, especially when funding is involved, so anyone who writes a proposal should be aware that we will open the floor for the community to comment or even deliver counter-proposals. The process may evolve as we make experiences and get feedback.
Below is our vision. Feel free to get in touch with us!
The TLC’s shared vision
We believe that Haskell is awesome and that it can provide unique value to everyone, because it sits at an interesting intersection between human craftsmanship and machine automation:
- It is good for humans to write by hand: Type safety, powerful abstractions and referential transparency have enabled engineers all over the world in startups and big tech to deliver and maintain complex systems.
- It is good for responsible AI workflows: review is a significant bottleneck in modern AI-workflows and Haskell’s strengths heavily favor the reviewer. Additionally, the ability to quickly design eDSLs and express constraints where it matters allows to set up strong guardrails for code generation, without losing flexibility.
There may be better languages for formal verification or for vibecoding, but there’s no better language for “human in the loop”. Haskell is where we maintain control of our work, build institutional knowledge and ship responsibly.
We’re driven to improve Haskell for everyone:
- Beginners: the onboarding experience must be our benchmark for usability
- Experts: enabling advanced workflows and improving productivity
- Industry: focussing on stability and scaling
A vision of productivity
Imagine Haskell had the iteration speed of golang, the debugging capabilities of C# and the onboarding experience of rust. And all that while providing stronger guarantees and better performance!
Iteration speed
Modern workflows require fast iteration times: compilation, interactive repl, remote dependency caching, CI and test integration. All of this affects both human and machine workflows.
While Haskell scales well in terms of code complexity… it doesn’t scale well in terms of build times. As an example, GHC’s own CI has turnaround times of 6 hours. We want to bring them down to 2.
Debugging and profiling
Instead of helplessly dumping logs and binaries into LLM chats, we envision deterministic tools that allow precise and ergonomic enquiries:
- live step-through debugging
- actually useful stack traces
- beautiful performance analysis reports
And all of that in your editor/IDE as well as through powerful CLI and scripting interfaces, the latter enabling power users as well as LLM-driven debugging (natively, without requiring any custom adapters like MCP).
Debugging must be fun again, without littering Debug.Trace.trace all over your codebase.
Documentation and interactive exploration will assist newcomers in understanding all caveats of laziness and GHC and should clearly explain how to leverage these tools.
Onboarding and DevX
In these times of low frustration tolerance, we are going to build the best onboarding experience in the programming language ecosystem, because it is the ultimate benchmark for developer experience as a whole.
- installation so quick that you’re wondering whether the installer even worked
- documentation so comprehensive and accessible that you never get stuck
- tooling so intuitive that you don’t have to constantly ask for support
This can only be done if we commit to unix principles, which we summarize as:
- write programs that do one thing and do it well
- write programs that work together
- write programs that are extensible
Documentation should be a joy to read, written by humans, for humans.
A vision of a secure and trustworthy ecosystem
Security and trust don’t get established by accident. Once trust is lost, it’s often irrecoverably lost.
To protect our community assets and reputation, we need to:
- have the fastest incident response time among language ecosystems
- have penetration testers go through our infrastructure regularly
- make end users aware of vulnerabilities and mitigations
- build a robust cryptography library stack
- harden our supply chain
We should further support attempts to utilize or extend the strengths of the Haskell language to boost practical applications of secure design and architecture, such as Information Flow Control or Mandatory Access Control. We’re also monitoring the efforts of dependent types and liquidhaskell.
A vision of stability
What if we could compile Haskell software that’s 10 years old with the latest GHC, without any source code changes?
While this might seem like an impossible goal to achieve… embarking on that journey is going to get us closer to a robust, stable and predictable ecosystem anyway.
The questions we need to answer along the way are:
- how can users opt out of breaking changes?
- how long can we keep support windows open?
- how can we make the upgrade experience better?
Not only requires this investment in GHC and core tooling, but also potentially support community efforts to maintain stable package sets or coordinate ecosystem upgrades, make cabal more ergonomic for complex use cases, spearhead and support stability policies and gather data about what parts of the ecosystem cause churn.
We want Haskell to not only be a choice for fast moving startups, but also for large or conservative companies that do not want their workflows or software stacks disrupted. Stability will benefit both of these types of users, reduce cost and friction and build a reputation for Haskell as a modern language with a predictable ecosystem.
A vision of a flourishing open source ecosystem
All of the above is an attempt to support the Haskell open source ecosystem that we love. We are committed to open source, its values and the social aspect of it. Collaboration is how we engage with and inspire each other.
We want to enable all the maintainers and volunteers who keep this ecosystem alive, many of them unknown, doing their part on a Saturday evening at the other end of the world.
We code because we love to ship products. But we also code, because we want to socialize, meet people and have a good time. We’re committed to both of these values.

Amodei Says AI Will Cure Cancer in a Decade. Doctors Are Skeptical
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:34:13 UTC
23:34:13 KST

A cure for cancer is less than a decade away, if you ask Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei.
If you ask some in the medical community, though, that timeline is a bit ambitious, if not altogether unrealistic.
In a 42-minute IPO roadshow video the company released on Tuesday, Amodei made the audacious prediction that "cancer, Alzheimer's, heart disease, mental illness — these will be relics of the past in, I think, less than a decade."
The claim that all those diseases, which have bedeviled medicine's best minds for centuries, will be suddenly cured by AI riled some AI naysayers and physicians, who took to X to voice their disbelief.
"Curing cancer, Alzheimer's and mental illnesses can definitely benefit from AI. But that is impossible in just 10 years," Karl Lauterbach, Germany's former health minister, said on X.
"Unclear whether people like @DarioAmodei know so little about medicine or are deliberately misleading the public," he added.
Unklar, ob Leute wie @DarioAmodei so wenig über Medizin wissen oder die Öffentlichkeit bewusst täuschen. Heilung von Krebs, Alzheimer und psychischen Erkrankungen kann definitiv von KI profitieren. Aber das geschieht unmöglich in nur 10 Jahren. Wir sollten auf Prävention setzen https://t.co/FzELuSx7VC
— Prof. Karl Lauterbach (@Karl_Lauterbach) November 11, 2026
Melinda B. Chu, an AI medical researcher with an MD from Saint Louis University, said Amodei made those predictions just to stoke hype for the company's IPO.
"Anthropic has done nothing to suggest they themselves will contribute meaningfully to these goals," Chu said.
Rami Elghandour, a biotech executive, said Amodei's prediction reeks of desperation. "I've taken two companies public. These are insane things to say in an IPO pitch," he said.
I’ve taken two companies public. These are insane things to say in an IPO pitch and wreaks of desperation. https://t.co/AZ5J9Df5ov
— Rami Elghandour (@RamiElghandour) November 11, 2026
Amodei, long prone to waxing poetic about how AI could be misused by bad actors, has sought to soften his doomer reputation by, in part, highlighting AI's potential benefit to medicine.
Anthropic has moved to expand its reach into drug development by buying several biotech startups, including a one-person company focused on developing treatments for muscular dystrophy.
In an October blog post, the company said it had "begun drug discovery efforts of our own."
The company also opened a "wet lab" in the Bay Area to explore how AI technology can support research in fundamental biology, a person familiar with the matter said.
In an August X post, Amodei said that the best way to win over AI skeptics is for AI companies to "actually cure cancer." In that post, however, he also noted that "saying that AI will cure cancer is more a cliché than it is inspiring, and most people think it is deceptive."
With the company's looming IPO, as it seeks to convince investors to buy stock at a trillion-dollar valuation, Amodei's calculus has apparently shifted.
"I think if we do this right, we can create an age of miracles," Amodei said in the video.

Easy Homemade Cornbread
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:30:00 UTC
23:30:00 KST

Cornbread is a highly underrated side dish. You can whip up this deliciously sweet and moist Homemade Cornbread recipe with just a few pantry staples, and it makes a perfect side dish for breakfast, lunch, or dinner! Serve it slathered with butter at breakfast, crumbled into your soup or chili at lunch, or as a side with your collard greens and pork chops for dinner. It’s inexpensive, comforting, and seriously easy to make, with 8 pieces costing just $1.27 ($0.16 each)! So toss that boxed mix and let me show you how it’s done for real. 😉
All recipes are rigorously tested in our Nashville Test Kitchen to ensure they are easy, affordable, and delicious.
“Perfect cornbread! My cornbread always turned out too dry and crumbly – no more! Moist but still with the bit of crumbly and lightness cornbread should have. Really like cooking it in a pie dish and having wedges as well. Thank you!”
Rebecca
A Humble Side with Staying Power
I originally made this cornbread back in 2015 to help stretch a pot of stew further during one of my budget challenges, and it’s been a favorite ever since. I make it completely from scratch with yellow cornmeal, flour, sugar, baking powder, salt, milk, an egg, and cooking oil, most of which you’ve probably already got in your cupboard or fridge! It’s as simple as whisking the wet ingredients, combining them with the dry ingredients, and baking. I don’t even use a mixer and there’s no rising time to worry about, either.
But what I love most about this classic recipe is that it’s lightly sweetened, sitting somewhere between the sweeter, almost cake-like cornbread’s and the more savory Southern-style versions. Just ¼ cup of sugar adds a little sweetness while still letting that lovely corn flavor come through. I also use regular milk instead of buttermilk (so there’s no need to buy a whole carton that you might struggle to use up!).
It’s a simple everyday side, but I love serving a slice alongside a Thanksgiving or Christmas roast, too. You can even bake it the day before, which means one less thing to worry about when you’ve got a big holiday meal to prepare!
Recipe Success Tips
- Use baking powder, NOT baking soda! They’re not interchangeable. This recipe uses baking powder because it contains the ingredients needed to give the cornbread a tender crumb and good rise. Baking soda used in its place can leave an unpleasant, bitter taste and won’t give the same rise or result.
- Mix gently for tender cornbread. I know it’s tempting to keep stirring until everything is completely smooth, but you don’t want to do that here! Overmixing can develop the gluten in the flour and make the finished bread a little rubbery. Stir until everything is moistened. A few lumps are perfectly fine.
- Use the baking dish you’ve got. This amount of batter fits well into an 8×8 casserole dish, 9-inch pie plate, or a 10-inch cast iron skillet (although you should preheat the skillet as the oven preheats). Glass, ceramic, and cast iron work best, but you can also use metal baking dishes.
- Prefer savory over sweet? You can reduce the sugar to suit your tastes. Some readers have even left it out completely with good results, although changing the sugar can affect the browning and texture slightly.
- Make corn muffins instead! This is a great option if you want individual servings or something easy to grab and pack for lunch. Pour the batter into an oiled muffin tin, filling each well about ¾ full. Bake at the same temperature (425°F) for 16-18 minutes, or until the tops are golden brown. I usually get about 10 corn muffins from one batch (see photo).
Three Ways to Avoid Dry Cornbread
A little crumbliness is part of cornbread’s charm, but it shouldn’t fall apart the moment you slice it. Too much flour or cornmeal, or leaving it in the oven too long, can quickly turn a moist crumb dry. Here’s how I help keep this recipe tender without losing that classic cornmeal texture:
- The flour adds structure, and the oil keeps things tender. Cornmeal doesn’t form gluten, so I use equal cups of all-purpose flour and cornmeal to help the bread hold together. Then the oil helps keep the crumb soft, even after it cools, because unlike butter, it stays liquid at room temperature.
- Measure your flour and cornmeal carefully. Too much of either can throw off the balance of wet and dry ingredients, leaving you with a dry and crumbly bread. I recommend weighing them with a kitchen scale for the most consistent results (140 g flour and 150 g cornmeal).
- Don’t overbake! This recipe takes about 20 minutes at 425°F, but the exact time can vary depending on your baking dish. Look for golden-brown edges and a golden top, then insert a toothpick into the center. It should come out clean or with a few moist crumbs (see photo below). There’s no need to keep baking once it’s done, or you risk drying it out.
What to Serve with Cornbread
Cornbread is a great side dish for any thick and hearty soup, stew, or chili. I especially love serving it with a big bowl of my classic chili. I’ll usually either dip a warm slice into the sauce or crumble it straight into my bowl (my favorite way to use up day-old leftovers!). The mild sweetness works really well with smoky spices and a little heat. But really, you can serve this recipe in place of your usual dipping bread with any soup or stew you love. It’s also delicious layered into an easy cornbread salad with beans, corn, and veggies for a potluck or gathering (recipe coming soon!)
I also love a warm slice with a pat of butter melting right on top. Add a drizzle of honey (as much or as little as you like!), and you’ve got a delicious afternoon snack with a cup of coffee or a sweet side for your eggs and bacon at breakfast.
And don’t forget about the holidays! I’d happily swap the usual dinner rolls for a slice of cornbread alongside some roasted turkey breast, a batch of my homemade cranberry sauce, and all the Thanksgiving fixings. You can also take it a step further and use this recipe to make our cornbread dressing, which is loaded with savory herbs and vegetables and makes enough to feed a crowd!
How to Make Cornbread from Scratch step-by-step photos
Preheat the oven and prepare your baking dish: Gather and measure your ingredients, then preheat the oven to 425°F (220°C/200°C fan/Gas Mark 7). Grease a 9-inch pie plate, 10-inch cast iron skillet, or 8×8-inch baking dish with non-stick spray (or butter for a little extra flavor). I’ve always loved using my glass pie dish, but this time we used cast iron, and any of these options will work.
Combine the dry ingredients: In a large bowl, stir together 1 cup (150 g) yellow cornmeal, 1 cup (140 g) all-purpose flour, ¼ cup (50 g) sugar, 2 tsp (9 g) baking powder, and ½ tsp salt. Make sure everything is well mixed so the baking powder is evenly distributed throughout.
Whisk the wet ingredients: In a separate bowl, whisk together 1 cup (240 ml) milk, 1 large egg, and ¼ cup (60 ml) cooking oil until the egg is fully broken up and everything is well combined.
Gently mix the batter: Pour the wet ingredients into the dry ingredients and stir just until everything is moistened and there are no dry pockets of flour. Don’t keep stirring until the batter is completely smooth. A few lumps are perfectly fine, and overmixing can make the finished bread a little rubbery.
Bake until golden brown: Pour the batter into your prepared dish and spread it evenly. Bake for about 20 minutes, or until the top and edges are golden brown.
The top may crack a little, and that’s perfectly normal! To check it’s done, insert a toothpick into the center. It should come out clean or with a few moist crumbs. Cut into 8 pieces and serve. Enjoy!
More Cornbread Recipes to Try
- Give the classic recipe a cheesy, slightly spicy twist with my Chili Cheese Cornbread, made with cheddar, chili powder, and sweet corn kernels.
- I love combining two comfort food favorites in my Chili Cornbread Skillet. The homemade cornbread bakes right on top of a hearty bean chili, with a layer of melted cheddar in between!
- Mashed sweet potato, sour cream, cinnamon, and nutmeg make this Sweet Potato Cornbread extra moist, rich, and full of warm fall flavors.
Other Easy Classic Bread Recipes
Our Easy Homemade Cornbread recipe was originally published 9/5/15. It was retested, reworked, and republished to be better than ever 11/13/26.

Lonewolf 0.3 – A standalone XMPP server written in Rust
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:28:40 UTC
23:28:40 KST

Repository navigation
The big one in this release is federation: Lonewolf can now talk to other XMPP servers. We also added direct TLS and a handful of extensions that desktop clients rely on.
Lonewolf is still pre-1.0 and runs as a single node. Full XMPP compliance and clustering are on the roadmap.
What's new
Federation
Your users can now exchange messages, presence, IQs, and roster subscriptions with people on other XMPP servers. If a host has a certificate and a public DNS name, federation is on by default, with nothing extra to configure.
Lonewolf authenticates peers by certificate only. The other server needs a trusted certificate and SASL EXTERNAL support. We don't support Server Dialback.
When the other server supports it, both directions share a single connection (XEP-0288). Servers also tell each other their stanza size and idle limits up front (XEP-0478).
Federation checks certificate revocation lists published by the CA, fetched over plain HTTP. If a CRL can't be downloaded, the connection goes ahead anyway. You can turn downloads off with s2s.tls.fetch_crls = false, or point s2s.tls.crls_path at a local file to use only that.
Direct TLS
Clients and servers can now connect with TLS from the first byte (XEP-0368), using new dedicated listeners. Outbound federation discovers both STARTTLS and direct TLS endpoints.
More for desktop clients
- Private XML Storage (XEP-0049) gives clients a place to keep private settings and legacy bookmarks.
- Software Version (XEP-0092) answers "what server is this?" queries. It doesn't reveal the operating system unless you turn that on.
Both are enabled by default.
Short-lived certificates
Use letsencrypt.certificate_profile to pick one of the profiles your CA advertises, including short-lived certificates. If you change the profile, Lonewolf renews early so the new one takes effect right away.
SCRAM-SHA-512 (opt-in)
You can now enable SCRAM-SHA-512 and SCRAM-SHA-512-PLUS. Be aware that they come from an expired Internet-Draft, not an RFC, and aren't registered with IANA.
Even if you leave SHA-512 login disabled, new accounts and password changes now store credentials that v0.2.0 can't read, so rolling back isn't straightforward.
See the standards reference for exactly what's supported and the configuration reference for every setting and its default.
Build from source
Grab Source code (tar.gz) or Source code (zip) below, extract it, and run:
cargo build --release --locked -p lonewolf -p lonewolfctl -p lonewolfbench
The benchmark tool is now called lonewolfbench instead of lonewolf-bench, so update any scripts that call it. All you need is a stable Rust toolchain and a system linker on Linux or macOS. The quick start walks you through setup.
Full changelog: v0.2.0…v0.3.0
Licensed under Apache-2.0.
