====================================================================================================

The 42-Year Journey to Bring 'Neuromancer' to the Screen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:28:32 UTC
23:28:32 KST

Briana Middleton has something to show me. “Wait!” she says just as we’re about to end our Zoom call, reaching for something just beyond her screen.
“When I found out we were going to be doing this,” she says, “I was at the thrift shop in my neighborhood and I found this.” Then she points it at the camera: an issue of WIRED from 1995 featuring Keanu Reeves on the cover alongside the line “William Gibson on the making of Johnny Mnemonic.”
Very apropos. The 1995 film Johnny Mnemonic was based on a short story by Gibson, who also wrote the screenplay. Molly, the “razorgirl” Middleton plays in Apple TV’s forthcoming Neuromancer adaptation, made her first appearance in that short story. Her relationship with the protagonist Case, played in the series by Callum Turner, mirrors the one Reeves’ Neo had with Trinity in The Matrix, which is steeped in Gibsoneque lore.
For Middleton, to be giving an interview to WIRED about Neuromancer’s epic journey to the screen not long after finding a 1995 issue of the magazine in a secondhand store is so uncanny it almost feels like a glitch. “Dude, it was eight bucks,” she says, agreeing that eight dollars is a steal for a vintage WIRED. “I was like, ‘This is crazy.’”
Maybe all things happen exactly when they’re supposed to. Gibson originally released Neuromancer in 1984, back before hackers were cool. Back before hackers were CEOs. It focused on Case, a young computer jockey who can get online through a jack in his head. He teams up with Molly and a former military man named Armitage to break into the offworld home of the Tessier-Ashpool family, who became astoundingly rich thanks to the tech company they founded.
The book’s novel ideas—cyberspace, brain-computer interfaces, all-powerful AIs—were years away from being mainstream, let alone possible. In the years since, there’s been much talk of turning Neuromancer into a movie or TV show, but it never took off, even as Hollywood mined sci-fi for hits.
On January 22, all of that will change when Apple TV releases its long-anticipated adaptation. Ahead of its release, WIRED got in touch with Middleton, Turner, and showrunners Graham Roland and JD Dillard to find out how they became the first to bring the book to screens—and the burden of taking on one of sci-fi’s classics.
On adapting an iconic sci-fi novel:
Graham Roland: It's a huge seminal book. I had not read it until the summer of 2020, but I was aware it was one of those white whales. It inspired a lot of things, and people had been trying to adapt it for years.
Callum Turner: The world will be taken on a wild ride. It’s an epic. It’s Blade Runner; it’s 2001: A Space Odyssey; it’s The Fifth Element. This is the first time it comes onto our screens, and that's a big moment for us and fans of sci-fi.
Briana Middleton: When we were shooting it and in the thick of it, it was easier to not get freaked out by the expectations. It felt nice to be in a little bubble with the world that we had created. I tried to look at the expectations that people clearly have and the love that people have for this book and what these characters mean. I definitely get freaked out sometimes and I just have to shut my computer and not think about it.
On getting the rights:
Roland: I had a meeting at Skydance, and they had given me the book and said, “Hey, the rights of this book are going to come available again. We’re going to try to adapt it as a TV show as opposed to a movie or several movies." It made so much sense. So I read the book, and really responded to it right away. JD and I had been friends for years prior to that, and were looking for a way to work together. He had also had a meeting at Skydance, and they had also given him the book.
JD Dillard: Yeah, there was a wonderful kismet of this moment, and we were like, “Wait, we’re both talking to people about this. Why don’t we do it together?”
On how Drake became a producer:
Dillard: Drake has always been a fan of Neuromancer and as a storyteller has great taste, but that's only part of it. The real magic is that he’s built an incredible team at [his production company] DreamCrew that he trusts to bring things to life once he identifies something he loves or believes in. Jason Shrier, who leads film and TV for DreamCrew, is one of the folks in our little brain trust on this show, and someone we pride as always having his finger on the pulse. But he has a relationship with [entertainment company] Anonymous Content, and Skydance, so it was kind of serendipitous that as Drake and DreamCrew had identified the books, all of these parties were starting to form and realize that they could work together to go get the rights. Drake, individually and by way of DreamCrew, is part of the mix and remains an important part of our process.
On working with William Gibson:
Roland: The way it was set up was that William Gibson was going to be the decider of who was going to get the rights when they came available. So we went to work on a pitch and with the hope that we would be the people he would choose.
We had heard that there were some bigger names, much bigger than JD and I. I just felt like there was a counterculture vibe about him and I was like, I don't think it matters that we're nobodies. I think if we can somehow communicate our passion for his book, and not just go and say, “Oh, obviously we’ll take care of your book, you can trust us,” but actually go in and do the work and come up with a thoughtful presentation and a thoughtful pitch, then I think we've got as good a shot as any.
Dillard: I'm sure Bill would not mind us sharing this part of it, but it's very easy to look at these big pieces of literature or movies that have stood the test of time and just think that they were immaculately conceived. Just to hear him talk about where he struggled and what he was self-conscious about and then what he was surprised worked and what he wished he could have done, what he wished he had time to do—it was the first time Graham and I both looked at Neuromancer as a living text.
On making Neuromancer their own:
Dillard: One of the first questions we asked ourselves was “How do you make this feel new in spite of inspiring so many things that have now already come out ahead of this?” It was never going to be our ambition to out-Cyberpunk 2077 Cyberpunk 2077. But what we could do is talk to Gibson and find out where, for him, some of those big tonal touchpoints are. An interesting one that came up really early in talking to him was that he had never perceived the world of Neuromancer as a dystopia. Even that was a nice thing to just remember as we found our way through it.
The best we could do is bring people to the experience of what it felt like reading it for the first time in 1984. Because anything beyond that, your reference points are already going to be derivative. If you put up Blade Runner reference art, you're already behind it. The joke that we were even making when hiring [production designer] Luke [Hull] was like: Here's someone who's done Chernobyl and Andor, and in our minds Neuromancer is Chernobyl plus Andor. It's old-school and hi-fi and colliding those things together.
Roland: We didn't want the world of Neuromancer to feel like X amount of years after the world that we were living in. In the teaser that opens the show, you see this piece of technology in the late '60s, early '70s that we know did not exist back then in our world. We wanted to announce right out of the gate: Don't be looking for the iteration of whatever phone you have in your pocket 50 years from now. We want this to be its own world.
Dillard: Watching someone hack will only be exciting for so long. But what is stakes-driven is the danger to Case in doing it and the toll it takes on his body. Is he going to get the thing they need for the larger story for himself? Can he prove to himself that he can still do it? All of those things were immediately stickier than just watching him do it.
A thing Bill [Gibson] said really early on is, he had always sort of imagined Case as a neurological athlete. We took that and ran with it, in that it should take a toll. There's an expenditure to do this work.
On adapting a book beloved by fans:
Turner: I try not to think about how important it is and focus on my job of playing the scenes in front of me. JD and Graham had William Gibson’s approval, the scripts were brilliant, and the world so detailed and real. You can smell this world, which feels unique.
Roland: I'm so glad that one of the first things we did was go see Bill Gibson up in Vancouver. He took the burden off of us and let us know that he wasn't expecting a literal translation of his text. He's the one that we're really beholden to.
Even in those early conversations with him, he mentioned, “Oh, I always wanted to do a bigger Tessier-Ashpool story, the story of the family [behind the powerful AI called Wintermute]. I had a book deadline, and I never really got to expand on that story. But if you find yourself looking for other things, other liberties or places to go into and make your own, that would be one.” So that's why we came up with that storyline.
On the set design:
Roland: Before we ever got to hiring the heads of any departments, JD planted a flag and said, “As much as we can, we want an in-camera aesthetic. We want to be practical.” That really drove the decision to go to Japan, because trying to recreate Chiba City, we already have set extensions, obviously, because it takes place in the future, but the textures of Japan [are there], and we wanted it to feel like you were actually there.
The other thing we did, the thing I had never done on any other show, was we hired our production designer first, before any other head of a department. We knew that while we were still writing we needed that person to help us realize the world. What they were going to do practically—from a props perspective, from a concept art perspective—was going to inform our writing as well.
Dillard: It is a very bespoke version of the future; everything had to be machined and built and designed. We didn’t have all of these things that you could normally just go to the prop shop and get. But that is what drives the feeling of Neuromancer, feeling textured and grounded and real and a place you could go to, and it is both familiar to the past but aspirational to the future.
On the design of Case’s deck, the terminal he uses to access the virtual world of the matrix:
Dillard: We saw a lot of decks. If I remember correctly, I feel like we were on the way to being like, "OK, this is the deck." Then Luke was like, "Sorry, before we make that, here's another one." That is the one we went with. But we were all resistant to it just feeling like a laptop. We wanted something that was a little bit like a station.
On getting the synth-rock band Son Lux for the score:
Dillard: Graham and I knew from the beginning we wanted Neuromancer to feel different, not feel cyberpunk. In the same way that every other department had to honor retro-futurism in their own way, we wanted to charge our composer with doing the same.
They're the best, and a band we've been fans of for a long time. I don't know how many Son Lux concerts I've gone to, and it was really cool in the last few years to see their turn into scores. Obviously, Everything Everywhere All at Once was a big one for them. Ryan [Lott] and the gang just really found Neuromancer's own sonic signature.
On casting Case:
Roland: We had finished two or three scripts, and we started having conversations about casting. Apple had had a great experience with Callum on Masters of the Air and they encouraged us to meet with him. It was the first and only meeting we had. He was in LA for something else and he came to meet JD and I, and within five minutes of hearing his voice it was like, “This is the guy.”
Turner: My vision was aligned with theirs, and luckily they asked me to join. I hadn’t read the book, but I was in a sci-fi mode. I’d read I Am Legend and Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep? when this came through. I remember we had [Neuromancer] in our house when I was a kid, but I’d never picked it up. Graham and JD had written a wonderful character, and he felt so nuanced and detailed on the page—I jumped off from their starting point.
Case has the right mix of dangerous, courageous, and naive. Joe Strummer was a big influence. Thom Yorke. Iggy Pop. There’s an incredible documentary called Streetwise that I used as a base. There are always many, many things that go into a pot.
On maybe casting the actor who will be the next James Bond:
Dillard: We're crossing our fingers and toes.
On casting Molly:
Dillard: We had Case, so it was about “What completes the duo?” It’s a vibe thing; it’s a physicality thing. Callum's quite tall. And not that our shorter friends can't kick ass too, but you just wanted it to make sense on screen. Callum was shooting in Vancouver, and we brought a few people to read across from him. It was Briana for sure. They just had such a fun chemistry, and she just had the kind of no-nonsense grit to her. She could find these neat moments of warmth, but also not have that break her hard shell. I know everybody says this in their interviews about their show, but we really lucked out.
Middleton: I was not familiar with the book, and I had a meeting with David Levine at Anonymous Content. When he initially told me about the character and the fact that she's got lenses over her eyes, I was like, “I don't think I want to do that. That doesn't sound fun at all.” Then I met with JD and Graham months later, and they just sold me on it.
I feel like Molly is very funny in our show, and in the book, Molly is very cool, and there's a lot that we don't see that is very clearly there. Molly is a guarded character, but there is so much going on, and we do get to see some more of the very human part of her outside of the “razorgirl” badass trope.
On Molly’s razorgirl lenses:
Middleton: They were stuck on my face. It was double-sided sticky tape basically, but they held on. I think they fell off maybe once or twice throughout the entire shoot, and that includes fight scenes, so they held up. Steps were always a daunting thing. I couldn't really tell when I was going to step off something or be fine.
Whenever I took them off, it would feel weird. I would feel kind of exposed. I think at some point in the shoot, they started to feel probably how Molly feels, that they are very comforting and very much like, I can hide a little bit.
On having the jack—a brain-computer interface—attached to the back of your neck:
Turner: Actually, it was fine.
On William Gibson’s cameo as a patron at the Chatsubo dive bar:
Roland: We invited him to the set in London. I knew we had a lot of fans of his book working on our crew, but it wasn’t until he arrived that I saw the number of people that had brought their personal copy of Neuromancer hoping to get it signed. I was like, “Oh wow, I guess they're not here for the scripts.” [Laughs]
Dillard: I think we were like, “We want him to get out here, and we know it's a long slog from Vancouver, but if we can get him out here, we got to put him in. We have to just immortalize his presence in the show.” So part of it was timing, but it actually worked out for what we wanted because Chatsubo is such an iconic location in the book, and to see him there as a barfly just was like, “This will be so fun.”
On whether the other books in William Gibson’s Sprawl Trilogy—Count Zero and Mona Lisa Overdrive—could find their way onto the small screen:
Roland: It's too early to say at this point. I think we're still kind of focused on getting this one out into the world, but we'll see. I hope so.
On tech entrepreneurs being inspired by Neuromancer:
Dillard: There's some great tweet that went viral last year that is sort of like, "New tech CEO says they're launching a product called Skynet.” It's like you're missing the point if you're naming the thing after the bad thing.
Middleton: Our show is asking what happens whenever corporations have unchecked power and when a technology is unchecked and there's no regulations, and our relationship with that technology goes unchecked. Our show is asking what we lose without oversight. I think life gets much, much worse. It's scary. I'm scared.

====================================================================================================

Dillard: Watching someone hack will only be exciting for so long. But what is stakes-driven is the danger to Case in doing it and the toll it takes on his body. Is he going to get the thing they need for the larger story for himself? Can he prove to himself that he can still do it? All of those things were immediately stickier than just watching him do it.
A thing Bill [Gibson] said really early on is, he had always sort of imagined Case as a neurological athlete. We took that and ran with it, in that it should take a toll. There's an expenditure to do this work.
On adapting a book beloved by fans:
Turner: I try not to think about how important it is and focus on my job of playing the scenes in front of me. JD and Graham had William Gibson’s approval, the scripts were brilliant, and the world so detailed and real. You can smell this world, which feels unique.
Roland: I'm so glad that one of the first things we did was go see Bill Gibson up in Vancouver. He took the burden off of us and let us know that he wasn't expecting a literal translation of his text. He's the one that we're really beholden to.
Even in those early conversations with him, he mentioned, “Oh, I always wanted to do a bigger Tessier-Ashpool story, the story of the family [behind the powerful AI called Wintermute]. I had a book deadline, and I never really got to expand on that story. But if you find yourself looking for other things, other liberties or places to go into and make your own, that would be one.” So that's why we came up with that storyline.
On the set design:
Roland: Before we ever got to hiring the heads of any departments, JD planted a flag and said, “As much as we can, we want an in-camera aesthetic. We want to be practical.” That really drove the decision to go to Japan, because trying to recreate Chiba City, we already have set extensions, obviously, because it takes place in the future, but the textures of Japan [are there], and we wanted it to feel like you were actually there.
The other thing we did, the thing I had never done on any other show, was we hired our production designer first, before any other head of a department. We knew that while we were still writing we needed that person to help us realize the world. What they were going to do practically—from a props perspective, from a concept art perspective—was going to inform our writing as well.
Dillard: It is a very bespoke version of the future; everything had to be machined and built and designed. We didn’t have all of these things that you could normally just go to the prop shop and get. But that is what drives the feeling of Neuromancer, feeling textured and grounded and real and a place you could go to, and it is both familiar to the past but aspirational to the future.
On the design of Case’s deck, the terminal he uses to access the virtual world of the matrix:
Dillard: We saw a lot of decks. If I remember correctly, I feel like we were on the way to being like, "OK, this is the deck." Then Luke was like, "Sorry, before we make that, here's another one." That is the one we went with. But we were all resistant to it just feeling like a laptop. We wanted something that was a little bit like a station.
On getting the synth-rock band Son Lux for the score:
Dillard: Graham and I knew from the beginning we wanted Neuromancer to feel different, not feel cyberpunk. In the same way that every other department had to honor retro-futurism in their own way, we wanted to charge our composer with doing the same.
They're the best, and a band we've been fans of for a long time. I don't know how many Son Lux concerts I've gone to, and it was really cool in the last few years to see their turn into scores. Obviously, Everything Everywhere All at Once was a big one for them. Ryan [Lott] and the gang just really found Neuromancer's own sonic signature.
On casting Case:
Roland: We had finished two or three scripts, and we started having conversations about casting. Apple had had a great experience with Callum on Masters of the Air and they encouraged us to meet with him. It was the first and only meeting we had. He was in LA for something else and he came to meet JD and I, and within five minutes of hearing his voice it was like, “This is the guy.”
Turner: My vision was aligned with theirs, and luckily they asked me to join. I hadn’t read the book, but I was in a sci-fi mode. I’d read I Am Legend and Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep? when this came through. I remember we had [Neuromancer] in our house when I was a kid, but I’d never picked it up. Graham and JD had written a wonderful character, and he felt so nuanced and detailed on the page—I jumped off from their starting point.
Case has the right mix of dangerous, courageous, and naive. Joe Strummer was a big influence. Thom Yorke. Iggy Pop. There’s an incredible documentary called Streetwise that I used as a base. There are always many, many things that go into a pot.
On maybe casting the actor who will be the next James Bond:
Dillard: We're crossing our fingers and toes.
On casting Molly:
Dillard: We had Case, so it was about “What completes the duo?” It’s a vibe thing; it’s a physicality thing. Callum's quite tall. And not that our shorter friends can't kick ass too, but you just wanted it to make sense on screen. Callum was shooting in Vancouver, and we brought a few people to read across from him. It was Briana for sure. They just had such a fun chemistry, and she just had the kind of no-nonsense grit to her. She could find these neat moments of warmth, but also not have that break her hard shell. I know everybody says this in their interviews about their show, but we really lucked out.
Middleton: I was not familiar with the book, and I had a meeting with David Levine at Anonymous Content. When he initially told me about the character and the fact that she's got lenses over her eyes, I was like, “I don't think I want to do that. That doesn't sound fun at all.” Then I met with JD and Graham months later, and they just sold me on it.
I feel like Molly is very funny in our show, and in the book, Molly is very cool, and there's a lot that we don't see that is very clearly there. Molly is a guarded character, but there is so much going on, and we do get to see some more of the very human part of her outside of the “razorgirl” badass trope.
On Molly’s razorgirl lenses:
Middleton: They were stuck on my face. It was double-sided sticky tape basically, but they held on. I think they fell off maybe once or twice throughout the entire shoot, and that includes fight scenes, so they held up. Steps were always a daunting thing. I couldn't really tell when I was going to step off something or be fine.
Whenever I took them off, it would feel weird. I would feel kind of exposed. I think at some point in the shoot, they started to feel probably how Molly feels, that they are very comforting and very much like, I can hide a little bit.
On having the jack—a brain-computer interface—attached to the back of your neck:
Turner: Actually, it was fine.
On William Gibson’s cameo as a patron at the Chatsubo dive bar:
Roland: We invited him to the set in London. I knew we had a lot of fans of his book working on our crew, but it wasn’t until he arrived that I saw the number of people that had brought their personal copy of Neuromancer hoping to get it signed. I was like, “Oh wow, I guess they're not here for the scripts.” [Laughs]
Dillard: I think we were like, “We want him to get out here, and we know it's a long slog from Vancouver, but if we can get him out here, we got to put him in. We have to just immortalize his presence in the show.” So part of it was timing, but it actually worked out for what we wanted because Chatsubo is such an iconic location in the book, and to see him there as a barfly just was like, “This will be so fun.”
On whether the other books in William Gibson’s Sprawl Trilogy—Count Zero and Mona Lisa Overdrive—could find their way onto the small screen:
Roland: It's too early to say at this point. I think we're still kind of focused on getting this one out into the world, but we'll see. I hope so.
On tech entrepreneurs being inspired by Neuromancer:
Dillard: There's some great tweet that went viral last year that is sort of like, "New tech CEO says they're launching a product called Skynet.” It's like you're missing the point if you're naming the thing after the bad thing.
Middleton: Our show is asking what happens whenever corporations have unchecked power and when a technology is unchecked and there's no regulations, and our relationship with that technology goes unchecked. Our show is asking what we lose without oversight. I think life gets much, much worse. It's scary. I'm scared.

====================================================================================================

CERN Built the Large Hadron Collider's Next-Gen Magnets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:25:46 UTC
23:25:46 KST

This article is part of our exclusive IEEE Journal Watch series in partnership with IEEE Xplore.
CERN’s Large Hadron Collider (LHC) uses extremely strong magnets to collide particles at nearly the speed of light, breaking them up into smaller components for study. The LHC is already capable of slamming about 60 particles together 40 million times per second in a single experiment, which has led to some of the most important particle physics discoveries of the century.
But scientists at CERN are not stopping there. They are currently working on upgrading the LHC for upcoming experiments scheduled to begin after 2030. The new iteration of the particle collider, called the High-Luminosity LHC, will slam more particles together than before. (The phrase “high luminosity” refers to the increased number of particle collisions.) CERN’s goal is to achieve between 140 and 200 particle collisions 40 million times per second in each experiment in order to increase their chances of observing rare particles or phenomena. To succeed, the collider will need extremely compact and high-quality magnets.
In a study published 24 September in IEEE Transactions on Applied Superconductivity, researchers outline their journey to manufacture these next-generation magnets for the LHC, which require new materials and manufacturing techniques.
Developing Nb₃Sn Superconducting Magnets
The collider’s current dipole magnets contain coils made of niobium and titanium (NbTi) and can achieve strengths of 8.3 tesla. That is about 166,000 times as strong as Earth’s magnetic field. However, this magnetic force is the upper limit of what NbTi coils can achieve. For the LHC’s new quadrupole magnets, scientists are creating coils made of niobium and tin (Nb₃Sn), which will yield just under 12 T.
Susana Izquierdo Bermúdez, a magnet engineer at CERN who leads a team of about 60 people, has been involved in the development of these magnets since 2012. Over the years, she has contributed to many different aspects of the project, including early winding tests, electromagnetic design, mechanical design, manufacturing, and testing.
Izquierdo Bermúdez notes that while NbTi is ductile and can be wound after the superconducting compound has been formed, Nb₃Sn is brittle and cannot tolerate the large strains involved in coil winding. For this reason, the magnets are produced using a process known as wind-and-react: The cable is first wound into its final coil geometry, and the Nb₃Sn superconducting phase is formed only afterwards through a high-temperature heat treatment.
To start, the team takes a 760-meter-long cable composed of strands of niobium and tin and wraps it around a center pole to form a coil about 7.2 meters long with a 150 millimeter aperture.
Once wound, the coil undergoes a controlled heat treatment in an inert atmosphere. The temperature is increased over time, plateauing at about 210 °C and then 400 °C before peaking at about 650 °C. During this process, which takes several days, the niobium and tin in the strands react to form the brittle Nb₃Sn superconducting phase.
“After reaction, the coil must be handled very carefully because excessive deformation could damage the brittle superconducting material,” Izquierdo Bermúdez explains.
The coils are then smoothed over with a liquid epoxy resin, which fills any internal voids, cracks, or gaps, and finally is cured into a rigid polymer.
In their study, Izquierdo Bermúdez and her colleagues outline their efforts to manufacture more than 120 coils for 4.5-meter-long magnets and more than 70 coils for 7.2-meter-long magnets for the LHC. Four coils are incorporated into each quadrupole magnet.
In total, the team produced 250 coils, 25 of which failed either due to human error or challenges in the manufacturing process that left the coils with subpar electrical or mechanical properties. Izquierdo Bermúdez says that even the smallest imperfection can compromise a coil. In one instance, a coil had to be discarded because a single metal particle trapped between the coil and one of its protective components was suspected of interfering with its performance.
In September, Izquierdo Bermúdez’s team finished running quality control tests on the 7.2-meter magnets for CERN, confirming they reached peak magnetic forces of 11.3 T. The magnets are functioning well, and the team is now working to manufacture back-ups.
Izquierdo Bermúdez says that it is a great honor and responsibility to be a part of this work, which has been a large team effort. “It has taken many years of work by a large international team, from conductor and magnet development through manufacturing, testing and final integration. Seeing the technology reach this stage is something we can all be very proud of,” she says.
This story was updated on 10 November 2026 to correct the total number of particle collisions in each experiment.

- Taking the Large Hadron Collider to the Max ›
- Four Ways Engineers Are Trying to Break Physics ›
Michelle Hampson is a freelance writer based in Halifax. She frequently contributes to Spectrum's Journal Watch coverage, which highlights newsworthy studies published in IEEE journals.

Meta discussed attacking Jonathan Haidt — now even his haters are annoyed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:19:33 UTC
23:19:33 KST

Three years ago, Meta apparently considered a plan to turn public opinion against one of its most influential opponents, Jonathan Haidt. But since the proposal was revealed in court filings last month, it’s backfired. In fact, if there’s one thing Anxious Generation author Haidt and his critics can agree on, it’s being mad at Meta.
Meta discussed attacking Jonathan Haidt — now even his haters are annoyed
Meta has ticked off academics on all sides of the social media and mental health debate.
Recent court filings in California revealed that Meta employees had discussed in 2023 a “Whisper strategy” to “turn academics against Haidt,” who would soon publish a bestselling book arguing that platforms like Meta’s were hurting kids. Haidt had spent years popularizing his theory that social media and smartphones are to blame for the decline in teens’ mental health, including in his 2018 book The Coddling of the American Mind and before Congress in 2022. To counter it, Meta apparently considered a “Below-the-line campaign,” including one idea to “Get Gellman to write a take-down piece” — possibly a reference to Columbia University statistics and political science professor Andrew Gelman, who’d previously publicly critiqued Haidt, though the spelling differs.
Meta employees had discussed in 2023 a “Whisper strategy” to “turn academics against Haidt”
It’s unclear if Meta ever acted on the strategy those employees appeared to consider. But the revelation has stirred up a much louder backlash among both Haidt and his most prominent detractors, most of whom weren’t mentioned by name. Meta declined to comment for this article.
Several academics have come forward to insist Meta had no hand in their opposition to Haidt. “No. I was not paid to critique the Anxious Generation. I came to the conclusion that the science featured in it was flawed all on my own,” reads the title of a Substack post from Candice Odgers, a prominent psychology professor who studies the effects of tech on teens and has frequently critiqued Haidt’s work. “I have never been approached by Meta to shape what I say publicly about the research on adolescents and social media,” Odgers wrote. “My views are my own, and are grounded in the years of research I’ve done on this topic.”
“No. I was not paid to critique the Anxious Generation”
“These days, I have absolutely nothing nice to say about Meta-the-company despite knowing a few amazing people who work there. I think the company is unethical to the core,” wrote danah boyd, a communication professor at Cornell University who has studied how teens use social media. “But I still stand by my research on teens and the research of others who have done rigorous analyses putting teens’ lives into context.”
Haidt is an extremely influential but polarizing figure. Through his bestselling book, The Anxious Generation, and related writing, he has made the case that social media and smartphone access for kids has contributed to a significant decline in mental health. His work has found support among academics including frequent collaborator and San Diego State University psychology professor Jean Twenge, and Haidt has published responses to the debate over his findings. But other longtime researchers in the field say the available evidence does not ultimately support his theory, and social media’s impact on teens at the population level is at most mixed.
Though Gelman has previously critiqued Haidt’s methods, this time he came to his defense, denouncing Meta’s private discussions. “I think it’s horrible for Haidt that this happened to him,” Gelman told The Verge in an interview. He wrote on his blog that he’s “never been contacted by anyone at Facebook or Meta regarding Jonathan Haidt or his work.” While he said he has received money from the company in the past, including a $25,000 “no-strings-attached donation” in 2016 through Columbia for his research, the money has never been related to “social media’s impact on teen health.”
Gelman says that Meta has every right to be in the conversation on the topic — but it should do so openly. “Doing it behind the scenes, it’s creepy. It’s horrible. I have nothing good to say about that,” he said.
“The idea that Meta would create a whisper campaign or whatever against any scholar is gross”
“The idea that Meta would create a whisper campaign or whatever against any scholar is gross,” said Chris Ferguson, a Stetson University psychology professor, in an email to The Verge. Ferguson published a meta-analysis of experimental studies on social media’s impact on mental health that found limited evidence of a causal effect. Ferguson, who said he receives “no funding from Meta or any other tech company,” accused both Haidt and Meta of being “more similar than different.”
“Both have been aggressive, and a pernicious influence on our understanding of youth mental health,” Ferguson wrote, saying that Haidt’s viewpoint is not backed up by science. “It’s possible to view Meta as a big, ugly, cynical, unethical company and, at the same time, view Jonathan Haidt as a pseudoscientist who should know better [than] to peddle nonsense capitalizing on parental fears for profit.”
The Meta meeting notes were disclosed on a court docket for a group of consolidated lawsuits against Meta and other social media companies alleging they misled the public about their services’ safety risks to teens. Meta has recently faced several high-profile legal defeats in similar cases that could cost it billions in penalties, and separately settled claims from state attorneys general for $17.1 billion.
In a statement to The Verge, Haidt said he’s never suggested the researchers named in this article were paid by Meta to critique his work, and hasn’t seen evidence to suggest that. “As a social psychologist who has studied the benefits of viewpoint diversity, I welcome opposing views and civil, rigorous scientific debate. Criticism by these researchers has caused me and my research group to dig deeper and sharpen our arguments,” Haidt says, noting that his research group has published “multiple review papers” laying out evidence that heavy social media use is harmful.
“I am glad to see that so many social scientists are expressing strong criticism of Meta”
“Meta’s own internal studies confirm our finding. Meta’s internal documents also show that the company discussed a plan to distort the academic debate and process, a tactic straight out of the Big Tobacco playbook,” Haidt added. “That should concern anyone who cares about scientific integrity and the well-being of children. I am glad to see that so many social scientists are expressing strong criticism of Meta for doing so.”
Ferguson said it’s not healthy for science to be associated with any one individual, and that Haidt himself could do more to discourage that. “I think people are slowly realizing it’s possible both to believe that Meta is evil and Haidt is full of crap at the same time,” Ferguson said.

I’ve Made Hundreds of Pies, This is the Only Crust I Use for the Flakiest Result

I’ve Made Hundreds of Pies, This is the Only Crust I Use for the Flakiest Result

====================================================================================================

US university students tell surveillance cameras to Flock off
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:11:56 UTC
23:11:56 KST

Angela Nguyen, a senior at Stanford University, has been on a mission to make sure her school doesn’t use automated license plate readers, like those made by Flock Safety, for mass surveillance. The 21-year-old computer science major helped collect hundreds of signatures earlier this year from students, faculty, alumni and residents to demand that Stanford drop its current Flock contract and codify safeguards for similar tech tools the school may use in the future. The fear, Nguyen says, is that these cameras, which show where a vehicle was at a certain point in time, can be exploited to victimize vulnerable people.
Stanford cut ties with Flock this summer, and it’s not alone. The privacy reckoning over the company’s surveillance cameras is gaining momentum at schools nationwide. Since April, at least ten university campuses across seven states, have scrapped contracts with Flock. That includes Cornell University, Penn State University, Elgin Community College, Emory University, Binghamton University, Kent State University and the University of Wisconsin’s Madison, Milwaukee and Oshkosh branches. Last week, the University of Buffalo announced an indefinite pause on its plans for a 60-day pilot program.
Elgin Community College tried, unsuccessfully, to cancel its Flock contract before it expired in April. School officials said they resorted to placing black plastic trash bags over the cameras “out of an abundance of caution” until the company removed the cameras after the agreement lapsed.
A few schools – Appalachian State University and the University of North Carolina’s (UNC) Charlotte and Chapel Hill campuses – have kept Flock contracts but restricted national access to the cameras’ records. UNC Charlotte made the change after local news outlet WBTV found that the Houston police department had searched the school’s Flock camera network more than any other agency between March and May 2026: a total of 183,000 times.
Schools that haven’t yet restricted what agencies can access Flock or dropped contracts are feeling the pressure. At the University of Florida and Florida State University, activists have turned to protests, online campaigns and petitions to express their discontent. The state of Florida recently banned Flock cameras and other license plate readers from its highways, but those laws don’t extend to private property or local rights-of-way.
The uproar on campuses reflects a broader uprising. This year, dozens of cities have deactivated, canceled or rejected Flock contracts. Vigilantes have damaged and destroyed the devices to the delight of their social media followers.
Flock Safety said in an emailed statement that its cameras help solve crimes and increase public safety by giving campus police credible leads. The company highlighted cases in which local law enforcement said Flock cameras helped track down a shooter at Brown University, arrest a person who shot a gun in the parking lot of a high school in Colorado and find a suspect in an investigation about more than $100,000 in stolen equipment from an on-campus construction site at UNC Charlotte.
“We understand that schools and universities must evaluate public safety technology, and we respect each institution’s authority to make those decisions, but taking away these tools could impact the safety of students,” the company said in an emailed statement.
Flock also denies that its cameras fuel mass surveillance and that it partners with federal immigration authorities, although news reports have documented indirect ways that local police have used the network to help US Immigration and Customs Enforcement. This summer, Flock also introduced updated policies around auditing, as well as data retention and sharing.
Flock out, new cameras in
Student protesters are confronting many of the same challenges organizers in cities that have dropped Flock have faced: most commonly, a pivot to license plate readers from other vendors.
Stanford is already installing replacement automatic license plate recognition (ALPRs) from the Canadian company, Genetec. Nguyen, the Stanford student, still counts the move away from Flock as a win but says there’s still work to be done, as student organizers’ goal is “to prevent any possibility of mass surveillance on campus”. She’s speaking with the university’s transportation department to push for transparency around who can access the data captured by the cameras.
On the opposite coast, in New York state, Binghamton University has announced a switch to Axon, an even larger police tech company, after dropping Flock. Claims of police abuse have not dogged Axon’s cameras in the same way as Flock’s, although many activists still have data privacy concerns.
Hatim Husainy, a junior at Binghamton who canvassed in dining halls to get more supporters for a petition to drop Flock, said removing the company was a “huge win” but added that students’ goals are to push the university to end the use of any ALPRs.
At UW-Madison, Bradley Keenan, who protested Flock on campus, has said the devices are “a signal from the police and university that everybody is under suspicion”. But the sophomore didn’t celebrate when he learned that his university had dropped its contract with the company because UW-Madison indicated that it would be immediately looking for alternatives.
Keenan said organizers have since updated their demands to push the university not to pursue a contract with any ALPR company.
Stanford did not respond to a request for comment but has said that Genetec, a Canadian company, will continue to store data on its internal servers for 30 days and that the new system is “designed for use within a single institution”. UW-Madison did not respond to a request for comment. A spokesperson for Binghamton University, Ryan Yarosh, said in an emailed statement that school officials have met with concerned faculty and students, and respects their “right to express their concerns”.
Inspired by nationwide activism, high school students rally against campus cameras
High schoolers are getting in on Flock activism, too.
In northern Colorado, high school senior Gabe Sayer and a friend started a petition to ban Flock cameras in the city of Loveland, and took particular issue with devices placed outside three high schools, including his own. Gabe said he first learned about Flock last year in his government class when discussing constitutional rights. His research led him to believe that the network is a dragnet that violates the fourth amendment. Sayer has been tabling outside a local grocery store to get signatures on the petition and meeting city councillors to share his views on Flock cameras.
In Georgia, two seniors at Alpharetta high school created a petition to remove Flock cameras from their campus. Aditya Manchanda and Henry Jones, who are part of their school’s Young Democratic Socialists of America chapter, made a video on TikTok about ALPRs and their petition attracted local news coverage.
“Schools should be a safe place, and we don’t feel safe,” Aditya said. “We feel our privacy being violated with the presence of these cameras.”
After gathering signatures, the duo emailed the school’s principal. “I know he wasn’t too pleased because we got media attention, and that’s not a great look,” Aditya said. The principal referred the boys to Fulton county school board members. Their emails have so far gone unanswered, so they’re planning to show up to an upcoming board meeting to relay their concerns.

====================================================================================================

UW-Madison did not respond to a request for comment. A spokesperson for Binghamton University, Ryan Yarosh, said in an emailed statement that school officials have met with concerned faculty and students, and respects their “right to express their concerns”.
Inspired by nationwide activism, high school students rally against campus cameras
High schoolers are getting in on Flock activism, too.
In northern Colorado, high school senior Gabe Sayer and a friend started a petition to ban Flock cameras in the city of Loveland, and took particular issue with devices placed outside three high schools, including his own. Gabe said he first learned about Flock last year in his government class when discussing constitutional rights. His research led him to believe that the network is a dragnet that violates the fourth amendment. Sayer has been tabling outside a local grocery store to get signatures on the petition and meeting city councillors to share his views on Flock cameras.
In Georgia, two seniors at Alpharetta high school created a petition to remove Flock cameras from their campus. Aditya Manchanda and Henry Jones, who are part of their school’s Young Democratic Socialists of America chapter, made a video on TikTok about ALPRs and their petition attracted local news coverage.
“Schools should be a safe place, and we don’t feel safe,” Aditya said. “We feel our privacy being violated with the presence of these cameras.”
After gathering signatures, the duo emailed the school’s principal. “I know he wasn’t too pleased because we got media attention, and that’s not a great look,” Aditya said. The principal referred the boys to Fulton county school board members. Their emails have so far gone unanswered, so they’re planning to show up to an upcoming board meeting to relay their concerns.

Why Zig over C for Systems Programming
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
14:01:13 UTC
23:01:13 KST

Why Zig Over C for Systems Programming
After I wrote Why Zig Over Rust for Systems Programming, the obvious next question was C. The Rust question was about safety and how much ceremony I was willing to accept for it. The C question is different, because Zig and C want the same things. Both are small languages without a Garbage Collector and both stay close to what the machine does. So this post is not about leaving C for something completely different. It is about the parts of C that cost me time and how Zig deals with them. All the code was compiled on a Mac with Zig 0.17 and clang.
What C gets right Link to heading
C is the language of UNIX. The kernels, the C libraries and most of the system libraries that every other language calls in the end are written in C and the man pages describe them with C prototypes and C examples. A C program from the 1990s still compiles today and there is a C compiler for almost every CPU that was ever made. The language is small enough that one book, The C Programming Language by Kernighan and Ritchie, covers all of it. Zig itself does not try to replace any of that. It talks to C libraries directly and it uses the C ABI when it needs to. I am not writing this post because I think C is bad; C is my favorite programming language! I am writing it because I do not want to write new code in C any more.
Cleanup without goto Link to heading
The C code that I like the least is a function that acquires several resources and has to release them in reverse order when something fails halfway. The usual answer, which is also the one the Linux kernel uses, is goto:
struct user *user_new(const char *name, size_t n_groups)
{
    struct user *u = malloc(sizeof *u);
    if (u == NULL)
        goto fail;
    u->name = strdup(name);
    if (u->name == NULL)
        goto fail_user;
    u->groups = calloc(n_groups, sizeof *u->groups);
    if (u->groups == NULL)
        goto fail_name;
    return u;
fail_name:
    free(u->name);
fail_user:
    free(u);
fail:
    return NULL;
}
It works, but the cleanup is far away from the allocation and the order of the labels has to match the order of the allocations by hand. The same function in Zig looks like this:
fn newUser(allocator: std.mem.Allocator, name: []const u8, n_groups: usize) !*User {
    const u = try allocator.create(User);
    errdefer allocator.destroy(u);
    u.name = try allocator.dupe(u8, name);
    errdefer allocator.free(u.name);
    u.groups = try allocator.alloc(u32, n_groups);
    return u;
}
Each errdefer sits right below the allocation it undoes and runs only if the function returns an error. Zig runs them in reverse order, so there are no labels to keep in order. What I like even more is that I can test every failure path. The std.testing.FailingAllocator type fails on the first, second or third allocation, depending on the value of fail_index. The testing allocator below it reports any memory that is not freed:
test "newUser does not leak when an allocation fails" {
    for (0..3) |i| {
        var failing = std.testing.FailingAllocator.init(std.testing.allocator, .{ .fail_index = i });
        try std.testing.expectError(error.OutOfMemory, newUser(failing.allocator(), "mihalis", 4));
    }
}
$ zig test user.zig
1/1 user.test.newUser does not leak when an allocation fails...OK
All 1 tests passed.
Testing the same three paths in the C version needs a wrapper around malloc() or an external tool.
A slice knows its length Link to heading
In C an array that goes to a function becomes a pointer and the length travels separately, if at all. The following C function has an off-by-one error, <= instead of <:
int sum(const int *a, size_t n)
{
    int total = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i <= n; i++)
        total += a[i];
    return total;
}
With an array of {1, 2, 3} it prints sum = 7 on my Mac and exits with 0. The extra 1 came from whatever was in memory after the array. Neither clang with -Wall -Wextra nor the program itself complains. In Zig a slice carries its length, so the same mistake stops the program in the debug and safe modes:
fn sum(a: []const i32) i32 {
    var total: i32 = 0;
    for (0..a.len + 1) |i| total += a[i];
    return total;
}
$ zig run sum.zig
thread 1278459 panic: index out of bounds: index 3, len 3
sum.zig:5:38: 0x1023c5b4b in sum (sum)
    for (0..a.len + 1) |i| total += a[i];
                                     ^
In real Zig code you would write for (a) |x| and the mistake cannot happen at all. Strings work the same way. A Zig string is a slice of bytes with a length, so there is no strlen() call that walks the memory looking for a 0 byte and no buffer that is one byte short because somebody forgot the terminator.
Errors you cannot ignore Link to heading
In C a function reports an error by returning -1 or NULL and setting errno, or by returning an error code, or in some other way that the man page describes. Nothing forces you to check. A lot of C code calls close() and fclose() without looking at the return value and the compiler is fine with it. In Zig an error is part of the return type and the compiler refuses to compile a call whose error you ignore. You have to use try, catch or if. The failure path is in the source code, right next to the call, which is also what I liked about Go.
comptime instead of the preprocessor Link to heading
The C preprocessor works with text and knows nothing about C. The classic #define MAX(a, b) ((a) > (b) ? (a) : (b)) evaluates one of its arguments twice and a big C project usually has a lot of #ifdef lines for every Operating System it supports. In Zig, code that runs at compile time is ordinary Zig code. Instead of #ifdef __linux__ I write if (builtin.os.tag == .linux) and the compiler drops the branch that does not apply. Generic data structures are functions that take a type and return a type. The compiler checks all of it like the rest of the program. I wrote about this in Zig comptime.
Fewer undefined surprises Link to heading
In C the overflow of a signed integer is undefined behavior. The following C program adds 1 to INT_MAX:
int add(int a, int b)
{
    return a + b;
}
int main(void)
{
    printf("%d\n", add(INT_MAX, 1));
    return 0;
}
Compiled with clang it prints -2147483648 and exits with 0, but the C standard allows the compiler to do anything, including removing code that it decides can never run. In Zig the overflow of any integer, signed or unsigned, is checked in the debug and safe modes and the program stops with panic: integer overflow and the line where it happened. When I want wrapping, I write +% and when I want the result to stop at the maximum value, I write +|.
Zig is also a C compiler Link to heading
The zig cc command is a C compiler that you can use instead of gcc or clang. It also cross compiles out of the box. On my Mac, zig cc -target x86_64-linux-musl -o sum sum.c creates a static Linux executable without installing anything else. In the debug mode zig cc also turns on the undefined behavior sanitizer, so the add() program above compiled with zig cc stops with panic: signed integer overflow: 2147483647 + 1 cannot be represented in type 'int'. A build.zig file can compile C files next to Zig files, so you can move a C project to Zig one file at a time and keep using the same build. If your Zig code needs C header files, Zig 0.17 removed @cImport() and you use zig translate-c instead. I wrote about that in @cImport() Is gone in Zig 0.17.
The honest tradeoff Link to heading
Zig is not C with all the problems solved. It does not stop you from using memory after you free it or from keeping a pointer to a stack variable that no longer exists. In the fast and small modes most safety checks are turned off and you are back to undefined behavior, just as in C. C is also stable in a way that Zig is not yet. When Zig 0.17 came out, I had to change 35 of the 152 Zig files of my book, while C code from decades ago still compiles. C has more tools, more books, more people who know it and compilers for chips that Zig does not support. If you write code for a microcontroller that only has a vendor C compiler, the choice is made for you.
Conclusion Link to heading
C is not going away and I still read a lot of it, because the UNIX source code and most system libraries are written in C and their man pages document them with C prototypes. But for new code I choose Zig. It lets me write the same kind of code as C, close to the machine and with no runtime, while the compiler and the debug mode catch many of the mistakes that C lets through without a word.
Happy coding in Zig!
Want to learn Zig? Look at my book Systems Programming with Zig.

Building collapses in Brooklyn with 8 people dead so far
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
13:55:32 UTC
22:55:32 KST

Officials End Rescue Operations at Site of Fatal Building Collapse in Brooklyn
Mayor Zohran Mamdani said eight people were killed, including two children, when a three-story building fell onto a construction site. At least 18 others were injured.
- Dave Sanders for The New York Times
- Angelina Katsanis for The New York Times
- Angelina Katsanis for The New York Times
- Bing Guan for The New York Times
- Angelina Katsanis for The New York Times
- Bing Guan for The New York Times
- Bing Guan for The New York Times
- Angelina Katsanis for The New York Times
- Bing Guan for The New York Times
- Andrea Barnes/The New York Times
- Reuters
- Dave Sanders for The New York Times
Para leer en español haz clic aquí.
Latest
Rescue workers stopped searching the rubble of a collapsed apartment building in Brooklyn on Thursday, saying they had determined there were no more victims.
Eight people, including two children, were killed in the collapse.
Earlier Thursday, Mayor Zohran Mamdani said at least 18 people, including four firefighters, had been injured when the building collapsed on Wednesday afternoon. Rescuers spent almost 24 hours digging through the site, even as a downpour on Thursday left shin-high pools of water on the surrounding streets.
“This has been a devastating evening and morning for our city,” Mr. Mamdani said. “We are mourning too many people who we love.”
Mr. Mamdani said the rescue effort had included hundreds of firefighters who worked through the night. He repeatedly praised the Fire Department’s response, saying the department had arrived at the scene “within one minute” after receiving the emergency call.
The collapsed building was adjacent to a construction site, where excavation had been ongoing.
Mr. Mamdani pledged “a full and thorough” investigation into the collapse, and said the Department of Buildings was “commencing sweeps” of roughly 100 other sites across the city that were “associated” with “five entities” that had ties to the construction site, he said.
He said that he was “incredibly troubled” by reports that construction continued at the site even though an inspection report showed there were cracks in the north facade of the collapsed building. He said the city would hold anyone responsible for unsafe conditions “fully accountable.”
The three-story building at 1321 Flatbush Avenue collapsed just after 4 p.m. on Wednesday. It fell into the gaping hole created by excavation at the construction site.
The collapsed building contained four apartments, with a commercial space on the ground floor, according to city information. Seven children were among those injured, officials said.
The rescue operation involved K-9 units, drones, helicopters and fire trucks alongside excavation machinery. The wall of an adjacent building was torn off by the collapse, leaving a staircase, a pink bedroom and closets filled with clothing exposed to the elements.
The Department of Buildings said the developers of the construction site adjacent to the collapsed building had an active permit for excavation and foundation work on the lot. The city’s buildings commissioner, Ahmed Tigani, told reporters that workers had been excavating it at the time of the collapse. A full stop-work order has been issued for the site.
Leanor Yupa waited at the site for news of her 22-year-old son, Gabriel Siguencia, an Ecuadorean immigrant who had started working as a carpenter there two weeks ago. She fell to her knees, screaming, when she learned he was among the dead.
Here’s what else to know:
- Immigrant community: Mr. Mamdani said the people killed and injured in the collapse included immigrants from Mexico, Colombia, Ecuador, Trinidad and elsewhere.
- Evacuations and street closures: Flatbush Avenue was closed from Clarendon Road to Bedford Avenue. Officials asked people to avoid the area. Vacate orders are in place on three nearby buildings: 1323, 1325, and 1327 Flatbush Avenues. Residents of other buildings who were temporarily displaced were allowed to return home, the mayor said.
- The building: The collapsed apartment building had been home to a number of businesses over the decades, including a medical clothes store and a tax-services office, according to city records. Read more ›
- Nearby site: A one-story building used to occupy the construction site next to the collapse. In March, the Department of Buildings ordered the property owner to halt demolition work after it found that there was inadequate protection for nearby properties. The order was fully lifted in August after the department found that the project team had corrected the violations.
Speaking at an aide center near the collapse, the city’s public advocate, Jumaane Williams, said that he was concerned by early information that seemed to indicate that the construction next door contributed to the building’s collapse. “I’m angry at the things that I’m hearing about why this might have happened,” Williams said, after visiting aid workers and displaced residents. “I’m trying my best not to speak out of turn until we get information, but learning that this building fell the way it fell — not straight down, on an angle — might indicate that it wasn’t supported the way it was supposed to.” He called for an analysis to see if construction projects in lower income neighborhoods in Brooklyn received the same scrutiny as projects in Manhattan.
As darkness fell on the scene, a yellow backhoe with an oversize jaw excavated loads of debris into an oversize red dumpster. It was one of four red dumpsters overflowing with splintered wood, twisted metal, and torn scraps of clothing. Uniformed firefighters stood beside ladder trucks in the glare of NYPD spotlights. Workers set up tall tents in expectation of more cold, soaking rain.
