BEDFORD AVE.
Vacate orders were in place on Thursday at buildings adjacent to site of the collapsed Brooklyn apartment building. Residents of other buildings who were temporarily displaced were allowed to return home, according to Mayor Zohran Mamdani.
Gov. Kathy Hochul of New York called the collapse “absolutely, absolutely” heartbreaking on Thursday morning at a news media event on a small business initiative in Harlem. She said her office remained in contact with the city and that the state was ready to provide resources if needed. Ms. Hochul also sang praises of the Fire Department and its quick response.
“New Yorkers are stunned by what they’re seeing, but the investigators are already at work,” she said. “And New Yorkers deserve to know how and why this happened, and they’ll get to the bottom of it.”
Nearly 24 hours after a three-story apartment building collapsed in Brooklyn, rescue workers remain locked in a fight to identify and rescue any survivors trapped in the rubble.
The extensive search and rescue operations have involved canine units, helicopters and excavation machinery. So far, eight people have been confirmed dead and over a dozen are injured. Fire officials have said the search efforts are likely to continue for days.
It is not yet clear why the building, in the Flatbush neighborhood, collapsed. But the Fire Department’s search and rescue mission is following a standard playbook, one that dates back to World War II. It has been deployed countless times after similar disasters around the world, including the 2010 earthquake in Haiti and a condo collapse in 2021 in Surfside, Fla., that killed nearly 100 people.
Here’s how these search and rescue operations work.
What happens first?
After determining the basics of the collapsed building — including whether it is residential or commercial — rescuers look for any people who may be trapped on the surface of the wreckage, said Dave Downey, a retired fire chief from Miami-Dade County in Florida who has responded to many disasters involving collapsed buildings, including the Sept. 11 terror attacks and the 1995 Oklahoma City bombing.
After that, Mr. Downey said, rescuers try to determine the manner in which the building collapsed, which informs their further search efforts.
If the building is still partially standing and there is open space between the floors, there could be more survivable space for any people trapped inside. Rescuers would then start looking inside these so-called void spaces, “the areas where there’s the greatest chance for survival,” Mr. Downey said.
Search efforts are of course more difficult when a building falls apart in a way that leaves little open space, as happened with the Surfside condo, which collapsed like a pancake, floor by floor, according to Mr. Downey.
What other factors must rescuers account for?
Rescuers need to also consider external factors like the weather. In the case of the Flatbush collapse, rainy conditions on Thursday afternoon may complicate the rescue efforts.
“You want to control any of the utilities, whether it’s gas, whether it’s water, whether it’s power,” Mr. Downey said. “You certainly don’t want water flooding any of these void spaces.”
Water mains were shut off temporarily after the collapse, but by noon on Thursday water service in the area had been restored, according to the city’s Department of Environmental Protection.
Rescuers may also seek to stabilize whatever parts of the building remain standing. That could mean using wood to shore up parts of the structure, allowing workers to access the rubble where more survivors could be trapped.
What technology is used?
When looking for survivors, rescuers tend to use specialized equipment like drones, listening devices and search cameras.
In Brooklyn, the Police Department has deployed drones to capture the scene while members of its aviation unit search from the sky, a police spokeswoman said on Thursday. Officers from the department’s Emergency Service Unit were also searching the area with dogs from the K-9 team.
Heavy equipment like grapplers were also being used on Thursday to remove rubble, said Lillian Bonsignore, the fire commissioner.
When does an operation turn to recovery?
Ms. Bonsignore said on Thursday afternoon that the operation was still focused on rescuing survivors. But at a certain point, officials will have to make the difficult choice to shift the operation to recovery, Mr. Downey said.
“When the decision is made that we no longer have persons that have survived, we transition to recovery,” Mr. Downey said.
Such a determination can take days and depends on a complex set of factors. One of the last to be considered is the amount of time that has elapsed since the collapse, Mr. Downey said. More important is the safety of the rescuers, which has to be weighed against the chances that anyone is still alive.
Still, a transition to recovery does not preclude the possibility that survivors will be found. When he was in Haiti after the 2010 earthquake, Mr. Downey said, his team found a 2-year-old child alive in the rubble of a building eight days after it collapsed.
“We promise, with everything we have, we will not stop working until everyone is rescued or recovered,” Ms. Bonsignore said.
Chelsia Rose Marcius, Caitlyn Freeman and Hurubie Meko contributed reporting.
The consul general of Ecuador, Gicela Andrade, has been visiting with city officials at a staging area near the scene. Andrade said that at least two Ecuadorians have been identified as victims. The remains of one, Jose Luis Caguana Cosquillo, will be sent to Ecuador to be buried, she said. Andrade accompanied Leanor Yupa, the mother of a second Ecuadorian victim, Gabriel Ariolfo Siguencia, to meet with detectives this afternoon.“When we heard the sad news we immediately got to ground zero where this tragedy occured and we’ve been in contact with the mother of one of these victims who has been missing since yesterday,” Andrade said in Spanish. “The authorities have just confirmed the death of our compatriot, and his mother was in that moment learning of the death of her son.”
Monzurat Oni, the chairwoman of Brooklyn’s Community Board 17, where the collapse took place, said the developer of the construction site did not come to a board meeting this year to answer the public’s questions about the project. Under ballot measures passed last year that sped up the process for housing projects, the community board has less input, she said, even as the neighborhood is seeing a surge in new developments.
“I’m not against new construction,” Oni said. “I know New York needs to grow. We want new construction to be responsive to the existing community.”
Julie Menin, the City Council speaker, began her comments at a council meeting on Thursday with a moment of silence for the victims of the building collapse.
“Today, my colleagues and I joined New Yorkers across the five boroughs in mourning the eight lives that were lost in the tragic collapse of a building in Flatbush,” she said, adding, “We’re incredibly grateful for the approximately 250 first responders who raced to the scene and who have been working tirelessly throughout the night on this heroic rescue efforts.”
She added that she was praying for the recovery of those injured.
As uniformed firefighters entered closed storefronts beside the collapsed building, representatives from the mayor’s office visited nearby businesses that remained open. With the streets blocked with emergency vehicles, the city’s representatives offered to help with any problems getting packages delivered or trash removed.
First responders, investigators and city officials have a hard job in the wake of a building collapse: finding missing people, securing the area and notifying family members. A Y.M.C.A. on Flatbush Avenue has become a de facto staging area for families looking for information regarding loved ones. Throughout Thursday, teary people have entered the recreation center searching for information. A representative from Ecuador’s government visited the site earlier, accompanying the family of Gabriel Siguencia as they sought to confirm their son’s death. Aid groups and local restuarants, including the nearby Chick-fil-A, are dropping off supplies.
A spokesperson for the American Red Cross said 14 adults and five children across seven households had registered with the organization for emergency assistance, which includes temporary lodging.
On Thursday, Ethan Clark and his roommate, DeAndre Finley, left their third-floor apartment on Flatbush Avenue three buildings down from the collapse, holding umbrellas over their heads and carrying several bags of clothes. They were traveling to Harlem to stay with some friends. “If it becomes an extended thing, I don’t know what we’ll do,” Finley said.
The eight people killed when a building in Brooklyn collapsed into a construction site on Wednesday were a cross-section of the bustling, fast-growing Flatbush neighborhood.
They included elementary-school-age cousins, construction workers from Ecuador and Guatemala, and a DoorDash deliveryman and an Amazon worker from Guinea in West Africa, according to an internal police report and family and friends of the victims.
Six died at the building at 1321 Flatbush Avenue and the construction site next door, where workers had been excavating when the building crashed down around 4 p.m. on Wednesday. Two others were pulled from the wreckage alive but died at hospitals, Mayor Zohran Mamdani said at a news conference on Thursday.
By midafternoon Thursday, firefighters had halted rescue operations after determining there were no more victims in the rubble.
“This has been a devastating evening and morning for our city,” the mayor said earlier.
Another 18 people, including some firefighters, had been injured, Mr. Mamdani said. Officials have not determined what caused the collapse.
Here’s what we know about the victims:
- Nicole Daniela Salvala Rodriguez, age 9
- Kevin Santiago Reyes Gonzalez, 10
- Juan Daniel Salvada Guzman, 31
Edgar Santiago Sapón Gutierrez, 26
Edgar Santiago Sapón Gutierrez, a 26-year-old construction worker, was working at the site when the building collapsed.
Relatives spent the entire night looking for him, said Marisela Vasquez, 35, whose niece is married to Mr. Gutierrez.
“We went to the morgue and didn’t find him,” Ms. Vasquez said in Spanish. They returned to the scene of the collapsed building at about 5 or 6 a.m. and saw emergency workers pulling bodies out of the rubble.
“Unfortunately, they told us that he had died,” she said.
Mr. Gutierrez migrated from Guatemala three years ago, Eliseo Vásquez Talé, his uncle, said. He has a 6-year-old son, and his wife had arrived at the scene Wednesday evening after hearing of the collapse, hoping to find him.
“We came to this country to find a better life,” said Mr. Vásquez Talé. “Unfortunately our government is corrupt.”
On Thursday morning, they returned to the morgue, trying to see if they could identify his body. Nobody wants to believe he is dead, Ms. Vasquez said.
“We want to make sure it’s him,” she said.
Mamadou Bhoye Barry, 23
Mamadou Bhoye Barry, 23, a food delivery worker from Guinea, was pulled from the rubble early Thursday and pronounced dead at the scene.
He had been getting ready to work a night shift when the building collapsed, relatives said.
Mamadou Bhoye N’Diaye, 31
The body of Mamadou Bhoye N’Diaye, 31, Mr. Barry’s relative and an Amazon worker, was pulled from the rubble about an hour before Mr. Barry’s. But he remained unidentified for much of the day, frustrating his family’s search for answers.
On Thursday morning, Mr. N’Diaye’s brother, Habib N’Diaye, 32, sat slumped on the pavement outside a deli near the building waiting for updates with their cousin, Abubakar Diallo, 47.
Mr. Diallo expressed frustration at the lack of information from the authorities. He said they did not know what to tell family waiting for news in Guinea, a West African country racked by political instability and poverty.
“That missing room is his room,” Diallo said, referring to Habib N’Diaye as he pointed to a mound of rubble near the back of the building. “What is he going to tell his mother back home?”
José Luis Caguana, 26
José Luis Caguana, 26, a construction worker at the site of the collapse, had recently found work after a period of unemployment.
While he was looking for work in September, he would pick up his 4-year-old son at his pre-K in Bushwick, Brooklyn, Alexandra Simonian, the son’s teacher, said.
“When the dad would drop him off, he didn’t want to leave the dad, ever,” Ms. Simonian said. “They were very attached, a very sweet kind of family.”
Mr. Caguana, an immigrant from Ecuador who came to New York at least four years ago, was supporting a growing family: His wife is expecting a child this month, Ms. Simonian said.
On Thursday, Ms. Simonian said, Mr. Caguana’s wife, Nataly, and son came early to school.
“She was asking if it’s OK if he doesn’t come to school this week, because of the dad passing away,” Ms. Simonian said. “She was crying, and we were all hugging her.”
Mr. Caguana was also an avid soccer player. An official from his soccer club in New York, Club Deportivo La Bahía, posted on Facebook, “I will always remember you as a unique player, with that special magic in your feet.”
Gabriel Siguencia, 22
Gabriel Siguencia also came to New York from Ecuador in search of work, arriving in March 2025, his family said.
“He came here looking for a better life, a means to live, like so many migrants do,” Javier Ortega, a family friend, said in Spanish.
About two weeks ago, Mr. Siguencia started a new job: He was a carpenter at the construction site on Flatbush Avenue, his mother, Leanor Yupa, said.
Ms. Yupa arrived at the site of the building collapse at 8 p.m. Wednesday night in search of news about her son. She and her husband, draped in a blanket, waited all night on the sidewalk, watching emergency workers haul debris out of the site.
On Thursday afternoon, Ms. Yupa and family members sheltered from the pouring rain beneath an overhang. When she finally heard her son had died, Ms. Yupa dropped to her knees and screamed.
Kaja Andric, Miles G. Cohen, Maria Cramer, Ana Ley, Chelsia Rose Marcius, Wesley Parnell and Rachel Sherman contributed reporting. Kitty Bennett contributed research.
An hour and a half after a building collapsed into a construction site in the Flatbush neighborhood of Brooklyn on Wednesday, a man walked through a crowd of onlookers and arrived at a strip of yellow police tape. He tried to push his way into the scene, but emergency workers blocked his path. It was unsafe to enter, they said.
The man, who did not give his name, paused. His wife was trapped inside, he said. And she was six months pregnant.
He walked across Flatbush Avenue, where he stood on the sidewalk surrounded by police officers, journalists and Jumaane Williams, the city’s public advocate. After nearly an hour of waiting, a journalist relayed the news at 6:30 p.m.: The man’s wife had been found, alive.
The man broke down crying and was embraced by Mr. Williams.
“She is still alive,” the man said in an impromptu news conference near the scene. “I am very happy.”
More than a dozen firefighters worked to lift the woman, who was strapped to an orange stretcher, out of the pit. She was carried to the open doors of an ambulance. Her husband stood by her stretcher for a few minutes before she was loaded into the back. The man climbed into the cab of the ambulance, which took them to a hospital.
The woman is in stable condition, said Farah Louis, the councilwoman who represents the neighborhood. The rescued woman is from Guinea, and she lived in the building with other residents who were immigrants, including from West Africa, Ms. Louis said.
“These folks are our neighbors,” Ms. Louis said. “They’re our friends. That’s why the community is so heartbroken.”
The collapse has killed at least eight people and injured at least 17. In addition to the pregnant woman, other people were removed from the rubble in stretchers, including a small child.
A 26-year-old construction worker, Edgar Santiago Sapón Gutierrez, has been identified as a victim of the collapse.
Relatives had spent the entire night looking for him, said Marisela Vasquez, 35, whose niece is married to Gutierrez. “We went to the morgue and didn’t find him,” Vasquez said. They returned to the collapse site early in the morning and saw emergency workers pulling bodies out of the rubble. “Unfortunately, they told us that he had died,” she said. Gutierrez has a 6-year-old son.
By noon, officials had confirmed that Gutiérrez was among those killed, according to an internal police report.
The Buildings Department said that the license of the special inspector overseeing foundation work at the construction site adjacent to the collapsed building was active, and that city records that indicated that the license had expired was based on information on the old portal. It remains unclear if the inspector had been on site recently.
A complaint filed with the Department of Buildings on Wednesday night, after the building collapse, raised concerns about the excavation work at the adjacent development site, at 2625 Bedford Avenue. The anonymous complainant said the “subcontractor may have excavated too much of the area along the neighboring foundation” on a site with sandy soil and high groundwater.
Complaints are sometimes filed with the city after a major building failure, but it is unclear who submitted the report. The Buildings Department said it was investigating the report, and noted that the complaint was filed “by a member of the public” at 9 p.m., five hours after the collapse.
The site of the collapsed building in Flatbush, Brooklyn, was somber on Thursday as emergency workers removed debris and rubble. The wall of a neighboring building appeared to have come down in the collapse. A bedroom with a pink motif was visible on the second floor. On the first floor, clothes were hanging in a closet.
Mamdani said that he was “incredibly troubled” by reports that construction continued at the site even though an inspection report showed there were cracks in the north facade of the collapsed building. He stressed that the city would conduct a full investigation of the situation and hold anyone responsible “fully accountable.”

US test reactor cleared for operations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
13:54:49 UTC
22:54:49 KST

The test reactor, near Lockhart in Caldwell County, Texas reached first criticality in August, less than a year after groundbreaking. It is one of 11 advanced reactor projects selected by the US Department of Energy (DOE) in August 2025 for the Nuclear Reactor Pilot Program, a programme aiming to expedite the testing of advanced reactor designs under a DOE authorisation pathway.
The DOE has now cleared the final operations hold point for the reactor - also known as Groves-1 - allowing the facility to advance into its research, testing, and training phase. This will help Oklo prepare the teams and systems needed to support the efficient delivery and operation of future nuclear projects, the company said. Operating experience from Groves-1 will directly inform Groves-2 Forge, Oklo's planned isotope production and processing pilot facility, which is part of the DOE's Nuclear Energy Launch Pad initiative to promote the rapid development and implementation of advanced nuclear technologies by private industry.
With Groves-1 now cleared for unrestricted operations, Oklo said it will test its capabilities relevant to future isotope reactors and the company's fast fission reactor, the Aurora powerhouse.
Groves-1 will serve as a key training and qualification platform for nuclear personnel at future reactor deployments, alongside further equipment testing, including continued evaluations of control rod drive mechanism performance and reliability. It will also enable criticality and reactivity benchmarking operations that support Oklo's isotope production capabilities, its engineering and design models, and potentially light water reactors and the large light water reactor fleet. The company says the resulting data could also help improve fuel performance and power output in small and large light water reactors.
"Taking Groves-1 from greenfield to criticality in less than a year was an important demonstration of what our team can accomplish," Oklo CEO and co-founder Jacob DeWitte said. "Now we're building on that experience by operating the reactor, testing new capabilities, and learning directly from the work. Those lessons can help us develop the people, systems, and processes needed as we advance additional isotope projects and Aurora powerhouses."
"DOE's Reactor Pilot Program reset the nuclear industry's expectations for how quickly we can move from advanced reactor design to demonstration while upholding best-in-class safety standards," Acting Deputy Assistant Secretary for Nuclear Reactors Chimi Zacot said. "Successes like Groves-1's criticality are now paving the way for what comes next."
New reactor designs for commercial use in the USA are reviewed and approved by the US Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC), but under the Atomic Energy Act - the fundamental law governing civilian uses of nuclear materials and facilities in the USA - the DOE has the authority to approve reactor authorisations for research, development and demonstration. That authority is now being used to enable advanced reactor developers to certify, build and test prototype reactors that can inform full-scale commercial deployment under the Reactor Pilot Program pathway.
In response to executive orders issued in May 2025 that called for at least three prototype reactors to reach criticality by 4 July 2026, the DOE revamped its authorisation pathway to streamline the process.
In October, the NRC finalised a rule allowing applicants to use relevant analyses, test data and other information from DOE or Department of War (formerly the Department of Defense) reactor demonstration programmes to support commercial reactor licence applications. The rule is designed to make NRC reviews more efficient by allowing the agency to build on existing technical evidence while independently evaluating the safety of proposed commercial projects.

Jensen Huang Admitted He Was Facing an "Avalanche" of Excess Wafers and Memory
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
13:08:48 UTC
22:08:48 KST

For the past two years, the narrative around inflated GPU and RAM prices has been the insatiable AI demand eating up wafer at TSMC and every module on Earth must go to AI data centers. However, the narrative is cracking ever since reports of channel stuffing and over commitments came out.
Now with Nvidia’s former CEO Jensen Huang gone, more details are out that corroborate our earlier reports on inflated demand on AI. According to Wall Street Journal, Huang admitted in August that major cloud providers are moving away from Nvidia in favor of in-house chips, leaving Nvidia staring down a massive oversupply of prepaid TSMC silicon and memory that was “coming at him like an ‘avalanche'”.
The WSJ report details a heated clash in August 2026 between Huang and Nvidia’s longtime Chief Financial Officer, Colette Kress, the executive who has now taken over as Nvidia’s interim CEO.
Kress was concerned about Nvidia’s growing financial exposure and wanted to rein in the company’s “circular financing” commitments to cloud providers, and AI companies like OpenAI and Anthropic.
While early talks had floated backstopping roughly 8 gigawatts of OpenAI compute (worth up to $250 billion), Kress successfully scaled Nvidia’s signed guarantee cap down to roughly 4.25 gigawatts, or up to $125 billion.
In an August meeting, Huang belittled Kress for questioning the deals, calling her “a deer in the headlights”. To justify why Nvidia needed to financially backstop OpenAI and smaller cloud providers to keep buying its GPUs, Huang admitted that Nvidia was caught in a massive squeeze.
Then he went on to describe the situation the company was in. Major cloud providers were running away from Nvidia, making plans to use alternative chips and lessen their reliance on the firm. Even worse, Huang said, the vast supply of chip wafers and memory Nvidia had committed to buying were coming at him like an ‘avalanche.’
Per the WSJ
Nvidia has reportedly contacted TSMC to wind down their chip orders.
For PC builders and hardware enthusiast the reported admission is validating, and quite glaring. Jensen Huang, instead of allowing excess TSMC wafer capacity and memory flow back into reasonable priced GeForce products and broader DRAM production, allegedly scrambled to use its own balance sheet to finance data center buildouts so AI startups and absorb Nvidia’s over-ordered chips.
Reports of glut of unsold hardware have been around for a while, but only now mainstream media is catching up to what Nvidia was reportedly doing. Many industry analysts including Ed Zitron reported months ago how Nvidia and other companies have exposed themselves to devastating financial risk, and also made sure to channel stuff META, Google, Amazon, and others by leveraging their FOMO.
Zitron estimated that at least $200 billion worth of Nvidia AI chips are sitting idle in warehouses, purchased by cloud giants that lacked the electrical grid capacity or immediate end-user demand to plug them in.
The bottom line is, AI demand is no way near as high that it would consume this much silicon.

Israel’s VCs see opportunity for challengers in Nvidia’s turmoil
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
12:52:19 UTC
21:52:19 KST

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s resignation has been received with worry in Israel, the company’s “second home,” as Huang described it in 2023. The company’s importance flows both ways, as the semiconductor giant has provided a major boost to Israel’s economic forecasts in recent years.
Intel, Microsoft, and many homegrown startups also conduct a sizable share of their R&D in Israel. But owing to Nvidia’s stronghold on AI given the centrality of its GPUs for training LLMs and using them, many in the country are wondering how much of a reshuffle will result from the company’s leadership crisis.
As with everything at Nvidia, Huang drove the company to acquire Israeli chipmaker Mellanox for $6.9 billion in 2019 and use the company as the base for its largest R&D facility outside of the U.S. Nvidia also made plans to build a multibillion-dollar campus in northern Israel.
“Jensen brought Nvidia to Israel with the acquisition of Mellanox, so there is a strong positive sentiment towards Nvidia and Jensen in Israel’s tech community,” Israeli repeat cybersecurity entrepreneur Pavel Gurvich told TechCrunch.
Beyond the 6,000 locals Nvidia employs, the bigger question is whether the company will remain as committed to Israel with someone else at the helm. As a result, his line of succession is being observed just as closely by those who hope the company continues to do so as by those who don’t want it to happen.
The latter have already gathered that they may be in for disappointment. In a 2023 interview that is now doing the rounds on X, interim CEO Colette Kress had declared that as Nvidia’s CFO, she typically started her day “with Israel meetings, and only then continue[d] to California.”
Kress may not remain in charge in the long run, but the many names floated for the role include two Israelis, Apple’s Johny Srouji and Nvidia’s Michael Kagan — although they’re not at the top of the still quite tentative list.
Regardless of who gets chosen, Huang’s successor will likely have less of a grip on Nvidia and the tech industry as a whole. But investors don’t think that would cause demand for Nvidia’s product to fade away.
According to Yanai Oron, a general partner at Vertex Ventures Israel, “slower decisions at Nvidia could give competitors an opening, but if that uncertainty also slows AI spending, they could end up with a bigger share of a smaller opportunity.”
Nvidia has been responsible for a vast share of the spending in the global AI industry. Under Huang’s leadership, the company used its cash reserves to massively invest across the spectrum, both in companies and in its own next-generation chips.
Such an empire may not be doomed to fall with its architect’s ouster, but it does seem less likely to become a full-on monopoly. According to Adi Levanon, a founder and managing partner at Selah Ventures, “[Huang’s] departure doesn’t dismantle Nvidia’s technology or ecosystem, but it could give competitors more room to shape what comes next.”
And now that companies globally are deploying increasingly demanding AI agents into their workflows, there’s been a lot of interest — and some venture capital — flowing into developers of AI-specific chips and inference software that could compete with Nvidia’s.
“These challengers now have a window to prove a different approach delivers better economics; a leadership change alone won’t win them the business,” Levanon said.

====================================================================================================

Microsoft&#8217;s massive Outlook outage continues
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
12:48:03 UTC
21:48:03 KST

Service is gradually starting to recover for Microsoft Outlook and 365 after an almost two-day outage impacting millions of users around the world. While some apps and features are still running in a degraded state, some people (including Verge staffers) are reporting that access to their emails has been restored, though the flow of Outlook mail might be delayed while efforts to fully fix the outage continue.
Microsoft’s massive Outlook outage continues
Microsoft 365 is starting to recover as its outage enters a second day.
Charles Lamanna, Copilot, agents, and platform leader at Microsoft said in a statement to The Verge that the situation is continuing to improve:
“We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure. We know our customers rely on our services to get important work done every day, and regret the impact these outages had on those who depend on us. Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward.”
Senior Verge correspondent Tom Warren reports that according to his sources, the outage is linked to Azure VNet config changes. Microsoft has said that it hasn’t found any evidence of the outage being connected to “malicious activity,” and that a full report will be shared after the issues are mitigated. In a statement reported by Sky News tech correspondent Rowland Manthorpe, Microsoft described the outage as a “technical issue,” and says it isn’t related to “cyber, AI/agents, DDOS, etc.”
The scope of the outage also affects features in other Microsoft services, including SharePoint Online, Teams, Copilot AI, and 365 apps. In a status update posted just before 10AM ET, Microsoft said that “some users may still experience intermittent connectivity issues or delayed email delivery” while restoration efforts are underway.
Update, November 13th, 11:16AM ET: Added the latest information from Microsoft.

Jensen Huang didn’t show confidence in his successor, report says
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
12:28:23 UTC
21:28:23 KST

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang described Colette Kress, the company’s CFO who has now replaced him as interim CEO, as “a deer in the headlights” during a presentation earlier in the summer, the Wall Street Journal reported, citing an anonymous source.
Huang made the comment during a meeting with Nvidia’s finance team about the company’s plans to provide guarantees worth billions of dollars to data center operators that couldn’t afford to buy its chips up front, according to the report.
He reportedly also said Kress was a person who couldn’t help him steer strategy or decision-making.
Kress has been with Nvidia for over 13 years now, after serving as SVP and CFO of Cisco’s Business Technology and Operations Finance. The report said she has questioned Nvidia’s program-focused, credit-based deals involving Huang’s son-in-law Nico Caprez, and wanted to know how to decide which deals are beneficial, how to value guarantees, and determine returns.
Earlier in the year, she had scaled back on Nvidia’s commitment for OpenAI’s ambitious Ohio data center project. The company was in talks to provide $250 billion in credit to OpenAI but ended up committing $105 billion.
Huang resigned Thursday after reports of two extramarital affairs, including one with the company’s VP for healthcare, Kimberly Powell. The company is also investigating a possible Chinese espionage plot.
After Huang’s resignation, Reuters reported that he was having a third affair with another employee. Nvidia is currently searching for a permanent CEO to replace Huang, who had the top job for over 33 years.

Show HN: Reflota – Open-source data recovery for NTFS/FAT disks with phased copy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
12:17:52 UTC
21:17:52 KST

Recuperá archivos de discos, pendrives y tarjetas (NTFS, FAT32, FAT16, FAT12 y exFAT), incluso los que borraste. Gratis, de código abierto y en modo solo lectura.
Cuando Windows dice "el disco no está formateado", una carpeta no abre o el explorador se cuelga, muchas veces los archivos siguen ahí. Reflota los reconstruye leyendo el disco directamente: en NTFS lee toda la tabla maestra (MFT) y arma el árbol de carpetas con la información que cada archivo guarda de su propia carpeta; en FAT y exFAT lee las carpetas y sigue las cadenas de clusters.
También encuentra archivos borrados y te dice cuántos de ellos siguen recuperables. Y si el disco se formateó o ya no tiene nombres, puede buscar archivos por su contenido, encontrar particiones perdidas y hacer una copia segura de un disco que está fallando.
Estado: beta. Funciona, pero cada disco dañado es distinto. Probalo primero con una copia de lo importante si podés.
Novedades todavía sin probar en discos reales (se verificaron con imágenes de prueba, no con hardware): FAT16/FAT12, búsqueda de RAW de cámara/PSD/RAR, reconstrucción de JPEG fragmentados, buscador por nombre y copia por fases. Si probás alguna con un disco real, contá cómo te fue en Issues.
Bajá Reflota.exe desde la sección Releases. No necesita instalación ni Python.
Como el programa no está firmado digitalmente, Windows puede mostrar "Windows protegió su PC": tocá Más información → Ejecutar de todas formas. Algunos antivirus desconfían de los programas que leen discos de forma directa; el código es abierto y podés compilar el .exe vos mismo (ver más abajo). Junto a cada Release se publica el hash SHA256 para comprobar que la descarga no se corrompió.
- Abrí Reflota. Aparece la lista de tus discos y unidades.
- Tildá lo que querés recuperar, todo en el mismo árbol:
  - Tildar un disco o una partición → se copia todo.
  - Entrá en la partición y tildá solo carpetas o archivos → se copia solo eso.
  - El tamaño total se calcula solo mientras tildás.
  - Activá Incluir archivos borrados para ver también lo que se borró: aparece en rojo, en su carpeta original, con su estado.
  - Buscar por nombre (arriba del árbol): escribí parte del nombre (factura), un patrón (*.pdf, foto_??.jpg) y apretá Enter. Busca sin importar mayúsculas ni acentos, en la partición elegida (o en todas las que ya abriste), incluye lo borrado y muestra los resultados como un grupo nuevo del árbol que se tilda igual que lo demás. Primero abrí la partición (clic en su flecha).
- Abajo, elegí la carpeta donde copiar (en otro disco) y apretá RECUPERAR.
Podés parar cuando quieras y volver a empezar: lo que ya se copió no se repite.
Windows no deja leer un disco "en crudo" sin permisos de administrador (le pasa a cualquier programa de recuperación). Reflota abre sin pedirlos y los pide recién cuando tocás un disco para leerlo; ahí se reabre con el aviso de Windows. El programa nunca escribe en el disco de origen.
Elegí primero un disco o una partición en la lista.
Buscar archivos por contenido. Reconoce los archivos por su forma interna, sin necesitar nombres ni carpetas. Sirve cuando el disco se formateó, la tabla de archivos está destruida o el archivo borrado ya perdió su nombre. Podés buscar solo en el espacio libre (rápido, ideal para lo borrado) o en todo el disco o la partición. Los resultados aparecen en el mismo árbol, agrupados por tipo, y se recuperan igual que lo demás (tildar y RECUPERAR). Detecta: fotos (JPG, PNG, GIF, BMP, WebP, HEIC, TIFF), fotos RAW de cámara (CR2, CR3, NEF, ARW, DNG, ORF, RW2, PEF, RAF), PSD/PSB de Photoshop, videos (MP4/MOV/3GP, AVI, MKV/WebM), audio (MP3, M4A, WAV), documentos (PDF, DOCX, XLSX, PPTX, ODT/ODS/ODP, DOC/XLS/PPT, EPUB), comprimidos (ZIP, RAR, 7Z, JAR, APK) y bases de datos SQLite. El largo exacto de cada archivo se calcula leyendo su estructura (no se corta "a ojo"), y los archivos que contienen otros archivos adentro (como una miniatura en una foto) salen enteros. Los nombres originales no se pueden recuperar: salen numerados (recuperado_00012.jpg). Los archivos que estaban fragmentados (partidos en pedazos no contiguos) normalmente no se pueden rearmar y pueden salir incompletos o mezclados, salvo las fotos JPEG si tildás Reconstruir fotos JPEG fragmentadas: Reflota decodifica la foto hasta donde deja de ser válida y busca el pedazo que la continúa; solo la entrega si el resultado cierra exacto (todos los bloques de imagen y el final del archivo). Es mucho más lento, funciona con JPEG «baseline» (los de cámaras y celulares; no los progresivos) y puede no lograrlo cuando hay muchos fragmentos o el hueco está lleno de otras fotos parecidas: en ese caso la foto simplemente no aparece (no entrega una foto equivocada a propósito, pero conviene mirar el resultado).
Buscar particiones perdidas. Recorre el disco entero buscando los inicios de particiones NTFS, FAT32, FAT16/12 y exFAT, aunque la tabla de particiones esté borrada y hasta con los sectores de arranque destruidos (usa sus copias de respaldo). Las que encuentra se agregan a la lista. Puede tardar bastante en discos grandes y se puede parar.
Crear imagen del disco (copia segura). Cuando un disco falla, lo mejor es copiarlo una sola vez a un archivo y trabajar sobre esa copia, sin seguir forzando el original. La copia va por fases, como hacen las herramientas de rescate profesionales: primero lee rápido todo lo que se lee bien (saltando las zonas que fallan para no insistir), después recorta los bordes de las zonas dañadas, luego las lee sector por sector y al final reintenta los sectores ilegibles. Así se rescata lo máximo con el mínimo castigo al disco. Los sectores que nunca se pudieron leer quedan en ceros y se anotan en *.malos.txt. El avance se guarda en *.mapa: si se interrumpe (o se corta la luz) se continúa exactamente desde ahí. Después agregás la imagen a la lista y recuperás desde ahí.
- Dejá de usar el disco dañado. Cada escritura nueva puede pisar lo que querés recuperar.
- Copiá siempre a otro disco. Reflota se niega a copiar al mismo disco que está leyendo.
- Si el disco hace ruidos raros o desaparece a cada rato, apagalo: ese es un problema físico y conviene un servicio profesional.
Hace
- Lee discos físicos, unidades USB, tarjetas e imágenes de disco (.img).
- Sigue adelante aunque haya sectores ilegibles: los rellena con ceros, marca el archivo como parcial y lo anota en recuperacion_log.csv.
- Si el sector de arranque está dañado, prueba la copia de respaldo (FAT32, exFAT) y otras ubicaciones típicas de la MFT (NTFS). FAT16/FAT12 no guardan copia del arranque.
- Muestra los archivos que no encuentran su carpeta en _huerfanos.
Sobre los archivos borrados
- Al borrar un archivo, el sistema suele conservar su nombre y dónde estaba el contenido, pero libera ese espacio. Si después se guardaron otros archivos encima, el contenido se pierde. Reflota lo detecta y lo muestra como recuperable, N% intacto o sobrescrito (los sobrescritos no se copian; los parciales sí, avisando).
- NTFS: usa los registros libres de la MFT y el mapa de espacio libre ($Bitmap). Es lo más confiable.
- FAT32, FAT16/12 y exFAT: usa las entradas de carpeta borradas. Estos formatos borran la cadena de clusters, así que Reflota asume que el archivo estaba en un solo bloque seguido; si estaba fragmentado, el resultado puede salir mezclado aunque diga "recuperable". En FAT32, un archivo con nombre corto (formato 8.3) pierde la primera letra del nombre al borrarse (queda _orrame.pdf).
- Si el disco se formateó o la carpeta que lo contenía ya no existe, los borrados pueden aparecer en _borrados (carpeta de origen desconocida).
- Siempre conviene recuperar a otro disco y dejar de usar el original apenas se nota el problema.
No hace (todavía)
- No recupera otros sistemas de archivos como ext4, APFS o ReFS, ni discos con BitLocker sin descifrar.
- La búsqueda por contenido no reconstruye nombres, carpetas ni fechas, y solo sabe unir pedazos de fotos JPEG baseline (no otros formatos fragmentados).
- No detecta formatos muy poco comunes en la búsqueda por contenido, ni los PSD con compresión ZIP ni los JPEG progresivos fragmentados.
- Los archivos cifrados (EFS) o comprimidos por NTFS se omiten y quedan en el log.
- No repara el disco ni arregla fallas físicas.
reflota.exe --source \\.\PhysicalDrive5 --particiones
reflota.exe --source \\.\PhysicalDrive5 --offset 608174080 --ls /Programacion --borrados
reflota.exe --source \\.\PhysicalDrive5 --offset 608174080 --copiar /Programacion --dest D:\Recuperado
Con Python 3.8+ (Windows):
python reflota.py              # correr directo, sin instalar nada
.\build_exe.ps1                # genera dist\Reflota.exe
El .exe se publica sin firma. Para firmarlo hace falta un certificado de firma de código (se compra, o se pide gratis para proyectos de código abierto a servicios como SignPath Foundation). Con el certificado en un .pfx se firma así (requiere signtool del Windows SDK):
.\firmar_exe.ps1 -Pfx C:\ruta\certificado.pfx
El script incluido está sin probar (no se pudo verificar sin un certificado real). Aun firmado, SmartScreen puede avisar un tiempo hasta que el programa gane reputación.
MIT: usalo, copialo y modificalo libremente. Ver LICENSE.
Reflota is a free, open-source, read-only file recovery tool for NTFS, FAT32, FAT16/FAT12 and exFAT. On NTFS it reads the whole $MFT and rebuilds the folder tree from each file's parent reference; on FAT32/exFAT it walks the directories and cluster chains. It also lists deleted files in their original folders and tells you how much of each is still intact. One tree shows disks → partitions → folders: tick a disk to copy everything, or tick only what you want; the size is computed automatically. It has a name search (wildcards, accent-insensitive, includes deleted files). It can also carve files by content (30+ formats including camera RAW, PSD and RAR, with exact lengths read from their structure, plus an optional, slower reassembly of fragmented baseline JPEGs), find lost partitions even with destroyed boot sectors, and make a safe, phased, resumable image (ddrescue-style: easy areas first, then trim, scrape, retry) of a failing disk. Recent additions were verified on synthetic test images, not yet on real hardware. Never writes to the source disk. Windows requires administrator rights for raw disk access; Reflota starts without them and asks only when you open a disk. MIT licensed.

FAA Expands AI Advice to Manage Air Traffic Congestion Across Entire US Airspace
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

FAA Expands AI Advice to Manage Air Traffic Congestion Across Entire US Airspace 41
			
		 	
				The FAA is expanding its $875 million AI-powered SMART system to all 22 U.S. air traffic control centers across the country. Since being implemented over Washington, DC, on September 21, the system has helped reroute 180 flights, saving an average of 10 minutes and nearly 80 gallons of fuel per flight. Ars Technica reports:  The FAA fact sheet describes SMART as a cloud-based system that combines information about "weather patterns, flight paths, traffic flow and controller staffing metrics" to predict air traffic patterns. It is designed to make scheduling recommendations, identify optimal flight routes, and determine the best order of arrivals for aircraft. However, a human team of FAA specialists based in DC is ultimately responsible for validating the SMART system recommendations before passing them along to air traffic control facilities across the country, according to the agency. Furthermore, the AI-powered SMART system "cannot be used to replace the critical role air traffic controllers play or take over control of an aircraft," the FAA clarified in the September 21 announcement about the initial deployment. Local air traffic controllers can decide whether or not to accept the SMART system recommendations.
[...] Following the initial deployment in airspace above DC, the FAA expanded the SMART system's coverage to air route traffic control centers in Atlanta along with Jacksonville and Miami in Florida, according to The Dallas Morning News. The FAA also shared an example with The Dallas Morning News about how SMART recommended a shorter and faster route for a cargo flight arriving at Dallas-Fort Worth International Airport, although the newspaper noted that the agency did not identify the flight involved and has not provided information about how SMART may have assisted passenger flight traffic.

Is there a 'right amount' of AI?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
09:27:05 UTC
18:27:05 KST

Gonna go have a panic attack now.
Is there a ‘right amount’ of AI?
While I’ve made my skepticism of AI clear by now, I’m not an anti-AI purist fighting against “no apps with any hint of AI coding allowed on my machine.” For one thing, it’s almost certainly a lost battle. Microsoft and Apple are both using coding agents for OS development, and while I can’t prove that most people running Linux have at least some LLM-touched code somewhere on their system, I’d be incredibly surprised if it’s not there.
Just this week, I moved back to Overcast from Castro as my podcast player. (I’ve spent a decade or so switching between them. Castro’s queue system is terrific, but the app has always been buggier than Overcast, its subscription price skyrocketed a year or so ago, and it’s now on its third owner—a shady-looking company that’s already diluted the privacy policy.) I know that developer Marco Arment has become an enthusiastic “agentic coder”, and that this means Overcast has Claude-generated code in it. Ditto his fun new app Fineliner, which I’m using on my new iPad mini (more about which later). I don’t think either of these programs are going to erase my data, make my iPad overheat, or send my credit card information to Russian hackers. In theory, what Lex Friedman wrote recently on the subject of vibe-coding, well, vibes with me:
AI has dramatically reduced the cost of producing an app, but it hasn’t reduced the work required to produce a great app. There’s a difference between generating something that works and something that works reliably, handles unexpected situations, feels intuitive, respects platform conventions, and continues improving. […] I will gladly pay for apps that feel crafted. I want apps that show refinement, testing, and thoughtfulness. I want apps from developers I trust will keep on building and iterating. I want apps from developers who feel invested—those are the ones I’m willing, even eager, to invest in.
(Note that’s Lex Friedman, who is cool, not Lex Fridman, who sucks.) And, yes, I think Marco is a developer who feels invested. He shows deep concern for code quality, to the point where he rather (in)famously eschewed using existing libraries for functions he could code himself. He wanted to understand his code inside and out.
So. Let’s talk about Big Walk.
If you haven’t heard of it, Big Walk is a game from House House, the Untitled Goose Game people, published by technically-still-Mac-developers Panic. It was a favorite for this year’s Independent Games Festival awards, but a group of IGF jurists issued a statement declaring they would “decline to vote for any game that uses generative AI in any capacity.” They specifically refused to make a distinction between using genAI for assets (e.g., graphics and sound) and code, writing,
Programming is a creative discipline whose labour deserves the same respect we extend to any part of making a game. This extends to the past work used to train these models without the creators’ consent.
This led House House to confess that, okay, they “sparingly” used LLMs to “help work through difficult technical challenges and bugs”. Less than a percent of a percent of the code in Big Walk was touched in any way by LLMs, and they went out of their way to say “this is not an endorsement of, or argument for, the use of AI tools, however minimal,” and that they “support the work of those who are trying to limit the harm generative AI is doing to videogames and our world, and apologise that Big Walk’s LLM usage may not meet their standards.”
Based on everything above, I shouldn’t have any issue with this. And at first, I didn’t. It all struck me as a tempest in a (Utah) teapot.
But then I read this.
House House, an already very successful studio with not insignificant resources, made a real choice to rely on a morally abhorrent software system rather than engage the help of real people—whether in the form of paid contracting or just friendly help. I want to say this not to try to exaggerate the scope of what they did but to make it absolutely clear that there is a real, material dimension to this, even if some want to characterize this issue as a purely abstract test of ritual purity.
You may balk at “morally abhorrent software system,” but Bruno Dias (the blog post author) is not wrong when he writes “to use LLMs is to willingly participate in a noxious political project and to use the product of exploitative and morally unacceptable processes.” I wrote about that in “AI’s Chicken Sandwich Problem”; in short form, LLMs do not exist in a vacuum. As he continues,
I’m not going to try to argue that any and all LLM usage is equally bad, but either an ethical boundary exists or it doesn’t. There are some ethical faults we generally expect people to not be 100% compliant with, of course, but there are some ethical faults we think it’s reasonable to expect people to never breach without sanction. I think using a completely optional set of highly subsidized SaaS products that did not exist two years ago is in the latter set.
If you disagree, perhaps stop to think about how heavily you’ve been propagandized and how much you’ve mentally surrendered to the normalization of these systems. It is very possible to do things without using an LLM, even things that are hard; and if anything, one of the harms of the “AI” political project is the propaganda machine dedicated to making you feel stupid and helpless.
And, you know, this is something that concerns me.
I recently wrote, “I think I’d lose something meaningful, something vital, if I couldn’t understand the code [that I’ve written]—to be able to read it line by line and know what each API call does, why certain choices are better than others, how it all comes together.” While I’m not suggesting that everyone who goes whole hog for agentic coding is doomed to lose the ability to understand their own code, there’s increasing evidence that “de-skilling” is a real issue, as well as evidence that the more leeway you give coding agents the worse your codebase is likely to get. Anecdotal tales of discovering Claude has written four versions of the same function in different places, finding giant sections of code that never get called, and “bug-finding” agents that imagine and even create bugs are growing rampant. LLMs and agents appear to “know” your codebase inside and out, but they don’t actually understand anything other than statistical relationships between words and phrases in specific contexts. The LLMs themselves don’t even “know” they’re working with code.
When I listen to Accidental Tech Podcast and hear Arment getting so enthusiastic about coding agents, I admit I have trouble reconciling “I don’t like using libraries I haven’t written” with “I love having robots write code for me”. Sure, it’s absolutely possible for a human to check every bit of LLM-generated code; I have little doubt that the code in Big Walk is code House House fully understands. A lot of LLM-contributed code I’ve seen is short, clear fixes and improvements, absurd to point to as poisonous, incomprehensible slop. Reining in your agents flips the complaint in the last paragraph around: the less LLM-generated code you use, the easier that code is to vet.
The larger point here, though, isn’t about Big Walk, or Overcast, or how many long-standing minor bugs in macOS Claude fixed (or how many brand new minor bugs in macOS Claude may have introduced). It’s about treating AI-assisted coding as something that must be used now that it’s here, whether we see it as an unalloyed good or as a tool whose benefits outweigh its drawbacks, or even as an unavoidable evil. It is, as Dias writes, very possible to do things without using an LLM, even things that are hard.
One could use LLMs for coding in a limited, intentional way, and the language models we have now are mostly trained on open-source, publicly-available code, making them (arguably) less objectionable than generating images or music or annoying emails pitching vibe-coded Markdown viewers. But mostly does heavy lifting in that sentence—and even if the code is open source, that doesn’t mean it’s legal to train on. If Claude inadvertently incorporates a meaningful chunk of GPL-licensed code in your non-GPL software, you have a problem. I suspect this is a problem that already exists in thousands of codebases.
We can envision a future that solves the big problems with LLM-assisted coding. The models run locally, not in data centers that convert electricity and water into money funneled to Elon Musk and Sam Altman; the training corpus only contains code whose license allows training. I suspect any open source license that allows commercial reuse falls into this category, unless one adds an explicit “no AI” clause. That future could be right around the corner. But we have to deal with the LLMs we have, not the LLMs we wish for.
So…where does that leave me? Back where I started, I suppose. It’s practical for me to swear off LLMs for my work, but not practical to avoid it in every piece of software that I use. But I think it’s something that indie developers in particular should be more wary of than what I’m seeing in the community. As I was finishing this article up this morning, this post came across my Mastodon timeline, from the head of the venerable indie design/coding outfit Iconfactory:
And, you know, yeah, I get that. I can no longer make a living doing technical writing because of AI, and AI is bad at technical writing in precisely the same way it’s bad at everything: good enough to slide by if you don’t really care. It’s more than a little concerning that otherwise perceptive people don’t understand that the same issues that plague those AI-generated posters at their local brewpub apply to everything AI-generated, and everything includes code.
I’m not saying you can’t use generative AI in the creation of terrific apps; IGF award arguments aside, Big Walk seems to qualify. But LLMs, by definition, can’t make anything thoughtfully, because LLMs have no thoughts. Everything Lex wants—apps that feel crafted, that show refinement, that feel intuitive—requires a human developer not only with taste, but with understanding. And the more you delegate to your robot coding assistant, the less you understand. Again, the less LLM-generated code you use, the easier that code is to vet. And the least amount of LLM-generated code you can use is always zero.
To support my writing, throw me a tip on Ko-fi.com!

The Dawn of the Data Intelligence Engineer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
09:08:18 UTC
18:08:18 KST

I asked Nick Ursa, our lead data engineer (and MotherDuck co-founder), how AI had changed his job. His answer: "This is the first time in my career with no backlog."
You'd think that would leave him with plenty of time on his hands, but he's busier than ever. While the backlog is gone, the answers still have to be right, and a human still has to decide how much quality control to do. Whereas once upon a time his job was to make sure the numbers he was computing were right, now his job is to make sure that the numbers that an LLM or agent comes up with are right.
That's a different job from working a ticket queue. At Data Outpost on November 4, I proposed a name for this new role: the Data Intelligence Engineer. I think it's now the emergent role on an AI-enabled data team, and I’d like to share why.
What a data team is for?
A data team has one job: enable the business to make good decisions. The faster they can make those decisions, based on a more accurate picture of what is happening, the better those decisions can be.
The hard part has been the route a question takes to become an answer. It moves like a relay. A marketing leader comes into work on Monday and notices that there was a spike in churn over the last week. Why did that happen? They file a ticket with the data team to investigate. An analyst picks up the ticket, and as they dig in, they realize some key data is not exported from one of the data sources. They file a ticket to ask a data engineer to look into it. That needs a change in one of the data pipelines, so the data engineer makes the change, tests it, runs the backfill, tests the backfill, and resolves the ticket. Then the ball is back in the court of the analyst, who can build the dashboard that was needed. By the time it ships, the marketing person can no longer remember why they asked in the first place. The churn rate recovered a couple of weeks later on its own.
This isn’t calling anyone out as not operating effectively or urgently; nobody in that relay did anything wrong. But every handoff means waiting, context is lost, and the half-life of the ability to effectively act on the answer to the question slowly decays.
As an aside, we should note that there was a whole generation of AI enabled tools that didn’t really help; the co-pilot era made some of the turnarounds faster, but the problem was that there were too many handoffs to be able to act quickly enough.
Something is different this time
Before December 2025, the case against AI for analytics was easy to make. LLMs wrote bad SQL. Business logic was unknowable. The sensible conclusion was that this was never going to work. In fact, I used to talk about how data teams were more or less exempt from the disruption that was happening in other areas of software.
Then, all of a sudden, the ground moved. New models got better at SQL. Business logic turned out to be something an agent can figure out with some helpful context. And agents went multi-turn, which means they don't have to get it right the first time. They can try a query, look at what came back, and try again, just like a human analyst would do. Put those together and it … just … works.
Self-service BI has been right around the corner for decades, what makes this different? Well, as a bit of an existence proof, here’s an example; the presentation I gave at Data Outpost was not created as a powerpoint deck or Google Slides, it was an app that I built using MotherDuck and Claude. I used live datasets from four different data sources, built a number of interactive and animated visualizations. Anything I did could be done by virtually anyone; I didn’t look at a single line of code or SQL.
It is important to stress that this isn’t just a way to automate building your dashboards. BI is typically pretty static, with maybe a couple of drill-downs. But with AI, you can build much richer visualizations. And what’s more, if you can build a dashboard with a prompt, you can go beyond that and also build a full-blown app.
Allowing business stakeholders to answer questions with data on their own has an even broader benefit than just shortening the data team’s backlog; it actually allows them to make better decisions. By cutting down the amount of time it takes and the number of handoffs involved, they can ask more questions. They can ask follow up questions while they still have all of the context in their heads. This means they can make confident decisions faster.
Which of these jobs can be a prompt?
AI is dramatically changing the job information workers perform. Software engineers often find their time being spent babysitting Claude sessions and rubber stamping agent-written code. What about the traditional roles of the data team? Which of their jobs, to put it a bit provocatively, can be a prompt?
As uncomfortable as the answer might be, rather a lot can be done via a prompt. In the not too distant future, writing SQL queries, creating dashboards, and building dbt pipelines are not going to be things that require specialized knowledge to do. Agents are likely better than humans at these tasks already, and they’re only going to get better. Our Data Engineer, Nick, moved us from one version of Airflow to another in a weekend, with minimal supervision, which once would have taken a week.
Writing code or SQL or building dashboards was never the whole job, though. The need for accurate data and the ability to understand it doesn’t go away. And someone has to be accountable, who can sign off and say “yes, this is right”. That takes a human in the loop. Quoth Nick, one more time, “Data eng, regardless of tooling or who does it, is an accountability function."
What still needs a human
Once agents are writing the code, what’s left in the data world? It turns out quite a lot. Humans need to make sure that other stakeholders without the word “Data” in their title are able to solve problems with data. That means they need to ensure a clean data model with appropriate context and annotations, a clear ontology to ensure that terms and concepts are being applied consistently throughout, and also they need to make sure that things continue to work.
These aren’t things that a model can intuit from data or history.  A model knows SQL. It doesn't know how your company counts revenue, or which of several similar-looking tables is the real one (it is revenue_cpx_new_revised_v2_use_this_one ) . A model doesn’t know that when the sales team talks about revenue they mean commissionable revenue, and the finance team uses something different.
Context is a crutch that is difficult to maintain. What’s even better is just building a clean data model in the first place. There is a good analogy with software; I tend to consider it a user experience failure any time your user has to read the manual; a cleaner design would have made whatever they were trying to do obvious. Context is just a manual for agents; if you can make the data model simple and clear enough, it can save them a lot of time and energy (and tokens).
Meet the Data Intelligence Engineer
The jobs to be done for a data person in the age of AI don’t really match any of the typical responsibilities of other data team members. I think it is different enough to merit a new title, which I hereby dub the “Data Intelligence Engineer”
The Data Intelligence Engineer automates the data engineer's work (ingestion) and the analytics engineer's work (transformation and modeling) by creating and conducting software agents. They encode their knowledge of the system into shared context that can be used by other agents to answer questions about the organization. These include things like which fields are joinable but also what someone in the company means when they say “ARR”.
Evals are another big part of their responsibility; these are the system tests that validate that when you ask questions with a known answer, the answer is correct. A good eval suite can give confidence that the types of questions that users ask of the system are going to be answered correctly by the agent. Evals also are a way of knowing when something breaks.
The goal of a Data Intelligence Engineer is to enable self-service. That is, anyone in the organization should be able to open Claude or ChatGPT or another agent and answer questions correctly about the business, use that to build apps and visualizations, and in the end, make high quality decisions with the benefit of data. . When the Data Intelligence Engineer does the job well, everyone who asks a question gets the experience of working with the smartest analyst in the company.
Day to day, that means they are going to be building frameworks, building the data model, curating the context, preventing things from getting broken, fixing them when they break anyway, running evals, and being accountable for the surfaces the business depends on. We wrote the whole thing up as a job description, because we believe this is the way things are going and it's time to give it a name.
If you're a data engineer, an analytics engineer, or an analyst, you'll recognize parts of this. Those roles were already blending into each other, and morphing into something new. This is a role you play, whatever your title says.
Won't this be chaos?
It's a fair question. More people building means more chances to be wrong, with less oversight. There is the possibility of making stupendously silly mistakes by acting on the wrong data. I’ll leave it up to the reader whether they think that stupendously silly mistakes are something new or not. The scary thing is that now the data team can be held responsible for them.
There are two things that help. The first is that the closer you are to the problem, the better you can spot errors. The person who lives with the churn number notices when it looks off. From experience, we’ve found that results actually improve because the business owner has a fingertip feel on their area; they know when something isn’t right, and can often spot problems even in the source data.
The other thing that helps is that you don’t need to watch everything with the same level of vigilance. Another quotable quote from our friend Nick is that “Agents need supervision proportional to their blast radius." That is, if something is going to be used for important reporting or decisions, you should pay a lot of attention. If you’re trying to figure out how many sandwiches to order for lunch, it probably isn’t going to cause a riot if you order too many with habanero mustard because Claude imagines it is extra tasty.
You can think of this in three circles of supervision. The outer is the Wild West. Anyone can build, they hopefully build on a reasonable foundation, but the data team keeps their hands off. The next tier is the Curated set. This is a set of dashboards, visualizations, apps, and metrics that is important to the business in some form or another. When something breaks in these dashboards, the data team should make a best effort to fix them. Finally there is the inner circle, the Core. This contains key metrics for the organization, things that are important to get right. The data team should make sure that these are high quality and predictable at all times. The board number lives in the Core.
What does this all have to do with MotherDuck?
On MotherDuck, we build mechanisms to empower your AI enabled data team. Flights are vibe-codable pipelines that can pull data into your warehouse and translate it into a sane model. Context lives in Guides, which are markdown files describing your data model and your business. A Guide starts out as private, but an admin can promote it to apply to the whole organization so that every person and agent works from the same definitions. We call the data visualizations that can be created by agents “Dives”. Every person and agent gets its own Duckling, a dedicated DuckDB instance. All of these are reachable from SQL, so one person or agent can work a problem across ingestion, transformation, context, and apps without a handoff between tools.
The only way out is through
We're in a chaotic moment. Chaos breeds the biggest opportunities, and the tools hand you some real super-powers if you're willing to pick them up. We're all learning how to do this together.
The data team isn't going anywhere. Someone still has to own the data model, curate the context, keep things working, and answer for the results. No-backlog-Nick has more to do than ever, and but the job is changing and not going back.
So who's doing this at your company, and what do you call them?

The Netherlands Is Dismantling the International Criminal Court
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
07:55:49 UTC
16:55:49 KST

The Netherlands is negotiating with the US over the erosion of the powers of the International Criminal Court (ICC). This erosion is so drastic that, according to legal experts, it would amount to the dismantling of the Court. The Rights Forum has learnt this from diplomatic sources in New York, speaking on condition of anonymity.
The restrictions that would be under discussion include: a) granting immunity to heads of state; b) excluding the Court’s jurisdiction over nationals of non-state parties, even when they commit crimes on the territory of a state party; and c) denying the Court’s jurisdiction over cases covered by national legislation.
First and second restrictions
The first restriction would mean that heads of state and heads of government such as Netanyahu, Trump and Putin could not be prosecuted for crimes committed on their orders.
The second restriction would mean that the ICC could no longer prosecute nationals of non-state parties, even if they commit international crimes on the territory of a state that is a party to the Rome Statute – the founding treaty of the ICC.
Israel, for example, is not a party to the Rome Statute, whereas Palestine is. Consequently, under the current rules, the ICC can prosecute Israeli citizens and government officials for crimes they commit on Palestinian territory. On this basis, the Court has issued arrest warrants for Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and former Defence Minister Yoav Gallant.
However, if the second restriction were to be adopted, Israeli nationals could no longer be prosecuted for crimes committed on Palestinian territory. The same would apply to US or Russian nationals who commit crimes on the territory of other States Parties.
This would undermine a key principle of the International Criminal Court: that nationals of states which have not ratified the Rome Statute can also be held accountable for international crimes.
Third restriction
The third restriction would mean that the ICC would no longer be permitted to hear cases if a state has legislation enabling it, in principle, to punish offences committed within its own jurisdiction. At present, the Court may still intervene if a state is unwilling or unable to investigate and prosecute suspects (the so-called principle of complementarity). If the third restriction were introduced, the mere existence of national legislation could be sufficient to prevent prosecution by the ICC.
For example: Israel has legislation against torture. It could therefore argue that the ICC has no jurisdiction, even if, in practice, cases of torture are not investigated or prosecuted. In this way, Israel could avert prosecution by the ICC without taking action against the perpetrators itself.
In short: if the aforementioned restrictions are adopted, there will be nothing left of the Court.
In order to enshrine these legal restrictions, the Rome Statute – which established the International Criminal Court in 1998 and under which it has operated since 2002 – would need to be amended. This would require a two-thirds majority of the States Parties. The amendment must then be ratified by seven-eighths of the States Parties before it can enter into force. It is not known which countries would cooperate in this regard.
US sanctions
The reason the Netherlands is said to be negotiating on this matter are the sanctions imposed by the US against thirteen judges and prosecutors, and against the ICC as a whole. This was done primarily in retaliation for the Court’s investigation into Israeli crimes in Palestine, and the resulting arrest warrants for Netanyahu and Gallant. The undermining of the International Criminal Court by the US and Israel has now been going on for almost twelve years.
The Court has been on the US sanctions list since 9 October. However, a number of sanctions will not come into force until 7 April 2027. These would largely paralyse the institution, leaving the European Union with virtually no choice but to activate the far-reaching blocking mechanism. This law obliges European parties to disregard US sanctions and to continue supplying services and goods to the Court and its staff.
The EU, and EU countries like the Netherlands, are, however, reluctant to activate the mechanism due to the consequences it could have for European businesses. For instance, European banks that disregard the US sanctions might find it much more difficult to do business in the US – or could face sanctions themselves.
Opting for a compromise
That is why the Netherlands now appears to be opting for a compromise. By relinquishing certain aspects of the International Criminal Court, the US might be persuaded to lift or ease the sanctions against the Court. In practice, this would amount to amputating the Court’s limbs. What would then remain is a powerless trunk-court.
We are not quite there yet. The States Parties to the Rome Statute will meet in The Hague from 30 November to 7 December. The meeting was originally scheduled to take place in New York, but the US sanctions made it necessary to relocate it.
      
      Response from the ministry of Foreign Affairs    
    
    
      The Rights Forum has asked the Ministry of Foreign Affairs for a response to the reports, but it provides a general, meaningless statement and does not address the specific questions asked:
‘At the previous Meeting of States Parties (in December 2025), it was decided by consensus to encourage States Parties to engage in further dialogue with non-States Parties. In the run-up to the forthcoming Meeting of States Parties to the Rome Statute (30 November – 7 December), States Parties are continuing discussions on this matter and we are exploring the scope for dialogue with non-States Parties, within the framework of international law and with due regard for the independence and impartiality of the Court.
‘We cannot yet anticipate how such a dialogue will unfold, as this is not solely up to us: the Netherlands, together with the other 124 States Parties and our European partners, bears a shared responsibility to protect the Court. No concrete proposals are currently before the Assembly of States Parties.
‘In any case, we remain firmly committed to the Court and will continue to do our utmost to ensure it functions effectively: both as the host country and as a State Party. Independent courts and tribunals must be able to carry out their mandates unhindered. In these capacities, we also remain fully committed – together with other States Parties – to mitigating the impact of existing sanctions. It is important that the Court is able to function as freely as possible.’
 
          
        Lees verder
        Lees minder

Opting for a compromise
That is why the Netherlands now appears to be opting for a compromise. By relinquishing certain aspects of the International Criminal Court, the US might be persuaded to lift or ease the sanctions against the Court. In practice, this would amount to amputating the Court’s limbs. What would then remain is a powerless trunk-court.
We are not quite there yet. The States Parties to the Rome Statute will meet in The Hague from 30 November to 7 December. The meeting was originally scheduled to take place in New York, but the US sanctions made it necessary to relocate it.
      
      Response from the ministry of Foreign Affairs    
    
    
      The Rights Forum has asked the Ministry of Foreign Affairs for a response to the reports, but it provides a general, meaningless statement and does not address the specific questions asked:
‘At the previous Meeting of States Parties (in December 2025), it was decided by consensus to encourage States Parties to engage in further dialogue with non-States Parties. In the run-up to the forthcoming Meeting of States Parties to the Rome Statute (30 November – 7 December), States Parties are continuing discussions on this matter and we are exploring the scope for dialogue with non-States Parties, within the framework of international law and with due regard for the independence and impartiality of the Court.
‘We cannot yet anticipate how such a dialogue will unfold, as this is not solely up to us: the Netherlands, together with the other 124 States Parties and our European partners, bears a shared responsibility to protect the Court. No concrete proposals are currently before the Assembly of States Parties.
‘In any case, we remain firmly committed to the Court and will continue to do our utmost to ensure it functions effectively: both as the host country and as a State Party. Independent courts and tribunals must be able to carry out their mandates unhindered. In these capacities, we also remain fully committed – together with other States Parties – to mitigating the impact of existing sanctions. It is important that the Court is able to function as freely as possible.’
 
          
        Lees verder
        Lees minder
