Euclid Mission Data Release Spans More Than 900 Million Galaxies
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

Euclid Mission Data Release Spans More Than 900 Million Galaxies (reuters.com) 23
			
		 	
				The European Space Agency has released the largest dataset yet from its Euclid space telescope, containing 1.7 petabytes of observations covering more than 900 million galaxies. "The data unveiled on Thursday covered a survey of the sky equal to about 9,500 times the size of the moon as viewed from Earth," reports Reuters. "The mission's goal is to complete the "largest-ever three-dimensional atlas of the cosmos, spanning more than a third of the sky, in 2031." From the report:  "It marks a major milestone for ESA's Euclid mission and for everyone involved," said astrophysicist Valeria Pettorino, ESA's Euclid project scientist. Euclid, launched from Florida in 2023, is gathering data on how the universe has expanded and how its structure has formed over time while amassing information about the nature of dark energy and dark matter and the role of gravity on large scales.
"Euclid will not just take images of galaxies in the sky, in two dimensions, but will also measure their distance, the third dimension. This will tell us where galaxies are in space and time, across 10 billion years, and in all directions beyond our galactic plane," Pettorino said. "Galaxies cluster along filaments, surrounded by empty space, and form a network that we call the cosmic web. Euclid will reconstruct the cosmic web and how it has grown over time. This is important because the exact configuration of the cosmic web, how many clusters or voids are there, how fast it changed, depend on the properties of dark energy and dark matter," Pettorino said.
[...] Previous Euclid data releases covered only a few days of observations. The newly released dataset includes more than a year of data and still spans only 14% of the total sky area Euclid is due to survey in visible and near-infrared light over six years. This data release will be completed next year with the first results on dark energy and dark matter.

Global Microsoft Outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
06:18:23 UTC
15:18:23 KST

Where things stand
• Coming back online: Microsoft said it has addressed the underlying issue of a massive outage that affected millions around the world as it continues to restore service. The software giant apologized for disruptions, saying the outage was caused by a technical issue and not a cybersecurity breach.
• Economic ramifications: The cost of lost productivity from the outage could approach at least $7 billion, according to a think-tank estimate.
• What’s been impacted: The outage, which began around noon ET on Thursday, left workers unable to access Outlook email, Teams meeting tools and other staples of modern office life. AI companies said they were facing issues as well. Banks, governments, health services, railways and courts all reported disruptions across Microsoft products.
• Global disruption: Outage reports “reached 74 countries and territories in about an hour,” according to Downdetector. While some workers scrambled to find workarounds, others expressed relief in a respite from the work grind.
Updates
(28)
US city governments say services are returning after outage
US cities are beginning to report Microsoft services returning after a widespread outage that began yesterday hit governments across the country and world.
Jacksonville, Florida, said that it was “seeing some services restored this morning and remain cautiously optimistic they will continue making progress.”
Austin and Dallas also reported progress on their email services being restored. The sheriff’s office in Arapahoe County, Colorado, which had previously reported the outage affecting online bond payments, said the issue was resolved.
Microsoft said this morning that the “underlying issue” that caused the massive outage had been addressed and that it was seeing recovery across its services.
The police department of Greensboro, North Carolina, also saw its services being restored. “While it’s not the outcome we wanted, we would like to let you know that our email is working again,” the department jested.
Microsoft services returning to normal after hours of disruption. Catch up on the latest
After hours of disruption affecting millions of users worldwide, Outlook and other Microsoft services are finally returning to normal.
Microsoft has apologized for the outage, which could end up costing billions in lost productivity.
The company reported “encouraging recovery progress” saying that queues of emails waiting to be delivered are “significantly reduced and approaching normal levels.”
Here’s what to know:
What was disrupted? Any of the applications involving email or Microsoft’s Exchange Online Services were affected, the company said. That left users likely unable to access or send new emails, use Microsoft’s AI tool Copilot, or join pre-scheduled Teams meetings already in the calendar. Other services — like those that share documents, pool a company’s data or protect emails — might also have been affected.
What’s the impact? The outage left millions without the work tools they use every day and could cost $7 billion in lost productivity, according to an estimate by a Michigan think tank. Patrick Anderson, Anderson Economic Group’s president, said that perhaps as many as 26 million users on Thursday and 51 million users on Friday have actually seen their operations disrupted in a way that caused an economic loss. And he said the cost will likely increase beyond this initial estimate.
City governments across the US reported disruption, encouraging residents to call local offices if necessary. Some Brits experienced delays in getting their drug prescriptions and several UK police forces told the public to report crimes or issues by telephone rather than through their websites.
Meanwhile, bank workers in Hong Kong had their work disrupted, government employees in Japan resorted to creating physical copies of documents and courts in the Philippines were hindered by the outage. Some railway networks experienced disruption to their customer email services and routing schedules while Australian schools, courts and government services reported disruption.
What caused this? Microsoft said the outage was caused by a “technical issue” rather than any cybersecurity breach. “The incident was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced following a recent network change,” the company said.
CNN’s Gage Goulding, Anna Cooban, August Phillips, Hanna Ziady, Karina Tsui, Helen Regan, Kathleen Magramo and Chris Isidore contributed reporting
Microsoft apologizes, says underlying cause of outage has been addressed
Microsoft has addressed the “underlying issue” that caused a massive outage affecting millions across the world, the tech company said, adding that it was “seeing broad recovery and stability across our services.”
Microsoft offered some explanation of what caused the outage that began yesterday, confirming it was a “technical issue” and not a cybersecurity or AI-related concern.
“The incident was introduced by an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade that surfaced following a recent network change,” Microsoft said.
The software giant said it would release a “full post-incident report” after a review.
Cost of Microsoft outage could top $7 billion, according to estimate
The cost of lost productivity from the Microsoft outage could approach $7 billion, according to an estimate by a Michigan think tank on Friday.
Anderson Economic Group, which is known for cautious estimates on the costs of economic disruptions, including labor strikes and tariffs, said that many of the reported 430 million Microsoft users who have been affected likely only are dealing with annoyances, rather than true lost productivity.
But Patrick Anderson, the firm’s president, said that perhaps as many as 26 million users on Thursday and 51 million users on Friday have actually seen their operations disrupted in a way that caused an economic loss. And he said the cost will likely increase beyond this initial estimate.
“This is a starting point, with all the limitations on what we know. We don’t know complete scale,” he told CNN on Friday.
Some companies might be able to be compensated by cybersecurity insurance policies for the costs associated with the outage – but that would only shift the cost from the Microsoft users to their insurers.
In addition, Anderson estimated that Microsoft could conceivably need to refund customers about $800 million a day in subscription fees for services they pay for under contracts but could not access. While not all customers will seek refunds, those who do so are likely to push the total cost of the outage above $7 billion, Anderson said.
The outage, which began around noon ET on Thursday, left workers unable to access Outlook email, Teams meeting tools and other staples of modern office life, as well as the company’s Copilot AI chatbot.
Emails are "approaching normal levels” after outage, Microsoft says
Outlook is returning to normal after hours of disruption, as queues of email waiting to be delivered are “significantly reduced and approaching normal levels,” Microsoft said.
The company is continuing to observe “improvements in service availability,” it said in a statement this morning. “Some users may still experience intermittent access issues or delayed email delivery as recovery continues,” it added.
Microsoft seeing "broader improvements" in North America
Microsoft just said it is seeing “encouraging recovery progress” at the same time as some social media users and CNN journalists report receiving emails in their inboxes again.
In a statement on X, the company reported “email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected Microsoft 365 infrastructure,” but warned some users may still “experience intermittent issues.”
While some social media users reported getting emails again, others said they were still unable to access them.
In an additional update issued later, Microsoft outlined the uneven pace of recovery in different regions. The company indicated that North America was recovering faster than Europe and Asia.
It reported “broader improvements in North America, sustained improvements across much of Europe, and encouraging recovery signals in Asia Pacific.”
UK police forces urge public to report crimes via telephone, not online
Several UK police forces told the public Friday to report crimes or issues by telephone rather than through their websites amid the Microsoft outage.
Police forces in Merseyside, Greater Manchester and Bedfordshire informed visitors to their websites that online submission forms were disrupted.
East Hertfordshire Police said in a statement posted to X that “some online form submissions on our website may be affected” because of the outage.
In another Friday post on X, Dorset Police said it was “unable to receive online form submissions.”
All police forces urged the public to call 101 if their issue is “time-critical but not an emergency.” (The UK emergency number is 999).
Microsoft says services are being "gradually restored"
Microsoft says it is making “additional progress toward recovery” and that traffic is “being gradually restored” as its engineers scramble to fix the worldwide outage that has left millions without the work tools they use every day.
In a statement, Microsoft said it is “seeing improvements in key scenarios, including mailbox access and delayed email processing, though some intermittent issues may continue.”
What to know as you're waking up in the US without email
Outlook is still down, as Microsoft’s rolling outage – now extending into a second day – continues to affect services for millions of people around the world.
The outage, which is also affecting Teams and Copilot, is disrupting government and business operations in the United States and elsewhere, with at least one company postponing a quarterly earnings call.
Microsoft executive Charles Lamanna said in the early hours of Friday that there is currently no indication that the outage is the result “of malicious or cyber activity.”
Here’s what to know:
Email down: Outlook remains down and users are also having trouble accessing their Exchange Online mailboxes.
In an update at around 4:15 a.m. ET Friday, Microsoft said it would extend its “email timeout window” to 48 hours “to help protect delayed messages while service recovery continues.”
The email timeout window is the maximum amount of time that a mail server or email client such as Outlook will spend waiting to receive an email before returning an error.
AI companies affected: OpenAI said it was facing issues with Microsoft connectors, with some tools, including ChatGPT, not working “as expected.” Meanwhile, thousands of Claude AI users reported issues Friday morning, according to Downdetector. Claude creator Anthropic said shortly after 6 a.m. ET that a fix had been deployed and it was “monitoring for recovery.”
US lawmakers: The US House of Representatives said the outage impacted its users and its engineers were working with Microsoft, according to Reuters. Some congressional staffers expressed frustration on X. “Shall we start conducting all official business via DM?” said Sharon Eliza Nichols, communications director for Congresswoman Eleanor Holmes Norton.
US cities: City governments across the United States have reported email disruptions and have encouraged residents to call local offices and agencies if necessary.
- Several police and sheriff departments said the outage was affecting their email and communication platforms, including Colorado’s Arapahoe Sheriff’s Office, which said the outage had delayed the processing of prisoners’ bail payments.
- The outages are affecting email services in Miami Beach, Austin, and Jacksonville, though officials assured residents that emergency services were still active.
- In California, officials from Pasadena, Fremont, Burbank, and Santa Barbara also issued statements warning of downed email use.
- Baltimore County officials said on X that government staff could not send or receive emails and urged residents to contact relevant departments by phone.
Earnings call delayed: Workhorse Group, a Detroit-based electric vehicle manufacturer, pushed its third quarter earnings call to Monday, citing “business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outages.”
CNN’s Gage Goulding contributed reporting.
What is affected by the outage?
Some office workers rely on Microsoft’s suite of applications for practically everything – emails, meetings, AI, creating spreadsheets, and writing and sharing documents. Any of those functions which involve email or Microsoft’s Exchange Online services are impacted, the company said.
Outlook: Email is the main thing impacted by the outage, leaving users likely unable to access or send any new emails.
Copilot: Users may also be unable to load chats with or receive responses from Microsoft’s AI tool. Copilot also cannot access the data it normally draws from, like emails, files and calendars.
Teams: Anything that has already been scheduled and requires Teams — Microsoft’s communications platform — to connect with a user’s calendar is likely impacted. But if users are simply just joining a call or exchanging messages, that is still working.
Word/Excel/Powerpoint: The basic functions of these apps are still working. However, other functions may be impacted, like if users are trying to print documents over the cloud or share documents with Sharepoint. Users may also not be able to view their most recently used files.
Other, more specialist Microsoft services – like those which pool a company’s data or allow IT to monitor a company’s devices – may also be affected.
It's a disappointing day for some workers using Gmail
While many workers using Microsoft’s systems have spent Friday rejoicing that they’re forced to take a break — unable to send or receive emails or conduct their usual Teams calls — those using Google services are lamenting in memes shared on social media.
Google systems including Gmail, Google Docs and Google Meet appear to be functioning just fine, or at least well enough to promise users an average working Friday.
One video on Instagram shows someone lying in bed with the word “Outlook” above their head. Another sits at a desk working on their laptop with the word “Gmail” above theirs.
Another video shows a meme of actor Pedro Pascal appearing to sob uncontrollably under text reading: “When you hear Outlook is down and start planning your afternoon wines… Then remember your workplace uses Google Workspace.”
Railways report communications delay due to Microsoft service disruption
Some railway networks are experiencing disruptions to their customer email services and routing schedules because of the Microsoft outage.
VIA Rail Canada said passengers “may not be able to receive email communications about their trip” due to technical issues with Microsoft services. The railway asked passengers to check their web-based train tracker or subscribe to SMS notifications for information on their journeys.
Germany’s Deutsche Bahn said Microsoft Outlook is experiencing “severe sluggishness,” causing significant delays in its abilities to send out and receive emails.
“As a result, the distribution of rerouting schedules is severely restricted. All relevant teams are involved in resolving the issue; current indications point to a server-side problem on Microsoft’s end,” it said in a statement.
In the UK, National Rail, which runs passenger rail services in England, Scotland and Wales, said the ongoing outage may be affecting customers’ ability to access train tickets by email.
It advised travelers to visit the website or app where tickets were purchased, or to contact the ticket retailer directly.
“If you are still unable to access your ticket, please contact a member of onboard staff or station staff for advice,” National Rail said on its website.
Rail Delivery Group, which manages National Rail, said there were no current disruptions to operations or train services as a result of the outage.
CNN’s Nina Avramova contributed to this report.
Microsoft extends email timeout window to 48 hours amid outage
Microsoft said that it would extend its email timeout window to 48 hours “to help protect delayed messages while service recovery continues,” according to an update posted at around 4:15 a.m. ET Friday.
The email timeout window is the maximum amount of time that a mail server or email client such as Outlook will spend waiting to receive an email before returning an error.
Microsoft said recovery efforts are ongoing and that it’s seeing “early signs of stabilization,” though service capacity has not returned to normal.
It's Friday and Microsoft is still down. (Some) workers are taking it easy
Memes and jokes abounded online as the global outage of Microsoft’s Outlook emails and Teams meeting platform crossed the 12-hour mark.
One joke that spread on Threads, X and LinkedIn was the rebrand of Microsoft365 to Microsoft364 – memorializing the one day of the year that the software company went dark.
Others reveled at not needing to respond to constant emails and notifications for at least one day.
Another account wrote: “Outlook is down. Your email does not find me well. It does not find me at all.”
Others on Reddit said they panicked and thought they were laid off when they could not access their work emails.
Here's where we're seeing the outage's impacts
At first, Microsoft said the connection issues were happening “in the Americas,” but over time it became clear the troubles were global.
Microsoft’s outage reports “reached 74 countries and territories in about an hour,” according to Downdetector, a website that tracks cyber outages.
Here’s where we’ve seen reports of outages:
- Australian schools, courts and government services started their Friday morning with Microsoft Outlook and other apps out of service.
- In Hong Kong, workers from banks including Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs told CNN their work was disrupted, as they were unable to access emails or Teams from the moment they signed on and all through their lunch break. “Sporadically some mails come in but there is probably millions of backlog to process,” one banker told CNN before heading off for lunch break.
- The disruption has also hit Japan’s government services, Deputy Chief Cabinet Secretary Masanao Ozaki said in a Friday presser. Government employees are responding to the outage by creating physical copies of documents, he added. Government ministries and agencies have been unable to send emails through most of the Friday workday.
- Courts in the Philippines are also being slowed down by the Microsoft disruption, the Supreme Court said. “During this period, responses to emails and other transactions may be delayed,” it added in a post on X.
- In the UK, a disruption to the National Health Service email system is expected to prevent some patients from getting their drug prescriptions.
CNN’s Brian Stelter, Hilary Whiteman and Junko Ogura contributed to this report.
Britons can expect delays in accessing prescribed medication today, says UK pharmacy group
A Microsoft outage causing disruptions to the email system of the UK’s National Health Service is expected to prevent some patients from getting their drug prescriptions Friday.
The outage “affects a pharmacy’s ability to communicate with GP practices and some patients,” a spokesperson for the National Pharmacy Association said in a Friday statement.
GPs are equivalent to family doctors in the US.
An NPA spokesperson told CNN in a separate phone call that, while many patients will get their medications, those with more complex prescriptions requiring a pharmacist to contact the prescribing doctor to check or query any details could experience delays.
The spokesperson added that Fridays are typically busy days at pharmacies.
“It’s just a massive pain. It will be slow today,” they said.
Microsoft reports "marginal success" in patching servers
More than 12 hours after Microsoft first warned of a global outage affecting some of its most popular products, the company said it had “observed marginal success” in fixing the problem.
“We’re continuing to monitor activity across regions to evaluate server status and determine actions that can be taken to improve capacity,” Microsoft said in a statement posted on its Service Health Status page early Friday.
“At this point, we have observed marginal success,” the company wrote.
Around 30 minutes later, Microsoft posted another statement saying the company has seen “positive results for service stability and increased capacity as a result of our latest traffic optimization actions.”
Reiterating what it said earlier Thursday, Microsoft stated it was not aware of any malicious activity causing the outage but will continue to treat the incident “with urgency and priority until full resolution.”
More than 12 hours into Microsoft's outage, here's what to know
The Microsoft global outage is ongoing after the US tech giant first reported problems with its email services on Thursday.
More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps and more than 90% of Fortune 500 companies use its Copilot AI chatbot, according to the company’s website.
Millions worldwide lost access to Outlook email, Teams meeting tools and other essentials of modern office life as the disruption quickly spread.
The company reported “marginal success” in repairing the outage in its latest update, early Friday ET. A Microsoft executive confirmed the outage was not the result of a security breach.
As Europe woke on Friday, the outage remained widespread.
Here’s the latest if you’re just tuning in:
- Return to sender: The outage hit in 74 countries and territories in around an hour, according to tracking website Downdetector. Several airlines reported disruptions to their email support.
- In the US: City governments countrywide reported widespread email disruptions and encouraged residents to call local offices and agencies if necessary. At least one American company postponed its quarterly earnings call.
- In Asia Pacific: Some of Japan’s government services switched to paper documents due to the outage and courts in the Philippines experienced delays. Australian schools, courts and government services were also affected. In Hong Kong, staff at Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs told CNN their work was disrupted through to their lunch break.
- Scams warning: Banks warned customers to beware of scams and phishing attempts as cybercriminals may use the outage to target individuals and businesses.
CNN’s Helen Regan, Hilary Whiteman, Karina Tsui, Kathleen Magramo and Ross Adkin contributed reporting.
Some airlines report comms disruptions due to Microsoft outage
Several airlines are reporting disruptions to their email support and communications due to the Microsoft outage, posting statements to customers on social media.
Indian budget carrier IndiGo said its “email support services are currently impacted, and we may not be able to receive your emails.” It directed customers to reach out via their website or mobile app.
Akasa Air, another low-cost Indian carrier, similarly said its “email support services are currently experiencing delays.”
And South African carrier CemAir said it was “experiencing some system disruptions due to the global Microsoft outage.”
None of the carriers reported flight disruptions.
Indian flag carrier Air India said it was “closely monitoring the ongoing Microsoft outage” and has rerouted emails. The airline said its flight operations “remain unaffected” and “there is currently no impact on any of our communication channels.”
“Consider this your cue to plan that getaway.”
Outage comes as Europe seeks to reduce reliance on US tech
The Microsoft outage comes at a time when Europe is pushing for greater tech sovereignty, aiming to run its own servers and reduce dependency on foreign providers.
Europe lacks the kind of mega tech companies that abound in the US, making it reliant on American firms for its digital services, but there is sentiment in Europe that it should become less reliant on the United States for its tech needs.
That sentiment has hardened during US President Donald Trump’s second term, when he has hit the continent with huge tariffs and threatened to take over the Danish territory of Greenland.
Earlier this year French Prime Minister Sebastien Lecornu said that government officials will ditch Zoom and other US-owned video conferencing tools and begin using French-owned software. The decision is intended partly to reduce “dependencies on non-European actors,” Lecornu said.
“The internet in Europe is essentially a US construct, a US system. And Europe doesn’t really have a competitor to any of that,” Neil Shearing, group chief economist at Capital Economics, told CNN earlier this year.
On Friday, that question resurfaced as Microsoft’s outage stretched beyond 12 hours, disrupting countries across the globe.
“That tech sovereignty conversation, while it’s been mostly political, I bet it’s real sharp today,” technology journalist and founder of The Rip Current podcast Jacob Ward told CNN.
CNN’s Anna Cooban contributed reporting.

East Hertfordshire Police said in a statement posted to X that “some online form submissions on our website may be affected” because of the outage.
In another Friday post on X, Dorset Police said it was “unable to receive online form submissions.”
All police forces urged the public to call 101 if their issue is “time-critical but not an emergency.” (The UK emergency number is 999).
Microsoft says services are being "gradually restored"
Microsoft says it is making “additional progress toward recovery” and that traffic is “being gradually restored” as its engineers scramble to fix the worldwide outage that has left millions without the work tools they use every day.
In a statement, Microsoft said it is “seeing improvements in key scenarios, including mailbox access and delayed email processing, though some intermittent issues may continue.”
What to know as you're waking up in the US without email
Outlook is still down, as Microsoft’s rolling outage – now extending into a second day – continues to affect services for millions of people around the world.
The outage, which is also affecting Teams and Copilot, is disrupting government and business operations in the United States and elsewhere, with at least one company postponing a quarterly earnings call.
Microsoft executive Charles Lamanna said in the early hours of Friday that there is currently no indication that the outage is the result “of malicious or cyber activity.”
Here’s what to know:
Email down: Outlook remains down and users are also having trouble accessing their Exchange Online mailboxes.
In an update at around 4:15 a.m. ET Friday, Microsoft said it would extend its “email timeout window” to 48 hours “to help protect delayed messages while service recovery continues.”
The email timeout window is the maximum amount of time that a mail server or email client such as Outlook will spend waiting to receive an email before returning an error.
AI companies affected: OpenAI said it was facing issues with Microsoft connectors, with some tools, including ChatGPT, not working “as expected.” Meanwhile, thousands of Claude AI users reported issues Friday morning, according to Downdetector. Claude creator Anthropic said shortly after 6 a.m. ET that a fix had been deployed and it was “monitoring for recovery.”
US lawmakers: The US House of Representatives said the outage impacted its users and its engineers were working with Microsoft, according to Reuters. Some congressional staffers expressed frustration on X. “Shall we start conducting all official business via DM?” said Sharon Eliza Nichols, communications director for Congresswoman Eleanor Holmes Norton.
US cities: City governments across the United States have reported email disruptions and have encouraged residents to call local offices and agencies if necessary.
- Several police and sheriff departments said the outage was affecting their email and communication platforms, including Colorado’s Arapahoe Sheriff’s Office, which said the outage had delayed the processing of prisoners’ bail payments.
- The outages are affecting email services in Miami Beach, Austin, and Jacksonville, though officials assured residents that emergency services were still active.
- In California, officials from Pasadena, Fremont, Burbank, and Santa Barbara also issued statements warning of downed email use.
- Baltimore County officials said on X that government staff could not send or receive emails and urged residents to contact relevant departments by phone.
Earnings call delayed: Workhorse Group, a Detroit-based electric vehicle manufacturer, pushed its third quarter earnings call to Monday, citing “business disruptions caused by the global Microsoft outages.”
CNN’s Gage Goulding contributed reporting.
What is affected by the outage?
Some office workers rely on Microsoft’s suite of applications for practically everything – emails, meetings, AI, creating spreadsheets, and writing and sharing documents. Any of those functions which involve email or Microsoft’s Exchange Online services are impacted, the company said.
Outlook: Email is the main thing impacted by the outage, leaving users likely unable to access or send any new emails.
Copilot: Users may also be unable to load chats with or receive responses from Microsoft’s AI tool. Copilot also cannot access the data it normally draws from, like emails, files and calendars.
Teams: Anything that has already been scheduled and requires Teams — Microsoft’s communications platform — to connect with a user’s calendar is likely impacted. But if users are simply just joining a call or exchanging messages, that is still working.
Word/Excel/Powerpoint: The basic functions of these apps are still working. However, other functions may be impacted, like if users are trying to print documents over the cloud or share documents with Sharepoint. Users may also not be able to view their most recently used files.
Other, more specialist Microsoft services – like those which pool a company’s data or allow IT to monitor a company’s devices – may also be affected.
It's a disappointing day for some workers using Gmail
While many workers using Microsoft’s systems have spent Friday rejoicing that they’re forced to take a break — unable to send or receive emails or conduct their usual Teams calls — those using Google services are lamenting in memes shared on social media.
Google systems including Gmail, Google Docs and Google Meet appear to be functioning just fine, or at least well enough to promise users an average working Friday.
One video on Instagram shows someone lying in bed with the word “Outlook” above their head. Another sits at a desk working on their laptop with the word “Gmail” above theirs.
Another video shows a meme of actor Pedro Pascal appearing to sob uncontrollably under text reading: “When you hear Outlook is down and start planning your afternoon wines… Then remember your workplace uses Google Workspace.”
Railways report communications delay due to Microsoft service disruption
Some railway networks are experiencing disruptions to their customer email services and routing schedules because of the Microsoft outage.
VIA Rail Canada said passengers “may not be able to receive email communications about their trip” due to technical issues with Microsoft services. The railway asked passengers to check their web-based train tracker or subscribe to SMS notifications for information on their journeys.
Germany’s Deutsche Bahn said Microsoft Outlook is experiencing “severe sluggishness,” causing significant delays in its abilities to send out and receive emails.
“As a result, the distribution of rerouting schedules is severely restricted. All relevant teams are involved in resolving the issue; current indications point to a server-side problem on Microsoft’s end,” it said in a statement.
In the UK, National Rail, which runs passenger rail services in England, Scotland and Wales, said the ongoing outage may be affecting customers’ ability to access train tickets by email.
It advised travelers to visit the website or app where tickets were purchased, or to contact the ticket retailer directly.
“If you are still unable to access your ticket, please contact a member of onboard staff or station staff for advice,” National Rail said on its website.
Rail Delivery Group, which manages National Rail, said there were no current disruptions to operations or train services as a result of the outage.
CNN’s Nina Avramova contributed to this report.
Microsoft extends email timeout window to 48 hours amid outage
Microsoft said that it would extend its email timeout window to 48 hours “to help protect delayed messages while service recovery continues,” according to an update posted at around 4:15 a.m. ET Friday.
The email timeout window is the maximum amount of time that a mail server or email client such as Outlook will spend waiting to receive an email before returning an error.
Microsoft said recovery efforts are ongoing and that it’s seeing “early signs of stabilization,” though service capacity has not returned to normal.
It's Friday and Microsoft is still down. (Some) workers are taking it easy
Memes and jokes abounded online as the global outage of Microsoft’s Outlook emails and Teams meeting platform crossed the 12-hour mark.
One joke that spread on Threads, X and LinkedIn was the rebrand of Microsoft365 to Microsoft364 – memorializing the one day of the year that the software company went dark.
Others reveled at not needing to respond to constant emails and notifications for at least one day.
Another account wrote: “Outlook is down. Your email does not find me well. It does not find me at all.”
Others on Reddit said they panicked and thought they were laid off when they could not access their work emails.
Here's where we're seeing the outage's impacts
At first, Microsoft said the connection issues were happening “in the Americas,” but over time it became clear the troubles were global.
Microsoft’s outage reports “reached 74 countries and territories in about an hour,” according to Downdetector, a website that tracks cyber outages.
Here’s where we’ve seen reports of outages:
- Australian schools, courts and government services started their Friday morning with Microsoft Outlook and other apps out of service.
- In Hong Kong, workers from banks including Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs told CNN their work was disrupted, as they were unable to access emails or Teams from the moment they signed on and all through their lunch break. “Sporadically some mails come in but there is probably millions of backlog to process,” one banker told CNN before heading off for lunch break.
- The disruption has also hit Japan’s government services, Deputy Chief Cabinet Secretary Masanao Ozaki said in a Friday presser. Government employees are responding to the outage by creating physical copies of documents, he added. Government ministries and agencies have been unable to send emails through most of the Friday workday.
- Courts in the Philippines are also being slowed down by the Microsoft disruption, the Supreme Court said. “During this period, responses to emails and other transactions may be delayed,” it added in a post on X.
- In the UK, a disruption to the National Health Service email system is expected to prevent some patients from getting their drug prescriptions.
CNN’s Brian Stelter, Hilary Whiteman and Junko Ogura contributed to this report.
Britons can expect delays in accessing prescribed medication today, says UK pharmacy group
A Microsoft outage causing disruptions to the email system of the UK’s National Health Service is expected to prevent some patients from getting their drug prescriptions Friday.
The outage “affects a pharmacy’s ability to communicate with GP practices and some patients,” a spokesperson for the National Pharmacy Association said in a Friday statement.
GPs are equivalent to family doctors in the US.
An NPA spokesperson told CNN in a separate phone call that, while many patients will get their medications, those with more complex prescriptions requiring a pharmacist to contact the prescribing doctor to check or query any details could experience delays.
The spokesperson added that Fridays are typically busy days at pharmacies.
“It’s just a massive pain. It will be slow today,” they said.
Microsoft reports "marginal success" in patching servers
More than 12 hours after Microsoft first warned of a global outage affecting some of its most popular products, the company said it had “observed marginal success” in fixing the problem.
“We’re continuing to monitor activity across regions to evaluate server status and determine actions that can be taken to improve capacity,” Microsoft said in a statement posted on its Service Health Status page early Friday.
“At this point, we have observed marginal success,” the company wrote.
Around 30 minutes later, Microsoft posted another statement saying the company has seen “positive results for service stability and increased capacity as a result of our latest traffic optimization actions.”
Reiterating what it said earlier Thursday, Microsoft stated it was not aware of any malicious activity causing the outage but will continue to treat the incident “with urgency and priority until full resolution.”
More than 12 hours into Microsoft's outage, here's what to know
The Microsoft global outage is ongoing after the US tech giant first reported problems with its email services on Thursday.
More than 430 million people use Microsoft 365 apps and more than 90% of Fortune 500 companies use its Copilot AI chatbot, according to the company’s website.
Millions worldwide lost access to Outlook email, Teams meeting tools and other essentials of modern office life as the disruption quickly spread.
The company reported “marginal success” in repairing the outage in its latest update, early Friday ET. A Microsoft executive confirmed the outage was not the result of a security breach.
As Europe woke on Friday, the outage remained widespread.
Here’s the latest if you’re just tuning in:
- Return to sender: The outage hit in 74 countries and territories in around an hour, according to tracking website Downdetector. Several airlines reported disruptions to their email support.
- In the US: City governments countrywide reported widespread email disruptions and encouraged residents to call local offices and agencies if necessary. At least one American company postponed its quarterly earnings call.
- In Asia Pacific: Some of Japan’s government services switched to paper documents due to the outage and courts in the Philippines experienced delays. Australian schools, courts and government services were also affected. In Hong Kong, staff at Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs told CNN their work was disrupted through to their lunch break.
- Scams warning: Banks warned customers to beware of scams and phishing attempts as cybercriminals may use the outage to target individuals and businesses.
CNN’s Helen Regan, Hilary Whiteman, Karina Tsui, Kathleen Magramo and Ross Adkin contributed reporting.
Some airlines report comms disruptions due to Microsoft outage
Several airlines are reporting disruptions to their email support and communications due to the Microsoft outage, posting statements to customers on social media.
Indian budget carrier IndiGo said its “email support services are currently impacted, and we may not be able to receive your emails.” It directed customers to reach out via their website or mobile app.
Akasa Air, another low-cost Indian carrier, similarly said its “email support services are currently experiencing delays.”
And South African carrier CemAir said it was “experiencing some system disruptions due to the global Microsoft outage.”
None of the carriers reported flight disruptions.
Indian flag carrier Air India said it was “closely monitoring the ongoing Microsoft outage” and has rerouted emails. The airline said its flight operations “remain unaffected” and “there is currently no impact on any of our communication channels.”
“Consider this your cue to plan that getaway.”
Outage comes as Europe seeks to reduce reliance on US tech
The Microsoft outage comes at a time when Europe is pushing for greater tech sovereignty, aiming to run its own servers and reduce dependency on foreign providers.
Europe lacks the kind of mega tech companies that abound in the US, making it reliant on American firms for its digital services, but there is sentiment in Europe that it should become less reliant on the United States for its tech needs.
That sentiment has hardened during US President Donald Trump’s second term, when he has hit the continent with huge tariffs and threatened to take over the Danish territory of Greenland.
Earlier this year French Prime Minister Sebastien Lecornu said that government officials will ditch Zoom and other US-owned video conferencing tools and begin using French-owned software. The decision is intended partly to reduce “dependencies on non-European actors,” Lecornu said.
“The internet in Europe is essentially a US construct, a US system. And Europe doesn’t really have a competitor to any of that,” Neil Shearing, group chief economist at Capital Economics, told CNN earlier this year.
On Friday, that question resurfaced as Microsoft’s outage stretched beyond 12 hours, disrupting countries across the globe.
“That tech sovereignty conversation, while it’s been mostly political, I bet it’s real sharp today,” technology journalist and founder of The Rip Current podcast Jacob Ward told CNN.
CNN’s Anna Cooban contributed reporting.

Any outage compensation likely to face hurdles, says UK lawyerpublished at 18:33 GMT
18:33 GMT
Tom Espiner Business reporter
Microsoft may face compensation claims over the Outlook
outage, but there would be lots of potential pitfalls for would-be claimants, a
lawyer says.
Whether an organisation or individual would stand a chance
of success in getting any compensation from Microsoft would depend on the
nature of the contract they have with the tech giant.
Customer terms, especially for free versions of Outlook, usually
specify that Microsoft is not liable for service interruptions, and courts
normally uphold these contracts if the terms are reasonable, says Riah Sattar,
a partner and head of dispute resolution at Thomas Mansfield Solicitors.
Businesses or individuals using paid-for versions of Outlook
are partly protected under the Consumer Rights Act in the UK, which says that paid-for
services must be delivered with reasonable care and skill.
However, the claimant would have to show that Microsoft was
liable for something it had control over, which is difficult to do.
The claimant would also have to show that the outage caused
them a loss.

How the Microsoft 365 outage unfolded - a timelinepublished at 17:16 GMT
17:16 GMT
Tinshui Yeung Live reporter
Image source, Downdetector
Image caption,
Website Downdetector noted hundreds of reports on Thursday evening and Friday morning
All timestamps are in GMT, according to posts published on X.
12 November 17:54: Microsoft first acknowledges reports that users in the Americas are unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes.
18:07: Microsoft warns that the disruption may affect Microsoft 365 connectivity globally, not just in the Americas.
19:31: The company says an investigation found that a number of services had become “unexpectedly unavailable”, straining the remaining infrastructure.
19:50: Engineers start focusing on an issue that caused servers to fail automated health checks and become unavailable.
23:04: Microsoft says it’s returning some affected servers to service, and they “remain stable”.
13 November 00:06: Microsoft says servers returned to service can become unresponsive again once they receive traffic, and it is analysing the data to find out why.
04:36: After a series of efforts, Microsoft says it is seeing “partial recovery” in some regions.
09:24: “We’re seeing early stabilization and capacity improvements as recovery actions continue,” the company says.
13:32: The company says there are “broader improvements” in North America, “sustained improvements” across much of Europe and “encouraging recovery signals” in the Asia Pacific region.
14:17: The company says it’s continuing to see improvements, with email queues “significantly reduced and approaching normal levels”.
15:39: Microsoft says it's "seeing encouraging recovery process", and confirms that "there is no indication this is the result of malicious or cyber activity".

Chances of this being some kind of cyber-attack are low - if we judge historypublished at 16:13 GMT
16:13 GMT
Joe Tidy Cyber correspondent
Image source, Getty Images
The exact words Microsoft is using are: "As of now, we
are not aware of any malicious activity that is causing this incident.”
This assessment might change of course.
Right now, the priority is not to find out the root cause,
but to get millions of people back online. So, any investigation into what
triggered this enormous outage can wait.
But the chances of this being some kind of cyber-attack are
low - if we judge history.
None of the mega outages of recent years have been down to a
malicious cyber-attack. Dull technical glitches, human error, or expired
software certificates are usually the culprit.
For example - “it’s always DNS” is an ongoing joke in the IT
world referring to the frequently problematic Domain Name System. There are
even t-shirts made with the slogan written on them.
It’s not always DNS of course. But the joke encapsulates the
problem of our modern digital world where there are single points of failure
that can bring down millions of users with them.

Microsoft says it is deeply sorry for a global service outage that has disrupted millions of users, saying it is "not related to cyber or AI/agents" and was introduced during an "earlier network capacity upgrade"
It says it is seeing "broad recovery and stability" across its services after the outage which left Teams, Outlook and AI software Copilot inaccessible in at least 70 countries
Flora Hamilton, executive director of the Small Business
Charter, says it is a "stark reminder" of how much firms "rely
on digital systems”.
"For many small and medium-sized enterprises, access to
email is fundamental to everything from communicating with customers and
suppliers to generating income," she says.
Artificial Intelligence strategist Walter Pasquarelli tells
the BBC that for governments, businesses and critical infrastructure, the
outage puts "their vulnerabilities and dependence in plain sight".
"Looking ahead, as AI assistants are plugged into our
email, calendars and files, a failure like this may spread even further,"
he says.
Gergely Orosz, a former leading software engineer at several
major tech companies, says what was unusual about this outage was the time it
took for Microsoft to recover. The resulting upset shows "our reliance on
a few large US corporations".
A spokesperson for Microsoft said "given the scale of
demand" it restored services gradually.
Any outage compensation likely to face hurdles, says UK lawyerpublished at 18:33 GMT
18:33 GMT
Tom Espiner Business reporter
Microsoft may face compensation claims over the Outlook
outage, but there would be lots of potential pitfalls for would-be claimants, a
lawyer says.
Whether an organisation or individual would stand a chance
of success in getting any compensation from Microsoft would depend on the
nature of the contract they have with the tech giant.
Customer terms, especially for free versions of Outlook, usually
specify that Microsoft is not liable for service interruptions, and courts
normally uphold these contracts if the terms are reasonable, says Riah Sattar,
a partner and head of dispute resolution at Thomas Mansfield Solicitors.
Businesses or individuals using paid-for versions of Outlook
are partly protected under the Consumer Rights Act in the UK, which says that paid-for
services must be delivered with reasonable care and skill.
However, the claimant would have to show that Microsoft was
liable for something it had control over, which is difficult to do.
The claimant would also have to show that the outage caused
them a loss.
'Hopefully it'll be a wake-up call': BBC readers react to Microsoft blackoutpublished at 18:07 GMT
18:07 GMT
As Microsoft systems continue to come back online following more than 20 hours of disruption, readers have been getting in touch with the BBC to describe their experiences.
It is fair to say some have had it better than others:
Wilber, London: "I’m exchanging the contract for my new home today. But since the email is not working, all the things [are] stuck and can’t move and we [have been] waiting this for a whole year!"
Callum, Ormskirk: "It's ridiculous that we've become so reliant on one company's products as a society that so many workplaces and educators are basically having to come to a standstill... hopefully it'll be a wake-up call"
Amar, Tanzania: "I'm in Zanzibar, Tanzania, where I'm working remotely, running my business. In the last 15 minutes, all the day's emails have come through... I spent the day reading on the beach, now it's time to catch up"
Microsoft outage had 'limited' impact on frontline services - UK cyber ministerpublished at 17:49 GMT
17:49 GMT
Image source, PA Media
Image caption,
Cyber minister Liz Lloyd has explained there has been "limited" impacts on frontline services
The UK cyber minister says the Microsoft outage has "mainly affected back-office functions such as messaging and email" and the government has seen "limited" reports of impact on frontline service delivery.
Liz Lloyd says 999 calls and NHS 111 services have been unaffected by the disruption and that HMRC and DWP phone lines are "fully operational" - although people may have had longer than usual waiting times.
She adds there has been no disruption to the transport network as a result of the outage.
Lloyd adds she has spoken to the CEO of Microsoft UK about their plans for a full return to normal services "as soon as possible".
She explains Microsoft is "seeing positive results".
"The government recognises this incident has been inconvenient and disruptive," she says.
"Affected departments and public sector organisations have plans in place for these events to mitigate potential impacts and continue their business operations."
Lloyd adds that the government will review the incident and use the lessons learnt to "inform our ongoing work to raise digital resilience across the UK".

How the Microsoft 365 outage unfolded - a timelinepublished at 17:16 GMT
17:16 GMT
Tinshui Yeung Live reporter
Image source, Downdetector
Image caption,
Website Downdetector noted hundreds of reports on Thursday evening and Friday morning
All timestamps are in GMT, according to posts published on X.
12 November 17:54: Microsoft first acknowledges reports that users in the Americas are unable to access Exchange Online mailboxes.
18:07: Microsoft warns that the disruption may affect Microsoft 365 connectivity globally, not just in the Americas.
19:31: The company says an investigation found that a number of services had become “unexpectedly unavailable”, straining the remaining infrastructure.
19:50: Engineers start focusing on an issue that caused servers to fail automated health checks and become unavailable.
23:04: Microsoft says it’s returning some affected servers to service, and they “remain stable”.
13 November 00:06: Microsoft says servers returned to service can become unresponsive again once they receive traffic, and it is analysing the data to find out why.
04:36: After a series of efforts, Microsoft says it is seeing “partial recovery” in some regions.
09:24: “We’re seeing early stabilization and capacity improvements as recovery actions continue,” the company says.
13:32: The company says there are “broader improvements” in North America, “sustained improvements” across much of Europe and “encouraging recovery signals” in the Asia Pacific region.
14:17: The company says it’s continuing to see improvements, with email queues “significantly reduced and approaching normal levels”.
15:39: Microsoft says it's "seeing encouraging recovery process", and confirms that "there is no indication this is the result of malicious or cyber activity".

Microsoft services are slowly returning online after a

What has happened?
Users
began reporting they were having trouble accessing Microsoft 365 products
including Outlook and Teams on Thursday - these are programmes used by
more than 430 million people worldwide
Microsoft
said the disruption was
not caused by a cyber-security breach, and attributed the disruption
to "elevated load" on its infrastructure - although we still
don't know the specific cause yet
More than 20 hours after the issues began, the firm said "encouraging progress" was being seen and that email queues were "approaching normal levels"
While services are now returning online, that doesn't mean everything is working perfectly behind the scenes - expect some delayed emails for a while yet, writes BBC tech reporter Tom Gerken.

sensitive data.
Chances of this being some kind of cyber-attack are low - if we judge historypublished at 16:13 GMT
16:13 GMT
Joe Tidy Cyber correspondent
Image source, Getty Images
The exact words Microsoft is using are: "As of now, we
are not aware of any malicious activity that is causing this incident.”
This assessment might change of course.
Right now, the priority is not to find out the root cause,
but to get millions of people back online. So, any investigation into what
triggered this enormous outage can wait.
But the chances of this being some kind of cyber-attack are
low - if we judge history.
None of the mega outages of recent years have been down to a
malicious cyber-attack. Dull technical glitches, human error, or expired
software certificates are usually the culprit.
For example - “it’s always DNS” is an ongoing joke in the IT
world referring to the frequently problematic Domain Name System. There are
even t-shirts made with the slogan written on them.
It’s not always DNS of course. But the joke encapsulates the
problem of our modern digital world where there are single points of failure
that can bring down millions of users with them.

recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance

"We know our customers rely on our services to get
important work done every day and regret the impact these outages had on those
who depend on us.
"Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can
prevent similar issues going forward."

Microsoft outage is 'prime time' for scammers, cyber expert warnspublished at 15:33 GMT
15:33 GMT
Shiona McCallum Senior technology reporter
Image source, Getty images
Cyber security expert Ken Munro warns businesses and
individuals who have been switching to messaging apps during the Microsoft
outage could potentially put personal data at risk.
"If email is down, many will move to WhatsApp and other
platforms, so there's a risk to sensitive data being shared on less secure
platforms," he says.
Munro says email outages and cyber events like this are
"a prime time for scammers".
He warns fake support messages about the outage could be
used to "extract passwords from people and install malware".
The warning, as ever, during these cyber security incidents
is to be careful of clicking on links and pause if something doesn't look
right.
Microsoft shares open flat as traders brush off outagepublished at 15:15 GMT

Mitchell Labiak Business reporter
Image source, Reuters
Shares in Microsoft dipped 0.2% in early trading in the US,
suggesting that investors are not all that worried about what the outage could
mean for the tech giant.
It seems remarkable considering the immense amount of disruption the outage has
caused around the world, but share price movements depend on earnings
predictions, not public sentiment.
As such, for Microsoft investors, there are only two big questions today: Will
the firm be liable for compensation as a result of this issue? And will its
customers cancel their subscriptions to its services en masse in response?
The share price movement, or lack thereof, implies that the majority of traders
believe the answer to both of those questions is "no".

Scientists claim the universe is twice as old as believed
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
05:13:30 UTC
14:13:30 KST

The universe could be 26.7 billion years old, almost twice as old as scientists generally believe, according to a researcher at the University of Ottawa (uOttawa) who says his model also works without dark matter or dark energy.
Rajendra Gupta, an adjunct professor of physics, and postdoctoral researcher Nikolaos Samaras have spent much of the past two years checking their model, known as CCC+TL (Covarying Coupling Constants plus Tired Light), against major astronomical observations.
Instead of relying on undetected dark matter and dark energy to explain the cosmos, CCC+TL modifies the laws of gravity and cosmic expansion by proposing that physical constants slowly shift over time and that light loses energy as it travels across space, meaning distant light took far longer to reach us, extending the universe’s estimated age to 26.7 billion years per the model.
“Most emerging models explain only a limited set of observations,” Gupta said in a statement. “A new model must not conflict with the pillars of cosmology and astrophysics, and our findings meet that challenge, including the yet-to-be-published Cosmic Microwave Background challenge.”
Putting the theory to the test
In computer simulations testing the framework, the researchers found that their model accurately predicted the observed abundance of hydrogen and helium created in the Big Bang, while also matching the universe’s historical expansion rate without requiring dark energy.
In a study published in Monthly Notices of the Royal Astronomical Society, the team reported that their model could also explain how stars and gas orbit in 175 galaxies, something that has long been one of the main arguments for dark matter.
The model could also help with a puzzle raised by the James Webb Space Telescope, which has found galaxies that seem far too big and developed for how early in the universe’s history they appear. If the universe is older, those galaxies would simply have had more time to grow.
“The success should encourage astronomers to take the new model seriously,” Gupta noted. “It is challenging to understand and apply, but we believe it extends the physics of the Universe beyond ΛCDM limits.”
The idea is still well outside the mainstream. Some of the team’s work is still under review, and the standard model continues to explain a wide range of observations, so other scientists will need to test the findings independently. Upcoming Webb observations that search for even more distant galaxies could help.
Sources: uOttawa, Monthly Notices, MDPI, Science Daily

Scientists claim the universe is twice as old as believed

====================================================================================================

The average cost for a new vehicle in the U.S. hits a record high: over $51,000
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
05:10:27 UTC
14:10:27 KST

The average cost for a new vehicle in the U.S. hits a record high: over $51,000
Stay up to date with our Up First newsletter, sent every weekday morning.
The average transaction cost for a new vehicle has reached a record high: $51,109. That's according to a report out today from the Kelley Blue Book, which is part of the analytics firm Cox Automotive.
That's about $500 above the last record, set in December 2025.
Car prices have been stubbornly high for years, especially since the pandemic, mainly driven by supply chain issues. And they've gotten higher recently, in part thanks to tariffs and to buyers flocking to, and car companies making, larger, fancier vehicles.
Yet there's a little good news: Cars are getting more expensive … more slowly.
While that $51,109 figure is an all-time high, that's only up 2.1% in the past year.
"A 2.1% year-over-year increase is actually below inflation," said Erin Keating, an executive analyst at Cox Automotive who co-authored the report. Between 2012 and 2021, cars got 3.4% more expensive a year on average, she continued.
The Kelley Blue Book's transaction cost is based on what people wound up paying for a new vehicle — that means whatever's on the sticker price, plus add-ons for fancier features, and minus any incentives, such as dealer discounts.
It also doesn't include one other expense that adds to the cost of owning a car: interest on auto loans. The cost of borrowing has risen over the past several years as the Federal Reserve has kept interest rates high in an effort to drive down inflation. Auto loan rates are now about 11% for used vehicles and over 7% for new ones, according to Edmunds, a car analytics company.
Keating says all of this can add up to some sticker shock for buyers — partly because people don't buy cars all that often and can be surprised by how expensive they've gotten, and partly because of interest rates.
"I mean, we are seeing a true shift in the percentage of your monthly payment that's going towards interest versus towards principal," she said.
Used cars are not a steal
The Kelley Blue Book's average includes all kinds of vehicles — SUVs, pickup trucks, electric vehicles — from all kinds of auto companies, including Toyota, Subaru, Tesla and Mercedes-Benz.
It doesn't, however, include used car sales, which normally make up about two-thirds of total sales. Ivan Drury, director of industry insights at Edmunds, crunched those numbers too and found the same trend. "No matter what you're looking at, the price is up," he said.
The average used car now costs close to $27,000, 6% higher than last year, according to Cox Automotive. Vehicles that cost less than that are a "sliver" of the industry, Drury said.
Supply chain challenges, including a semiconductor shortage, during the COVID pandemic meant fewer cars got made. Carmakers had to pick and choose which vehicles to prioritize making — they picked their most profitable models. As a result, fewer used vehicles entered car lots in the early 2020s. So today there's less inventory to choose from.
That's good for people who already own a car, Drury said. It means people can upgrade to a new car without much additional money up front.
But, he continued, "The consumer that I sympathize with the absolute most? The first-time car buyer." Those buyers face a steep down payment, with nothing to trade in.
No matter whether buyers are choosing new or used, data from Edmunds shows that in 2026, they have been financing with lower down payments and longer auto term lengths. "We've just seen those explode," Drury said.
A report from Edmunds found that car buyers financed more than ever, taking out loans worth an average of $44,664 in the past quarter. They also found that more than a quarter of car buyers financed using loans with 84-month-long terms — or longer.
The combination of a small down payment — sometimes in the range of $500 or $1,000 — and a lengthy loan term can get really expensive for buyers in the long term, Drury said, especially with high interest rates.
"So people just say, 'Oh, forget it,' you know. 'I'll just, I'll just choose the longer term length and put less money down. I've got other bills that are pulling at me,'" Drury said.
Why are new cars so expensive?
In part, vehicles cost more because the cost of making them has gone up for automakers. There are now more tariffs on steel and aluminum, as well as on goods imported from Canada. That matters for American automakers who assemble vehicles on both sides of the border. The cost of computer chips (used in sensors and electronics) and copper (used for automotive wiring) has also risen thanks to competing demand from data centers.
But vehicles also cost more because Americans like bigger cars, SUVs and trucks, which tend to come with a higher price tag. The average sticker price for a full-size pickup truck is nearly $72,000, according to the Kelley Blue Book.
American carmakers have largely shifted their gears away from compact sedans. In earnings calls with investors, they've often cited American demand for bigger vehicles.
Plus, as analyst Mike Wall at Mobility Global points out, smaller vehicles offer lower margins for carmakers. "They were sort of that loss leader. It gets the eyeballs in the door," he said.
For the carmakers, they're not making as much money as they would compared to their bigger vehicles with pricier features.
Drury notes, people appreciate those features. They've gotten used to driving with 360-degree cameras, heated seats and parking assist systems. All of these drive up the cost of buying. "No one wants entry-level anymore," he said. "Everybody goes mid-to-upper trim levels."
"People love walking up to their car that already has the air conditioner going when it's 110 degrees outside, and think, 'You know what? I don't have to get in here and potentially burn my hands,'" Drury continued. "So as we get accustomed to more features, more options, we naturally kind of pile on, too. And automakers, they're very aware of that."
A challenging time for automakers
Over the past few years, "automakers have been under incredible stress," Keating said. Yes, there are tariffs and other rising production costs. Many also poured money into developing electric vehicle technology, but EVs still only make up a small slice of the U.S. car market. About 6% of new cars sold are electric, according to Edmunds.
And the market for car buyers is smaller. About 16 million people buy new cars every year — about 1 million fewer since the pandemic.
Notably, fewer people in their late 30s and early 40s are buying cars. "They're just simply holding onto their cars longer," Keating said. Her hypothesis? "That age group and that cohort is simply just dealing with different household pressures for their money and are making the choice to stay in the vehicles that they have."
For the most part, the people buying new cars tend to be older and wealthier. Keating said that might be because older drivers are staying on the road longer. " We have seen this interesting shift towards individuals keeping their licenses longer and therefore more 65 and up, 75 and up individuals are purchasing vehicles than ever before," she said.
For automakers, a drop in car buyers means they have to compete more with each other for sales.
"At the end of the day, each of these competitors are sharing, trying to go after a pie that isn't necessarily growing," Keating said. So instead, " they're stealing share from their competitors as opposed to counting on more consumers entering the picture."

Used cars are not a steal
The Kelley Blue Book's average includes all kinds of vehicles — SUVs, pickup trucks, electric vehicles — from all kinds of auto companies, including Toyota, Subaru, Tesla and Mercedes-Benz.
It doesn't, however, include used car sales, which normally make up about two-thirds of total sales. Ivan Drury, director of industry insights at Edmunds, crunched those numbers too and found the same trend. "No matter what you're looking at, the price is up," he said.
The average used car now costs close to $27,000, 6% higher than last year, according to Cox Automotive. Vehicles that cost less than that are a "sliver" of the industry, Drury said.
Supply chain challenges, including a semiconductor shortage, during the COVID pandemic meant fewer cars got made. Carmakers had to pick and choose which vehicles to prioritize making — they picked their most profitable models. As a result, fewer used vehicles entered car lots in the early 2020s. So today there's less inventory to choose from.
That's good for people who already own a car, Drury said. It means people can upgrade to a new car without much additional money up front.
But, he continued, "The consumer that I sympathize with the absolute most? The first-time car buyer." Those buyers face a steep down payment, with nothing to trade in.
No matter whether buyers are choosing new or used, data from Edmunds shows that in 2026, they have been financing with lower down payments and longer auto term lengths. "We've just seen those explode," Drury said.
A report from Edmunds found that car buyers financed more than ever, taking out loans worth an average of $44,664 in the past quarter. They also found that more than a quarter of car buyers financed using loans with 84-month-long terms — or longer.
The combination of a small down payment — sometimes in the range of $500 or $1,000 — and a lengthy loan term can get really expensive for buyers in the long term, Drury said, especially with high interest rates.
"So people just say, 'Oh, forget it,' you know. 'I'll just, I'll just choose the longer term length and put less money down. I've got other bills that are pulling at me,'" Drury said.
Why are new cars so expensive?
In part, vehicles cost more because the cost of making them has gone up for automakers. There are now more tariffs on steel and aluminum, as well as on goods imported from Canada. That matters for American automakers who assemble vehicles on both sides of the border. The cost of computer chips (used in sensors and electronics) and copper (used for automotive wiring) has also risen thanks to competing demand from data centers.
But vehicles also cost more because Americans like bigger cars, SUVs and trucks, which tend to come with a higher price tag. The average sticker price for a full-size pickup truck is nearly $72,000, according to the Kelley Blue Book.
American carmakers have largely shifted their gears away from compact sedans. In earnings calls with investors, they've often cited American demand for bigger vehicles.
Plus, as analyst Mike Wall at Mobility Global points out, smaller vehicles offer lower margins for carmakers. "They were sort of that loss leader. It gets the eyeballs in the door," he said.
For the carmakers, they're not making as much money as they would compared to their bigger vehicles with pricier features.
Drury notes, people appreciate those features. They've gotten used to driving with 360-degree cameras, heated seats and parking assist systems. All of these drive up the cost of buying. "No one wants entry-level anymore," he said. "Everybody goes mid-to-upper trim levels."
"People love walking up to their car that already has the air conditioner going when it's 110 degrees outside, and think, 'You know what? I don't have to get in here and potentially burn my hands,'" Drury continued. "So as we get accustomed to more features, more options, we naturally kind of pile on, too. And automakers, they're very aware of that."
A challenging time for automakers
Over the past few years, "automakers have been under incredible stress," Keating said. Yes, there are tariffs and other rising production costs. Many also poured money into developing electric vehicle technology, but EVs still only make up a small slice of the U.S. car market. About 6% of new cars sold are electric, according to Edmunds.
And the market for car buyers is smaller. About 16 million people buy new cars every year — about 1 million fewer since the pandemic.
Notably, fewer people in their late 30s and early 40s are buying cars. "They're just simply holding onto their cars longer," Keating said. Her hypothesis? "That age group and that cohort is simply just dealing with different household pressures for their money and are making the choice to stay in the vehicles that they have."
For the most part, the people buying new cars tend to be older and wealthier. Keating said that might be because older drivers are staying on the road longer. " We have seen this interesting shift towards individuals keeping their licenses longer and therefore more 65 and up, 75 and up individuals are purchasing vehicles than ever before," she said.
For automakers, a drop in car buyers means they have to compete more with each other for sales.
"At the end of the day, each of these competitors are sharing, trying to go after a pie that isn't necessarily growing," Keating said. So instead, " they're stealing share from their competitors as opposed to counting on more consumers entering the picture."
