Microsoft 365 Status on X: "We’ve monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected. We’re identifying alternate workstreams that can be applied to increase capacity in the face of sustained traffic management imbalances. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as https://t.co/7pjJdrpwOr if the admin center is not available to you."
We’ve monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected. We’re identifying alternate workstreams that can be applied to increase capacity in the face of sustained traffic management imbalances. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as msft.it/6013xBHsf if the admin center is not available to you.
We're investigating reports of users experiencing issues accessing Exchange Online mailboxes through multiple connection methods in the Americas. More details can be found in the Microsoft 365 admin center under EX1550543.
While we're working to mitigate impact, we've confirmed the issue may affect Microsoft 365 service connectivity for users in any region, more information will be provided in the Microsoft 365 admin center under EX1550543.
We've determined a portion of infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors. We're providing more information under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft
We've begun redistributing connection traffic across our broader worldwide infrastructure to help balance the load. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft if the admin center is not available to you.
We're aggressively pursuing all pathways to reduce impact. We've paused all deployments, we're moving additional traffic off the affected infrastructure, and we're restarting affected servers to return them to service. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft if the admin center is not available to you.
We determined the elevated load isn't driven by an increase in traffic, but by a number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable; straining the remaining infrastructure. We're working to safely return them to service. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft if the admin center is not available to you.
We're testing a configuration change to address a problem that's causing servers to fail automated health checks and become unavailable. In parallel, we've initiated a restart of all affected servers and are monitoring health as they return to service. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft if the admin center is not available to you.
We've not identified any recent change correlated with the start of impact; however, our investigation indicates the failed health checks may originate from a specific component that's responsible for processing server health. We've taken steps to prevent additional servers fromShow more
We've validated an alternative service health check method, which allowed a subset of affected servers to successfully return to service. We're now applying this change more broadly. We're also preparing automated recovery systems to continually detect and restart unresponsiveShow more
We're actively restarting and validating the affected servers to return them to service. In parallel, we're assessing whether reverting a small number of recent configuration updates we identified could help accelerate recovery. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as status.cloud.microsoft if the admin center is not available to you.
We’re opening all available pathways to restore service, as our actions taken thus far have not provided the anticipated relief. We found a core process on affected servers is becoming overwhelmed, causing capacity loss. To validate a potential recovery, we're isolating a portionShow more
We're returning a portion of affected servers to the service, and they remain stable so far. We're also shifting traffic to alternate routes wherever possible and observing limited recovery. In parallel, we've streamlined the process of returning the remaining affected servers toShow more
The servers we returned to service can become unresponsive again once they receive traffic; we're analyzing diagnostic data to determine what's triggering the behavior. In parallel, we're temporarily limiting which types of traffic can reach the affected infrastructure in someShow more
We’ve monitored the status of the changes applied to improve serviceability and confirmed they're not taking as expected. We’re identifying alternate workstreams that can be applied to increase capacity in the face of sustained traffic management imbalances. More info under EX1550543 and MO1550821 in the Microsoft 365 admin center, as well as msft.it/6013xBHsf if the admin center is not available to you.

Show HN: PharmA2A, an open protocol for pharmacy-to-supplier orders on A2A
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
04:58:53 UTC
13:58:53 KST

The open protocol for direct orders between a pharmacy and its suppliers, labs, wholesalers and buying platforms, on top of A2A.
Version 0.1, published on 12 November 2026. Site: pharma2a.org. Contact: contact@pharma2a.org. Version française.
In France, a community pharmacy orders directly from hundreds of suppliers: dermocosmetics, supplements, medical devices. Every order is a form, a mail, a phone call, then a check at delivery. PharmA2A fixes what the pharmacy's buying agent and the supplier's agent say to each other, so that an order is right the first time and the supplier learns, when the pharmacist allows it, what its products do on the shelf.
A2A (Agent2Agent, Linux Foundation) says how two agents talk.
PharmA2A says what they say: five messages, each a JSON Schema, declared as an A2A profile
extension, https://pharma2a.org/ext/v1. To our knowledge,
it is the first sector-specific dictionary built on A2A.
The field names are French: PharmA2A starts with French community pharmacy, its CIP 13 codes and its words. The specification and the English guide explain every message in English.
- No economic data of the pharmacy in any message. The supplier's own terms travel as it states them, never recomputed.
- Nothing invented. A field nobody stated stays null.
- Only the supplier speaks for the supplier, and a transcribed answer never confirms an order on its own.
- A message that says nothing is not a confirmation.
- The pharmacy calls, the supplier answers. No push, no stream.
- No patient data.
An order is transmitted once: the reference is its identity, per customer account.
The pharma2a package also carries what every part uses: the dictionary and its validation (pharma2a.dictionnaire).
Python 3.11 or newer.
git clone https://github.com/pharma2a/pharma2a && cd pharma2a
python3 -m venv .venv && . .venv/bin/activate && pip install -e ".[modele,test]"
The sample supplier, in one minute. Start it, then let the demo pharmacy order from it:
python examples/sample-supplier/agent.py
python -m pharma2a.officine --agent http://127.0.0.1:8766 --attendre 2    # in a second terminal
The supplier email bridge. It starts in draft mode: nothing is sent, mails are written to sorties/.
PHARMA2A_JETON=a-test-token python -m pharma2a
PHARMA2A_JETON=a-test-token python -m pharma2a.officine       # in a second terminal
Its agent card is at http://127.0.0.1:8765/.well-known/agent-card.json. Every setting is an
environment variable, listed in .env.example. python -m pytest runs the tests.
- Specification: the normative rules, for anyone who implements PharmA2A.
- The protocol in detail: the messages, the diagrams, the rules, A2A conformance.
- Connecting a supplier system: the backend contract, an ERP round trip in 10 minutes, the dictionary mapped to ERP fields.
- Decision records: why the protocol is the way it is.
- The guide: the complete guide for a supplier's IT staff, installation and configuration included. Version française.
- The dictionary itself: schema/, with an example of each message in schema/exemples/.
The pharmacy buying agent that SyderaAI (Paris) runs for its pharmacies speaks PharmA2A with their suppliers. No supplier runs an agent yet, so that buying agent hosts the supplier email bridge for each supplier: the supplier gets a clear mail with the order in its body, answers as usual, and the bridge transcribes the answer into the dictionary. The day a supplier serves its own agent, the pharmacy changes one URL.
Pharmacy buying agents that speak PharmA2A:
To add yours, open a pull request that adds one line to this table: the agent's name, its publisher, and a contact. Pour ajouter le vôtre, ouvrez une pull request qui ajoute une ligne à ce tableau.
Issues and pull requests are welcome: see CONTRIBUTING.md,
GOVERNANCE.md and CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md. Security
reports go through SECURITY.md, never through a public issue. Versions are in
CHANGELOG.md.
The dictionary (schema/) is public domain, CC0 1.0. The agent and its tests are Apache 2.0.
PharmA2A is a trademark of SyderaAI, Paris, filed with the French INPI: the content is free,
the name is not, see TRADEMARK.md. Anyone may implement, copy, fork and extend
the protocol without asking; the name is protected only so that nobody can call "PharmA2A" what
does not follow it, which is what keeps the common language common.

IceWM
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
04:54:53 UTC
13:54:53 KST

IceWM is a window manager for the X Window System. The goal of IceWM is speed, simplicity, and not getting in the user’s way. It comes with a taskbar with pager, global and per-window keybindings and a dynamic menu system. Application windows can be managed by keyboard and mouse. Windows can be iconified to the taskbar, to the tray, to the desktop or be made hidden. They are controllable by a quick switch window (Alt+Tab) and in a window list. A handful of configurable focus models are menu-selectable. Setups with multiple monitors are supported by RandR and Xinerama. IceWM is very configurable, themeable and well documented. It includes an optional external background wallpaper manager with transparency support, a simple session manager and a system tray. IceWM is available on popular Linux distributions like Debian, Ubuntu, Arch, OpenSUSE, Gentoo, Slackware, CentOS, antiX, NixOS and on most *BSDs.
Software
IceWM was created by Marko Maček in 1997 using C++. It is now maintained at Github.
Releases
The latest released version is 4.1.0 (2026-08-06).
Features
- Easy to use, simple and fast
- Standards compliant→
- Fully usable with keyboard
- Alt+Tab window switching
- Efficient resource usage
- Task bar (optional)
- Multiple workspaces
- Fully documented→
- A large number of themes→
- Usable with GNOME and KDE environments
- Regenerates menus when configuration changes
- Support for sound effects→
- Multiple focus modes
- Manual placement of windows option
- Autoraising of windows option
- Tooltips
- Configurable keybindings→
- Supports DockApps in a container
- Tabbed windows
Screenshots
The IceWM Screenshots Collection
Themes
IceWM is themeable.
The visual appearance of icewm can be changed by a theme,
which is a collection of settings and small images.
Over 450 themes can be downloaded from box-look.org.
Extra is a collection of 91 highlights. See icewm -i list.
Translations
IceWM is translated into three dozen languages. To improve the translation for your language at Weblate, first register an account either here or there, then update.
Documentation
Bug Tracking
Report your icewm bugs on Github. Only report it once and give relevant details.
Install the latest release as follows
$ wget https://github.com/ice-wm/icewm/releases/download/4.1.0/icewm-4.1.0.tar.lz
$ tar -x --lzip -vpf icewm-4.1.0.tar.lz
$ cd icewm-4.1.0
$ ./configure --prefix=/usr
$ make
$ sudo make install
Install from unreleased development source code
Gettext must be installed.
$ git clone https://github.com/bbidulock/icewm
$ cd icewm
$ ./autogen.sh
$ ./configure --prefix=/usr
$ make
$ sudo make install
If you have cmake, an alternative is:
$ git clone https://github.com/bbidulock/icewm
$ cd icewm
$ ./rebuild.sh -r --prefix=/usr
$ cd build
$ sudo make install
Install package dependencies
To install all package dependencies in one go use the following script.
$ wget https://ice-wm.org/scripts/os-depends.sh
$ sudo bash -x ./os-depends.sh
Verify package dependencies by:
$ pkg-config --modversion x11 xext xcomposite xcursor xdamage xfixes xres xrender xrandr xinerama xft fontconfig sm ice sndfile alsa ao gio-2.0 gio-unix-2.0 gdk-pixbuf-xlib-2.0 imlib2 librsvg-2.0 xpm libpng libjpeg
# or simpler:
$ ./rebuild.sh -d
More installation and configuration
Links
- IceWM Menuator for managing menu files with a GUI.
- IceWM sound files.
- IceWM release history.
- Compare window managers.
- List of all window managers and their helper programs.
- Extended window manager hints.
- The anatomy of the modern window manager (thesis).
License
IceWM is released under the terms of the GNU Library General Public License.
Donate
Contribute to ongoing development and support of IceWM by donating.

SpaceX To Invest $74 Billion Into Data Center Construction Projects In Texas, Arizona
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

SpaceX To Invest $74 Billion Into Data Center Construction Projects In Texas, Arizona 61
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from the Hill: Elon Musk's SpaceX plans to invest more than $74 billion into building two data centers in Texas and Arizona, the company announced Thursday, marketing them as projects that will boost local communities amid backlash surrounding such facilities. "These investments will expand local tax bases, generate substantial new revenue for schools and public services, and create economic opportunities that extend far beyond the facilities themselves," the company said in a release. SpaceX said it will put more than $24 billion into a new project in Texas's Rio Grande Valley, expanding the company's headquarters in Starbase -- a coastal town the tech giant established in 2025 in a previously unincorporated area of Cameron County. In Arizona, SpaceX will invest around $50 billion in Cochise County, a rural area east of Tucson bordering Mexico and New Mexico.
SpaceX pledged the new projects will be created cooperatively with the surrounding communities and will be environmentally conscious. The company said the project in Texas would generate its own power and invest $220 million to expand the water supply in nearby Brownsville. While in Arizona, it vowed to work with local electric cooperatives and "potentially" reduce electric bills, saying the site would use dry cooling without water consumption. "They require strong partnerships with local communities, responsible environmental stewardship, and stable, competitive policies that give companies the confidence to make long-term investments," the company said in a release. Both sites promise hundreds of permanent jobs even once construction is complete.

Switcher (2004)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
03:14:59 UTC
12:14:59 KST

The first commercial product that I worked on after going on leave of absence from Apple in March 1984 (see Leave Of Absence) was a low cost, high resolution scanner for the Macintosh called Thunderscan, that I created in collaboration with a tiny company named Thunderware (see Thunderscan).  I started working on it in June 1984, and by early October, it was almost complete.
Tom Petrie, one of the two principals at Thunderware (the other was Victor Bull, who I worked with on the Silentype thermal printer 
(see What Hath Woz Wrought)
, the first project that I did for Apple), arranged a few demos for various computer magazines, to curry favorable reviews to promote the product.  On October 11th, 1984, I drove with Tom to an office in Hillsborough, to demonstrate Thunderscan for Byte magazine.
The Byte reviewer was John Markoff, a technology scribe for the San Francisco Chronicle who was one of the best reporters covering the personal computer industry.  Tom described Thunderscan while I set up the demo and started scanning.  John asked a few questions, taking notes with his IBM PC, which was running a character-based text editor that I viewed with the typical pious disdain of a Macintosh purist.  As I was answering one of his questions, the phone rang.
"Excuse me," he told us, as he pressed a key combination on his keyboard, his monitor screen instantly changing to a different program.  He talked on the phone for a minute or two, occasionally typing, before he finished the conversation and pressed a key combination to switch back to his Thunderscan notes.
"What did you just do?," I asked John, curious about the software that he was running.  "How did you switch to another application so quickly?"
"Oh, I'm running Memory Shift.  Haven't you seen it?" John responded.  "It's a DOS utility program that keeps multiple applications resident in memory, and allows you to switch between them quickly.  I've been using it a lot lately."  John typed the switch command a few times in rapid succession, to show me how fast it could do its thing.
"You know, I think I could do that for the Macintosh", I suddenly blurted out, before I even thought about it consciously. 
The 512K Macintosh, with four times the memory of the original, had just started shipping a few weeks ago.  I had considered trying to run multiple applications simultaneously on the 512K Mac, but I was stymied by low memory conflicts and other potential gotchas.  But now, as I observed Memory Shift in action on John's PC, I suddenly saw a simple way to do it, which didn't seem to be too hard to implement.
"Yeah, that would be cool," John agreed.  I continued with the Thunderscan demo, but it was hard for me to concentrate on it, because I couldn't stop thinking about application switching.  There were a few intricate problems to solve, but it seemed eminently doable, and I thought it would be incredibly useful if I got it to work.
Tom Petrie noticed how excited I was about the new idea and, as he drove me home from the demo, he reminded me of my prior commitments, and made me promise that I would finish the alpha release of Thunderscan as soon as possible, before daring to start something new.  We agreed on a list of a dozen or so tasks, which I thought I could accomplish within two weeks.  Once the alpha release was completed, I could take a short hiatus, in order to work on application switching, before diving back into Thunderscan to finish the 1.0 release, due by the end of November.
During the next two weeks, I focused on polishing Thunderscan to get it ready for the alpha release, but I also spent idle moments pondering the design of the application switcher.  One fundamental decision was whether or not to load all of the applications into a single heap, which would make optimal use of memory by minimizing fragmentation, or to allocate separate "heap zones" for each application.  I decided to opt for separate heap zones to better isolate the applications, but I wasn't sure that was right.
There were lots of little problems to solve.  The most crucial one was that the system software kept lots of application-specific global variables in low memory 
(see Mea Culpa)
, which needed to be swapped during context switching, so each application could maintain its own set of them.  The hard part was coming up with the precise list of exactly what needed to be swapped; many of the variables were obvious, but some were quite subtle, and dependent on how applications were using them.  I knew that my first cut wouldn't be perfect, but I was confident that I could debug the inevitable problems once I saw how the applications were actually failing.
A few days after starting the push to finish Thunderscan, I received an intriguing phone call from Jeff Harbers, the manager of Microsoft's Macintosh applications team.  Jeff told me that Microsoft had a very strategic project that they needed for the Macintosh, and that they thought that I was the ideal person to implement it.  He wouldn't tell me anything else over the phone, but he offered to fly me up to Seattle to discuss it in person.  Even though I was right in the middle of trying to complete Thunderscan, I was intrigued enough to accept his offer to fly up to Seattle and visit with him the following Tuesday.
Jeff picked me up at the airport, and we drove to Microsoft's main building where we were joined by Neil Konzen, a talented 23 year old who was Microsoft's main systems programmer on the Macintosh.  I knew Neil from his days as an early Apple II hobbyist, when we collaborated on adding features to an assembly language development system when he was only 16.
Jeff asked me what I was working on, and I told him about Thunderscan, which he seemed to be interested in.  But when I mentioned that I was about to start some experiments with an application switching utility, his jaw dropped, and he looked like he couldn't believe what I said.
"That's just what we wanted to talk with you about!", he exclaimed, "It's great that you're already working on it".
Jeff explained that Microsoft had put a lot of effort into getting their applications to run well in the tiny space available in the 128K Macintosh, which they considered to be a key competitive advantage.  But as things stood, the 512K Mac would undermine their efforts, since it allowed applications to be much larger.  Plus, Lotus had recently announced an integrated application suite for the 512K Macintosh called Jazz that made it easy to quickly switch between different functional areas.   But if the Macintosh could run multiple applications simultaneously, the small memory footprint of the Microsoft apps would continue to be advantageous, since their lower memory requirements meant that more of them could run concurrently, and users could put together customized application suites on their own.  The purpose of the visit was to convince me to write an applications switcher under contract to Microsoft.
Neil Konzen had contemplated a potential design, which he conveyed to me in front of a whiteboard.  He decided to use the single heap approach that I had rejected, along with a few interesting twists to minimize memory fragmentation.  I told him about the alternate approach of using separate heapzones, and how I thought that it was probably worth it to trade some memory fragmentation for greater robustness.  I told him that I would give his approach some more thought.
Finally, my afternoon at Microsoft culminated in a private meeting with their CEO, Bill Gates.  Jeff ushered me into Bill's office and reviewed the afternoon's discussions for him, before excusing himself to leave us alone to negotiate a development deal.  I had met Bill a few times during the course of Macintosh development, and while I respected his technical acumen, I was wary of his burgeoning reputation as a conniving businessman.
After exchanging a few pleasantries, and telling me how much the Macintosh mattered to Microsoft, he looked me in the eye and said, "You're a really good programmer, right?  I think you must be a really good programmer."
"I guess so," I responded, not understanding why he was attempting to flatter me.
"Well, I think you are. How long do you think it will take to do this project?  A month or two?  I think a really good programmer like you could get it done in less than two months."
"I really have no idea," I replied, "I'm not far enough along to know if it's even feasible yet."
"Well, let's figure it out," he said in a slightly condescending tone.  "I don't think it could be more than 10,000 lines of code, and a really good programmer like you should be able to write at least a thousand lines of code per week, so I think it will take you less than 10 weeks to write it, if you're as good as I think you are."
I didn't know how to respond, so I kept quiet and let him continue.
"And how much do you think a really good programmer should get paid? Around here we pay our best programmers around two thousand dollars per week. Do you think you should be paid more than that?"
"I don't know," I replied.  I was finally beginning to see where he was coming from.  Bill was trying to get me to brag that I could write the application switcher really quickly, so he could justify paying me a lower price for it.
"Well, I don't think that you could expect to get more than four thousand dollars per week, tops.  Actually, I think that's too much, but let's go with that.  If it takes ten weeks, and you get paid four thousand dollars per week, that means you should get paid $40,000 for writing it."
$40,000 didn't sound like very much to me, especially if it was as strategic to Microsoft as it seemed to be. I think Bill was expecting me to make a counteroffer, but I wasn't very enthusiastic about selling it to Microsoft regardless of compensation, since it really should eventually be part of the Mac OS.
"Listen, I really want to write this, completely independently from you guys, so you won't have to pay me anything to do it.  I certainly don't want to negotiate a deal until I see how it turns out; there might be a show-stopper and I won't be able to get it to work at all. And if I pull it off, it really should be bundled with every 512K Macintosh."
Bill shifted his tactics.  "OK, I don't really care if Microsoft owns it as long as it's available to our users.  I want you to commit that you'll apply your best efforts to making sure that it runs well with our applications, and that you'll call Jeff if you run into any snags.  We can talk again about publishing it later if you want to after you're further along.  How does that sound?"
I told him that sounded good, and I promised to do my best to make it work great with Microsoft's applications, which I wanted to do anyway, since the Microsoft apps were important to most users.  We shook hands and I departed on a positive note.
By the time I returned to my house in Palo Alto that evening, I was burning with the desire to see if I could get something going quickly.  Even though still I had a few more days of work to complete the Thunderscan alpha release, I decided to see if I could write a proof of concept prototype of the application switcher first.
I would eventually have to write a user interface for selecting applications, but the proof of concept didn't have to worry about that; it was hardwired to run MacPaint, MacWrite, MacDraw and the Finder.  I worked for 20 hours straight writing the core of the program, which worked by patching traps to extend a few essential system calls, like GetNextEvent, Launch and ExitToShell.  The hardest part was going through all of the low memory locations, determining what needed to be swapped.  It was incredibly satisfying to see it begin to work, crashing all the time at first, but gradually stabilizing as I tracked down various problems.
I had it working for an hour or so when I saw Bud Tribble return home.  Bud had finally finished his M.D./Ph.D. program at the University of Washington, including interning for a year, but he decided that working on the Macintosh was more fun than being a doctor, so he had returned to Apple in his old job as software manager a few months earlier, in July 1984.  Bud was living at Burrell Smith's house, which was next door to mine.
I dragged Bud over to my house to show him how I could rapidly switch between MacPaint, MacWrite and MacDraw.  He was impressed, but to my surprise he complained that the switching, which was almost instantaneous, was actually too fast.  
"I think it might be confusing to switch from one application to another without any feedback," he told me.  "What if someone switches accidentally?  Maybe you could use animation to make a smoother transition."
That sounded like a great idea to me. We decided that one application should scroll off the screen horizontally while another was scrolling on, which gave the users a simple, concrete mental model of the applications wrapped around a sort of Lazy Susan, which they could rotate to move the desired application to the visible area.  I quickly wrote some fast scrolling routines, and was blown away by how cool it looked to see the applications zip across the screen.
I started showing my proof of concept demo to my friends at Apple, as well as a few user group demos, and it was very positively received.  Unfortunately, I still had to finish up the product release of Thunderscan, which I barely managed to do by the end of November as I had promised.   After taking a short vacation, I got back to work on what I was now calling "Switcher" in early December and by Christmas I had much of it implemented, including a simple UI for selecting applications and "Switcher Documents" for remembering sets of related applications.
In early January 1985, I got a phone call from Guy Kawasaki, one of Apple's third party evangelists, who told me that Apple was interested in buying Switcher, and that he was assigned to make that happen.  The first step was arranging a demo for Steve Jobs.
I entered Steve's office with a bit of trepidation, because I thought that Switcher was worth at least a quarter of a million dollars to Apple, but I was sure that Steve would never want to pay me that much.  But I was also proud of Switcher, and was interested in seeing how Steve would react to it.
I booted up my by now standard demo of MacWrite, MacPaint, MacDraw and the Finder, as well as a little maze generating program written by Steve Capps.  I configured Switcher with the scrolling animation initially turned off, so it would have more impact when I showed it later.  I demoed cutting and pasting between MacWrite, MacPaint and MacDraw, in seconds instead of minutes, and then I turned on the scrolling animation, and starting switching rapidly between them, in both directions.
"OK, I've seen enough, " Steve interrupted me.  "It's great.  Apple is going to bundle it with the Mac.  Congratulations."
But then he paused, and stared at me for a moment with an incredibly intense gaze, as if he was sizing me up or maybe just trying to scare me.
"But I don't want you taking advantage of this situation.  I'm not going to allow you to take advantage of Apple."
"What do you mean?" I asked him, genuinely puzzled.
"There's no way that you could have written that program without confidential information that you learned by working at Apple.  You don't have the right to charge whatever you like for it."
I started to get angry. "The program is only half finished, and if I don't think you're paying me fairly, I won't be motivated to finish it."
Steve gave me another intense stare as he paused for a few seconds.  Then he stated a single number, without explanation.
"One hundred thousand dollars."
"I don't know," I told him, "I think it's probably worth a lot more than that."
"Don't argue with me.  $100,000 is fair, and you know it."
I didn't seem to have any alternative but to capitulate to Steve's price setting, since he's difficult to argue with and I really wanted Switcher bundled with the Mac.  I eventually negotiated the final agreement with Guy Kawasaki, where, in addition to the $100,000, I managed to get a 10% royalty of the wholesale price if Apple sold Switcher separately, which Steve swore they would never do, but eventually the royalty delivered another $50,000.
Getting Switcher going wasn't that hard, but it was a very difficult program to finish, because it overturned some of the underlying assumptions that applications were making, yet it was committed to keeping everything working anyway.  Much of the work in the latter stages involved testing it with every application that I could get my hands on and debugging crashes.  Usually I could concoct some kind of technique that would mitigate the problem without causing worse problems elsewhere.
Predictably, the hardest part of finishing Switcher was making it work smoothly with the Microsoft applications.  That was partially because Microsoft was a very early developer, and took liberties with the system that most developers would shy away from, but it was mostly because the instructions comprising their applications were encoded in pseudo-code to save space, in the tradition of the byte-code interpreters from Xerox, which Charles Simonyi advocated.
Unfortunately, the pseudo-code kept me from disassembling the program when it crashed, which made it more difficult to debug.  I finally developed a debugging technique by single stepping through their interpreter, six instructions at a time to get to the instructions that were doing the work,  but it was pretty painful.  I was determined to slog through it because of the promise that I made when I visited Microsoft.
One of the last problems that I addressed before finishing the first release of Switcher in March 1985 had to do with applications hanging.  If you're running multiple applications, you don't want one application hanging to take down all of them.   I added a feature that allowed the user to kill the current application if it was hung up, by monitoring for a specific key combination during the vertical blanking interrupt handler.
I knew that I had to pick a very rare key combination, because you didn't want users killing their applications accidentally.  I decided on shift-command-option-period, four keys held down at once, which I thought would be pretty hard to stumble into accidentally.   But I was surprised when I got a call from Jeff Harbers at Microsoft.
"Hey, I like that abort feature that you just added, but you're going to have to change the key combination, because we're using that in Microsoft Word.", Jeff told me.  Microsoft Word was very complex, and it possessed an enormous range of keyboard shortcuts, way too many, as far as I was concerned.
"OK, suggest something for me to change it to and I'll consider it," I told Jeff.
Jeff didn't have anything specific in mind, so he told me that he would get back to me soon.  I had to laugh when he called me back the next day, and told me that he wanted to withdraw his request and that I should keep shift-command-option-period as the abort sequence.
"OK, that sounds good to me, " I told him.  "But why the change?  Doesn't it still conflict with Word?"
"We'll change Word in the next release not to use it.  The problem was that we couldn't find a safe sequence - I guess we're already using every key combination!"
I officially released the finished version of Switcher in April 1985, and I maintained it for a few versions after that.  Eventually, I handed it off to Phil Goldman, a tremendous young programmer from Princeton who was recently hired at Apple, who went on to write MultiFinder (with Erich Ringewald), Switcher's eventual successor, in 1987.

Steve gave me another intense stare as he paused for a few seconds.  Then he stated a single number, without explanation.
"One hundred thousand dollars."
"I don't know," I told him, "I think it's probably worth a lot more than that."
"Don't argue with me.  $100,000 is fair, and you know it."
I didn't seem to have any alternative but to capitulate to Steve's price setting, since he's difficult to argue with and I really wanted Switcher bundled with the Mac.  I eventually negotiated the final agreement with Guy Kawasaki, where, in addition to the $100,000, I managed to get a 10% royalty of the wholesale price if Apple sold Switcher separately, which Steve swore they would never do, but eventually the royalty delivered another $50,000.
Getting Switcher going wasn't that hard, but it was a very difficult program to finish, because it overturned some of the underlying assumptions that applications were making, yet it was committed to keeping everything working anyway.  Much of the work in the latter stages involved testing it with every application that I could get my hands on and debugging crashes.  Usually I could concoct some kind of technique that would mitigate the problem without causing worse problems elsewhere.
Predictably, the hardest part of finishing Switcher was making it work smoothly with the Microsoft applications.  That was partially because Microsoft was a very early developer, and took liberties with the system that most developers would shy away from, but it was mostly because the instructions comprising their applications were encoded in pseudo-code to save space, in the tradition of the byte-code interpreters from Xerox, which Charles Simonyi advocated.
Unfortunately, the pseudo-code kept me from disassembling the program when it crashed, which made it more difficult to debug.  I finally developed a debugging technique by single stepping through their interpreter, six instructions at a time to get to the instructions that were doing the work,  but it was pretty painful.  I was determined to slog through it because of the promise that I made when I visited Microsoft.
One of the last problems that I addressed before finishing the first release of Switcher in March 1985 had to do with applications hanging.  If you're running multiple applications, you don't want one application hanging to take down all of them.   I added a feature that allowed the user to kill the current application if it was hung up, by monitoring for a specific key combination during the vertical blanking interrupt handler.
I knew that I had to pick a very rare key combination, because you didn't want users killing their applications accidentally.  I decided on shift-command-option-period, four keys held down at once, which I thought would be pretty hard to stumble into accidentally.   But I was surprised when I got a call from Jeff Harbers at Microsoft.
"Hey, I like that abort feature that you just added, but you're going to have to change the key combination, because we're using that in Microsoft Word.", Jeff told me.  Microsoft Word was very complex, and it possessed an enormous range of keyboard shortcuts, way too many, as far as I was concerned.
"OK, suggest something for me to change it to and I'll consider it," I told Jeff.
Jeff didn't have anything specific in mind, so he told me that he would get back to me soon.  I had to laugh when he called me back the next day, and told me that he wanted to withdraw his request and that I should keep shift-command-option-period as the abort sequence.
"OK, that sounds good to me, " I told him.  "But why the change?  Doesn't it still conflict with Word?"
"We'll change Word in the next release not to use it.  The problem was that we couldn't find a safe sequence - I guess we're already using every key combination!"
I officially released the finished version of Switcher in April 1985, and I maintained it for a few versions after that.  Eventually, I handed it off to Phil Goldman, a tremendous young programmer from Princeton who was recently hired at Apple, who went on to write MultiFinder (with Erich Ringewald), Switcher's eventual successor, in 1987.

Brave Search API now accepts agentic payments via the x402 protocol
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
03:08:08 UTC
12:08:08 KST

The Brave Search API now accepts agentic payments via the x402 protocol
The Brave Search API, our developer tool for building applications with data from the Web, is adding support for instant, pay-as-you-go, agentic payments enabled by the open x402 payment protocol. Developers and autonomous AI agents that specifically require agentic search can now programmatically query Brave’s independent search index, ground agent responses, and settle requests in real time, which eliminates the need for traditional API key management.
Integrating x402 with the Brave Search API makes it easier for an agentic harness to leverage Brave Search API via a CLI wallet, as Brave runs its own facilitator accepting USDC on the Base blockchain, with acceptance on other networks and with other stablecoins incoming soon. This allows a Brave Search API user the ability to pay for LLM Context (agentic grounding) or general web search on-demand, without first creating an account and selecting a plan. Instead, simply transfer USDC stablecoin to your agent’s wallet via the Base network and it’s ready to access the same high-quality search results powered by Brave’s search engine.
How does it work?
Brave Search API endpoints behind x402 require payment on every request rather than authentication. A request sent without payment receives an HTTP 402 Payment Required response that states the price of that search and the payment details needed to pay it. The agent signs a USDC payment for that amount and retries the request with the signed payment attached. Once the payment is verified, the search runs and results are returned with a receipt of the transaction that occurred on the Base blockchain.
Get started today
Pricing follows Brave’s published API rates: LLM Context, an API designed specifically for providing search context for agents, will cost $0.005 ($5 per 1,000). Additionally, web (search for humans), news, image, video, and local endpoints will also be available for $0.005 ($5 per 1,000). Gas fees for transacting on the Base blockchain will be covered for a limited time. Since each request carries its own payment, a Brave Search API account is no longer needed to use the service.
The easiest path to query via x402 is to install Purl Wallet, a Stripe-developed CLI wallet for your agent. Set up your wallet, top it off using Base-native USDC, and start querying today.
Ready to build pay-as-you-go search into your apps or AI agents? Check out our Brave Search x402 Proxy Documentation and explore: https://api-dashboard.search.brave.com/documentation/guides/agent-payments

====================================================================================================

Nvidia employees petition to bring back Jensen Huang
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
02:16:24 UTC
11:16:24 KST

Some Nvidia employees are calling for Jensen Huang’s return even after his multiple reported extramarital affairs and abrupt resignation, according to internal correspondence reviewed by WIRED.
An internal “suggestion box” post submitted before Huang’s resignation by a longtime engineer at Nvidia urged the company’s board to take employee sentiment into consideration prior to taking any action. The submission has attracted more than 1,600 comments since then, with several asking board members to find a way to bring back Huang.
“If you believe in the mission, bring back THE boss,” wrote one anonymous commenter.
“We are all humans and can make mistakes. Jensen did so many good things and he needs a second chance,” wrote another.
One employee went as far as sending a companywide email demanding transparency into Huang’s exit. “I stand by Jensen,” the staffer wrote. “Not because I believe any person is infallible, but because I refuse to believe that standing for fairness must mean standing at a distance from someone whose leadership has meant so much to us.”
The employee couldn’t be reached for comment, while the engineer behind the suggestion box post declined to comment.
Nvidia and its new board chair, investor Harvey Jones, didn’t immediately respond to requests for comment.
Huang, a 63-year-old who had led Nvidia since its founding in 1993, wrote in an email to employees on Sunday that he had been “unfaithful to [his] wife” and that there was “no excuse.” The acknowledgement followed reports of an affair with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset. The board announced Huang’s resignation on Thursday morning, following reports of an alleged second affair with a direct report. (This employee has not commented on the matter.) Since then, Reuters reported on an alleged affair with yet another Nvidia employee. Huang hasn’t commented about these reports since his Sunday email.
Nvidia’s board is largely made up of early Nvidia investors and Huang’s longtime confidants. It named Nvidia chief financial officer Colette Kress interim CEO and appointed former Synopsys CEO Harvey Jones as chair of the board. The crisis has cost the company $1 trillion in market value since the allegations surfaced, prompted investigations from US lawmakers and the FBI, and raised the prospect of new export controls on chips.
It’s been business as usual inside the company throughout this week, but Huang’s resignation left many employees stunned. In the hours after, about two dozen employees posted on LinkedIn in support of Huang, with one describing their faith in his leadership as unwavering. Over the same period, WIRED couldn’t identify any posts from Nvidia employees in support of his resignation.
One employee who has been at Nvidia for a couple of years and wasn’t authorized to speak publicly says they perceive the desire for Huang’s reinstatement as widespread. They worry the company will eventually suffer without its founder at the helm and say that the board must bring him back. “Nvidia is Nvidia because of Jensen,” the employee tells WIRED.
Many employees had expected a board investigation to conclude before any big changes would occur. For instance, the suggestion-box post called on the board to share its findings with employees and formally solicit their sentiment “about Jensen’s continuing as our CEO.”
Instead, Huang left just as the inquiry had begun. Staffers are seeking to better understand why. “If America fails the man who helped build its future,” the employee in the companywide email stated, “then this world does not deserve Jensen Huang.”

NYC Council passes ban on 'surveillance pricing'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
02:04:39 UTC
11:04:39 KST

The City Council passed a bill Thursday that bans companies in New York City from using surveillance pricing, which charges different prices based on algorithms or shoppers’ personal data.
Surveillance pricing means shoppers see a price based on their personal data like demographics, credit, browsing history or purchasing history. The bill prohibits surveillance pricing both online and in stores, although the practice is mainly used on the web.
Speaker Julie Menin described surveillance pricing as an “insidious and predatory corporate practice” that erodes privacy and fairness.
“It's a practice that worsens the affordability crisis, and today it's a practice that we are going to be ending,” she said during a press conference ahead of the vote.
In one investigation cited by the Council, Target shoppers saw a jump in pricing based on location data showing whether they were inside or outside a store. Shoppers found that a 55-inch SmartTV cost $499 on the Target app when inside a store, and $599 when in the store parking lot.
Uber, Lyft, Target, Instacart and airlines are among the companies that have faced scrutiny for alleged surveillance pricing. Many of the companies have denied using the practice.
Under the bill, the city would be able to issue fines of up to $1,000 for each transaction that violates the ban.
Menin’s proposal follows legislation approved by Albany lawmakers in June that prohibits surveillance pricing online and in stores, with some exceptions.
Mayor Zohran Mamdani said in a statement that he supports the ban. He called surveillance pricing “just another way corporations are finding to squeeze working people.”
“New Yorkers’ personal data should never be used as a weapon against their own pocketbooks,” he added.
Business groups have warned that the ban could hinder discounts or loyalty programs for customers.
“If enacted, this law will make it harder for small restaurants with limited marketing budgets to reach customers, putting them at a disadvantage against national chains,” said Josh Gold, a spokesperson for Uber.
The company operates Uber Eats, which allows restaurants to offer discounts based on search history and previous orders.
Menin said the legislation would not affect discounts.

The Verge’s 2026 gift guide for dads
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
01:52:55 UTC
10:52:55 KST

Gone are the days when you could just buy your father a random tie or some leather-clad alcohol vessel or a shaving kit. Dads want — nay, deserve — something memorable. Luckily for you, it’s not that hard to find something special that he’ll approve of, and we’re here to guide you.
The Verge’s 2026 gift guide for dads
This year, why not get Dad a gift or two he won’t expect?
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
We’ve curated this year’s gift list with the modern father in mind, whether he’s a gamer, creator, chef, or simply a gadget lover. There’s a lot in here we think he’ll like, from noise-canceling headphones like the Sonos Ace Ultra and an autonomous drone to a brilliant QD-OLED monitor and a nifty light that’s perfect for illuminating his next campsite. Whatever your dad is into, we’ve got you covered.
Antigravity’s A1 is the ultimate gadget for the dad who dreams of owning the sky. The compact drone comes with specialized goggles that will make him feel like he’s in the cockpit, and it records always-on, 360-degree 8K video. It’s an exhilarating flight experience and an excellent creator’s tool in one slick package.
Dyson PencilVac Fluffycones
Cleaning gadgets like Dyson’s PencilVac FluffyCones are never cheap, but they’re often worth the cost and a pleasure to use. The lightweight, broom-replacing stick vacuum doesn’t do carpets, but its rolling bars pack incredible cleaning power for touching up hard floors, including stairs.
For those who love travel but hate the constraints of a hotel TV, Belkin’s ConnectAir is a slick solution. Plug the transmitter into any device with a USB-C video-out port and the receiver into the TV’s HDMI port, and voilà, you’re easily (and wirelessly) connected.
Sonos Ace Ultra
Sonos raised its headphones game on all fronts with the Ace Ultra, which deliver better noise canceling, improved sound, and the feature we’ve all been waiting for: Wi-Fi support. With the ability to bounce audio across your Sonos ecosystem, including from a TV via newer Sonos soundbars, Dad’s sonic sanctuary is finally complete.
Werewolf’s affordable dongle kit provides an almost magical way to power nearly any DC device from a USB-C charger or portable battery. Program it from a mobile app, connect it to your old gear, and everything from a PC monitor to an original Game Boy can accept USB-C charging.
Who would have thought a simple hook could make roaming the city with a backpack so much more convenient? After testing Peak Design’s well-designed bags, we’re convinced the clever addition is a game-changer for anyone who doesn’t want to leave their bag on the filthy ground.
WiiM’s first soundbar builds on its seamless Sonos-esque connectivity with a sleek design, detailed sound, and up-firing speakers for Dolby Atmos spatial audio. It also pairs with other speakers for surround sound through a no-fuss setup process, including ones you already own that are linked to one of WiiM’s amplifiers.
Alienware AW3426DW QD-OLED Gaming Monitor
Your dad needs an awesome monitor, but there are 10 billion options. Alienware’s 34-inch Penta Tandem QD-OLED delivers top-end picture quality, with vibrant colors and perfect blacks, plus a swift 280Hz refresh rate. Best of all, it’s not nearly as expensive as you might think.
If your dad is still reading books on his phone (or aging e-reader), it’s time for an upgrade. The latest in Amazon’s series of waterproof, auto-dimming e-readers loads books faster than the prior model and offers a slick aluminum upgrade. No matter which you choose, it’s a better way to bring the library along.
Sharge Disk Pro 2 SSD / HDMI dock
Give your father a little nostalgia and a lot of convenience in one package with this stylish USB-C dock and SSD combo gadget. The cassette-shaped Sharge Disk Pro 2 works as a hub with support for HDMI at 4K, SD and microSD card slots, USB-A and USB-C ports, along with up to 8TB of storage stored within.
Smart TVs are often pretty dumb. If your dad is always complaining about his TV, or if you want to get ahead of it, grab the latest Apple TV 4K. Although it’s not cheap, it stands out with built-in mics for Siri AI, lots of power, HomePod surround sound support, and a simple, ad-free interface.
The Switch 2 leverages the same ideas as its predecessor, only with a larger, 7.9-inch display, a faster chip, and a bevy of new social features. However, its most important trait is that it’s the newest Nintendo console, which means access to a host of weird, beautiful, family-friendly games that only Nintendo could dream up, now on a better screen.
Oura Ring 5
Oura didn’t reinvent the wheel with the Ring 5, but it did reshape it into a slimmer, thinner, more wearable design that could make all the difference. Inside are the same extensive health and fitness tracking features for charting everything from sleep to heart health, backed by long battery life.
The CS-Pro will make your dad feel like a private eye in the early ’80s. The retro-inspired digital camera serves up 16-megapixel stills and offers four filters accessible from its tactile dial, plus USB-C charging, a high-quality Xenon flash, and a 4GB SD card for plenty of vintage photo fun.
BougeRV T1 Portable Telescopic Camping Light
BougeRV’s fancy, compact lamp will spark joy for the glamper in your life, as well as everyone else. Its powerful, foldable spread of LEDs provides a blast of light anywhere plug-ins can’t reach, from the beach to the shed where that scary spider holds court, with up to 60 hours of light on low power.
The Rival Pro offers plenty of thoughtful extras, including swappable batteries and an ultra-wideband base station for a seamless, low-latency connection. The gaming mouse’s lightweight design and 8,000Hz polling rate also deliver both accurate and ergonomic performance for up to 16 hours per charge.
It’s time to help Dad ditch his collection of plastic reusable cups, which have clearly seen better days. NotNeutral’s hand-pressed, timeless tumbler offers ’70s vibes with a modern twist, making it the perfect vessel for whatever Dad twists into his favorite drink.
Give the gift that fixes past home improvement transgressions. These handy Japanese-made pliers from Vampire Tools have one fundamental purpose — to remove stripped screws — and they’re damn good at it. The best part? Watching Dad’s face light up seconds before he sprints to the garage for them.
One Piece Straw Hat Pirates Hockey Jersey
For the manga or live-action Netflix show lover on your list, this fashionable Pirates jersey, inspired by Monkey D. Luffy from the seminal pirate adventure series One Piece, will be as surprising as it is delightful.
If you’re after a cheap, useful stocking stuffer, Trond’s reliable three-prong extension cord is a practical pick that’s available in leftward or rightward configurations to fit nearly any outlet. Dad will probably say, “Oh, I’ve always wanted one of these,” and this time he’ll mean it.
Star Wars Zero Company
Zero Company, a strategy game set in the twilight of the Clone Wars, is a fun way to get lost in the Star Wars universe. The single-player, turn-based title lets players command a diverse team of characters across a gorgeous expanse of worlds, offering hours of solitary entertainment.
If your father already owns an Apple Watch, the Nomad Stratos is a foolproof upgrade idea. The refined watch band features titanium links with a rubbery interior for durable comfort, and it comes in an array of subtle color options that make it as suitable for the office as it is for the slopes.
Apple Crossbody Strap
We fell for Apple’s handy iPhone strap due to its effortless accessibility and comfy design, which uses flexible magnets to keep excess segments in check. From biking to hiking to concerts, it’s a great way to sling your iPhone 18 Pro, assuming you have a compatible phone case.
The new Fitbit Air provides the same great fitness tracking we’ve come to expect in a comfortable design that virtually disappears on your wrist. Google’s AI Coach can be overly assertive, but the tracker’s great battery life and metrics for everything from sleep to blood oxygen make it an excellent gift for anyone who needs a healthy hand.
Most of us have thousands of photos in the cloud, but if a photo never makes it into the real world, did it really happen? Existentialism aside, the Cricut StickerPix offers a new way to play with photos, providing you with everything you need to turn any picture into a pro-quality print or sticker.
SwitchBot Standing Circulator Fan
This battery-powered fan is a big upgrade over your average plug-in job. As a 3D circulator, it can tilt to push air in all directions. It can be controlled from an app and even detaches from its stand to sit on a desk or tabletop. Quiet, powerful, and versatile, it’s equipped for nearly any adventure on or off the grid.
Have you broken something fragile that seems impossible to mend? Maybe all you need is a Kintsugi Repair Kit, which provides the Japanese method and a special lacquer to repair and restore broken ceramics to good health. The resulting golden scar is merely to remind us that rebuilding is possible.
If your favorite cinephile is looking to get off the streaming carousel, the Kaleidescape Strato E is the key. The premium player lets library builders locally store and play their favorite films and TV shows in lossless 4K resolution with Dolby Atmos for sterling entertainment that’s all theirs.
Once The Great British Baking Show becomes an obsession, only a serious stand mixer will do for replicating recipes at home. This mixer’s timeless design adds new features in what KitchenAid calls its biggest upgrade since 1955, with a new half-speed setting and a control lever that lets you set speeds between its standard settings.
Instruo Pocket Scion Bio-Feedback Instrument
The Pocket Scion is unlike any musical instrument you’ve witnessed before, one that uses the “biofeedback” from plants to trigger built-in synths. The device offers MIDI-out to send digital signals to outboard synths, and it includes an app for both macOS and Windows, finally giving you a way to make your favorite fern a rockstar.
When it comes to jotting down notes, there’s nothing like a pen and paper. At least, that’s what we thought before we tried the Remarkable Paper Pure. Offering a strikingly realistic writing experience that saves everything in the digital realm, this tablet is a brilliant way to bridge the gap and save paper.
Fairphone 6 Plus
Electronics are becoming more sustainable, and Fairphone is leading the way. The Fairphone 6 Plus may seem like a regular Android phone on paper, but the ability to repair and replace its internals, from the screen to the battery, makes it anything but average.

Trump Had Urged Nvidia’s Jensen Huang to Fight for His Job
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
01:43:57 UTC
10:43:57 KST

Supported by
Trump Had Urged Nvidia’s Jensen Huang to Fight for His Job
The president called the Nvidia chief on Tuesday, continuing a pattern of supporting him even as the spy scandal was intensifying.
President Trump called Jensen Huang on Tuesday, before the Nvidia chief stepped down amid a rapidly unfolding spy scandal, and urged him to keep fighting for his job, according to two people briefed on the conversation.
The call was the latest instance of Mr. Trump defending and protecting Mr. Huang, who had become a high-profile ally and an influential adviser on artificial intelligence. The president continued to back him to the end, even after U.S. intelligence agencies had concluded that Mr. Huang was having an affair with a woman believed to be a Chinese spy.
Nvidia announced on Thursday that Mr. Huang, who built the company into the world’s leading supplier of advanced A.I. chips, had been replaced as chief executive following revelations not just of the affair with the woman suspected of being a spy, but also another with one of his employees.
A representative for Mr. Huang did not provide a comment for this article. Neither Mr. Trump nor the White House responded to requests for comment about his call to Mr. Huang.
The president had been warned at least twice about Mr. Huang’s relationship with a Chinese citizen known as Alice Huang, whom intelligence agencies concluded had been recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security, The New York Times has reported. The first warning was late last year, and the second was in the spring.
Mr. Trump took no action either time, and brought Mr. Huang with him on a trip to China in May. In recent days, he has publicly praised Mr. Huang and expressed sympathy for him, saying in a social media post on Thursday that he was a “legend.”

My favorite recipe app just got a big upgrade — and a subscription fee
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
01:41:27 UTC
10:41:27 KST

Nearly 10 years after the launch of Paprika 3, the recipe-managing, pantry-organizing, grocery list-making app has gotten a big upgrade that adds more functionality to nearly every corner of the experience. I tested Paprika 4 for several weeks while it was in open beta, and the update recently launched across Android (the version I’ve been using), iOS, Windows, and macOS.
My favorite recipe app just got a big upgrade — and a subscription fee
The subscription fee for newly launched Paprika 4 is well worth the price. And the free version is better, too.
Where Paprika 3 was a one-time purchase per platform, accessing most of the new features in Paprika 4 requires an annual subscription fee of $14.99 (it can also be gifted). With the subscription, you can access the app on any supported platform. New users, as well as those who bought Paprika 3 (or previous versions) but don’t want to pay for the subscription, will be moved to a free version of Paprika 4, which comes with a smaller selection of features that were available to test in beta, plus the ability to save up to 50 recipes.
Together, the new features locked behind the paywall make it possible to import recipes from social media and from physical cookbooks, scan product barcodes to organize your pantry or shopping list, and connect with other cooks via the app to share notes and recipes. They’re good, for the most part, and makes the subscription feel worth it. But the free version is upgraded in ways that make it worth trying if you’re hearing about Paprika for the first time.
My favorite of the new subscription-exclusive features solves a conundrum I often find myself in: My wife sends me a lot of recipe videos on Instagram. I often take screenshots of the text to piece the recipe together. Paprika 4 can pull the pertinent info from those screenshots and build a recipe — that is, if there’s a complete enough recipe in the text to autofill in Paprika’s recipe fields.
As an alternative to feeding the app a screenshot, you can send a social media post from TikTok, Instagram, YouTube, or Facebook to Paprika 4 straight from the share sheet, and it’ll scrape the caption and convert it in a few seconds. It won’t pull info from a video (which would be convenient — and controversial), and it’ll only work if there’s caption text in the photo or video post available to scrape. It sounds limited, but it’s a nice improvement, and it keeps the link to the social media post in the recipe so I can easily go back and view it as needed.
A lot of social media accounts hide their recipes from people scrolling by, requiring an extra step like “comment with TASTYYUMYUM to get my free recipe.” I respect the hustle, even if I hate seeing this as I’m searching for recipes. Paprika unsurprisingly stumbles when recipe posts are too thin on parseable info. But for ones that have enough, it’s really easy to get them into the app.
Paprika 4 also lets you import recipes with your phone camera. I’ve used it to take pictures from cookbooks that we own, and the app converts them accurately. On the free version, you’re limited to copy-pasting website URLs into the app’s browser and having it convert them into recipes that way. It works really well, but feels rudimentary by comparison.
The updated app is stuffed with other new features, several of which are a tad superfluous (and less useful to me) but still seem good to offer when you’re hoping to attract subscribers. For instance, you can compile your recipes into your own digital cookbook to share with other users (this feature is in the free version, too) and set up family sharing — even DMing links within the app to other Paprika users. Family sub-accounts can be added to your account for no extra cost beyond the subscription, so long as they haven’t made a Paprika account with the same email address.
Paprika 4 can guess the nutrition facts of your recipes, which is nice if you have fitness goals or just generally like to know what kind of heat your food is packing, so to speak. I wouldn’t take the results to be totally accurate, but it seems capable enough. This feature is locked behind the subscription paywall.
Another feature I could see myself using more of is the expanded pantry tool. Paprika 3 had a single “pantry,” but the paid and free versions of Paprika 4 let you specify where items are stored, such as in a cupboard, fridge, freezer, and anywhere else. I was also excited about the new barcode scanning feature, a premium feature that can quickly and easily add items to your pantry. While it didn’t work for me in beta, the scanning function works well in the current version. It recognized items that I frequently purchase at Trader Joe’s, Whole Foods, and a bodega. It adds what they are in simple terms (i.e., olive oil spray instead of “Trader Joe’s Organic Extra Virgin Olive Oil Spray”).
The Cook Mode is neat, and is available on both the free and paid versions of Paprika 4. When activated through a drop-down menu, the app reads the first step of the recipe out loud, waiting for you to chime in before continuing. You can ask it to repeat a step, go to the next one, double or halve the recipe, set timers, and strike steps or ingredients as you complete them, among other preset commands and questions. In this mode, the mic stays on by default, though the app won’t respond to unrelated chatter. If asking a prompt doesn’t get it to do anything, you can use the wake word “paprika” to reengage it. This feature has obvious accessibility benefits.
There’s extensive Alexa integration, too, if you pay the subscription fee. It gives Alexa access to your recipes, grocery list, pantry, and meal plan. You can manage grocery lists and pantries by voice with Echo speakers, and screen-equipped Echo devices like the Echo Show can display recipes and support hands-free cooking. The iOS version of Paprika 4 also adds widgets and an array of shortcuts for iOS and macOS, like adding items to a grocery list or pantry, showing scheduled meals in your meal plan, and more. These don’t require a subscription.
For people who are serious about staying organized in the kitchen — both in terms of their pantries and catalog of recipes — these paywalled features will likely get enough use each week to be worth paying for. Though, if you haven’t used Paprika before, the free version is a great place to start.
Even as someone who was content with the features in Paprika 3, I can see the value in paying for the subscription-exclusive features. During the beta, I landed on $2 being a figure I’d be comfortable paying each month for them, and Hindsight Labs settled on just $1.25 per month. It’s going to be getting a lot more money per user than it ever has, but like most Paprika users, I would have purchased the app multiple times over due to how useful it is for me. I guess I get to do that now.

Whoop Launches a Social Network Where Every User Has a 'Verified Heartbeat'
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
01:40:00 UTC
10:40:00 KST

Whoop Launches a Social Network Where Every User Has a 'Verified Heartbeat' (theverge.com) 34
			
		 	
				Whoop has launched Humans, a social network built into its fitness app where every account is a real person with a "verified heartbeat." The wearable company claims its platform is "entirely human," unlike other social media platforms that are "saturated with artificial intelligence, automated accounts and bots, and manufactured content." The Verge reports:  Starting Thursday, Whoop members can share their data from its wearables on the app's new Whoop Humans social network. Users can make their metrics, activities, and achievements visible to other Whoop wearers, post Story-like "Moments" with photos and captions, and add comments and reactions on other members' posts. [...]
In the Community tab in Whoop's app, users can scroll through Moments from people they're following, like tapping through your Stories feed on Instagram but with your friends' fitness tracker data. In one of Whoop's examples, swiping up on a user's Moments post with a photo from their run shows a map with their route and details like their pace and distance. The Community tab also shows cards for people in your "community" with their sleep, recovery, and strain scores.
Whoop says users choose which activities, achievements, and metrics they want to share on Humans, including Recovery, Strain, Sleep, HRV, and resting heart rate. Any activities marked as sensitive aren't shared by default. There's also an option to keep Moments posts private and a "Stealth Mode" for temporarily hiding health and performance data without changing your sharing settings.

Donald Trump really wants Tim Cook to run Nvidia
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
00:46:07 UTC
09:46:07 KST

Jensen Huang resigned this morning as Nvidia’s CEO following reports of affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. Right now, the company is being led by its CFO on an interim basis. But President Donald Trump is already throwing around one person he thinks should take over the chipmaking giant: former Apple CEO Tim Cook, who stepped down from leading the company on September 1st and moved into an executive chairman role.
Donald Trump really wants Tim Cook to run Nvidia
Nvidia is looking for a new CEO, and President Trump says the former Apple CEO would be ‘a great candidate.’
Shortly after Huang’s resignation was announced today, Trump posted his idea about Cook on Truth Social: “NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple,” Trump said. “A Legend replaces a Legend!”
In a new episode of Stephen A. Smith’s Straight Shooter podcast published today, Trump made his pitch for Cook again, saying that Apple “would never be what it is today” if Steve Jobs was still “around”:
Can [Huang] be replaced?
Well, you know, the expression, Anybody can be replaced? So it’s very hard to replace him, but I had an idea this morning that I sort of just released: Tim Cook. He just sort of semi-retired from Apple. He’s done one of the greatest jobs in the history of business.
I think if Tim Cook, who built Apple… If Steve Jobs were around, Apple would never be what it is today. Tim Cook took a lot of these brilliant ideas and designs and everything else from Jobs and others, and he made it into one of the great companies of the world in a fairly short period. I think Tim Cook would be a great candidate to run Nvidia.
Apple and Nvidia didn’t immediately reply to a request for comment. Cook is well-known for his ongoing relationship with President Trump, including gifting the president a 24k gold and glass statue last year. (Trump, after Cook announced his transition plan earlier this year, posted a story about having “the head of Apple calling to ‘kiss my ass.’”)
During the podcast, Trump said he spoke to Huang yesterday and that Huang is “going through hell”:
Should [Huang] have stepped down?
Well, the big thing is, was the woman a spy, and I don’t know if that’s been determined yet, but if the woman was a spy, yes, if the woman was not a spy, perhaps it would be different.
But I can tell you this. Somehow, he’ll end up on his feet. I mean, I know him, I know him pretty well. I always thought of him as a fine man.
I spoke to him yesterday. He’s going through hell. What he feels most badly about is his wife and family. That’s what he is, really… that’s what he told me. I’m president, and he called me. And he said, What I’ve done to my wife and family is... That’s what he talked about.
Trump, on the podcast, called Huang a “tremendous talent.” Other tech leaders have also publicly supported Huang following his resignation.

====================================================================================================

Chicago schools to Google – we'd rather lose Search than be forced to accept AI
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
00:38:41 UTC
09:38:41 KST

Ripple effect: Not long after Google announced its definitely-not-petulant response to schools banning its AI services by threatening to remove its search engine completely, Chicago Public Schools has said it will simply use a different search solution. Google's actions have also led to protests against the company, with demonstrators chanting the catchy slogan, "Hey Google, nice try. We don't want your Gemini."
At the start of November, Chris Phillips, Google's vice president and general manager for education, said K-12 schools will soon have to accept the company's AI services to retain student access to its search engine. The reason, he claimed, was that Search and AI are now deeply integrated services. Instead of the usual results, students will see a message from their school stating that the services are not available.
A lot of people didn't take this well, claiming Google was essentially blackmailing schools into using a service they didn't want. Chalkbeat Chicago reports that it led to protests in Cambridge, Massachusetts, and in Chicago, where the city's school district recently blocked student access to generative AI tools such as Gemini.
Chicago Public Schools (CPS) has yet to respond to Google's announcement, but a CPS spokesperson said in a statement that the district was "actively exploring all options to independently suppress" the AI Overviews that appears at the top of Google searches. The spokesperson added that if CPS cannot provide students access to Google's search engine without its AI feature, the district will "look to other search solutions."
It's not just CPS that's unhappy about Google's behavior. About 30 protesters, including parents, educators, and students, demonstrated outside Google's office in Chicago's West Loop.
"We are owed lessons and feedback from the people CPS hired, people who are passionate and dedicated to making learning fun," said Reese Mackey, a junior at Kenwood Academy High School. "Not by a bot whose only job is to be a yes man and compile random sources from the internet for a nonsensical answer."
A Google spokesperson said the company was working to improve Search for classroom use, including testing its "Guided Learning" feature, which uses AI to guide students through problems one step at a time instead of simply giving them answers.
Chicago is far from alone in pushing back. In October, Frederick County's school board voted 6 – 1 to restrict student AI use from pre-K through 12th grade, just weeks after allowing older students to try Gemini with teacher permission. Board members raised concerns about the lack of warning to parents and teachers' inability to disable the tool on student devices. Exceptions remain for lessons about AI and accommodations for students with disabilities.
New York City also introduced a one-year moratorium on student-facing generative AI from preschool through eighth grade for the 2026 – 2027 school year. High school use is limited to approved tools in supervised pilot or career-readiness programs, while Los Angeles has temporarily blocked generative AI tools on student devices across all grades.
Google faces an uphill battle to convince schools that using AI is the way forward for students, but threatening to remove search from those who say no is hardly going to help convince them otherwise.
