====================================================================================================

Claude’s Duet agent is a little neurotic and surprisingly helpful
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-13
00:23:32 UTC
09:23:32 KST

Normally, the products I test don’t offer to help me write up my experience with them. Duet is different.
Claude’s Duet agent is a little neurotic and surprisingly helpful
Duet is a decent helper, if a little jumpy at times.
Claude Duet is Anthropic’s AI agent for consumers, the latest attempt by an AI company to try to convince us that agents can be useful for people who don’t write code. It’s been an interesting couple of months: Instinct gave us an impressive text-based assistant, Meta offered us its cute little guy, Muse, to help us buy things, and OpenAI gave us the more work-oriented Dots. Claude Duet is even more focused on work than those. It’s also more consumer-friendly than Claude Cowork, which was more consumer-friendly than Claude Code — the coding agent that kicked off a whole hype cycle earlier this year. I’ve been testing it over the past week as I navigate a busy travel and work schedule. At turns it has been truly useful, kind of a nag, and very eager to write about itself.
Duet is still a “research preview” according to Anthropic, and access is limited to Pro, Max, and Ultra subscribers in the US, with a waitlist to join. It’s not here to order you burritos, either; it’s for “professionals and small business owners” and is meant to use its judgment about what needs your attention most.
Like Meta’s Muse, Duet has its own app. (If you’re keeping score at home, yes, it has been updated to fully support the iPhone Duo). Duet uses Claude’s characteristically cheery, minimalist design. The app is consumer-friendly and approachable. Once you’ve connected all the productivity and chat services you want, it’ll look for ways to help out with and suggest them as cards you can swipe left or right to hide or accept — kind of a to-do list Tinder.
In the name of testing, I went all in on Duet and gave it everything: my work email, calendar, Google Docs, and Slack. This gave it plenty of grist for the mill in more ways than I’d even realized at first. Because our biweekly staff meeting is automatically summarized in an email from Gemini, and Duet had access to my email, it was able to connect the dots between something said in a meeting and a direct message in Slack. Duet also worked out who my boss is because we have a shared Google Doc for our weekly 1:1 meetings. AI is at its most useful when it can suck up lots of data, and Duet wasted no time doing so.
Duet has started each morning by greeting me with a brief message and some quick highlights for my day, like reminding me to pack an item required for a video shoot before my flight. Below that, it would suggest to-dos, usually ending in an offer to help draft a message or add an item to an agenda.
This has often been helpful, though it sometimes felt like interacting with a slightly neurotic personal assistant. In one instance, I’d emailed someone I interviewed for a story that the article was likely to go up on Tuesday and that I’d send a link when it did. I submitted my draft to my editors late at night on Monday, and Tuesday morning Duet was beside itself. It wasn’t quite 9AM yet when it pointed out that the story was still waiting for an edit and I needed to get on that — or else I wouldn’t be able to send the link I promised. The concern was a bit misplaced. And it didn’t pick up on the nuance in my message implying that this was not a hard deadline, nor the fact that it was only 7AM local time for my editor.
This sometimes felt like interacting with a slightly neurotic personal assistant
In other cases, it was surprisingly helpful. In planning out the next few weeks I’d missed the fact that upcoming time off would only leave me with a couple of working days before another big draft was due. Duet proposed a solution, offered to update the Google Doc with weekly notes for my editor, and drafted a Slack message for him noting the update. Reader, the message was a little too good. It copied my millennial habit of writing in all lowercase — which it pointed out in a slightly self-congratulatory aside — and used an abbreviation we often use to refer to our CMS. But it also shortened the word “signature” to “sig,” which I never would and made me laugh out loud. In any case, there’s no fooling anyone; the Slack message was tagged with a “sent with Claude” label.
Duet often volunteered to help start my drafts, which is always a hard no for me. I’ll type up my own little blogs, thank you very much Claude. If you’re not a writer, I can see how taking care of little writing chores here and there would be helpful. But this started feeling like a Spider-Man-pointing-at-Spider-Man moment when the task it wanted to help with was reviewing itself.
Maybe that’s how CEOs live
It was a little uncanny, too. Even setting aside the fact that I was writing about Duet, it’s kind of weird knowing that it can read every email, draft, and Slack I send. It can read all of that because I opted in to sharing that data, and I knew full well what I was doing. But it’s another thing to see it in practice. I wonder if it would change how I work if I knew I didn’t really need to take responsibility for putting things on my calendar, writing down a to-do list, or paying attention in a meeting because Duet will just handle it. Maybe that’s how CEOs live. Not that I cherish creating calendar entries, but I wonder if I would end up feeling a bit disconnected from my workplace.
I don’t think Duet or any other AI agent is quite ready to make us feel like we’re all the CEOs of our own little worlds. There’s a key word in the title “personal assistant”: personal. A person can pick up on nuances in tone to understand what’s a big deal and what isn’t. A person will pick up on communication norms and deploy them sensibly. A person can be tactful. Anthropic’s blog post mentions that the company is continuing to “test its judgment about when to message you,” so it’s possible Duet will calibrate a little better over time. But AI overall isn’t good at understanding nuance or tone, and it’s not clear if it ever will be.
For me, Duet made it a little more clear what I want AI to do for me and what I don’t. Communication happens all over the place in a modern workplace — in emails, DMs, meetings, shared docs. Having AI find discrepancies that have been overlooked between all of these sources and point them out proactively is really helpful. I like it as a backstop for my own organizational systems, not as a replacement for them. I don’t like it in the creative process. Like, at all. I want to find “my own ways into and around a topic,” as Hank Green said of his own reliance on AI. If you send a robot to do the legwork, you miss the opportunity to stumble across something unexpected. It’s part of what makes the work enjoyable. And I’m certainly not about to let an agent review itself for me. You hear that, Duet?

Adding RSA Support to swift-nio-ssh
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:44:19 UTC
08:44:19 KST

LogRaker talks SSH through swift-nio-ssh, which is a great library with one gap: it has no RSA support. That is deliberate. Upstream supports modern primitives only, and for new deployments the newer ed25519 format is the better default.
It is also not a choice you always get to make. Amazon EC2 hands out
PKCS#1 RSA .pem key pairs, and only added ed25519 key pairs
in 2021. Any key pair older than that is RSA, the instances it opens are
still running, and nothing about the situation is negotiable from the
client side. A log viewer that cannot use the key sitting in
~/.ssh/ is not useful.
So LogRaker vendored a fork. Here is what that actually took.
There is no extension point
The obvious move would be to add the key type from outside the
module. Well, you can’t. NIOSSHPrivateKey stores its key in
an internal enum:
extension NIOSSHPrivateKey {
    internal enum BackingKey {
        case ed25519(Curve25519.Signing.PrivateKey)
        case ecdsaP256(P256.Signing.PrivateKey)
        // ...
    }
}
backingKey is internal, the memberwise
init(backingKey:) is private, and the only
public initializers are one per supported algorithm. There is no
protocol to conform to and no case to add. Adding an algorithm means
editing the module, which means a new fork.
Eight files, in the end: Package.swift to pull in
_RSA.Signing from swift-crypto’s CryptoExtras
product (it is not in Crypto), five sources under
Sources/NIOSSH/, and one upstream test that had to change,
because it used ssh-rsa as its example of an algorithm
nobody recognizes.
The trap: two different strings
This is the part worth reading even if you never touch nio-ssh.
A publickey userauth request carries a public key blob
and an algorithm name. For ed25519 and the ECDSA curves those
two strings are identical, so upstream writes the key’s own tag into the
algorithm-name field and never has to think about it:
writtenBytes += self.writeSSHString(key.keyPrefix)
RSA is the one algorithm where they differ. The key blob stays tagged
ssh-rsa, but the algorithm name has to name the
signature algorithm: rsa-sha2-256 or
rsa-sha2-512, from RFC 8332. The bare ssh-rsa
name means RSA with SHA-1, which OpenSSH 8.8 disabled by default in
2021. Offer that name to any current server and it is rejected outright,
no matter how good your key is.
So the fork adds signatureAlgorithmName next to
keyPrefix:
internal var signatureAlgorithmName: String.UTF8View {
    switch self.backingKey {
    case .rsa:
        return Self.rsaSHA512SignatureAlgorithm
    case .ed25519, .ecdsaP256, .ecdsaP384, .ecdsaP521, .certified:
        return self.keyPrefix
    }
}
The subtlety is not the property. It is that the algorithm name gets written in two places, and they have to agree byte for byte:
- SSHMessages.swift, into the wire message.
- UserAuthSignablePayload.swift, into the buffer that
gets signed.
One is what you claim, the other is what you sign. If they diverge, every byte you send is individually well-formed, the key is correct, the signature is a valid RSA signature, and yet the server rejects it anyway. There is no error message that points at the mismatch, because from the server’s side it is indistinguishable from a wrong key. This is the kind of bug you find by writing out both buffers and diffing them.
A test now pins the two together, so the next person to touch either write site gets a failure instead of a mystery.
Which name to offer
The fork always offers rsa-sha2-512 and does not
negotiate. That sounds lazy and is actually forced: nio-ssh does not
parse SSH_MSG_EXT_INFO, so the server-sig-algs
list never reaches any code that could act on it. There is nothing to
negotiate against.
It is a safe default anyway. rsa-sha2-512 has been
accepted since OpenSSH 7.2, in 2016. Anything a log viewer realistically
connects to is newer than that.
One related detail, easy to miss: the fork’s list of known algorithms
has to carry the key tag and both signature algorithm names,
because that list is checked against the algorithm-name field of
incoming userauth requests and PK_OK replies. One key tag,
several legal names.
Keeping the fork honest
A vendored fork rots quietly. Three things keep this one readable:
Every edit is marked. Each hunk carries a
LOGRAKER FORK: comment, so the whole diff against upstream
is one grep:
grep -rn "LOGRAKER FORK" Sources/ Tests/ Package.swift
That is also the rebase checklist: fetch upstream, rebase onto the new tag, and the markers show you which conflicts are yours.
One test is load-bearing. The serialization test
compares the generated public-key blob byte for byte against what
ssh-keygen -y prints for the same key. Hand-rolled SSH wire
format is exactly where a plausible-looking mistake survives review, and
OpenSSH is the only opinion that counts.
Security advisories will not find you. This is the real cost of vendoring, and it has nothing to do with RSA. A local SwiftPM package never reports a new version, so no tool will ever tell you that upstream shipped a fix. You have to go and look:
gh api repos/apple/swift-nio-ssh/security-advisories \
  --jq '.[] | "\(.severity) \(.ghsa_id) \(.summary)"'
Put that somewhere you will actually run it.
Should you do this?
Probably not, if you have a choice. Forking a security-relevant library means you own its upstream fixes forever, and the honest summary of this fork is “we added an algorithm its authors chose to leave out”.
But the constraint was real: the keys exist, they are RSA, and they open machines people need to watch. The fork touches eight files against upstream 0.15.0, every edit marked, and it can be deleted the day nio-ssh adds RSA support of its own.
The fork is public, at lograker/swift-nio-ssh, Apache 2.0 like upstream. Read its README before depending on it: it exists to serve one app, it is pinned to 0.15.0, and nobody has audited it.

====================================================================================================

Whoop launched a social network where every user has a ‘verified heartbeat’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:43:22 UTC
08:43:22 KST

Starting Thursday, Whoop members can share their data from its wearables on the app’s new Whoop Humans social network. Users can make their metrics, activities, and achievements visible to other Whoop wearers, post Story-like “Moments” with photos and captions, and add comments and reactions on other members’ posts.
Whoop launched a social network where every user has a ‘verified heartbeat’
Whoop’s new Humans platform is like Instagram Stories with updates from your friends’ fitness tracker data.
Whoop claims its platform is “entirely human,” unlike other social media platforms that are “saturated with artificial intelligence, automated accounts and bots, and manufactured content.”
Whoop CEO Will Ahmed says Humans is “the most verifiably human network ever launched. Every account represents a real person with a verified heartbeat.” Whoop says its existing Teams social feature “will remain available,” allowing people to share strain, recovery, and sleep data along with group leaderboards.
In the Community tab in Whoop’s app, users can scroll through Moments from people they’re following, like tapping through your Stories feed on Instagram but with your friends’ fitness tracker data. In one of Whoop’s examples, swiping up on a user’s Moments post with a photo from their run shows a map with their route and details like their pace and distance. The Community tab also shows cards for people in your “community” with their sleep, recovery, and strain scores.
Whoop says users choose which activities, achievements, and metrics they want to share on Humans, including Recovery, Strain, Sleep, HRV, and resting heart rate. Any activities marked as sensitive aren’t shared by default. There’s also an option to keep Moments posts private and a “Stealth Mode” for temporarily hiding health and performance data without changing your sharing settings.

The Humble Bundle storefront for buying games and other software recently got a new look, but on the settings page, it no longer offers a way to add or update two-factor verification methods, as reported by Kotaku. PC Gamer also points out that there are Humble Bundle support pages discussing two-factor verification, and the writer for the publication said that they had set up two-factor verification on the old version of the site.
Humble Bundle redesign removes two-factor verification from settings
A new look for the Humble Bundle storefront is missing an important security feature that was previously there.
However, it does appear that, at least as of now, there is some form of two-factor verification. When I tried logging into my Humble Bundle account while writing this article, I was prompted to check my email for an access code before I could access my account. For what it’s worth, my passwords app also shows that I set up a rotating two-factor code for Humble Bundle at some point. I was not asked to input that at any part of my login process today.

Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:21:44 UTC
08:21:44 KST

When Apple CEO John Ternus announced the first touchscreen Mac last month, the biggest reaction in the room wasn’t for the PROLED MacBook Pro’s OLED touchscreen or the thinner and lighter chassis; it was for the illuminated lid logo. It’s a throwback to the light-up logos on the old MacBooks, which Apple started phasing out in 2015, and which were completely gone by the 2018 MacBook Air redesign. It’s a nostalgia play, but boy did it work. It’s fun!
Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB
The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.
Apple should have made the MacBook logo RGB
The PROLED MacBook Pro’s logo is lit with boring white LEDs. Missed opportunity.
Just like in the old days, the new logo’s brightness is linked to the screen’s: The brighter or dimmer the screen, the brighter or dimmer the logo. But the light-up logo on the old MacBooks was illuminated by the screen’s backlight, and an OLED display doesn’t have one. Thanks to iFixit, we now know it’s lit by four white LEDs and synced with the screen’s brightness by software. But that means Apple could have used RGB LEDs.
Red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, violet, or anything in between? On the lid of your new MacBook Pro? Why not? After all, Apple insists it’s still serious about gaming, and nothing says “serious about gaming” like RGB lighting.
I was secretly hoping users would be able to customize the illuminated lid cutout much like the Xbox logo on Microsoft’s Elite Series 2 controllers. But alas, no luck. You can turn off the illumination with some Terminal commands or take control of it with a third-party app. But when it’s lit, it’s only lit with white light. And iFixit’s teardown also showed the difficulty of accessing those LEDs (the screen delaminated and broke in the process). So we’re unlikely to see LED RGB mods for it unless someone’s brave enough to void their warranty and risk a pricey screen repair on their $3,000 laptop.
The most fun you can have with the Apple lid logo is to install a free third-party app like LogoLight and have it pulse, breathe, match the beat of any music you’re playing, or flash some Morse code. There’s some fun there, but if you combined these features with a splash of color we’d really be cooking.
MacBooks aren’t gaming laptops, so RGB lighting may seem like a mismatch, but multicolor LEDs could have been a cool Easter egg. Nobody thought Apple would put a touchscreen on a MacBook either, and the iPhone Duo and HomeView also show Apple is in a new age of experimentation during the early Ternus era. Maybe if the light-up lid logo ever comes to future MacBook Airs or a MacBook Neo, Apple could cut loose a little.

====================================================================================================

Richard Posner has died
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:19:53 UTC
08:19:53 KST

Law School Mourns the Passing of Richard A. Posner, Senior Lecturer, Law and Economics Pioneer, and Former Federal Appellate Judge
Senior Lecturer Richard Allen Posner, a longtime University of Chicago Law School scholar, former federal appellate judge, and pioneer in the field of law and economics, died on November 10. He was 87.
Posner, who joined the Law School faculty in 1969 and spent 36 years as a judge on the US Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, is widely considered to be the most influential legal scholar of all time and, during his time on the bench, was considered to be among the most powerful jurists outside the Supreme Court. He authored thousands of court opinions, hundreds of academic articles, and dozens of books, and was a leading scholar of law and economics, a field born and developed at the University of Chicago beginning in the early 20th century.
The Law School’s eminence in the field—which brings the tools of economic theory to bear on legal rules and institutions, offering the potential to improve policymaking, market efficiency, and even judicial decision making—is what drew Posner to Chicago. He quickly became a top figure, provoking debate with views about the efficiency of common law and, in 1973, authoring the field’s seminal book The Economic Analysis of Law, which is now in its ninth edition.
“There has never been a legal scholar quite as prolific, influential, and endlessly curious as Dick Posner—he was a giant whose influence on our legal system, the field of law and economics, and on our law school cannot be overstated,” said Law School Dean Adam Chilton, the Howard G. Krane Professor of Law. “As a law and economics scholar and torts professor, I doubt there has been a week that’s gone by since I started teaching at the Law School where I haven’t talked about one of Dick’s ideas or opinions. We will mourn his loss deeply, and on behalf of the entire Law School community, I extend our deepest condolences to his family.”
In 1978, Posner was named the University of Chicago’s Lee and Brena Freeman Professor of Law, a title he held until 1981, when he was appointed by President Ronald Reagan to the US Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit. He was the chief judge of the court from 1993 to 2000 and retired from the bench in 2017 having written some 3,300 opinions on a wide range of issues. As a judge, Posner was known as a tough and often unrelenting questioner and a staunch advocate for legal pragmatism. He disdained jargon-filled opinions.
Editor’s Note: Please check back soon. In the coming days, we will be posting a longer tribute to Judge Posner’s extraordinary life and career, including remembrances from his colleagues.

Why You Should Expect an AI Crash

Why You Should Expect an AI Crash

====================================================================================================

Why You Should Expect an AI Crash
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:08:31 UTC
08:08:31 KST

Why You Should Expect an AI Crash

====================================================================================================

Edge0-145B: Frontier AI Capabilities on Your Own Computer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:06:17 UTC
08:06:17 KST

Today, we're open-sourcing Edge0-145B and Edge0-120B, together with our unified inference framework and deployment tools. Edge0-145B is the world's first open-source on-device AI model with 145 billion parameters. It brings coding, reasoning, and agent capabilities that rival frontier cloud models to your own computer.
On-device AI has long forced a tradeoff between model capability and what the hardware can run. Larger models demand more memory, bandwidth, and power; smaller models can struggle with complex work. Moving that work to the cloud adds model-provider fees, data-handling constraints, and a dependence on connectivity.
This release addresses those constraints in four ways:
We're releasing the full stack so developers can reproduce the results, evaluate or fine-tune the models, and integrate them into their own products.
Get the model weights, explore the inference framework, or try the Edge0 App.
How We Make 100B+ Parameter AI Run on a Personal Computer
Edge0-145B Laptop Performance: Memory, Speed, and Power
Edge0-145B makes a 145-billion-parameter model usable on a personal laptop. A useful local model has to fit in memory, process prompts promptly, generate fast enough to use, and keep power consumption under control. It also needs to sustain longer tasks.
Our earlier Edge0-35B phone release reported peak memory below 2.5GB. On an iPhone 18 Pro Max, Edge0-35B delivers 37+ tokens/s using approximately 2.5GB of memory. Edge0-145B has roughly four times as many parameters; its laptop comparison reports 80+ tokens/s and 28GB peak memory. These measurements apply to different models and hardware.
On-device model scale over time. The chart covers 67 open models of at least 1B parameters from 14 labs, intended for phones, laptops, and AI PCs. Same-size base, instruct, and reasoning variants count once; each line tracks a lab's largest model, including releases before July 2025. Filled points mark new highs and hollow points other releases. The scale is logarithmic below 40B and linear above it. Apple is excluded because its weights are not released. Sources: vendor announcements and model cards.
Why On-Device Models Have Stayed Small
Memory limits kept most on-device models below 10B parameters before September 2026. Apple Intelligence's on-device foundation model had approximately 3B parameters, while Google's Gemini Nano came in 1.8B and 3.25B versions. Even a 7B model could consume approximately 4GB of memory.
Larger models faced a memory wall. Model weights occupy storage and must be read during generation, competing for limited memory and bandwidth with the operating system and other apps. Even an aggressively compressed 35B model could need approximately 20GB of memory, far more than a phone could make available.
Compute, bandwidth, and power created competing constraints. Larger models offered more capability, but strained memory capacity, generation speed, and battery life. Making models smaller helped them fit, often at the cost of capability.
Privacy alone could not close that gap. Apple Intelligence protected user data, but Apple acknowledged that its on-device model was not designed for agentic applications requiring general world knowledge. The prevailing assumption was that on-device intelligence could not match the cloud.
Under the Hood of Edge0: Four Layers of Model and Inference Design
We designed Edge0-145B and its inference engine together to reduce the memory and data movement that make large models difficult to run locally. The design spans architecture, training, loading, and scheduling.
Architecture: share large matrices across sparse experts. Sharing matrices substantially reduces the weights that must be stored and moved while keeping the expert count unchanged. Each group of 64 experts shares two large matrices, while each expert retains a small set of parameters that captures its differences. Previously, every expert stored a complete set of matrices. Of the model's 145B total parameters, only approximately 10B participate in computation on each inference step.
Training: build for local execution from day one. We train the model in its on-device architecture from the outset, with deployment constraints built into its design. This avoids treating local deployment as a compression or pruning step after training a cloud model.
Loading: pipeline GPU memory, system memory, and SSD access. Reading and computation overlap across three storage tiers, avoiding serial waits. Frequently used “hot experts” remain in GPU memory. An expert library of approximately 40GB sits in system memory, with experts brought into GPU memory as needed. Approximately 48GB of vocabulary data resides on the SSD and is read on demand.
Scheduling: use the CPU and GPU together. The GPU computes cached experts while the CPU handles the remaining work in parallel. Speculative decoding verifies multiple candidate tokens at once, spreading the cost of reading weights across more output tokens. These choices support the reported 80+ tokens/s decode rate.
Model and inference-engine design work together to make 100B+ parameter inference practical on personal computers. Source: internal testing.
Together, these changes let parameter counts grow from tens of billions to more than a hundred billion without proportional increases in memory use, power consumption, and latency.
What Edge0 Can Do: Anything From Chat to Complex Work on Your Computer
Edge0-145B can carry a complex task through to a usable result. Its coding and reasoning capabilities support local workflows that combine planning, tool use, execution, debugging, and visual checks. The benchmarks establish its capabilities; the demonstrations show how it puts them to work.
Coding and reasoning that rival frontier cloud models
Edge0-145B is competitive with specific frontier cloud models across coding, reasoning, knowledge, and instruction following:
- Coding: It scores 64.6 on DeepSWE 1.1, ahead of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next at 58.7 and DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 54.4.
- Reasoning and knowledge: It scores 90.4 on GPQA-Diamond, close to DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 90.8 and Claude-Opus-4.6 \(Max\) at 91.3.
- Instruction following: It scores 80.6 on IFBench, ahead of DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 79.2 and Claude-Opus-4.6 \(Max\) at 62.5.
Its 86.52 on Terminal-Bench 2.1 also exceeds Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's 83.3, DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731's 82.7, and Claude's 78.2. On AA-LCR, Edge0-145B scores 79.3 to Claude's 78.0. These comparisons apply to the named models and evaluations.
Scores are percentages; higher is better. A dash means no public score is available, rather than a score of zero.
Edge0, Qwen, DeepSeek, and Claude results come from Edge0's internal evaluations. Claude-Opus-4.6 has no public DeepSWE 1.1 score. For Flint, Muse Glimmer, and Gemma 4, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and IFBench results come from the Recursive Flint evaluation; HLE, GPQA, and AA-LCR results come from Artificial Analysis.
The largest gains over Qwen3.8-27B, a mainstream on-device model baseline, are in agent and coding work: 18.5% on Terminal-Bench and 53.1% on DeepSWE. The relative gains on HLE, GPQA, and IFBench are 14.9%, 1.3%, and 1.4%, respectively.
DeepSWE 1.1 and Terminal-Bench 2.1 evaluate agent and coding work. HLE, GPQA-Diamond, and IFBench cover reasoning, knowledge, and instruction following. The complete evaluation setup, hardware environment, and reproduction instructions are being released with our technical report.
Competitive capability with a smaller memory footprint
Edge0-145B combines a 72.8 mean score across six benchmarks with 29GB measured peak memory. Qwen3.8-Flash-Next averages 71.7, with an estimated 70GB footprint for standard 4-bit deployment. That puts Edge0-145B at about 40% of the estimated baseline memory while delivering comparable or higher capability, enabling local use on a 48GB laptop.
The means cover DeepSWE 1.1, Terminal-Bench 2.1, HLE, GPQA-Diamond, IFBench, and AA-LCR. Edge0 memory figures are measured internal peaks. Asterisked baseline figures are estimates for standard 4-bit deployment and remain to be measured. Claude is excluded because it is cloud-only and has no DeepSWE score.
[Figure: Quality versus peak memory, with 48GB laptop and 64GB workstation capacities marked. Lower memory and higher scores are better; the chart's vertical axis does not start at zero.]
Local agents that plan, code, run, and check their work
A single natural-language request can start an entire local workflow. Edge0-145B breaks down the requirements, plans the steps, writes code, and calls tools to run it. When something fails, it debugs and repairs the code. It then takes screenshots to inspect the result and keeps iterating until the work is complete.
These projects often take dozens of rounds of planning, execution, and review. Local inference means those additional conversations, code revisions, and visual analyses incur no corresponding cloud-model token or API charges. Code and data can stay on the computer, and core model capabilities work offline.
Video production: generating Tesla's Roadster explainer within an hour
Edge0-145B produced an explainer about how Roadster could fly, handling technical research, scriptwriting, asset generation, and editing. We asked it to make the principles understandable to a general audience. The model ran locally without cloud-model calls or cloud token consumption, avoiding network round trips for inference.
The video was generated within an hour. Producing comparable work manually would have taken a director and scriptwriter, a 3D modeler, an editor, and a physics expert a month. An AI video workflow could previously cost more than hundreds of dollars; local inference eliminates the corresponding cloud-model token and API fees.
3D modeling: a Manhattan city scene in Blender
Edge0-145B built a 3D scene of Manhattan within an hour. Working in Blender from text and reference images, it constructed the scene, adjusted materials and lighting, and iterated on the rendered output.
The workflow brings visual inspection, code generation, and revision together inside Blender. The model works with objects, spatial relationships, and the creative goal, going beyond the pixels in a reference image. It inspects the result, writes code, and makes changes without handing those steps back to a person. The entire process stays local.
Creating a Manhattan-scale city scene manually would typically cost approximately $12,000-18,000 and take at least a month. A leading cloud model would still incur more than $150 in cloud token charges. Edge0-145B completes the local inference without those cloud-model token fees.
Game development: playable games and a medieval city
Edge0-145B can create playable games locally. We also used its 3D modeling capabilities to build an original medieval capital for a fantasy RPG and render a 20-second fly-through at game-trailer quality.
The model planned the city independently and built it from scratch in Blender: city walls, more than a thousand old-town homes, a plaza fountain, a Gothic twin-tower facade, a rib-vaulted nave, and stained-glass windows.
The fixed camera path made errors visible. Geometry clipping, occlusion, or incorrect proportions would appear along the route, so the model inspected the renders and corrected its work as it built. The workflow ran entirely locally.
A leading cloud model required hundreds of tool calls and hundreds of millions of tokens for a single completion, using the same prompt, reference images, and camera route. Edge0-145B delivered it on a laptop without cloud-model token or API fees for local inference. An art team would typically spend weeks and thousands of dollars producing a city scene to this standard.
Hardware control: code that flies a real drone
Edge0-145B wrote the program for a real drone from one instruction: “Write a drone program that navigates through obstacles and finds a red backpack.”
It generated the flight-control and visual-recognition programs locally. The drone then took off indoors, navigated through a dense row of obstacle poles, searched autonomously, and located the red backpack beside a wall. The task required no human-written code and no cloud call.
Hardware developers can describe the behavior they want and have the model turn it into a program for a real device, without moving repeatedly between an editor, documentation, and debugging tools.
PCB design: from KiCad layout to an assembly animation
Edge0-145B took a PCB design from component placement through an assembly visualization. We asked it to design a board for itself. It started from scratch in KiCad, placed components, routed a two-layer board, and imported the design into Unreal Engine 5. It then set up cameras and lighting and rendered an animation of PCB fabrication and assembly.
A single computer delivered work that previously required a hardware engineer and a 3D designer to hand tasks back and forth over several days. Schematics and board layouts can stay on the team's own hardware, supporting trade-secret protection and data-handling requirements without uploading the designs to a cloud model.
Why Edge0: A Full Stack for On-Device AI
Useful on-device AI requires model development, inference engineering, and hardware adaptation to work together. Our progress rests on three capabilities: developing complementary models quickly, optimizing the full stack, and carrying lessons from each deployment into the next.
Our September release of Edge0-35B showed what that approach could deliver on a phone. Its launch post passed 1.2 million views on its first day without paid promotion in overseas communities. Researchers and technical experts at OpenAI, NVIDIA, Qualcomm, and other companies shared it, and the model reached No. 1 on Hugging Face's overall Trending chart.
We released the model weights and unified inference framework together, giving companies and researchers a standard approach they could reproduce. The release helped move the discussion of on-device AI from a few billion parameters to the 35B tier.
Four Model Families for On-Device AI
Over the past year, we released 200 models across four families. Each handles a different part of the work, from listening and speaking to fast decisions, memory, and complex agent tasks.
The portfolio spans tens of millions to more than a hundred billion parameters. Both Edge0 and Audio8 have reached No. 1 on Hugging Face's Trending chart.
Models and Inference Systems Designed Together
We develop the model and the inference framework together, building hardware constraints into training and optimizing the engine for different chips. This connects model capability with the software and hardware needed to use it locally.
That work requires expertise in model development, inference frameworks, and low-level hardware adaptation. The combination is difficult for a company focused only on model development to reproduce.
Hardware Optimizations That Carry into Each Deployment
Each deployment gives us reusable optimizations for the next chip, model, or product. This matters because on-device hardware is fragmented: NPU architectures differ, as does support for numerical formats such as INT4, INT8, and FP4. A general-purpose model cannot automatically make full use of every chip.
We adapt to individual platforms. Our portfolio now covers 95% of mainstream chip platforms, including Qualcomm, BES, Rockchip, Allwinner, D-Robotics, and NVIDIA, across headphones, glasses, phones, PCs, and robots. This is portfolio-wide coverage; model requirements vary, and it does not mean every device can run Edge0-145B.
That deployment work now spans more than 5 million production devices. Our open-source models passed 15 million downloads within three months. By October 2026, we had more than 110,000 customer organizations.
The portfolio figures come from company papers and internal testing. They describe the broader Edge0 portfolio; the 145B performance results in this article apply specifically to Edge0-145B.
For Edge0-145B, day-one support covers macOS, Windows, and Linux, including Apple M-series chips and NVIDIA platforms such as RTX Spark, RTX 4090, and RTX 5090. The release includes one-click deployment tools and hardware guides.
The Future of On-Device AI and the Cloud
More complex work can stay on the device as local models approach cloud-level capability. Privacy, cost control, and offline access have long favored local execution; the remaining question was whether an on-device model could do the work. Edge0-145B expands the range of tasks for which it can.
Why More AI Work Can Stay Local
Local execution addresses four practical obstacles to adopting cloud-only AI:
- Control cloud-model costs. Local inference adds no corresponding cloud-model token or API fees as usage grows.
- Keep sensitive data local. Code, documents, and business records can stay on the hardware, giving teams control over data handling.
- Sustain a responsive experience. Model and inference-engine design address response speed, power use, system responsiveness, and continuous operation together.
- Keep core capabilities available offline. Local tasks continue when connectivity drops; features that depend on online information or services still need a connection.
From Cloud First to More Complex Work On-Device
We see the division of work between devices and the cloud shifting through three stages.
Before September 2026: cloud first, with on-device support. Apple Intelligence reflected this stage: it protected privacy, but handled a limited range of tasks locally while more demanding work relied on cloud capabilities.
After Edge0-35B's September 10 release: everyday tasks locally, complex tasks in the cloud. Local models could take on frequent requests that needed immediate responses. For one leading headphone manufacturer, our hybrid solution now handles 92% of AI usage on-device and 8% in the cloud. Everyday conversation accounts for most use cases on consumer wearables.
With Edge0-145B: toward on-device first, with cloud support. Vibe coding and other complex tasks can now run on a personal computer, giving teams more scope to choose local execution. This extends the range of work that can stay on the device while preserving the cloud for tasks and hardware that need it.
The diagram shows the shift from cloud-first AI to everyday local tasks and then more complex local work. Its colored areas are schematic, not measured usage shares.
Where Cloud AI Still Fits
Hardware constraints still determine which model fits. Compute capacity, heat, and battery life matter: Edge0-35B runs on phones, while Edge0-145B runs on suitably equipped laptops. A very low-compute device cannot currently run a 145B model locally. It needs a smaller model suited to its hardware or cloud access to a model of that size.
Cross-device long-term memory and searches requiring highly current information still require cloud services. Local and cloud models can work together; the appropriate split depends on the task, the hardware, and the information it needs.
The aim is to make local execution a practical choice for demanding work, with a capable model that can keep working close to the person using it. That is the next step toward our goal of enabling intelligence at zero distance for everybody.
Get Started with Edge0
Download the models to run locally, evaluate or fine-tune them, and build them into your own products:
- Model weights: Edge0-145B and Edge0-120B on Hugging Face.
- Inference framework and deployment guides: Edge0 on GitHub.
- Try it without environment setup: Download the Edge0 App. Its core model capabilities work offline, without an account or usage quota.
- Community: Join us on Discord.
For hardware or product integrations, reach the Edge0 team through GitHub or contact@autoark.ai.

Speculative decoding verifies multiple candidate tokens at once, spreading the cost of reading weights across more output tokens. These choices support the reported 80+ tokens/s decode rate.
Model and inference-engine design work together to make 100B+ parameter inference practical on personal computers. Source: internal testing.
Together, these changes let parameter counts grow from tens of billions to more than a hundred billion without proportional increases in memory use, power consumption, and latency.
What Edge0 Can Do: Anything From Chat to Complex Work on Your Computer
Edge0-145B can carry a complex task through to a usable result. Its coding and reasoning capabilities support local workflows that combine planning, tool use, execution, debugging, and visual checks. The benchmarks establish its capabilities; the demonstrations show how it puts them to work.
Coding and reasoning that rival frontier cloud models
Edge0-145B is competitive with specific frontier cloud models across coding, reasoning, knowledge, and instruction following:
- Coding: It scores 64.6 on DeepSWE 1.1, ahead of Qwen3.8-Flash-Next at 58.7 and DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 54.4.
- Reasoning and knowledge: It scores 90.4 on GPQA-Diamond, close to DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 90.8 and Claude-Opus-4.6 \(Max\) at 91.3.
- Instruction following: It scores 80.6 on IFBench, ahead of DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731 at 79.2 and Claude-Opus-4.6 \(Max\) at 62.5.
Its 86.52 on Terminal-Bench 2.1 also exceeds Qwen3.8-Flash-Next's 83.3, DeepSeek-V4-Flash-0731's 82.7, and Claude's 78.2. On AA-LCR, Edge0-145B scores 79.3 to Claude's 78.0. These comparisons apply to the named models and evaluations.
Scores are percentages; higher is better. A dash means no public score is available, rather than a score of zero.
Edge0, Qwen, DeepSeek, and Claude results come from Edge0's internal evaluations. Claude-Opus-4.6 has no public DeepSWE 1.1 score. For Flint, Muse Glimmer, and Gemma 4, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and IFBench results come from the Recursive Flint evaluation; HLE, GPQA, and AA-LCR results come from Artificial Analysis.
The largest gains over Qwen3.8-27B, a mainstream on-device model baseline, are in agent and coding work: 18.5% on Terminal-Bench and 53.1% on DeepSWE. The relative gains on HLE, GPQA, and IFBench are 14.9%, 1.3%, and 1.4%, respectively.
DeepSWE 1.1 and Terminal-Bench 2.1 evaluate agent and coding work. HLE, GPQA-Diamond, and IFBench cover reasoning, knowledge, and instruction following. The complete evaluation setup, hardware environment, and reproduction instructions are being released with our technical report.
Competitive capability with a smaller memory footprint
Edge0-145B combines a 72.8 mean score across six benchmarks with 29GB measured peak memory. Qwen3.8-Flash-Next averages 71.7, with an estimated 70GB footprint for standard 4-bit deployment. That puts Edge0-145B at about 40% of the estimated baseline memory while delivering comparable or higher capability, enabling local use on a 48GB laptop.
The means cover DeepSWE 1.1, Terminal-Bench 2.1, HLE, GPQA-Diamond, IFBench, and AA-LCR. Edge0 memory figures are measured internal peaks. Asterisked baseline figures are estimates for standard 4-bit deployment and remain to be measured. Claude is excluded because it is cloud-only and has no DeepSWE score.
[Figure: Quality versus peak memory, with 48GB laptop and 64GB workstation capacities marked. Lower memory and higher scores are better; the chart's vertical axis does not start at zero.]
Local agents that plan, code, run, and check their work
A single natural-language request can start an entire local workflow. Edge0-145B breaks down the requirements, plans the steps, writes code, and calls tools to run it. When something fails, it debugs and repairs the code. It then takes screenshots to inspect the result and keeps iterating until the work is complete.
These projects often take dozens of rounds of planning, execution, and review. Local inference means those additional conversations, code revisions, and visual analyses incur no corresponding cloud-model token or API charges. Code and data can stay on the computer, and core model capabilities work offline.
Video production: generating Tesla's Roadster explainer within an hour
Edge0-145B produced an explainer about how Roadster could fly, handling technical research, scriptwriting, asset generation, and editing. We asked it to make the principles understandable to a general audience. The model ran locally without cloud-model calls or cloud token consumption, avoiding network round trips for inference.
The video was generated within an hour. Producing comparable work manually would have taken a director and scriptwriter, a 3D modeler, an editor, and a physics expert a month. An AI video workflow could previously cost more than hundreds of dollars; local inference eliminates the corresponding cloud-model token and API fees.
3D modeling: a Manhattan city scene in Blender
Edge0-145B built a 3D scene of Manhattan within an hour. Working in Blender from text and reference images, it constructed the scene, adjusted materials and lighting, and iterated on the rendered output.
The workflow brings visual inspection, code generation, and revision together inside Blender. The model works with objects, spatial relationships, and the creative goal, going beyond the pixels in a reference image. It inspects the result, writes code, and makes changes without handing those steps back to a person. The entire process stays local.
Creating a Manhattan-scale city scene manually would typically cost approximately $12,000-18,000 and take at least a month. A leading cloud model would still incur more than $150 in cloud token charges. Edge0-145B completes the local inference without those cloud-model token fees.
Game development: playable games and a medieval city
Edge0-145B can create playable games locally. We also used its 3D modeling capabilities to build an original medieval capital for a fantasy RPG and render a 20-second fly-through at game-trailer quality.
The model planned the city independently and built it from scratch in Blender: city walls, more than a thousand old-town homes, a plaza fountain, a Gothic twin-tower facade, a rib-vaulted nave, and stained-glass windows.
The fixed camera path made errors visible. Geometry clipping, occlusion, or incorrect proportions would appear along the route, so the model inspected the renders and corrected its work as it built. The workflow ran entirely locally.
A leading cloud model required hundreds of tool calls and hundreds of millions of tokens for a single completion, using the same prompt, reference images, and camera route. Edge0-145B delivered it on a laptop without cloud-model token or API fees for local inference. An art team would typically spend weeks and thousands of dollars producing a city scene to this standard.
Hardware control: code that flies a real drone
Edge0-145B wrote the program for a real drone from one instruction: “Write a drone program that navigates through obstacles and finds a red backpack.”
It generated the flight-control and visual-recognition programs locally. The drone then took off indoors, navigated through a dense row of obstacle poles, searched autonomously, and located the red backpack beside a wall. The task required no human-written code and no cloud call.
Hardware developers can describe the behavior they want and have the model turn it into a program for a real device, without moving repeatedly between an editor, documentation, and debugging tools.
PCB design: from KiCad layout to an assembly animation
Edge0-145B took a PCB design from component placement through an assembly visualization. We asked it to design a board for itself. It started from scratch in KiCad, placed components, routed a two-layer board, and imported the design into Unreal Engine 5. It then set up cameras and lighting and rendered an animation of PCB fabrication and assembly.
A single computer delivered work that previously required a hardware engineer and a 3D designer to hand tasks back and forth over several days. Schematics and board layouts can stay on the team's own hardware, supporting trade-secret protection and data-handling requirements without uploading the designs to a cloud model.
Why Edge0: A Full Stack for On-Device AI
Useful on-device AI requires model development, inference engineering, and hardware adaptation to work together. Our progress rests on three capabilities: developing complementary models quickly, optimizing the full stack, and carrying lessons from each deployment into the next.
Our September release of Edge0-35B showed what that approach could deliver on a phone. Its launch post passed 1.2 million views on its first day without paid promotion in overseas communities. Researchers and technical experts at OpenAI, NVIDIA, Qualcomm, and other companies shared it, and the model reached No. 1 on Hugging Face's overall Trending chart.
We released the model weights and unified inference framework together, giving companies and researchers a standard approach they could reproduce. The release helped move the discussion of on-device AI from a few billion parameters to the 35B tier.
Four Model Families for On-Device AI
Over the past year, we released 200 models across four families. Each handles a different part of the work, from listening and speaking to fast decisions, memory, and complex agent tasks.
The portfolio spans tens of millions to more than a hundred billion parameters. Both Edge0 and Audio8 have reached No. 1 on Hugging Face's Trending chart.
Models and Inference Systems Designed Together
We develop the model and the inference framework together, building hardware constraints into training and optimizing the engine for different chips. This connects model capability with the software and hardware needed to use it locally.
That work requires expertise in model development, inference frameworks, and low-level hardware adaptation. The combination is difficult for a company focused only on model development to reproduce.
Hardware Optimizations That Carry into Each Deployment
Each deployment gives us reusable optimizations for the next chip, model, or product. This matters because on-device hardware is fragmented: NPU architectures differ, as does support for numerical formats such as INT4, INT8, and FP4. A general-purpose model cannot automatically make full use of every chip.
We adapt to individual platforms. Our portfolio now covers 95% of mainstream chip platforms, including Qualcomm, BES, Rockchip, Allwinner, D-Robotics, and NVIDIA, across headphones, glasses, phones, PCs, and robots. This is portfolio-wide coverage; model requirements vary, and it does not mean every device can run Edge0-145B.
That deployment work now spans more than 5 million production devices. Our open-source models passed 15 million downloads within three months. By October 2026, we had more than 110,000 customer organizations.
The portfolio figures come from company papers and internal testing. They describe the broader Edge0 portfolio; the 145B performance results in this article apply specifically to Edge0-145B.
For Edge0-145B, day-one support covers macOS, Windows, and Linux, including Apple M-series chips and NVIDIA platforms such as RTX Spark, RTX 4090, and RTX 5090. The release includes one-click deployment tools and hardware guides.
The Future of On-Device AI and the Cloud
More complex work can stay on the device as local models approach cloud-level capability. Privacy, cost control, and offline access have long favored local execution; the remaining question was whether an on-device model could do the work. Edge0-145B expands the range of tasks for which it can.
Why More AI Work Can Stay Local
Local execution addresses four practical obstacles to adopting cloud-only AI:
- Control cloud-model costs. Local inference adds no corresponding cloud-model token or API fees as usage grows.
- Keep sensitive data local. Code, documents, and business records can stay on the hardware, giving teams control over data handling.
- Sustain a responsive experience. Model and inference-engine design address response speed, power use, system responsiveness, and continuous operation together.
- Keep core capabilities available offline. Local tasks continue when connectivity drops; features that depend on online information or services still need a connection.
From Cloud First to More Complex Work On-Device
We see the division of work between devices and the cloud shifting through three stages.
Before September 2026: cloud first, with on-device support. Apple Intelligence reflected this stage: it protected privacy, but handled a limited range of tasks locally while more demanding work relied on cloud capabilities.
After Edge0-35B's September 10 release: everyday tasks locally, complex tasks in the cloud. Local models could take on frequent requests that needed immediate responses. For one leading headphone manufacturer, our hybrid solution now handles 92% of AI usage on-device and 8% in the cloud. Everyday conversation accounts for most use cases on consumer wearables.
With Edge0-145B: toward on-device first, with cloud support. Vibe coding and other complex tasks can now run on a personal computer, giving teams more scope to choose local execution. This extends the range of work that can stay on the device while preserving the cloud for tasks and hardware that need it.
The diagram shows the shift from cloud-first AI to everyday local tasks and then more complex local work. Its colored areas are schematic, not measured usage shares.
Where Cloud AI Still Fits
Hardware constraints still determine which model fits. Compute capacity, heat, and battery life matter: Edge0-35B runs on phones, while Edge0-145B runs on suitably equipped laptops. A very low-compute device cannot currently run a 145B model locally. It needs a smaller model suited to its hardware or cloud access to a model of that size.
Cross-device long-term memory and searches requiring highly current information still require cloud services. Local and cloud models can work together; the appropriate split depends on the task, the hardware, and the information it needs.
The aim is to make local execution a practical choice for demanding work, with a capable model that can keep working close to the person using it. That is the next step toward our goal of enabling intelligence at zero distance for everybody.
Get Started with Edge0
Download the models to run locally, evaluate or fine-tune them, and build them into your own products:
- Model weights: Edge0-145B and Edge0-120B on Hugging Face.
- Inference framework and deployment guides: Edge0 on GitHub.
- Try it without environment setup: Download the Edge0 App. Its core model capabilities work offline, without an account or usage quota.
- Community: Join us on Discord.
For hardware or product integrations, reach the Edge0 team through GitHub or contact@autoark.ai.

Nintendo Takes Down Switch Emulators, Mod Chip Firmware in Massive GitHub DMCA Sweep
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Nintendo Takes Down Switch Emulators, Mod Chip Firmware in Massive GitHub DMCA Sweep (ign.com) 42
			
		 	
				Nintendo has issued four DMCA takedown notices targeting two Nintendo Switch emulators, Yuzu and Citron Neo, along with mod chip firmware such as Picofly, SX Core and HWFLY. As a result, 144 repositories containing firmware used to circumvent the console's copy protections have been removed by GitHub and replaced by a DMCA takedown notice. IGN reports:  GitHub has posted Nintendo's November 6 DMCA notice, which says, "The Nintendo Switch console and video games contain technological protection measures ('TPMs') that permit the Nintendo Switch console to interact only with legitimate Nintendo video game files. This process protects Nintendo's copyright-protected video games, (...) by preventing users from playing pirated copies of Nintendo's video games on the Nintendo Switch console and by preventing users from unlawfully copying and playing Nintendo's video games on unauthorized devices."
However, while this takedown has effectively wiped out a ton of repositories with mod chip firmware, it seems like it's still being hosted elsewhere on the site, according to TorrentFreak. It's possible, though, that Nintendo isn't finished yet; back in 2024, Nintendo effectively took down 8,500 copies of Yuzu by issuing a takedown that targeted 12 different mirrors of the emulator. This particular takedown also comes a few days after emulators were able to run the remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time on PC, so this could be a sign that Nintendo is winding up its lawyers to crack down on emulation once again.

Democrats launch Nvidia investigation, write scathing letter on Huang’s ‘scheme’ with Trump
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
22:42:51 UTC
07:42:51 KST

Democrats on Thursday opened an inquiry into how the Trump administration handled Nvidia’s chip sales to China. Earlier on Thursday, the company ousted its CEO Jensen Huang. He also agreed to testify to a different set of Republican-led Congressional committees.
Huang was removed from the company he founded after he confessed to an extramarital affair with a woman accused of being a Chinese spy. (He has since been accused of having more affairs, with Nvidia employees.)
The alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, was an executive of Nvidia customer Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company that U.S. officials suspect of helping route advanced Nvidia chips into China. The Democratic inquiry comes from Rep. Richard Neal (D-Mass.), the top Democrat on the House Ways and Means Committee, and Sen. Ron Wyden (D-Ore.), the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee. As the minority party, they can ask questions but can’t compel answers.
Their humdinger of a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick asks the department to preserve records about an arrangement they find questionable and, they allege, illegal.
“The scheme worked like this,” as the letter describes it. President Trump agreed to sales of Nvidia’s H200 chips to China as long as the chips, manufactured by Taiwan, were first shipped to the United States. On arriving, Trump added a tariff “effectively taking a cut of Nvidia’s sales to China through what appears to be an unconstitutional export tax system,” the letter said. Then the Commerce Department, under Lutnick, issued an export license.
By the way, the deal between Trump and Nvidia to share revenue was public. In August 2025, Nvidia and AMD both agreed to share 15% of their revenue from chip sales to China with the U.S. government as part of a deal to secure export licenses, the AP reported at the time.
But the letter questions why Lutnick continued to allow Megaspeed to buy chips even after he was reportedly informed by U.S. intelligence of their suspicions of Alice Huang and of Jensen Huang’s relationship with her. It questions why the department reportedly green-lit other Asian companies to buy Nvidia chips “with no physical presence at their listed addresses.” So the inquiry is aimed at the administration, not just at Nvidia and Huang.
The letter also questions how Nvidia’s donations to Trump’s projects factored in. It notes that the export deal was approved after Trump learned of “Mr. Huang’s compromised position” in sleeping with an alleged spy, and after Nvidia donated $26 million toward Trump’s projects, including the ballroom. It appears to be asking if Trump used his knowledge of the affair to secure donations from Nvidia.
Interestingly, in a “60 Minutes” interview with President Trump that aired on Sunday, Trump mentioned how he and his team had threatened an unnamed AI exec with jail, and after that, the exec “totally changed.”
This Congressional investigation into Nvidia’s China sales and its dealings with Trump is the second one, so far, since news of the alleged spy affair broke. The Republican-led group involves leaders from the House Select Committee on the Chinese Communist Party, the House Committee on Foreign Affairs, and the House Intelligence Committee. That group has demanded that Huang come to them and testify in a closed-door session. They had threatened to subpoena him if he didn’t voluntarily agree. On Thursday, after his resignation, he agreed.

Muse can now place calls to US businesses — if you pay
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
22:22:28 UTC
07:22:28 KST

After initially testing the feature in September, Meta’s consumer AI agent Muse is now able to place calls to U.S. businesses on behalf of its users. However, only paid subscribers have access to the feature, the company says.
Meta’s Muse, which now has over 10 million users, the company said last month, has been working to make consumer AI a mainstream product for everyday users. Placing calls is one of the ways it aims to get there, as it could handle another task on people’s to-do lists.
Notes Alexandr Wang, Meta’s chief AI officer and head of its Superintelligence Labs division, the feature could be used to take care of calls people don’t want to make, like those to their cable company, the DMV, or to a restaurant that doesn’t offer online reservations.
Previously, only a select number of Muse early adopters were able to try this feature after asking the AI for access. Now, anyone who pays for Muse can make this type of request. Currently, Muse subscription plans start at $20 per month for 500 million Muse tokens per week. Heavier AI users can opt to pay $100 per month for 3 billion tokens.
Muse is not the only AI agent able to place calls to businesses; the startup Instinct has been offering a similar option, as has Google’s Gemini. However, while the AI tools are now certainly capable of handling the task, the reception on the other end of the line can be mixed. Many businesses and receptionists hang up on calls from AI agents as soon as they identify that it’s not a human on the phone. But with AI now better able to mimic a real human voice, being able to tell the difference between a customer and an AI working on the customer’s behalf will get tougher.
Despite the challenges, this feature is one that could appeal to consumers who have grown frustrated with the state of customer service over the years. For too long, customers have been forced to navigate complex phone trees, and then sit on hold waiting for a human representative to pick up. If they’re lucky, that person can actually help them, and not transfer them to another department. Now, those consumers could potentially use their AI, with its infinite patience, for this tedious task instead.
While calls remain a paid-only feature for the time being, the recently added option to make a Muse email address will soon roll out to free users, Wang said this week.
