Reverse Engineering My Coway Air Purifier to Ditch the Cloud
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
22:03:09 UTC
07:03:09 KST

The year is 2017. A string of annual wildfires rocks the West Coast. The sky looks like this in the daytime:
My home, like many in the Bay Area, has no insulation and isn’t really air sealed. To avoid choking on soot, a lot of people started buying air purifiers like this one:
It’s an overpriced box fan with a HEPA filter and an air quality sensor. It has some controls for the fan speed, and it can also automatically ramp up if the air quality is bad. It’s ugly, but they’re so ubiquitous around here that I feel like no one even notices them anymore. Also, FWIW, the device works. It really does improve air quality.
This device became a staple item in our household when my wife eventually started cranking up the fan at night for white noise (a dual purpose!). Over time, I have become accustomed to it and now also require it to sleep.
The Good Night button
Our home also has smart lighting, and it’s all integrated with Apple Home. At night, I always press the “Good Night” button, which shuts off all the lights. I really wanted to tie the fan into that. I’d press Good Night, the lights would shut off, and the fan would crank up to my wife’s preferred white noise volume.
The old Airmega didn’t have Wi-Fi, so there was no easy way to integrate it with Apple Home. When it eventually broke, I bought the now-discontinued Coway Airmega IconS (for non-discontinued Wi-Fi models, check out the Airmega 250S or Airmega MightyS). It’s still just a box fan with a HEPA filter, but it’s considerably more expensive, aims to look like a piece of fancy mid-century furniture, and most importantly, can be controlled over Wi-Fi (sort of).
The existing Home Assistant integration
Coway’s iPhone app is an absolutely classic IoT affair. It takes forever to load, and it asks me to change my password nearly every time I launch it. Thankfully, a brave soul reverse-engineered the app and wrote an integration for Home Assistant. The integration more or less pretends to be the phone app. It polls the Coway cloud service for status updates and sends commands through it to control the fan. Home Assistant bridges that to Apple Home and… voilà!
Why ditch the cloud?
The downside of this approach is that you’re still at the mercy (or lack thereof) of the Coway cloud service. It seems like at least once or twice a year, it breaks.
Here are some of the reasons I have seen it break:
- Coway didn’t like the integration hammering their servers and banned it. After a protracted argument with the community, they eventually settled on allowing it.
- Coway rewrote their phone app and rebranded it from IoCare to IoCare+, so the integration had to be rewritten as well.
- Coway made minor changes to the layout of the HTML that the integration scrapes, which broke it.
- Coway’s servers were periodically offline, which prevented any remote control of the device.
- Coway banned my account at one point for running an old version of the integration. I couldn’t control the fan remotely for weeks while I waited for their support team to reverse the ban.
On top of this, it seems unlikely that Coway will maintain this service forever. I suspect that eventually, like all IoT manufacturers, they’ll either charge for this service or discontinue it. My fan model is already discontinued (though there are other Wi-Fi enabled models, so… who knows).
A way in
I admit, I have been lazy. For years, I put off trying to figure out a way to ditch the Coway cloud, because if the device was really locked down, it would be pretty tough. Presumably, when the fan starts up, it connects to coway.com (or whatever) and checks that the server’s SSL certificate is valid. Most likely, I wouldn’t be able to make a fake certificate that passes that check, so the fan wouldn’t connect to my server.
Also, setting up a man-in-the-middle (MitM) environment is just a pain in the neck. The fan would have to connect to an isolated Wi-Fi network that routes all its traffic through my own server on the way out to the internet. To do that, you have to configure a bunch of routing and firewall rules to redirect traffic. None of this is impossible (I’ve done it before), but it’s a hassle to set up, and it’s always finicky to get right. And once you have it set up, that’s just step one.
All that said, two major things have changed.
First, there was a recent security incident (October 2026) where someone discovered that eMudhra, one of the trusted internet certificate authorities, had used a very weak parameter for some of its private keys.
So, if the device is unpatched AND it has hardcoded root certificates AND it doesn’t pin the cloud service’s certificate, then… maybe that’s a way in. Admittedly, that’s a lot of ifs, but assuming the device doesn’t have any major security mistakes in its design, this could be the foothold needed to take control of it.
The second thing is simply AI. An LLM can set up everything you need for a MitM environment, so you can get straight to sleuthing out how the fan works. It can do this unattended, in minutes.
Once you can sit between the fan and the internet, you can see how it communicates, which should help us understand how to get the fan to talk to us instead of the cloud.
Our man in the middle
Initially, I tried to set it up like this:
This actually didn’t work, because a Raspberry Pi uses a smallish PCB trace antenna that doesn’t get a ton of signal strength. With the Pi in the room with my router, the signal was too weak to reach the fan. I guess I could’ve moved the fan into the room with the router, but why do that when I can solve this with a convoluted technical solution?
The Raspberry Pi uses its Wi-Fi radio to run its own access point for the fan to connect to. It can’t easily do that while also connecting over Wi-Fi to the home network. So, I moved the Pi into the room with the fan and plugged its Ethernet port into my laptop, which bridges it to the home network over Wi-Fi:
Next, we need to configure the Pi.
I’m not really making a value judgment here on whether this is a fair thing to block, but Claude will not oblige the request, not even for… uhh… testing purposes.
Luckily, ChatGPT has no such compunctions.
After a few rounds of that, here are the nftables rules it ended up with:
scottjg@pi:~ $ sudo nft -a list ruleset
table inet mitmproxy_ap { # handle 8
	set client_interfaces { # handle 4
		type ifname
		elements = { "wlan0" }
	}
	chain prerouting { # handle 1
		type nat hook prerouting priority dstnat; policy accept;
		meta nfproto ipv4 iifname @client_interfaces tcp dport { 80, 443 } counter packets 2 bytes 120 redirect to :8080 # handle 6
	}
	chain input { # handle 2
		type filter hook input priority filter - 10; policy accept;
		iifname != "lo" iifname != @client_interfaces tcp dport 8080 counter packets 0 bytes 0 reject with tcp reset # handle 7
		iifname @client_interfaces meta nfproto ipv6 counter packets 9 bytes 2397 drop # handle 8
		iifname @client_interfaces ct state established,related accept # handle 9
		iifname @client_interfaces udp dport { 53, 67 } accept # handle 11
		iifname @client_interfaces tcp dport { 53, 8080 } accept # handle 13
		iifname @client_interfaces ip protocol icmp accept # handle 14
		iifname @client_interfaces counter packets 23 bytes 4697 reject with icmpx admin-prohibited # handle 15
	}
	chain forward { # handle 3
		type filter hook forward priority filter - 10; policy accept;
		iifname @client_interfaces meta nfproto ipv6 counter packets 0 bytes 0 drop # handle 16
		oifname @client_interfaces meta nfproto ipv6 counter packets 0 bytes 0 drop # handle 17
		iifname @client_interfaces udp dport 443 counter packets 1 bytes 57 reject # handle 18
		iifname @client_interfaces oifname != "eth0" counter packets 0 bytes 0 reject with icmpx admin-prohibited # handle 19
	}
}
table ip nm-shared-wlan0 { # handle 12
	chain nat_postrouting { # handle 1
		type nat hook postrouting priority srcnat; policy accept;
		ip saddr 10.42.0.0/24 ip daddr != 10.42.0.0/24 masquerade # handle 2
	}
	chain filter_forward { # handle 3
		type filter hook forward priority filter; policy accept;
		ip daddr 10.42.0.0/24 oifname "wlan0" ct state { established, related } accept # handle 5
		ip saddr 10.42.0.0/24 iifname "wlan0" accept # handle 6
		iifname "wlan0" oifname "wlan0" accept # handle 7
		iifname "wlan0" reject # handle 8
		oifname "wlan0" reject # handle 9
	}
}
Basically, the masquerade rule translates the traffic from the isolated Wi-Fi network out through our home internet, and the redirect rule forwards HTTP/HTTPS to a running instance of mitmproxy.
Also on the Pi, mitmproxy runs with its web interface, mitmweb, using a command line like this:
mitmweb \
  --mode transparent --showhost \
  --listen-host 0.0.0.0 --listen-port 8080 \
  --web-host 127.0.0.1 --web-port 8081 \
  --set web_open_browser=false \
  --emudhra-old
Yes, off the shelf, mitmproxy has a built-in option (--emudhra-old) to automatically use the eMudhra root CA to intercept traffic.
Snooping on the fan
Next, I used the iPhone app to set up the fan on our Pi-MITM-Test Wi-Fi network. Partway through, the app reported that setup failed. Let’s glance at the mitmproxy web interface:
So, we know there was some kind of connection issue, since the app said the fan failed to connect. But it clearly got onto our isolated Wi-Fi, because it made a request.
The request is very suspicious. It looks like the fan is downloading the root certificate for Amazon’s certificate authority over plain HTTP. This interface only shows connections that got far enough to make an actual HTTP request. If, for example, the fan doesn’t accept the eMudhra cert we’re using to impersonate the real servers, its HTTPS connections will fail before that point and never show up in the list.
Hypothesis: The device downloads this public certificate over HTTP so it has something to validate its later (currently unseen) HTTPS connections against. It doesn’t have any root certificates built in at all.
mitmproxy has a plugin mode where you can modify specific requests. We can use it to swap in mitmproxy’s own generated CA certificate in place of this public one. If our hypothesis is correct, the fan will trust that CA, and we can intercept the rest of its traffic.
"""Serve this lab's public RSA CA only to the authorized Coway test device."""
from pathlib import Path
from mitmproxy import ctx, http
def response(flow: http.HTTPFlow) -> None:
    if (
        flow.client_conn.peername[0] == "10.42.0.93" # device IP
        and flow.request.scheme == "http"
        and flow.request.method == "GET"
        and flow.request.pretty_host == "rootca-fs.iocareus.com.s3.ap-northeast-2.amazonaws.com"
        and flow.request.path.split("?", 1)[0] == "/AmazonRootCA1.pem"
        and flow.response is not None
        and flow.response.status_code == 200
    ):
        certificate = Path("/var/lib/mitmproxy-ap/mitmproxy-ca-cert.pem").read_bytes()
        flow.response = http.Response.make(
            200,
            certificate,
            {
                "Content-Type": "application/x-pem-file",
                "Cache-Control": "no-store",
            },
        )
        flow.metadata["coway_test_ca_served"] = True
        ctx.log.info("Served lab RSA CA to the Coway's HTTP CA bootstrap request")
Sure enough, the device now fully connects, and we can see some new requests:
Hypothesis confirmed
We can intercept the traffic between the fan and the server, which means we could probably build our own server to replace the Coway IoCare cloud. It also turns out we don’t need the eMudhra hack at all. The fan downloads its root CA cert over plain HTTP. It was always MitM-able.
The one thing missing is that none of these requests have anything to do with changing the fan speed or anything else you can do in the app. That’s easy to figure out, though. If you run tcpdump on the Pi while clicking buttons in the app, you can see encrypted traffic on port 8883, the standard port for MQTT over TLS. MQTT is a message bus protocol commonly used in IoT devices.
Going back to the POST in the request list above, here’s what that API call returned:
{
    "header": {
        "success": true,
        "result": "S1000",
        "message": "SUCCESS"
    },
    "payload": {
        "macAddr": "<redacted>",
        "certToken": "<redacted>",
        "host": "a29vouc2qruduj-ats.iot.ap-northeast-2.amazonaws.com",
        "port": 8883,
        "serial": "<redacted>",
        "certSvrUrl": "https://cert.iotp.coway.com/iot/v1/cert/r0",
        "caUrl": "http://rootca-fs.iocareus.com.s3.ap-northeast-2.amazonaws.com/AmazonRootCA1.pem",
        "privateUrl": "https://icert-fs.iocareus.com/<redacted>/55d66923e9-private.pem.key",
        "certUrl": "https://icert-fs.iocareus.com/<redacted>/55d66923e9-certificate.pem.crt",
        "svcCode": "002AIR007",
        "countryCd": "US"
    }
}
So, if we control the response to this call, we control which keys the fan uses to connect to the MQTT server, and also where that MQTT server is.
For now, we can write another mitmproxy plugin that injects our own key into this response, and then use that to proxy the MQTT traffic. The plugin code gets kinda long, so I didn’t include it here, but AI generates it fairly quickly. mitmproxy doesn’t decode MQTT, but it can still show us the raw stream:
The data looks like a mix of binary and ASCII gibberish, but it’s a standard MQTT protocol stream. When I adjust the fan speed in the app, you can see a new message come from the Coway server to the device. Usually, I’d load this into a traffic dissector and make a table of what gibberish comes in when I press each button in the app, but now…
and after a few minutes…
There’s enough here now to build our own web server that hands the fan its certificates and configuration during setup, plus our own MQTT server to send it commands. That completely cuts the Coway cloud out of the picture during normal operation, but we’re still using the Coway app to set the device up. We also still need a fairly annoying MitM environment to get the fan to connect to us instead of Coway.
Bluetooth setup process
When you use the Coway app to set up the fan, it searches for the device via Bluetooth. I was hoping we could figure out a way to configure the device ourselves, without the app. Plus, if we’re lucky, we might find something else useful along the way.
I have reverse-engineered Bluetooth devices before. My old strategy was to get the Android version of the app, decompile it, and then just pore over the code. It’s not always as straightforward as it sounds. Developers often obfuscate their apps, which makes the decompiled code harder to read. Some bytecode also doesn’t decompile cleanly back to source code, which means you end up having to read the raw bytecode.
Still, in my opinion, this is usually easier than dealing with iPhone apps. Android apps are usually written in Java (or Kotlin), and it’s just easier to reverse-engineer Java apps than apps compiled from Swift or Objective-C.
This time, I tried a new strategy, which was to use the built-in Android Bluetooth debug logs.
Then, I connected the phone over USB and asked the AI to drive the phone via adb, look at the Bluetooth logs, and reconstruct the protocol. The AI took a while to churn through this task. Eventually, it identified that the message carrying the Wi-Fi configuration was encrypted. Since we would ultimately need to figure out what algorithm was used for the encryption, we would probably have to look at the decompiled app. Sure enough, the next thing it did was pull the Coway app off the phone, decompile it. Then, it started matching up the source code with the Bluetooth logs.
After all that, we can see the provisioning works like this:
- Read the fan’s identity over Bluetooth from characteristic FFA0 (a “characteristic” is a Bluetooth endpoint you can read or write). This gives you some basic information about the device, including its MAC address.
- Write an encryption key (20 random lowercase ASCII letters) to characteristic FFA2:"P001" + protobuf {
  command: 1,
  key: Base64(random_letters)
}
- Construct the AES key by concatenating the key from step 2 with the 12 uppercase ASCII characters of the MAC address.
- Build the configuration payload, which looks like this:{
  "cmd": 2,
  "ssid": "Example Wi-Fi",
  "pwd": "example-wifi-password",
  "bssid": "AABBCCDDEEFF",
  "type": 1,
  "macAddr": "112233445566",
  "caUrl": "http://192.0.2.1:8080/ca.pem",
  "certSvrUrl": "https://192.0.2.1:9443/iot/v1/cert/r0",
  "certToken": "example-enrollment-token",
  "serial": "EXAMPLE000000000001"
}
That gets wrapped in protobuf, padded with PKCS#7, and encrypted using the AES key from step 3. Then it gets prefixed with the string “P002” and sent to FFA2 as well.
Needless to say, figuring this out end to end from the decompiled Android app would have been very time consuming to do by hand. It’d be at least hours of work (likely days). AI really comes in clutch here. It took the AI about 20 minutes.
The good part, though, is that all the data you need to decrypt that payload is in the Bluetooth logs we captured from the phone, so we can check that our reimplementation is correct just from those. From there, the AI made quick work of reimplementing the whole setup flow in a little Python script.
I was watching the progress pretty carefully while this was going on. The AI never actually showed me the full payload, but I could see it in the intermediate output of the scripts it was writing. And that’s when I noticed something. Two of the URLs from our capture are set right in this payload (caUrl and certSvrUrl). The first tells the fan where to download the root CA cert over plain HTTP, and the second tells it where to POST over HTTPS to get its MQTT configuration.
Since we’re the ones sending this payload now, we can point those URLs anywhere we want. Most importantly, that means we don’t have to do any MitM anymore. We just have to run our own server and plug in its URLs when we set up the fan.
Home Assistant integration
Based on the findings in this article, I was able to build a Home Assistant custom integration for the fan. My Home Assistant server doesn’t have Bluetooth, and it’s pretty far away from my fan, so I opted to do the provisioning with Web Bluetooth. That way you can bring your laptop next to the fan and provision it to talk to Home Assistant directly.
After that, the device talks directly over Wi-Fi to Home Assistant.
And, more importantly, it gets bridged from Home Assistant to my Apple Home.
All in a day’s work. Now, I can finally click Good Night.

How Sky Glabush Conjures Atmospheric Landscapes in Sand and Oil Paint

How Sky Glabush Conjures Atmospheric Landscapes in Sand and Oil Paint
Looking at the serene paintings that emerge from Sky Glabush’ s studio, it’s easy to feel a soft breeze sliding off bare summer shoulders or hear the crunch of leaves underfoot, their parched fibers reverberating across the autumn landscape. Transportive and atmospherically charged, the landscapes harness the allure of texture to draw the viewer in and invite them to stay a while.
Raised on the west coast of British Columbia and currently based in rural Ontario, Glabush has long been attuned to the natural world. He often identifies the feelings he associates with a particular place and translates that through color and intuitive interpretations of the terrain: the spindly, strangely straight tree trunks of a forest, for example, or the ways a sunflower echoes the radial form of the sun.
Glabush typically utilizes limited palettes that adhere to just one or two color families. He also mixes oil paint with granular particles, sand being the most common, although he’s used crushed quartz and raw pigment. Originally, the idea of combining materials emerged because the artist wanted, he says, to “obliterate the image… But I realized it didn’t destroy the image. It was still present and humming under that surface layer. So it gave me the freedom of deciding what I wanted to hold on to or what I wanted to erase.”
Adding and removing has since become integral to Glabush’s practice, which involves adding thin stains of paint and turpentine to the point of the painting becoming relief-like. He tends to begin with an architectural structure that anchors the work. Sometimes a grid and others a radial arrangement, the foundational patterns inform each resulting layer. Color, though, is always “the unifying principle, it is the energy and dynamism, it is the element of surprise. Each painting often goes through several iterations of colour before deciding on a direction.”
Although he’s worked with other materials in the past and has particularly enjoyed carving, the conventions and limitations of painting always drew him back. “That period of experimentation with sculpture allowed me to treat my paintings in a more physically robust and experimental way. But even during my most experimental period, I saw myself as a painter and wanted to find a way to express myself within that language,” he tells Colossal.
Some of the works here will be on view in Glabush’s solo exhibition, If the Wild Bird Could Speak, on view from November 20 to January 9 at Alison Jacques in London. Until then, explore more of his paintings on Instagram.

Chinese frontier AI models carry the fingerprint of export-controlled GPUs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:56:11 UTC
06:56:11 KST

Top Chinese Frontier AI Models Carry the Fingerprint of Export-Controlled NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs
Executive Summary
Our forensic analysis of publicly released AI models finds evidence that DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group have gained access to NVIDIA’s Blackwell GPUs, despite restrictions on exporting these chips to China, and are using that hardware to create their AI models. Six models released by these companies since April 2026 contain numerical patterns matching Blackwell hardware, whereas earlier models from the same companies match NVIDIA’s older Hopper generation. The findings suggest that several leading Chinese AI developers have begun using Blackwell to train their newest models. U.S. officials and press reports had already alleged that DeepSeek and Moonshot AI were using Blackwell chips, without publishing technical evidence.
The evidence comes entirely from the models’ own published weights, rather than from leaks or insider accounts. We recover traces of the models’ initial values, and then search for the hardware configurations and initial configurations that reproduce them; for DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Kimi K3, we also confirmed the match on physical Blackwell chips. The fingerprint identifies how the weights were created and supports, without proving, the inference that Blackwell also performed the training. Specially configured AMD hardware, which is also restricted from export to China, is the only alternative we have identified; we have excluded it for DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and consider it very unlikely for the other five models. Notably, our findings provide technical evidence of access to advanced computing hardware, but do not establish where the chips were located, how access was obtained, or whether export controls were violated.
Table 1. Hardware fingerprints in successive models from DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group Link ↗
• Requires a U.S. license for export to China; none have been reported.
A Hopper fingerprint cannot distinguish an H800 from an H100 SXM or H200; the H800 identifications above are lab disclosures, not findings of this method.
Full list of models in Appendix 2 ↓
Main Findings
- Six models from four Chinese labs read as Blackwell-trained. DeepSeek-V4-Flash and MiniMax-M3 match NVIDIA’s Blackwell-class B200 or B300 chips, which have 148 processor cores (called streaming multiprocessors, or SMs). DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Kimi K3, Kimi K3.1-Pro and Ling-3.1-flash match the GB200 or GB300 chips, also Blackwell-class, which have 152 processor cores. Kimi K3.1-Pro starts from the same seed as K3, and may be a checkpoint of K3 with further post-training.
- Chinese labs started training on Blackwell relatively recently. DeepSeek’s models read as Hopper-trained through DeepSeek-V3 (released in December 2024), and as Blackwell from DeepSeek V4 (released in April 2026). Moonshot’s models read as Hopper through Kimi K2.5 (January 2026) and as Blackwell from K3 (July 2026). One Moonshot model sits in between: Kimi K2.7-Code, posted six weeks before K3, is built on the Hopper-era weights but contains a small part that matches the B200 or B300. MiniMax’s models read as Hopper through M2.7 (April 2026) and as Blackwell from M3 (June 2026). Ant Group’s Ling-3.0-flash (August 2026) reads as Hopper, while its Ling-3.1-flash (October 2026) reads as Blackwell. The Hopper readings do not identify a specific chip: H800s, H100 SXMs and H200s share the same fingerprint.
- Not every Chinese AI lab has switched to Blackwell. The newest models we could read from Alibaba (Qwen), Zhipu (GLM), Tencent, Xiaomi and StepFun all read as Hopper-trained. Meituan says its LongCat-2.0 was trained entirely on “AI ASIC superpods,” and we found no NVIDIA fingerprint in the model weights it’s released.
- The method gets known answers right. For 24 specific models, the developer has publicly said what hardware was used. In every case, our method agrees with the public announcement. NVIDIA’s own Nemotron-3 Ultra, whose technical report names the GB200 for one phase of its training, reads as 152 SMs, the same as Kimi K3.
- The matches are far stronger than chance would explain. Even unrelated numbers can appear to match when enough combinations are tried. Across the roughly 6 trillion seed-and-layout combinations in our search, the strongest chance match would be expected to reach about 8 sigma—a measure of how far a result stands above ordinary random variation. The strongest evidence for each of the six models ranges from 49 to 1,385 sigma. Even the weakest individual test we report reaches 20 sigma.
- The training runs were data-center scale. From each model’s size and the amount of data it was trained on, we estimate that the two largest runs, Kimi K3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro, each needed thousands of Blackwell GPUs for two to three months, assuming each model was trained on the hardware that initialized it. Our best estimates are about 4,500 and 3,000, the equivalent of about 11,500 and 7,500 H100s, the previous-generation NVIDIA chip in which AI training clusters are commonly sized, and about 12,000 Blackwell GPUs across the five runs (Table 2). At roughly $70,000 per GPU—the cost implied by a Wall Street Journal report that about 36,000 Blackwell chips being installed in Malaysia for ByteDance’s use would cost more than $2.5 billion—the largest of these clusters would cost roughly $200 million to $300 million, and the five together more than half a billion dollars. These figures cover only the final training runs; the labs’ total spending on export-controlled GPUs is likely in the billions of dollars.
Table 2. Rough scale of each training run Link ↗
Each estimate is a run’s training compute divided by what one Blackwell GPU delivers over the run, rounded to the nearest 500. Training compute is about six operations per active parameter for every training token, plus attention. We assume the GPUs ran at about 20% of their peak speed, in line with other disclosed Blackwell training runs (25% for DeepSeek, which has reported 39% for its previous model), and for the run lengths shown, which we estimate from release timelines. H100 equivalents convert at NVIDIA’s published peak speeds: a B200 or B300 equals about 2.3 H100s, and a GB200 or GB300 about 2.5.
We are not confident in these estimates. They rest on assumptions that the weights cannot confirm: how efficiently each lab used its GPUs (often called MFU), which no lab has published for these runs; how many tokens each model was trained on (DeepSeek’s counts are its own, and we take them on trust; the others are our estimates, made from the weights by a method we have not yet published); how long each run lasted, which no lab has stated; and that training stayed on the hardware that created the weights. Run length matters most, because halving it doubles the GPU count. Two disclosed runs on the same hardware show the spread: Mistral says it trained Mistral Large 4, which has the same number of active parameters as DeepSeek-V4-Pro, on 3,800 Grace Blackwell GPUs, while Microsoft says it trained MAI-Base-2, a model of similar size trained on more data, on 16,000 in 38 days. At that pace the two largest runs here would have needed about 8,000 to 9,000 Blackwell GPUs. The figures cover only the final training run of each model, not experiments, earlier runs or serving.
Revised November 12, 2026. The first version of this report gave 2,000 to 3,000 Blackwell GPUs for the largest runs and $150 million to $200 million for the largest cluster. Those figures assumed the GPUs ran as efficiently as NVIDIA’s own benchmarks, and training runs disclosed on Blackwell have been less efficient. The estimates above replace them. We have also added H100 equivalents and estimated run lengths.
Our investigation
A model’s weights, also called parameters, are the billions of adjustable numbers that determine how it processes information and produces answers. Training repeatedly adjusts these numbers; but before training can begin, they need initial values. In the software examined here, those values come from a pseudorandom number generation (PRNG) algorithm called Philox: Philox produces seemingly random numbers in a deterministic way. The starting input for Philox is a number called the “seed.” Given the same seed and settings, Philox produces the same numbers every time. This lets engineers repeat an experiment from the same starting point.
To fill billions of weights quickly, PyTorch, the software framework used in the development of most major AI models, divides the work that Philox does among many parallel workers on the GPU, called threads. (It does this through cuRAND, NVIDIA’s library for random number generation.) Each thread generates its own sequence of numbers from the seed and fills a particular set of positions. The number of threads then determines how those positions are shared out. On the NVIDIA chips examined here, a sufficiently large initialization uses the maximum of 2,048 threads for each processing unit, called a streaming multiprocessor (SM). A chip with more SMs therefore assigns the same job to more workers, changing where their numbers go.
Figure 1 illustrates how this works on a simplified GPU with six SMs and eight threads per SM, giving 48 threads in total. Imagine the weights as a long row of empty boxes, wrapped into a grid to fit on the page. The first thread fills the first box, the second fills the second box, and so on through all 48 threads. The first thread’s next number then goes into box 49, its next into box 97, and so forth. The black outlines follow this one thread through the grid: the first thread fills every 48th position. The colors identify which processing unit supplies each set of values, while the brightness in the right-hand panel represents the value itself.
Now keep the seed fixed, but use a GPU with five SMs instead of six. In our simplified example, that means 40 threads rather than 48. The first thread generates exactly the same sequence, but now puts its numbers in boxes 1, 41, 81, and so on, rather than boxes 1, 49, 97. Figure 2 follows these numbers as their positions change. Across the grid, only 6 percent of the values match the original arrangement. The seed is right, but the worker count is wrong. That is what makes the arrangement of model weights a clue to the chip used to create it.
Reproducing the weights therefore requires two things to be right at once. The seed must generate the right numbers, and the thread count must put them in the right places. A correct seed with the wrong thread count mostly gives the right numbers in the wrong positions; a correct thread count with the wrong seed gives different numbers. Figure 3 shows why only the correct combination recreates the original arrangement.
On real chips, the thread counts are much larger, but the principle is the same. In the NVIDIA configurations examined here, the Hopper H100 SXM, H200 and H800 have 132 SMs and use 270,336 threads; the Blackwell B200/B300 have 148 SMs and use 303,104 threads; and the Blackwell GB200/GB300 have 152 SMs and use 311,296 threads. Recovering the thread count therefore tells us which of these hardware groups matches the starting arrangement, although it cannot distinguish chips within a group. A Hopper match, for example, cannot tell an H800 from an H100 or H200.
The question is whether enough of that arrangement survives training for us to recover it. Some weights, such as those reserved for words or symbols never encountered during training, can remain untouched and retain their starting values almost exactly. We can compare these preserved values directly with the starting values generated by a candidate seed and thread count. Figure 4 shows such a comparison: DeepSeek-V3’s preserved weights match the Hopper arrangement, while DeepSeek-V4-Flash’s match the Blackwell arrangement.
Even weights that training has changed can retain a faint trace of their starting values. Each final weight is its starting value plus all the adjustments made during training. If those adjustments do not completely cancel or overwhelm the starting differences, some of those differences survive. For example, two weights that begin at 0.2 and 0.8 and each increase by about 0.1 will still be ordered the same way afterward. Real adjustments vary much more, so one weight tells us little. But across hundreds of thousands of weights, we can test whether higher starting values still tend to correspond to higher final values. Figure 5 shows this weak but measurable relationship. It is a finding in these weights, not a property guaranteed to survive every training run.
These surviving traces let us work backward. Suppose, in an invented example using Figure 1’s layout, seed 7 makes the first thread produce 0.2, −0.5 and 0.8. With 48 threads, those values go into positions 1, 49 and 97; with 40 threads, they go into positions 1, 41 and 81. If the published weights preserve the first pattern, we can try candidate seeds and thread counts until we recreate it. In the actual investigation, we compare thousands of values in their corresponding positions. Untouched weights can match exactly; trained weights may show only the weaker resemblance described above. In either case, we look for the combination that best reproduces the surviving pattern.
We then test that combination on weights we kept out of the search. Having chosen a seed and thread count using one set of weights, we use them to predict the starting values of a different set, without changing either setting to improve the fit. A match on these previously unused weights is stronger evidence than a match confined to the weights that helped us choose the combination. Figure 6 shows the comparisons: DeepSeek-V3 matches the initialization layout of Hopper H100 SXM, H200 or H800 chips; DeepSeek-V4-Flash matches Blackwell B200/B300; and Kimi K3 matches Blackwell GB200/GB300.
In our investigations, we found patterns strongly suggestive of initialization on Blackwell chips in six models from four Chinese companies: DeepSeek-V4-Flash and DeepSeek-V4-Pro from DeepSeek, Kimi K3 and Kimi K3.1-Pro from Moonshot AI, MiniMax-M3 from MiniMax, and Ling-3.1-flash from Ant Group. V4-Flash and MiniMax-M3 match the 148-SM layout; the other four match the 152-SM layout. These are fingerprints of the starting weights, which support, but do not by themselves prove, the use of Blackwell chips for training AI models.
Limits of our investigation
Notably, the forensic method we used to identify the hardware on which these models were initialized only works for certain models. It needs published weights, so closed models cannot be checked. It also needs a recognizable, hardware-dependent pattern from the software that generated the starting weights. PyTorch on the GPUs examined here leaves such a pattern; Baidu’s PaddlePaddle framework, which was used to train Baidu’s ERNIE-5.5 model, does not. We were able to recover the seed for ERNIE-5.5 (2760), but could not identify which hardware was used to initialize it. Likewise, weights initialized on an ordinary processor rather than a GPU leave no GPU fingerprint; several of Alibaba’s Qwen3 models were initialized this way, and thus leave no trace of which GPUs were used to train them. We also found no fingerprint in Meituan’s LongCat-2.0, which the company says was trained on “AI ASIC superpods,” or in DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, despite an exhaustive search.
The method identifies the hardware that generated the starting weights, not every chip that later helped train the model. When a training run creates its weights on the same GPUs that will train it, the fingerprint points to both. But a lab could generate the weights on one system and move them to another, or continue training an existing model on new hardware. The weights alone cannot rule out those possibilities. A mixed cluster introduces the same limitation. If NVIDIA chips generated some weights and Huawei chips later helped train them, the NVIDIA pattern could survive without revealing Huawei’s participation. Different NVIDIA generations could leave different patterns in the parts they initialized, if those traces survived. In Kimi K3, at least 64 separately seeded processes each left the same 152-SM layout. A Blackwell-compatible pattern therefore supports its use at initialization, subject to the AMD alternative below; it does not establish that the entire training run used Blackwell. A Hopper pattern likewise does not rule out later training on Blackwell.
The weights also cannot reveal who owned the chips or where they were located. Blackwell systems can be rented from data centers outside China. The evidence concerns access to the hardware; by itself, it does not establish that chips were smuggled into China or that an export-control violation occurred.
Could a different chip produce one of these Blackwell-compatible patterns? Among the NVIDIA configurations tested here, the older chips cannot: software can use fewer of a chip’s processing units, but cannot give it more than it physically has. Under the standard PyTorch behavior examined here, they cannot produce the 148- or 152-SM layouts. The China-market Blackwell graphics cards use a different number of threads per SM and do not produce either total.
AMD presents a different possibility. An MI300X normally uses 304 processing units, but it can be divided into two groups of 152 or configured to use only 148. Its random-number software can also generate the same underlying sequences as NVIDIA’s. Such configurations would be very unusual, but we confirmed on a physical MI300X that, limited to 152 or 148 of its units, it reproduces the layouts we associate with Blackwell, so the layout alone does not settle the question.
To distinguish them, we looked for small numerical differences in the values themselves. AMD and NVIDIA perform one calculation in the number-generating process slightly differently. On a physical MI300X, DeepSeek-V4-Flash’s preserved weights point to NVIDIA: in the 377 values where the two vendors’ results differ, the published weight matches NVIDIA’s in 363 and AMD’s in none. The same test could not settle the other five models, but we consider the AMD explanation very unlikely for them. In its normal configuration, an MI300X produces neither layout; a lab would have had to configure an entire cluster to use the same number of processing units as a Blackwell chip. Other public evidence also points to Blackwell: U.S. officials and press reports have alleged that DeepSeek and Moonshot AI were using Blackwell chips, and Ant Group’s public code includes benchmark results generated on a GB300.
Verifying our findings
To reproduce the result that linked DeepSeek-V3 to Hopper and DeepSeek-V4-Flash to Blackwell, we ran unmodified PyTorch on a Hopper-class NVIDIA H100 and a Blackwell-class NVIDIA B300. We had identified DeepSeek’s seed number as 953049583, and found that it was used in the initialization of both DeepSeek-V3 and DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Using that seed, the H100 reproduced the preserved starting values in DeepSeek-V3, but not V4-Flash; the B300 reproduced the preserved values in V4-Flash, but not V3. On the B300, unmodified PyTorch reproduced 461 of the 469 selected rows in DeepSeek-V4-Flash with correlations above 0.99, including 445 entire rows that matched bit for bit. Eight selected rows were partly changed by training. On the H100, it reproduced all 57,344 preserved values in DeepSeek-V3 bit for bit. Neither chip reproduced the other model’s preserved rows. Changing the seed by one reproduced no matching rows on either machine. On a rented GB200, unmodified PyTorch likewise reproduced all 13 of Kimi K3’s preserved rows using Moonshot’s seed, 20256586; changing the seed by one reproduced none. The code and the preserved weights needed to repeat these checks are public; the same comparison also runs without a GPU.
Our forensic method also gets the right answer when the answer is already known. There are 24 cases where a developer has publicly named the hardware behind a specific model; our forensic method agrees with every one. Meta’s Llama 3.1 models, trained on H100s, read as 132; eight of EleutherAI’s Pythia models, trained on A100s, read as 108. NVIDIA’s own Nemotron-3 Ultra, whose technical report names the GB200 for one phase of training, reads as 152, the same as Kimi K3. DeepSeek and Moonshot have reported H800 use for earlier models. Our Hopper readings are consistent with those disclosures, but would also be consistent with H100 SXM or H200 hardware; they do not independently verify H800 use.
When we compare the published weights with the numbers generated by a candidate seed and chip layout, some resemblance is inevitable even if the candidate is wrong. How much resemblance counts as evidence depends on how many weights we compare: agreement across a handful of numbers could easily be accidental, while agreement across thousands is much harder to explain by chance. Our statistical model accounts for the number of weights compared and measures how far the observed match exceeds the resemblance expected by chance. We express that distance in standard deviations, or sigma. A higher score means stronger evidence against an accidental match, provided the statistical model correctly describes the random variation in the data.
We must also account for how many candidates we tried. Searching roughly six trillion combinations of seed and layout gives us trillions of opportunities to find a lucky match. Under a normal-distribution model, a positive match of five sigma or more occurs by chance about once in 3.5 million comparisons. That would be striking in a single test, but unsurprising somewhere in a search as large as ours. We therefore use approximately eight sigma as our benchmark for assessing accidental matches across the full search.
Our findings exceed that benchmark by a wide margin. We first select a seed and layout using one set of weights, and then test that same combination on a separate set that played no part in choosing it. This checks whether the apparent fingerprint extends beyond the numbers that led us to it. The strongest evidence for each of the six models ranges from 49 to 1,385 sigma; even the weakest individual test reported reaches 20 sigma. Under our statistical model, these results are far too strong to attribute to accidental resemblance. They establish evidence of a shared initialization pattern; identifying precisely which computing hardware produced that pattern, and whether it also performed the training, requires the separate arguments discussed above.
Conclusion
Based on our forensic investigation, we believe that we have strong evidence that six Chinese AI models were initialized on export-controlled NVIDIA hardware, and that for DeepSeek-V4-Flash the evidence is conclusive; we believe that this initialization also strongly suggests that they trained on the same export-controlled hardware. While this leaves unanswered other questions—such as the circumstances through which the companies that developed these models acquired export-controlled NVIDIA systems—we believe that this fact deserves urgent attention from the public.

Chance could not reach 8σ even after searching every 32-bit seed and every grid; the lowest of the six Blackwell results, on V4-Flash’s trained attention weights, is 20σ.

====================================================================================================

Chinese frontier AI models carry the fingerprint of export-controlled GPUs
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:56:11 UTC
06:56:11 KST

Top Chinese Frontier AI Models Carry the Fingerprint of Export-Controlled NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs
Executive Summary
Our forensic analysis of publicly released AI models finds evidence that DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group have gained access to NVIDIA’s Blackwell GPUs, despite restrictions on exporting these chips to China, and are using that hardware to create their AI models. Six models released by these companies since April 2026 contain numerical patterns matching Blackwell hardware, whereas earlier models from the same companies match NVIDIA’s older Hopper generation. The findings suggest that several leading Chinese AI developers have begun using Blackwell to train their newest models. U.S. officials and press reports had already alleged that DeepSeek and Moonshot AI were using Blackwell chips, without publishing technical evidence.
The evidence comes entirely from the models’ own published weights, rather than from leaks or insider accounts. We recover traces of the models’ initial values, and then search for the hardware configurations and initial configurations that reproduce them; for DeepSeek-V4-Flash and Kimi K3, we also confirmed the match on physical Blackwell chips. The fingerprint identifies how the weights were created and supports, without proving, the inference that Blackwell also performed the training. Specially configured AMD hardware, which is also restricted from export to China, is the only alternative we have identified; we have excluded it for DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and consider it very unlikely for the other five models. Notably, our findings provide technical evidence of access to advanced computing hardware, but do not establish where the chips were located, how access was obtained, or whether export controls were violated.
Table 1. Hardware fingerprints in successive models from DeepSeek, Moonshot AI, MiniMax and Ant Group Link ↗
• Requires a U.S. license for export to China; none have been reported.
A Hopper fingerprint cannot distinguish an H800 from an H100 SXM or H200; the H800 identifications above are lab disclosures, not findings of this method.
Full list of models in Appendix 2 ↓
Main Findings
- Six models from four Chinese labs read as Blackwell-trained. DeepSeek-V4-Flash and MiniMax-M3 match NVIDIA’s Blackwell-class B200 or B300 chips, which have 148 processor cores (called streaming multiprocessors, or SMs). DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Kimi K3, Kimi K3.1-Pro and Ling-3.1-flash match the GB200 or GB300 chips, also Blackwell-class, which have 152 processor cores. Kimi K3.1-Pro starts from the same seed as K3, and may be a checkpoint of K3 with further post-training.
- Chinese labs started training on Blackwell relatively recently. DeepSeek’s models read as Hopper-trained through DeepSeek-V3 (released in December 2024), and as Blackwell from DeepSeek V4 (released in April 2026). Moonshot’s models read as Hopper through Kimi K2.5 (January 2026) and as Blackwell from K3 (July 2026). One Moonshot model sits in between: Kimi K2.7-Code, posted six weeks before K3, is built on the Hopper-era weights but contains a small part that matches the B200 or B300. MiniMax’s models read as Hopper through M2.7 (April 2026) and as Blackwell from M3 (June 2026). Ant Group’s Ling-3.0-flash (August 2026) reads as Hopper, while its Ling-3.1-flash (October 2026) reads as Blackwell. The Hopper readings do not identify a specific chip: H800s, H100 SXMs and H200s share the same fingerprint.
- Not every Chinese AI lab has switched to Blackwell. The newest models we could read from Alibaba (Qwen), Zhipu (GLM), Tencent, Xiaomi and StepFun all read as Hopper-trained. Meituan says its LongCat-2.0 was trained entirely on “AI ASIC superpods,” and we found no NVIDIA fingerprint in the model weights it’s released.
- The method gets known answers right. For 24 specific models, the developer has publicly said what hardware was used. In every case, our method agrees with the public announcement. NVIDIA’s own Nemotron-3 Ultra, whose technical report names the GB200 for one phase of its training, reads as 152 SMs, the same as Kimi K3.
- The matches are far stronger than chance would explain. Even unrelated numbers can appear to match when enough combinations are tried. Across the roughly 6 trillion seed-and-layout combinations in our search, the strongest chance match would be expected to reach about 8 sigma—a measure of how far a result stands above ordinary random variation. The strongest evidence for each of the six models ranges from 49 to 1,385 sigma. Even the weakest individual test we report reaches 20 sigma.
- The training runs were data-center scale. From each model’s size and the amount of data it was trained on, we estimate that the two largest runs, Kimi K3 and DeepSeek-V4-Pro, each needed thousands of Blackwell GPUs for two to three months, assuming each model was trained on the hardware that initialized it. Our best estimates are about 4,500 and 3,000, the equivalent of about 11,500 and 7,500 H100s, the previous-generation NVIDIA chip in which AI training clusters are commonly sized, and about 12,000 Blackwell GPUs across the five runs (Table 2). At roughly $70,000 per GPU—the cost implied by a Wall Street Journal report that about 36,000 Blackwell chips being installed in Malaysia for ByteDance’s use would cost more than $2.5 billion—the largest of these clusters would cost roughly $200 million to $300 million, and the five together more than half a billion dollars. These figures cover only the final training runs; the labs’ total spending on export-controlled GPUs is likely in the billions of dollars.
Table 2. Rough scale of each training run Link ↗
Each estimate is a run’s training compute divided by what one Blackwell GPU delivers over the run, rounded to the nearest 500. Training compute is about six operations per active parameter for every training token, plus attention. We assume the GPUs ran at about 20% of their peak speed, in line with other disclosed Blackwell training runs (25% for DeepSeek, which has reported 39% for its previous model), and for the run lengths shown, which we estimate from release timelines. H100 equivalents convert at NVIDIA’s published peak speeds: a B200 or B300 equals about 2.3 H100s, and a GB200 or GB300 about 2.5.
We are not confident in these estimates. They rest on assumptions that the weights cannot confirm: how efficiently each lab used its GPUs (often called MFU), which no lab has published for these runs; how many tokens each model was trained on (DeepSeek’s counts are its own, and we take them on trust; the others are our estimates, made from the weights by a method we have not yet published); how long each run lasted, which no lab has stated; and that training stayed on the hardware that created the weights. Run length matters most, because halving it doubles the GPU count. Two disclosed runs on the same hardware show the spread: Mistral says it trained Mistral Large 4, which has the same number of active parameters as DeepSeek-V4-Pro, on 3,800 Grace Blackwell GPUs, while Microsoft says it trained MAI-Base-2, a model of similar size trained on more data, on 16,000 in 38 days. At that pace the two largest runs here would have needed about 8,000 to 9,000 Blackwell GPUs. The figures cover only the final training run of each model, not experiments, earlier runs or serving.
Revised November 12, 2026. The first version of this report gave 2,000 to 3,000 Blackwell GPUs for the largest runs and $150 million to $200 million for the largest cluster. Those figures assumed the GPUs ran as efficiently as NVIDIA’s own benchmarks, and training runs disclosed on Blackwell have been less efficient. The estimates above replace them. We have also added H100 equivalents and estimated run lengths.
Our investigation
A model’s weights, also called parameters, are the billions of adjustable numbers that determine how it processes information and produces answers. Training repeatedly adjusts these numbers; but before training can begin, they need initial values. In the software examined here, those values come from a pseudorandom number generation (PRNG) algorithm called Philox: Philox produces seemingly random numbers in a deterministic way. The starting input for Philox is a number called the “seed.” Given the same seed and settings, Philox produces the same numbers every time. This lets engineers repeat an experiment from the same starting point.
To fill billions of weights quickly, PyTorch, the software framework used in the development of most major AI models, divides the work that Philox does among many parallel workers on the GPU, called threads. (It does this through cuRAND, NVIDIA’s library for random number generation.) Each thread generates its own sequence of numbers from the seed and fills a particular set of positions. The number of threads then determines how those positions are shared out. On the NVIDIA chips examined here, a sufficiently large initialization uses the maximum of 2,048 threads for each processing unit, called a streaming multiprocessor (SM). A chip with more SMs therefore assigns the same job to more workers, changing where their numbers go.
Figure 1 illustrates how this works on a simplified GPU with six SMs and eight threads per SM, giving 48 threads in total. Imagine the weights as a long row of empty boxes, wrapped into a grid to fit on the page. The first thread fills the first box, the second fills the second box, and so on through all 48 threads. The first thread’s next number then goes into box 49, its next into box 97, and so forth. The black outlines follow this one thread through the grid: the first thread fills every 48th position. The colors identify which processing unit supplies each set of values, while the brightness in the right-hand panel represents the value itself.
Now keep the seed fixed, but use a GPU with five SMs instead of six. In our simplified example, that means 40 threads rather than 48. The first thread generates exactly the same sequence, but now puts its numbers in boxes 1, 41, 81, and so on, rather than boxes 1, 49, 97. Figure 2 follows these numbers as their positions change. Across the grid, only 6 percent of the values match the original arrangement. The seed is right, but the worker count is wrong. That is what makes the arrangement of model weights a clue to the chip used to create it.
Reproducing the weights therefore requires two things to be right at once. The seed must generate the right numbers, and the thread count must put them in the right places. A correct seed with the wrong thread count mostly gives the right numbers in the wrong positions; a correct thread count with the wrong seed gives different numbers. Figure 3 shows why only the correct combination recreates the original arrangement.
On real chips, the thread counts are much larger, but the principle is the same. In the NVIDIA configurations examined here, the Hopper H100 SXM, H200 and H800 have 132 SMs and use 270,336 threads; the Blackwell B200/B300 have 148 SMs and use 303,104 threads; and the Blackwell GB200/GB300 have 152 SMs and use 311,296 threads. Recovering the thread count therefore tells us which of these hardware groups matches the starting arrangement, although it cannot distinguish chips within a group. A Hopper match, for example, cannot tell an H800 from an H100 or H200.
The question is whether enough of that arrangement survives training for us to recover it. Some weights, such as those reserved for words or symbols never encountered during training, can remain untouched and retain their starting values almost exactly. We can compare these preserved values directly with the starting values generated by a candidate seed and thread count. Figure 4 shows such a comparison: DeepSeek-V3’s preserved weights match the Hopper arrangement, while DeepSeek-V4-Flash’s match the Blackwell arrangement.
Even weights that training has changed can retain a faint trace of their starting values. Each final weight is its starting value plus all the adjustments made during training. If those adjustments do not completely cancel or overwhelm the starting differences, some of those differences survive. For example, two weights that begin at 0.2 and 0.8 and each increase by about 0.1 will still be ordered the same way afterward. Real adjustments vary much more, so one weight tells us little. But across hundreds of thousands of weights, we can test whether higher starting values still tend to correspond to higher final values. Figure 5 shows this weak but measurable relationship. It is a finding in these weights, not a property guaranteed to survive every training run.
These surviving traces let us work backward. Suppose, in an invented example using Figure 1’s layout, seed 7 makes the first thread produce 0.2, −0.5 and 0.8. With 48 threads, those values go into positions 1, 49 and 97; with 40 threads, they go into positions 1, 41 and 81. If the published weights preserve the first pattern, we can try candidate seeds and thread counts until we recreate it. In the actual investigation, we compare thousands of values in their corresponding positions. Untouched weights can match exactly; trained weights may show only the weaker resemblance described above. In either case, we look for the combination that best reproduces the surviving pattern.
We then test that combination on weights we kept out of the search. Having chosen a seed and thread count using one set of weights, we use them to predict the starting values of a different set, without changing either setting to improve the fit. A match on these previously unused weights is stronger evidence than a match confined to the weights that helped us choose the combination. Figure 6 shows the comparisons: DeepSeek-V3 matches the initialization layout of Hopper H100 SXM, H200 or H800 chips; DeepSeek-V4-Flash matches Blackwell B200/B300; and Kimi K3 matches Blackwell GB200/GB300.
In our investigations, we found patterns strongly suggestive of initialization on Blackwell chips in six models from four Chinese companies: DeepSeek-V4-Flash and DeepSeek-V4-Pro from DeepSeek, Kimi K3 and Kimi K3.1-Pro from Moonshot AI, MiniMax-M3 from MiniMax, and Ling-3.1-flash from Ant Group. V4-Flash and MiniMax-M3 match the 148-SM layout; the other four match the 152-SM layout. These are fingerprints of the starting weights, which support, but do not by themselves prove, the use of Blackwell chips for training AI models.
Limits of our investigation
Notably, the forensic method we used to identify the hardware on which these models were initialized only works for certain models. It needs published weights, so closed models cannot be checked. It also needs a recognizable, hardware-dependent pattern from the software that generated the starting weights. PyTorch on the GPUs examined here leaves such a pattern; Baidu’s PaddlePaddle framework, which was used to train Baidu’s ERNIE-5.5 model, does not. We were able to recover the seed for ERNIE-5.5 (2760), but could not identify which hardware was used to initialize it. Likewise, weights initialized on an ordinary processor rather than a GPU leave no GPU fingerprint; several of Alibaba’s Qwen3 models were initialized this way, and thus leave no trace of which GPUs were used to train them. We also found no fingerprint in Meituan’s LongCat-2.0, which the company says was trained on “AI ASIC superpods,” or in DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, despite an exhaustive search.
The method identifies the hardware that generated the starting weights, not every chip that later helped train the model. When a training run creates its weights on the same GPUs that will train it, the fingerprint points to both. But a lab could generate the weights on one system and move them to another, or continue training an existing model on new hardware. The weights alone cannot rule out those possibilities. A mixed cluster introduces the same limitation. If NVIDIA chips generated some weights and Huawei chips later helped train them, the NVIDIA pattern could survive without revealing Huawei’s participation. Different NVIDIA generations could leave different patterns in the parts they initialized, if those traces survived. In Kimi K3, at least 64 separately seeded processes each left the same 152-SM layout. A Blackwell-compatible pattern therefore supports its use at initialization, subject to the AMD alternative below; it does not establish that the entire training run used Blackwell. A Hopper pattern likewise does not rule out later training on Blackwell.
The weights also cannot reveal who owned the chips or where they were located. Blackwell systems can be rented from data centers outside China. The evidence concerns access to the hardware; by itself, it does not establish that chips were smuggled into China or that an export-control violation occurred.
Could a different chip produce one of these Blackwell-compatible patterns? Among the NVIDIA configurations tested here, the older chips cannot: software can use fewer of a chip’s processing units, but cannot give it more than it physically has. Under the standard PyTorch behavior examined here, they cannot produce the 148- or 152-SM layouts. The China-market Blackwell graphics cards use a different number of threads per SM and do not produce either total.
AMD presents a different possibility. An MI300X normally uses 304 processing units, but it can be divided into two groups of 152 or configured to use only 148. Its random-number software can also generate the same underlying sequences as NVIDIA’s. Such configurations would be very unusual, but we confirmed on a physical MI300X that, limited to 152 or 148 of its units, it reproduces the layouts we associate with Blackwell, so the layout alone does not settle the question.
To distinguish them, we looked for small numerical differences in the values themselves. AMD and NVIDIA perform one calculation in the number-generating process slightly differently. On a physical MI300X, DeepSeek-V4-Flash’s preserved weights point to NVIDIA: in the 377 values where the two vendors’ results differ, the published weight matches NVIDIA’s in 363 and AMD’s in none. The same test could not settle the other five models, but we consider the AMD explanation very unlikely for them. In its normal configuration, an MI300X produces neither layout; a lab would have had to configure an entire cluster to use the same number of processing units as a Blackwell chip. Other public evidence also points to Blackwell: U.S. officials and press reports have alleged that DeepSeek and Moonshot AI were using Blackwell chips, and Ant Group’s public code includes benchmark results generated on a GB300.
Verifying our findings
To reproduce the result that linked DeepSeek-V3 to Hopper and DeepSeek-V4-Flash to Blackwell, we ran unmodified PyTorch on a Hopper-class NVIDIA H100 and a Blackwell-class NVIDIA B300. We had identified DeepSeek’s seed number as 953049583, and found that it was used in the initialization of both DeepSeek-V3 and DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Using that seed, the H100 reproduced the preserved starting values in DeepSeek-V3, but not V4-Flash; the B300 reproduced the preserved values in V4-Flash, but not V3. On the B300, unmodified PyTorch reproduced 461 of the 469 selected rows in DeepSeek-V4-Flash with correlations above 0.99, including 445 entire rows that matched bit for bit. Eight selected rows were partly changed by training. On the H100, it reproduced all 57,344 preserved values in DeepSeek-V3 bit for bit. Neither chip reproduced the other model’s preserved rows. Changing the seed by one reproduced no matching rows on either machine. On a rented GB200, unmodified PyTorch likewise reproduced all 13 of Kimi K3’s preserved rows using Moonshot’s seed, 20256586; changing the seed by one reproduced none. The code and the preserved weights needed to repeat these checks are public; the same comparison also runs without a GPU.
Our forensic method also gets the right answer when the answer is already known. There are 24 cases where a developer has publicly named the hardware behind a specific model; our forensic method agrees with every one. Meta’s Llama 3.1 models, trained on H100s, read as 132; eight of EleutherAI’s Pythia models, trained on A100s, read as 108. NVIDIA’s own Nemotron-3 Ultra, whose technical report names the GB200 for one phase of training, reads as 152, the same as Kimi K3. DeepSeek and Moonshot have reported H800 use for earlier models. Our Hopper readings are consistent with those disclosures, but would also be consistent with H100 SXM or H200 hardware; they do not independently verify H800 use.
When we compare the published weights with the numbers generated by a candidate seed and chip layout, some resemblance is inevitable even if the candidate is wrong. How much resemblance counts as evidence depends on how many weights we compare: agreement across a handful of numbers could easily be accidental, while agreement across thousands is much harder to explain by chance. Our statistical model accounts for the number of weights compared and measures how far the observed match exceeds the resemblance expected by chance. We express that distance in standard deviations, or sigma. A higher score means stronger evidence against an accidental match, provided the statistical model correctly describes the random variation in the data.
We must also account for how many candidates we tried. Searching roughly six trillion combinations of seed and layout gives us trillions of opportunities to find a lucky match. Under a normal-distribution model, a positive match of five sigma or more occurs by chance about once in 3.5 million comparisons. That would be striking in a single test, but unsurprising somewhere in a search as large as ours. We therefore use approximately eight sigma as our benchmark for assessing accidental matches across the full search.
Our findings exceed that benchmark by a wide margin. We first select a seed and layout using one set of weights, and then test that same combination on a separate set that played no part in choosing it. This checks whether the apparent fingerprint extends beyond the numbers that led us to it. The strongest evidence for each of the six models ranges from 49 to 1,385 sigma; even the weakest individual test reported reaches 20 sigma. Under our statistical model, these results are far too strong to attribute to accidental resemblance. They establish evidence of a shared initialization pattern; identifying precisely which computing hardware produced that pattern, and whether it also performed the training, requires the separate arguments discussed above.
Conclusion
Based on our forensic investigation, we believe that we have strong evidence that six Chinese AI models were initialized on export-controlled NVIDIA hardware, and that for DeepSeek-V4-Flash the evidence is conclusive; we believe that this initialization also strongly suggests that they trained on the same export-controlled hardware. While this leaves unanswered other questions—such as the circumstances through which the companies that developed these models acquired export-controlled NVIDIA systems—we believe that this fact deserves urgent attention from the public.
Appendix 1: Each Chinese lab’s hardware over timeHardware fingerprints across successive models from each lab.
DeepSeek reuses one seed across model generations, and Moonshot uses one rule to derive its seeds, so we can follow each lab’s hardware from model to model. Dates are when the weights were posted.
How to read the evidence column. Exact copy: weights that training never updated reproduce the generated values with a correlation (r) above 0.999. Untrained rows: the same, for models whose starting values were clipped or rounded, which lowers r. Partial survival: trained weights keep part of their starting values. Figures from grid scans use cells held out of the search. Chance could not reach 8σ even after searching every 32-bit seed and every grid; the lowest of the six Blackwell results, on V4-Flash’s trained attention weights, is 20σ.
† Result from one analysis pass, not yet re-run.
Kimi K2.7-Code. Its 254 reserved rows are a blend of two sources: about 80% are the Hopper-layout values inherited from K2.5, and about 20% are a new draw that matches the 148-SM layout. The blend is the same in every row, which points to a merged checkpoint rather than mixed hardware; K2.7-Code is not counted among the six Blackwell models.
Public code. Ant Group’s cuLA repository includes benchmark results generated on a GB300 on March 31, 2026. In June 2025 a DeepSeek engineer wrote on GitHub that “we don’t have SM100 GPUs” (SM100 is Blackwell).
Appendix 2: Every model identifiedComplete model catalogue, disclosed hardware, and evidence notes.
Our readings are consistent with all 24 cases where a developer has publicly stated the hardware for a specific model: 21 from our original survey, plus Kimi K2.5 (H800), Step-3.5-Flash (H800) and ZAYA1 (AMD MI300X). Eleven of these are step-zero checkpoints, and related models are not independent, so this is a consistency check rather than a measured accuracy rate. “None found” means a search of the model’s card, paper and repository history turned up no statement about hardware.
¹ Report-wide disclosure. DeepSeek-V2 and V2-Lite share one “H800 cluster” sentence, MoE-16B says “A100 or H800,” and Coder says “A100 and H800.” MiniMax’s MiniMax-01 paper describes a cluster of H800s, and Ant Group’s Ling 2.0 report says its training ran on “Hopper GPUs.” These are consistent with our readings but are not independent tests. V2-Lite reuses MoE-16B’s initialization: their preserved rows are byte-identical.
² License required. The B200, B300, GB200 and GB300 have needed a U.S. export license for China since before they shipped, and none have been reported. The AMD MI300X is also controlled.
³ Compliant variant existed. The A100/H100 class was controlled from October 2022, but the A800 and H800, which have the same SM counts, could be exported until October 2023.
⁴ Unresolved Hopper hardware. These models were first published from December 2024 onward and show a 132-SM fingerprint compatible with H800, H100 SXM or H200 hardware. DeepSeek and Moonshot have reported H800 use for some models, but the fingerprint cannot distinguish these chips. A model’s release date does not establish when or where its hardware was acquired, and this reading alone does not establish export-control compliance.
⁵ 148 part. See the note on Kimi K2.7-Code under the results table.
† Reported by one analysis pass and not yet independently re-run. In every other row added for this brief, the first model named was re-run independently; models listed as sharing its initialization were not.
These flags describe the hardware class under the U.S. export rules of October 2022 and October 2023. They are not a finding that any law was broken.
No GPU signature recovered. A blank says nothing about a model’s hardware. We recovered no signature from DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash (after an exhaustive search), Qwen3.5-122B, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, Qwen3-0.6B and 1.7B, gpt-oss-120b and Grok-1. Qwen3-235B, Qwen3-32B and Qwen3.5-9B were initialized on the CPU, which leaves no GPU layout. Baidu’s ERNIE-5.5 uses PaddlePaddle, whose generator carries no chip layout (we recovered its seed, 2760); ERNIE-4.5 gave no result. Meituan’s LongCat-2.0 gave no result. Its model card says training ran “entirely on AI ASIC superpods,” and a since-deleted fine-tuning configuration sets device_arch to 910 with NPU and HCCL options, which we read as Huawei Ascend 910. Intern-S2 is built on Alibaba’s Qwen3.5-397B and carries its initialization. DBRX and Llama-4-Scout could not be downloaded.
Code. The code and preserved rows needed to reproduce the results for DeepSeek-V3, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, Kimi K3, MiniMax-M3 and Ling-3.1-flash are at github.com/macrogrants/checkpoint-forensics. The check runs on a laptop in a few seconds, and a second script repeats it with unmodified PyTorch on a GPU.

Google’s new Gemini model is better at understanding complicated questions
Google’s Robby Stein says Gemini 4 Hydrogen is ‘the biggest model upgrade to Search yet.’

‘It’s a mistake’: reactions from tech leaders on Jensen Huang’s resignation

Nvidia announced this morning that Jensen Huang has resigned as CEO following reports of two affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy and one with a direct report. The situation surrounding Huang has unfolded quickly, with The New York Times only reporting on Friday that Huang and the suspected spy were “close” before other dominoes started to fall, leading to a swift fall from grace for one of the biggest names in technology. (Hours after Huang’s resignation was announced, Reuters reported that he also had an “intimate relationship” with another Nvidia employee.)
‘It’s a mistake’: reactions from tech leaders on Jensen Huang’s resignation
Many prominent voices in technology and the president of the US have publicly supported the former Nvidia CEO despite his ouster.
Prominent voices in technology have come out in support of Huang, with some calling the resignation a “waste” and a sad day for “all humanity.” The narrative among those leaders on X, at least, is that Huang’s resignation was rushed — perhaps an overreaction to salacious news stories about his personal life. Some others have had a more neutral reaction, commenting more on Huang’s achievements at the company’s helm. You can see a collection of those responses below.
President Donald Trump
“NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!”, Trump said on Truth Social.
Elon Musk, SpaceX and Tesla CEO
Elon Musk said in a reply to an X user that it would “be an honor” to hire Huang. He also replied to a post that said “You’d think we’d have learned from Steve Jobs and Travis Kalanick that one does not just simply replace the founder…” by saying “True words.”
Tim Sweeney, Epic Games CEO
Sweeney said that today is “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction.”
Ahead of Huang’s resignation, Sweeney posted about how Huang “saved” a GDC 2015 presentation by dropping off a graphics card and said that “The dude truly is the visionary driver of the future of tech from the gaming GPU era to CUDA to AI supercomputers.”
Palmer Luckey, Anduril founder
“China wants Jensen out, so I want him in,” Luckey said last night, before Huang’s resignation was announced.
Matt Mullenweg, Automattic co-founder and CEO
Mullenweg reposted an X post that followed his brief September ouster as CEO: “Founder advice: If you don’t have a coup attempt every few years, you’re not hiring strong enough leaders.” He said, “Just putting this back out there, for no reason whatsoever.”
David Heinemeier Hansson, 37signals CTO and creator of the Omarchy Linux distribution
“What a waste,” Hansson said this morning, replying to his own post from hours before: “NVIDIA would be wrong to fire Jensen. His marital indiscretions are between him, his wife, and a reprimand from the board. Have some proportionality.”
Clément Delangue, co-founder and CEO of Hugging Face (which Nvidia recently acquired for almost $13 billion)
“This whole thing sucks, and I wish none of it had happened,” Delangue said. “It’s a really sad day! But I want to make one thing clear: we’ll keep fighting for open-source AI, harder than ever. This is too important to slow down now. Let’s go!
Changpeng Zhao, former Binance CEO
“It’s a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign,” Zhao said.
Peter Steinberger, OpenClaw creator and OpenAI employee
“If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” Steinberger said. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.” (“I agree,” replied Sweeney.)
Aravind Srinivas, Perplexity co-founder and CEO
Srinivas thanked Huang for “33 years of bold leadership” in a post this morning. “You built a small startup into America’s most valuable company, with relentless improvement. Your grit and perseverance should always be remembered. Your personal generosity with time and advice to founders like me has secured a bright future for America and AI.”
Jason Calacanis, All-In Podcast host
Calacanis reposted Trump’s post, tagging Tim Cook and telling him to “Keep the seat warm” for Huang while “the investigation is sorted out” and saying that “America needs you Mr. Cook.” Last night, he also said that Nvidia shareholders should “decide Jensen’s fate after the investigation is complete — not the press or online mobs.”
Lex Fridman, Lex Fridman Podcast host
In a post talking about how he has seen Huang show “kindness, humility, care to everyone around him,” Fridman said that “I’m in shock frankly at all of this, including the mass hysteria around it. I love America, and I think NVIDIA letting Jensen go is terrible for America. I stand with Jensen.”
