Show HN: RealtimeVAD – trading false positives for 110ms earlier end-of-speech
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:30:57 UTC
06:30:57 KST

Know that the speaker stopped, about 100 ms before Silero does.
RealtimeSTT · RealtimeTTS · Live demo · Spec · LiveKit plugin
A voice assistant can't do anything until it knows the user has stopped talking. Speech recognition, the LLM call and the voice output all wait for that one decision, and a normal voice activity detector takes a few hundred milliseconds to make it.
RealtimeVAD makes it sooner. It keeps Silero VAD to decide what is speech, and adds a second, faster check: every 10 ms it compares the audio level with the room's noise and with the speaker's own level. The moment the audio goes quiet, you get an event, long before Silero's probability has fallen.
- Input: 16 kHz mono audio in chunks of any size (float32, int16 or int16 bytes).
- Output: start, pause and end events, plus the audio of the running segment at any time.
- Lead: speech-off a median 100 ms before Silero's probability drops, and 206 ms before Silero's VADIterator reports the end, on 3,279 real turns. The end itself comes before Silero's in 82 % of turns, by a median 70 ms.
- Price: it also fires in short gaps between words, about ten times as often as Silero.
- Quiet input: a quiet speaker's soft word endings are not silence; on a noisy quiet line the lead is gone instead (details).
- Runs on: one CPU thread, far faster than realtime. Needs only numpy and onnxruntime; the Silero model is bundled, no torch.
- Status: alpha (0.0.5). The API may still change.
demo.mp4
The video uses a synthetic voice and shows a median lead of 92 ms. The 100 ms above is the median on real voice-assistant turns.
demo/ runs this page on your own microphone. It shows, turn by turn, how much sooner RealtimeVAD knew that you stopped than Silero alone, on the same audio.
pip install realtimevadfrom RealtimeVAD import VAD
vad = VAD()
for chunk in microphone_chunks():
    for event in vad.feed(chunk):
        if event.kind == "start":
            stream_to_asr(vad.audio())       # includes the audio just before speech began
        elif event.kind == "pause":
            speculate(vad.audio())           # the speaker may be done; this isn't the end yet
        elif event.kind == "end":
            finalize(event.segment.audio)
vad.audio() returns the running segment at any time, starting about 0.3 s
before the speech. end carries the finished segment with its audio, start
time, end time and the time the speech stopped.
If your assistant talks while the microphone is open, pass
vad.feed(chunk, playback=True) for those chunks so its own voice is handled
as possible echo. Every threshold and duration can be configured;
SPEC.md lists what is guaranteed.
pause is early and often wrong, on purpose. It fires on every breath, every
short gap between words and every stop consonant, so most pause events are
followed by more speech.
- Use it to start work you can cancel: finalize the transcript, call the
LLM, prepare the voice output. Cancel when speech continues. A wrong pause then costs almost nothing, and a right one saves hundreds of milliseconds.
- Never treat it as the end of the turn. Only end closes a segment.
- Can't cancel quickly? Ignore pause and wait for end. The extra
false alarms would cost you more than they save.
Change the import:
from silero_vad import load_silero_vad
from RealtimeVAD import VADIterator          # was: from silero_vad import VADIterator
vad_iterator = VADIterator(load_silero_vad(), sampling_rate=16000)
speech_dict = vad_iterator(chunk, return_seconds=True)   # {'start': ...} / {'end': ...} / None
The constructor and the results stay the same, but end arrives as soon as the
audio goes quiet. Without a model argument, it uses the bundled Silero model and
doesn't need torch.
livekit-plugins-realtimevad/
is a VAD plugin: realtimevad.VAD.load() in place of silero.VAD.load(). Its
README shows how the events map and what it measured against the Silero plugin
on the same audio. In short: against the Silero plugin's defaults the end of
speech comes about 380 ms sooner, but most of that is the shorter silence
setting (0.25 s instead of 0.55 s), which Silero can be given too. At the
same setting RealtimeVAD's end is 65 to 110 ms earlier, and LiveKit's turn
detector starts 95 to 180 ms earlier. Inside a LiveKit session the turn was
committed about 150 ms sooner than with Silero's defaults and about 70 ms
sooner than with Silero at 0.25 s.
Measured on 3,279 turns from a live voice assistant, each with its trailing
silence, with default settings. Silero's decisions are computed from the very
same frame probabilities, so both see identical input. "Speech-off" is
RealtimeVAD's last pause before the turn ended, against the first frame of
Silero's final run below its threshold.
Until 0.0.4 the first row read 110 ms. The 10 ms went into not cutting quiet speakers, see the next section.
The end event, against the end Silero's own silence count gives with the
same thresholds and the same 0.24 s (2,323 turns where both closed the same
segments inside the recording):
Up to 0.0.4 a single loud 10 ms block inside the quiet after a word (a click, a lip smack, a breath) stopped the quiet count for good and left the end to Silero. That happened in a third of 680 sampled turns. The count now starts again after it.
Reproduce with your own recordings (16 kHz mono WAVs, one turn each, with trailing silence):
python benchmarks/speech_off_lead.py <wav dir> out.json
Up to 0.0.4, "quiet" had a fixed floor (quiet_rms, about −54 dBFS). For a
speaker around −32 dBFS that is fine. For a quiet one (a phone line recorded at
source level, a distant microphone) the floor sits only a few dB under the
speech, their soft word endings counted as silence, and 0.24 s later the
segment ended while they were still talking. Since 0.0.5 the floor follows
the speaker: it is quiet_rms or 20 dB below the speaker's own level,
whichever is lower (floor_margin_db).
The same 3,401 turns, attenuated and sent through a simulated phone line (300–3400 Hz, 8 kHz, μ-law, line noise). "Ended while talking" counts turns with an end that the same detector does not make at the recorded level and that is followed by more speech. "Silero alone" is the same VAD with the quiet clock off, on the same audio.
What this says, and what it doesn't:
- Quiet speakers are no longer cut. Ends while the speaker talks and lost speech are now where Silero alone has them. In all of these runs RealtimeVAD's last end was never later than Silero's.
- A noisy quiet line has no lead. When the noise is less than about 20 dB below the speaker (the room-noise row, and the quiet phone line with its line noise), nothing can be called quiet without calling soft speech quiet. RealtimeVAD then ends where Silero does: correct, and no faster.
- Very quiet and narrowband speech is a Silero limit. At −62 dBFS, and on the phone line, Silero itself is unsure. Speech it never scores 0.9 used to start no segment at all (476 of the phone-line turns, 1,264 at −62 dBFS). The soft start halves that (186 and 380); Silero at its own 0.5 threshold still hears more (139 and 353). More turns found also means more ends counted in the phone rows. Amplifying such input before the VAD helps Silero most.
- Borderline endings. Ends with speech of the original recording inside the 0.24 s that counted as quiet are rarer than in 0.0.4 by a factor of 10 at −52 dBFS (1,970 turns then, 182 now), but restarting the count after a click added some back (141 without it). Ends followed by more speech and lost speech did not rise with it.
- The phone path is simulated, and the reference for "still talking" is the original wideband recording. One speaker, one microphone.
What to set:
python benchmarks/quiet_input.py <wav dir> out.json
Silero is less sure about quiet speech, most of all on a phone line: it finds
fewer turns and drops out inside them. VAD(normalize_to_dbfs=-30) amplifies
the frames Silero sees, by up to 20 dB and never into clipping, until speech
sits at −30 dBFS. Levels, the quiet clock and the audio you get back use the
input as it came. Same turns as above:
It is off by default because it is not free at normal level: 2 of 3,401 turns lost more than 100 ms, speech-off was earlier than Silero in 96 % instead of 98 % of turns, and in one phone-line turn the end came later than Silero alone. It needs Silero to score the speech 0.5 at least once before it acts, so the first quiet words of a stream can still be missed. On noisy quiet input the ends moved by more than the benchmark's "ended while talking" count can judge, so that count is not given here; borderline endings did not rise.
"mm-hmm", "yeah", "okay": 200 real ones from meeting recordings (AMI corpus,
close microphone, 0.15–0.8 s long). Up to 0.0.4, 50 of them were not reported
as speech at all: Silero scored 35 between 0.5 and 0.9, below the start
threshold, and 15 below 0.5. With the soft start 25 are missed, 15 of them
Silero's own. A start comes a median 160 ms after the word begins. Whether
such a sound is a backchannel, a turn or an interruption is not a VAD
question and not answered here.
- The numbers come from one speaker and one capture chain: browser capture with noise suppression and echo cancellation, mostly quiet rooms.
- The lead needs audio that actually goes quiet after a word, and noise suppression helps that. In the 137 noisier turns (room level above −70 dBFS) the median lead was 102–124 ms. Without noise suppression in a loud room, expect less; with noise within about 20 dB of the speaker, none.
- Earlier is not the same as correct: at the recorded level, whether a speech-off sometimes lands inside a soft word ending was not checked against word timings. The quiet-input table above checks it against the same audio at normal level.
- The 100 ms are for speech-off. end waits 0.24 s after speech-off in both
cases; its own gain over Silero is smaller (a median 70 ms, none in 18 % of
turns) and depends on the noise.
- Whisper is mostly not detected (Silero scores it below 0.5). Laughter, coughs and sneezes often are.
- A DC offset in the input hides the silence from the quiet clock: no early end then, nothing lost.
- 16 kHz mono only.
pip install -e ".[dev]"
pytest
The edge-case tests in tests/audio/ run on about 100 MB of freely licensed
audio that is not in git (speech in 8 languages, noise, music, coughs and
laughter, whisper, real backchannels and hesitations). Without it they are
skipped. To get it (about 1.3 GB of downloads, checked by sha256):
python tests/audio/fetch_audio.py
python tests/audio/fetch_backchannels.py
pytest tests/audio
They take about 10 s with 8 worker processes and print what each test guards, with the measured value and its limit. Sources and licenses: LICENSES-audio.md, LICENSES-backchannels.md.
MIT. The bundled speech model is Silero VAD (MIT).

Nebius tests the neocloud playbook with $40 billion in planned spending
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:27:10 UTC
06:27:10 KST

In its third-quarter earnings call Thursday, the neocloud company Nebius laid out $40 billion in planned spending for 2027, far more than Wall Street had expected. It’s an ambitious plan, but it rests on a string of planned projects that are already starting to show cracks.
Nebius plans to fund the buildout by relying on customer pre-payments and loans backed by customer contracts — essentially using the future value of its data centers to finance their construction. But that can be a risky strategy if those projects get bogged down by delays or cancellations.
“Building at this scale is hard, and delays are part of our industry right now,” CEO Arkady Volozh said during Thursday’s earnings call. “We are not immune.”
The earnings announcement comes on the heels of reports that Microsoft is cooling on neocloud deals that aren’t delivering on time. That’s a problem for companies like Nebius, whose financing strategy is becoming more dependent on repayments. Because if it can’t get to capacity on time, it will keep piling on costs while revenues slip.
One of Nebius’ largest customers is Microsoft, which is paying for AI computing capacity at a Vineland, New Jersey, data center that is now months behind schedule. Nebius’ contract with Microsoft is worth up to $19.4 billion, but it’s already letting deadlines slip. Only just over half of the Microsoft capacity is now planned for delivery by the end of the year, with the rest coming in Q2 2027.
The delay is because DataOne, the third-party operator of the site, “decided to wind down the temporary generation faster than it was expected,” according to Andrey Korolenko, chief product and infrastructure officer, as the site switches over to Bloom Energy fuel cells.
Still, that transition wasn’t entirely by choice. In August, the investigative site Floodlight identified at least 45 natural gas generators in operation at the DataOne site, even though it had not received any permits for operating gas generators. New Jersey regulators fined the DataOne facility $1.1 million in response, finding that the use of the generators violated federal law.
DataOne is also facing a noise-complaint lawsuit from nearby residents over a constant humming noise allegedly emitted by the data center.
According to Nebius’ summary of its Microsoft contract, Microsoft can terminate affected service after a grace period if dates are missed and no alternative capacity is offered. The tranches were originally meant to be deployed in 2025 and 2026, so a Q2 2027 delivery date falls well beyond that window. The contract also includes financial penalties for late delivery.
Nebius said that Microsoft “has been part of the planning process.” Nebius did not say whether Microsoft has agreed to the new dates, but Microsoft has not publicly canceled anything.
TechCrunch has reached out to Nebius, Microsoft, and DataOne for comment.
On the call, Marc Boroditsky, Nebius’s chief revenue officer, said the company’s deals with Microsoft and Meta both came with significant prepayment, which arrived long before delivery. But Bloomberg reported last week that Microsoft is tightening oversight on projects run by third parties, including Nebius, IREN, and Nscale, and debating whether it’s worth signing new agreements. Microsoft also sent employees to the site in New Jersey, per Bloomberg.
In its own quarterly earnings report, Microsoft noted that its capex spend was lower than expected due to third-party delays. The company is clearly not above pulling back from planned agreements where its partner doesn’t meet its share of the bargain. Bloomberg also reported that Microsoft won’t use an option to more than double its Nscale agreement for a Texas data center and recently stopped being an anchor customer for Nscale’s planned Essex site after power delays.
Nebius says the delays it faces are coming from sites it doesn’t control, with the data centers it is building itself coming online on time or ahead of schedule. Those, Nebius says, will supply most of its new capacity from 2027. New deals signed in Q3 came with prepayments that more than cover the cost of building capacity behind them, says Boroditsky. Nebius ended the quarter with $7.4 billion in cash.
Total prepayments plus asset-backed financing of investment-grade contracts are expected to cover up to 85% of the $40 to $45 billion 2027 capex spend. CFO Maria Del Dado Alonso Sanchez named Microsoft and Meta as contracts that can be financed this way. Boroditsky also said Nebius could “tap prepayments to shore up any additional cash requirements.”
Nebius forecast 2027 revenue of $12 to $13 billion, and said it expects to break even on a pre-tax basis by the fourth quarter.
Nebius reported third-quarter revenue of $981 million, up 571% from a year earlier. Roughly a third of that was a one-time payment tied to a new partnership model. Nebius cut its 2026 revenue forecast by $200 million to $3 billion, citing delays to third-party sites like New Jersey. Shares ended the day down 17%.

====================================================================================================

Foundation Models framework
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:13:26 UTC
06:13:26 KST

Get started with the Foundation Models framework
The Foundation Models framework is the best way to use any large language model in your app. Whether you’re generating a quick summary or creating dynamic agents, the framework has what you need.
The Foundation Models framework is a native Swift API for integrating any large language model into your app — including direct access to the Apple Foundation Models on-device and in Private Cloud Compute. Powerful capabilities like guided generation, tool calling, image understanding, and Dynamic Profiles are built in, so you have what you need to build unique experiences.
Multimodal prompts let you pass images alongside text so your app can reason about visual content. This opens up new categories of experiences you can build with image understanding — it's as simple as attaching an image to your prompt. The Vision framework is also integrated directly, giving your model purpose-built tools like OCR for precise text extraction and barcode readers for quick code scanning — all on-device.
The Foundation Models framework gives you direct access to the on-device foundation model at the core of Apple Intelligence so you can build experiences that are smart, private, and work without internet connectivity.
On-device models are highly useful for many tasks, yet sometimes you need a larger model for a more complex workflow. The Foundation Models framework now extends to server models — like Claude, Gemini, and more — all through the same Swift API, with full support for features like tool calling and guided generation. Any model provider can create a Swift package conforming to the Language Model protocol, a fundamental component of the framework that allows you to pick the right language model for your app.
For those just getting started, developers who are enrolled in the App Store Small Business Program and have fewer than two million first-time App Store downloads can access the Apple Foundation Models running on Private Cloud Compute with no cloud API cost.* This delivers frontier-level intelligence with unparalleled privacy protections, ensuring that your users' data is not stored or accessible to Apple or anyone else.
Learn more about Private Cloud Compute
Dynamic Profiles are new declarative APIs for continuously updating your session's model, tools, and instructions on the fly. Rather than creating a session with a fixed configuration, you define profiles — each with its own model, temperature, reasoning level, and toolset — and swap between them within a single continuous session. The session transcript is shared across every profile switch, so your app gets more contextual intelligence with less prompting. Dynamic Profiles are designed to be adaptable building blocks: use them to build AI agents, skills, or any other high-level abstraction your app needs.
The Evaluations framework gives you the ability to test your prompts and validate that your intelligence-powered features work correctly across dynamic conditions, going beyond what unit tests alone can catch. Whether you're iterating on instructions, switching models, or tuning a Dynamic Profile, Evaluations gives you confidence that your app's behavior holds up before you ship.
The new fm command line tool in macOS 27 lets you streamline scripting, automate model workflows, and accelerate your development without writing a line of app code. You can prototype quickly, giving you a fast path from idea to implementation.
To help you get started with adopting emerging AI patterns, the Foundation Models framework utilities package adds experimental abstractions on top of the framework — including custom skills, context management helpers, and a Chat Completions client that connects to a hosted model of your choice.
The Foundation Models SDK for Python extends the framework into the Python ecosystem, letting you integrate with popular tooling and evaluation packages in Python.
The Acceptable Use Requirements for the Foundation Models framework outline prohibited uses of Apple Foundation Models. Additionally, pursuant to Article 53(1)(b) of the AI Act, we make available information and documentation to providers of AI systems who intend to integrate AFM 3 Cloud into their AI systems.

====================================================================================================

AI is turning Apple's app review process into a nightmare for synth developers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:09:49 UTC
06:09:49 KST

The surge of AI-generated apps is overwhelming Apple’s app review process, leaving developers caught in the crossfire – two examples among many.
A flood of new iOS apps has emerged over the last few months. An estimated 10–20 new iOS music apps enter the market every day. This is because AI now makes it possible for anyone to vibe code their own apps and AUv3 plugins.
However, getting an app into the Apple App Store requires going through the app review process. AI has affected not only the number of new releases per day but also Apple’s holy app review process, creating more nightmares than joy for many developers.
What Is The Apple App Review Process?
Developing and releasing a VST plugin is simple. You set up a website, upload your installers, set a price, and you’re good to go. It’s more complicated on iOS.
To publish an app, you must first join the Apple Developer Program, which costs $99 per year. As a member, you can submit apps with AUv3 support, but they won’t become available immediately.
Whether they are hand-coded or created with the help of AI, all apps must go through Apple’s review process. Every app, whether a synthesizer, effects, game, or utility app, must first get through this barrier.
This process verifies that the app meets requirements, including security standards. That’s a major advantage over many new, freely available VST plugins . You can be confident that everything you download to your iOS or macOS device has been checked and is free of anything suspicious.
So far, the process from submission to release has taken 24 to 48 hours, and sometimes a few days. Developers say the typical review takes a day or two. Apple’s own App Review guidelines state:
App Review works around the clock, typically reviewing at least 50% of submissions in less than 24 hours and 90% in less than 48 hours.”
AI And The Impact Of The Apple App Review Process
24-48 days or a few days! That’s what most developers are currently dreaming of. The Apple App Review process is currently on the verge of collapsing due to the flood of new iOS apps (AI or not), or even hallucinating, if you ask iOS developers.
Various developers, including AudioThing, Igor Vasiliev, Sugar Bytes, and others, say their new apps have been stuck in App Review for weeks rather than days, with no updates. As a result, many releases focused on cross-platform compatibility are currently struggling.
Take Pittsburgh Modular, for example: their new Microvolt 3901 synth was released as a VST on October 27th, while the iOS version only arrived today, November 12th. However, they planned a simultaneous release.
Igor Vasiliev‘s new HollowTone app (go check out that brilliant app!) also spent weeks stuck in the review process. Most developers can handle a few delays, provided the app is good and ultimately gets purchased.
The problem arises when the app review process becomes erratic—or stops accepting the app altogether—even though it’s just another synth. Some developers have reported this.
Unfiltered Audio Sinusoidal Run Rhythm
Unfiltered Audio—which offers a range of high-quality AUv3 plugins in the App Store, including the brilliant Battalion drum machine—had trouble bringing its Sinusoidal Run Rhythm to market.
Michael Hetrick of Unfiltered Audio, who’s been in the iOS game for years, wrote on the Loopy Pro forum:
Apple seems to be using AI for automated App Store reviews. SRR got rejected 4 times. The first time, they asked for a video of me demonstrating it (which almost always happens with our plugins).
The second time, the editor hallucinated that “Polyharmonic” was an audio technology that we needed to show a documented license for to use the term in the description. The third time, it asked for a video again, this time with the app connected to the “required proprietary hardware”. The fourth time, it flagged SRR MIDI as spam because of its similarity to SRR.
It was over a month of back-and-forth, so I threw my hands up and released it audio-only for now. What we will do is bundle the MIDI build into the binary so both AUv3s install.
The app is now available, but only in the audio version. The MIDI version is still not out.
AudioThing Materia took 25 days to get approved (1 rejection), and the latest Seconds, in collaboration with Hainbach, has been in review for 29 days and counting.
The second case shows that this new Apple app review process AI reality can also cost app developers their livelihoods—especially those who have spent months coding their apps.
The CRUX Synth Problem
Marek Dendes is a new developer who is currently caught in this precarious situation. He has been working for several months on a flagship Synthesizer called CRUX Synth—a sort of “Serum 2” for iOS.
I’ve been testing the beta for a few weeks now, and it truly is a flagship product. At the moment, it seems the synthesizer won’t be released, as the app has been rejected multiple times by Apple’s App Review process.
He wrote in the Loopy Pro forum:
My app was REJECTED from submission FOUR TIMES in row. Always same reason (eg. my app is “design spam” and “duplicates existing apps” and it is createed using “app generation template services”) as shown bellow in screenshots. Which is obvious nonsense.
I replied on every rejection – no change, again rejected. Obviously nobody reads my explanations. Thing is it goes from “Waiting for Review” into “In Review” and then instantly in second to “Rejected” so this is some automated process and nobody even reads anything i write – so it’s a bit dead end.
I submitted multiple appeals to the App Review Board – that did not help, did not get any reply (it’s about 5 days since first appeal submit). Tried also to submit only CRUX without effects to avoid multiple apps submitted at once – same result. There is nothing more i can do.
I reached out to my contacts at Apple, but I didn’t get any real help from them regarding this case.
My Two Cents (Opinion)
iOS/iPadOS is a fantastic platform for making music on the go. It’s been part of my musical toolkit from the start, and for more than six years, it’s also played an integral role in my hybrid live performance setup.
If you need to submit an app now for release before year’s end, that’s genuinely problematic: you could wait three weeks only to have it rejected. I’m in daily contact with iOS developers.
Many have lost their former euphoria; instead, they express disillusionment and doubt that things will ever be the same. Developers are frustrated by lengthy release delays—and remember, not all submissions are music apps.
With so many new apps launching, even great ones get lost. Do you remember what was published two days ago? This reflects a problem Apple created years ago: ending its affiliate program left media outlets unable to cover iOS meaningfully.
That’s why I’m on Patreon. Today, written coverage of iOS music apps is rare unless they’re backed by major names like Roland, Korg, or Moog.
The iOS market is currently off-kilter—not just because of the flood of AI apps, but also because of Apple itself. Where is the real Apple Intelligence when you actually need it?
If they don’t get a handle on this and start reaching out to iOS developers in person again, as they did early on, I can see the market entering a strange period.
Hey Claude, take me back to the App Store era of 2022. Right now!
More information here: Apple

Singapore Says US Not Helpful On Investigation Into Suspected Spy's Company
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

Singapore Says US Not Helpful On Investigation Into Suspected Spy's Company (nytimes.com) 27
			
		 	
				Singapore says U.S. authorities provided limited assistance in its investigation of Megaspeed, the company run by Alice Huang, the Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence suspects is a Chinese spy and whose relationship with Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has drawn scrutiny. According to Singapore, authorities held Alice Huang's passport from June 2025 until February while investigating "potential false declarations," but ultimately released her with a "stern warning" and barred her from re-entering the country. The New York Times reports:  The statement raises new questions about the response of the Trump administration to the case and why the U.S. government did not more forcefully intervene. According to the timeline in the statement, Singaporean authorities had been holding Ms. Huang's passport well after President Trump was briefed last fall about her relationship with Nvidia's chief executive and the belief that she was a Chinese intelligence asset. The New York Times has reported that Taiwan began monitoring Ms. Huang in 2024 at the guidance of the U.S. government.
In its statement, Singapore said that it had sought information from an American server maker and close Nvidia business partner, Supermicro, but that the company had provided "limited cooperation." Supermicro, a firm that packages Nvidia's A.I. chips onto servers that can be used in data centers, has been at the center of a number of chip smuggling investigations across the globe. Supermicro was a supplier to Megaspeed, a company incorporated in Singapore and run by Ms. Huang, according to two people with knowledge of the matter.
Singapore said in the statement its authorities raised Supermicro's "limited cooperation" with the U.S. Department of Justice and Federal Bureau of Investigation in July 2025 and "requested their assistance in securing the company's cooperation." "Despite these repeated requests, Singapore has to date received limited information or assistance in respect of the company," the statement said.

Here are the 25 original Xbox games you can now play on PC
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
20:59:27 UTC
05:59:27 KST

For the 25th anniversary of Xbox, Microsoft is beginning to make good on its paid “next-gen game preservation” promise. Starting today, you can play 25 different original Xbox games on your Windows PC, as long as you have (or are willing to pay) for digital copies.
Here are the 25 original Xbox games you can now play on PC
Microsoft’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, too.
In July, Microsoft originally launched the idea with just four Xbox games, so 25 is progress, and Microsoft says all these games are now playable with keyboard and mouse.
Plus, Microsoft says it’s adding achievements to original Xbox games for the first time, starting with four games: Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic and its sequel, Blinx: The Time Sweeper, and Conker: Live and Reloaded. You can unlock achievements playing them on Xbox One and Xbox Series consoles as well, after a system update. More games should get them in future.
Here’s the full list of Xbox on PC games, with the 21 new additions in bold:
- Armed and Dangerous
- Blinx: The Time Sweeper
- Conker: Live and Reloaded
- Crimson Skies: High Road to Revenge
- Disney’s Chicken Little
- The Elder Scrolls III: Morrowind
- Fuzion Frenzy
- Grabbed by the Ghoulies
- Indiana Jones and the Emperor’s Tomb
- Red Faction II
- Secret Weapons Over Normandy
- Star Wars Battlefront
- Star Wars Battlefront II
- Star Wars Episode III: Revenge of the Sith
- Star Wars Jedi Knight: Jedi Academy
- Star Wars Jedi Knight II Jedi Outcast
- Star Wars: Jedi Starfighter
- Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic
- Star Wars: Knights of the Old Republic II – The Sith Lords
- Star Wars: Republic Commando
- Star Wars: Starfighter – Special Edition
- Star Wars: The Clone Wars
- Thrillville
- TimeSplitters 2
- TimeSplitters: Future Perfect
Yes, that’s a lot of Star Wars games, most of which already have a PC version, and few games that were exclusive to Xbox at the time. (But many of those Star Wars games were excellent.)
You also won’t be able to use an original Xbox disc to play these games, I’m afraid, as original Xbox (and Xbox 360) discs aren’t part of Microsoft’s disc-to-digital program; they predate the unique serial number that Microsoft embedded into Xbox One discs. In case you’re wondering, Xbox on PC backward compatibility appears to be powered by an emulator.
Also, most of the new games aren’t currently included with Xbox Game Pass, according to Microsoft; just Blinx, Conker, Crimson Skies, Fusion Frenzy, Morrowind, and Grabbed by the Ghoulies.
Leaked Microsoft documents previously showed us that October 2026 would be the “full launch” of original Xbox games on PC, while Xbox 360 games would have a “gradual” rollout to next-gen consoles between 2027 and 2028, which are when Microsoft is expected to roll out a handheld and a Helix box respectively. The way things are shaping up, the Xbox Helix should play games from every generation of Xbox, even if Helix fundamentally turns out to be a Windows PC.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: Cerulion – ROS 2-compatible robotics runtime with multi-process replay
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
20:53:04 UTC
05:53:04 KST

Cerulion (YC S24) is the operating system for physical AI: an open-source, zero-copy robot runtime that records runs, replays failures against new code, and reaches your robots from anywhere. It's ROS 2 compatible.
Install · Run the example · Performance · ROS 2 · Architecture · Docs · Guides
curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/cerulion-inc/cerulion/main/tools/scripts/install.sh | sh && . "$HOME/.cerulion/env"
The installer links cerulion into ~/.local/bin, adds one line to your shell startup files and installs Rust when Rust 1.95 or newer is missing. Then sign in once with cerulion login, because every command runs under a free Cerulion account, which is used for robot pairing and works offline afterwards. To uninstall, run the same script with sh -s -- --uninstall.
Homebrew
brew tap cerulion-inc/cerulion
brew install cerulion-inc/cerulion/cerulion
If Homebrew 6.0.x refuses the tap, run brew trust --formula cerulion-inc/cerulion/cerulion first and try again.
Debian / Ubuntu
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y ca-certificates curl gnupg
keyring=$(mktemp) &&
  curl -fsSL https://apt.cerulion.com/cerulion-archive-keyring.gpg -o "$keyring" &&
  [ "$(gpg --show-keys --with-colons "$keyring" | awk -F: '$1 == "fpr" { print $10 }' | sort -u)" = "$(printf '%s\n' 953C69FD279D9256FA4B71C5DA5FDE5D0DC6D91B | sort -u)" ] &&
  sudo install -m 0644 "$keyring" /usr/share/keyrings/cerulion-archive-keyring.gpg &&
  echo "deb [arch=$(dpkg --print-architecture) signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/cerulion-archive-keyring.gpg] https://apt.cerulion.com stable main" | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/cerulion.list >/dev/null &&
  rm -f "$keyring" &&
  sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install cerulion
Each step runs only if the one before it succeeded, and the APT distribution page pins the same key fingerprints and has a longer form that names a failed step.
Build from crates.io or source
cargo install --locked cerulion_cli cerulion_netd
The release installer places cerulion-connectd (the desk side of a remote connection) and cerulion-remoted (the robot side) beside the CLI. See the installation guide or CONTRIBUTING for the complete source-build route.
Platforms, ROS 2 libraries and toolchain
In fish, end the line with && source ~/.cerulion/env.fish instead.
Homebrew and the Debian package do not provision Rust. To build your own nodes, run cerulion-install-rust once, which installs current stable Rust unless a Rust 1.95 or newer is already there, and put ${CARGO_HOME:-$HOME/.cargo}/bin on your PATH. On a terminal, cerulion workspace create and cerulion node build offer to install it when Rust 1.95 or newer is missing.
Release binaries support Linux x86_64 and ARM64 (glibc 2.31+, Ubuntu 20.04 and later) and macOS Intel and Apple Silicon. The Linux packages also carry the ROS 2 Jazzy rmw and heap hook that cerulion ros2 run and cerulion ros2 launch use. The macOS packages do not, because ROS 2 Jazzy has no macOS binaries. The rmw libraries run where their ROS 2 distro runs, which for Jazzy is Ubuntu 24.04. For ROS 2 Lyrical, each release also publishes cerulion-rmw-lyrical-<version>-<target>.tar.gz for Linux x86_64 and ARM64. Unpack it and set CERULION_LIB_DIR to the unpacked directory so cerulion ros2 run and cerulion ros2 launch load the Lyrical rmw. That library is built and tested in CI.
Building nodes needs a C toolchain (build-essential on Debian and Ubuntu, xcode-select --install on macOS) and Rust 1.95 or newer. Nodes load through a compiler-independent interface, so any Rust 1.95 or newer can build them. The compiler does not have to match the one that built the cerulion CLI, and a new workspace pins no compiler. To bring a workspace from Cerulion 1.0, follow the migration steps in the 2.0.0 release notes. They move its =1.0.0 pins to =2.0.0 and finish with one cerulion node build <node> per node. The installation details cover the toolchain setup.
Using a coding agent? Paste this prompt into Claude Code, Codex or another agent that runs commands in its own terminal:
Install Cerulion: curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/cerulion-inc/cerulion/main/tools/scripts/install.sh | sh && . "$HOME/.cerulion/env"
Run cerulion login in the background, show me the URL and code it prints, and wait for it to finish while I approve the sign-in in my browser.
Then run the quickstart at https://github.com/cerulion-inc/cerulion#quickstart-run-record-and-verify in your own terminal:
cerulion workspace create safety_demo --starter obstacle_avoidance, then cd safety_demo.
Build both nodes (cerulion node build laser_scanner --release, then cerulion node build safety_controller --release; the first build takes a few minutes).
Run cerulion graph validate obstacle_avoidance, then cerulion graph run obstacle_avoidance --release --record for at least five seconds, and stop it with Ctrl+C.
Verify the recording it printed: cerulion bag play "<recording>" --resim all --verify
In nodes/safety_controller/src/lib.rs, change the forward speed from 0.3 to 0.25, run cerulion node build safety_controller --release, and verify the same recording again.
Report what changed between the two verifications.
Finally, ask me whether I want to star https://github.com/cerulion-inc/cerulion. Only if I say yes, star it with gh api -X PUT user/starred/cerulion-inc/cerulion, or give me the link if gh is not signed in.
This example connects a synthetic laser scanner to a safety controller. The controller publishes a forward velocity of 0.3 when the path is clear and 0.0 when an obstacle is within half a meter.
cerulion workspace create safety_demo --starter obstacle_avoidance
cd safety_demo
To write nodes from scratch instead, follow the getting-started tutorial.
Sign in once per machine. Cerulion commands run under an account, and the result is read locally from then on, so later commands keep working offline.
cerulion login
A robot or a headless box prints a short code to approve from a browser on any machine.
Build both nodes, check the graph, then run it with recording on. The first node build also compiles the Cerulion runtime, so it takes a few minutes on a laptop. Later builds take seconds.
cerulion node build laser_scanner --release
cerulion node build safety_controller --release
cerulion graph validate obstacle_avoidance
cerulion graph run obstacle_avoidance --release --record
The run splits into one process per node without changing the graph file, as the multi-process guide explains.
By default the run is readable from any computer signed in to your account, with no addresses or flags. Add --network off to keep a run on this machine.
In a second terminal, watch the controller react:
cerulion topic echo /obstacle_avoidance/safety_controller/linear_velocity
The x field switches between 0.3 and 0.0 as the synthetic scan crosses the distance threshold. Let it run for at least five seconds, then stop it with Ctrl+C. Cerulion prints the completed recording's path:
recordings/obstacle_avoidance_20260918T181449Z.mcap
Set BAG to that printed path, then verify the unchanged code first:
BAG="recordings/obstacle_avoidance_<timestamp>.mcap"
cerulion bag play "$BAG" --resim all --verify
A successful verification prints replay PASS and exits successfully: the outputs covered by the report match the recording.
Now open nodes/safety_controller/src/lib.rs and change the clear-path velocity from 0.3 to 0.25. Rebuild that node and verify against the same recording:
cerulion node build safety_controller --release
cerulion bag play "$BAG" --resim all --verify
This time verification reports a divergence and returns a nonzero exit code. On the same sensor inputs, the changed controller would command a different speed. In CI, that verdict flags the changed behavior for review before the code reaches a robot. The animation at the top of this page shows this whole run.
If Cerulion is useful to you, a star helps other robotics engineers find it.
What replay verification checks
Cerulion re-executes the graph with recorded inputs and execution context, then compares the covered outputs against the recording. A pass means the changed code produces the same covered outputs on those recorded inputs, and each recording you keep adds another case to check against.
- Exit 0: compared frames match.
- Exit 1: output data diverged, and the report names the topic or field.
- Exit 6: the execution schedule diverged.
- Exits 2 through 5: verification could not complete, and the error says why.
Comparisons are byte-exact by default. Add --tolerance for acceptable per-field differences and --report for a machine-readable report.
Guide: Record and replay
The perception example records a synthetic camera and pixel-based detector, verifies the baseline, then tests a detector whose boxes move by eight pixels. Its bounding-box overlap tolerance catches the change and names the affected field.
From the repository root:
cd examples/perceptionRECORD_SECS=8 ./run_replay_demo.sh
Choose tolerances that match the behavior you care about: bounding-box IoU, absolute or relative error, RMSE and set comparisons. Comparisons are byte-exact unless you set a field, topic or document-wide tolerance. The same checks can run on pull requests against recordings your team keeps as regression cases.
On one x86-64 Linux desktop (24-core Intel Core Ultra 9 285K, stock settings, recording on, nominal 100 Hz load, 2026-09-16), a 1 MiB round trip has a median of 4.08 µs with the default multi-process layout and 10,952 µs on ROS 2 Jazzy with rmw_fastrtps_cpp defaults: about 2,700× faster than ROS 2 Jazzy defaults at 1 MiB. One machine, not a guarantee. The raw results are in docs/benchmarks/results/8a84baf25d5d1710-2026-09-16-fixed100-heroes.
Each cell is p50 / p99 in microseconds, measured on the Linux x86_64 host above at a nominal 100 Hz offered load with the advertised payload size on both legs. Timing starts after the publisher's loan and ends on echo receipt, so sensor production and payload processing add their own work. The Cerulion figures include recording and the ROS 2 figures do not.
The stock ROS 2 lane pipelines requests, so at 16 MiB a round trip outlasts the 10 ms pacing interval and requests overlap in flight. The benchmark suite documents the process layouts and timing boundaries.
Free run, the multi-process default, against barrier lockstep: Free run against lockstep.
The rmw_cerulion line in the chart above is ROS 2 Jazzy running over Cerulion's transport, measured in the same harness, posture and pacing as every other line: three ROS 2 nodes in three processes, with loaned publishing and loaned subscription takes. At 1 MiB that is 11.12 µs p50 / 37.21 µs p99, beside 11.0 ms / 43.4 ms for stock rmw_fastrtps_cpp, the chart's own stock ROS 2 line. The median stays between 10.43 and 10.65 µs from 64 B to 256 KiB and reaches 17.47 µs at 16 MiB. Methodology · Results and provenance.
The Apple M4 row is measured on Cerulion 2.0.0 under macOS 27.2.1, a later build than the x86-64 and Jetson rows, in which a multi-process consumer wakes on the publish. macOS two-process data wake measures that change on its own, before and after, on an Apple M3 Max.
Source measurements, build provenance and configuration receipts are in the benchmark results. Lines show p50 and bands extend to p99. Both figures show round trips.
A typed input, a typed output and a tick method define the quickstart's safety controller. The macro supplies the port plumbing. The graph declares the wiring, and the runtime handles scheduling, transport, recording and observability. You do not configure an executor, register callbacks or write a spin loop.
use cerulion_core::prelude::*;
use native_ros2_messages::geometry_msgs::Vector3;
use native_ros2_messages::sensor_msgs::LaserScan;
#[cerulion_node]
#[derive(Default)]
struct SafetyControllerNode {
    #[input(trigger, depth = 1, expect_within_ms = 100)]
    scan: LaserScan,
    #[output(promise_within_ms = 100)]
    linear_velocity: Vector3,
}
#[cerulion_node_impl]
impl SafetyControllerNode {
    fn tick(&mut self) -> Result<(), NodeError> {
        let ranges = self.scan.ranges();
        let stop = ranges.is_empty()
            || ranges.iter().any(|&r| r.is_nan() || r < 0.5);
        self.linear_velocity.x = if stop { 0.0 } else { 0.3 };
        Ok(())
    }
}
Nodes can also be written in Python: cerulion node create <type> --lang python --policy period_ms=<N> writes one that graph run, recording and replay treat like a Rust node. It needs the cerulion Python package and a cerulion CLI, both built from a Cerulion source checkout, as Python describes.
Here, #[input(trigger)] runs the controller when a scan arrives. The port deadlines make timing expectations observable through miss counters, alongside delivery-failure counts.
Other nodes can run on synchronized input sets or external events. For a periodic node, use #[cerulion_node(period_ms = 10)] instead of a data trigger. Set queue depth and backpressure per input. For example, backpressure = sample(3) limits reads to one per 3 ms.
Fixed fields write directly into the shared-memory slot. Variable-length fields have loaned writers too, and self.image.data.fill_from(producer)? lets a camera or generator fill the final buffer in place. An untouched output publishes nothing. If this node's tick returns an error, its outputs are discarded.
Guides: Define a node · Wire a graph · Backpressure and deadlines
When you control startup, keep your launch file and start it through Cerulion:
cerulion ros2 launch my_robot_bringup camera.launch.py
cerulion ros2 run my_robot_drivers imu_node
Both set up rmw_cerulion and run the ROS 2 command, one launch file or one executable at a time. Your nodes keep their ROS 2 APIs and executors. Neither rewrites your source, so publishers that copy keep copying until you migrate them. Inspect their topics with cerulion topic list and cerulion topic echo, which read the shared-memory plane. The rmw's discovery is process-local, so the ros2 CLI graph tools (ros2 topic list, ros2 topic echo, rqt) do not list them.
Optional: migrate C++ publishers to zero-copy loans
cerulion ros2 migrate --workspace ~/ros2_ws
cerulion ros2 migrate --workspace ~/ros2_ws --write
The first command previews which C++ publish sites can move to the loaned-message API, and --write applies the rewrite. Publishing and subscribing with loans are separate choices, and the ROS 2 guide covers both and what to expect from each.
When the stack is already running, attach to its DDS topics:
cerulion ros2 attach --iface <local-interface-IP>
Use the IP address of this computer's interface on the robot's LAN. Attach discovers the existing DDS stack and bridges its topics. It does not restart those nodes.
Use ros2: entries to launch ROS 2 processes beside native nodes in one graph. The MoveIt 2 example shows an unmodified planning stack running over rmw_cerulion.
rmw_cerulion is tested in CI on Foxy, Galactic, Humble, Jazzy and Lyrical, the Linux releases ship it prebuilt for Jazzy and Lyrical, and cerulion ros2 attach is validated on Jazzy and on Foxy and Humble with local type resolution, as ROS 2 compatibility details. Exact replay of one eligible ROS 2 process at a time (cerulion ros2 run --record-deliveries and --replay-deliveries) is runtime-tested on Humble. A replay publishes live on its machine and still drives any device the process writes to directly, such as a serial port or CAN bus, so run it where neither reaches a live robot. ROS 2 replay lists which processes qualify and where a replay is safe to run.
Guides: Bridge ROS 2 · Compatibility and configuration
- On the robot: the scheduler derives dependency levels from your graph, waits on data and timers and executes ready nodes across process groups. Nodes exchange loaned shared-memory messages, and capture records the data and execution context.
- Across machines: robots on your account, and robots this computer is already paired with, are discovered automatically. Iroh connects directly when it can, including on one LAN, and through a relay when it cannot. The network daemon mirrors subscribed topics locally for your tools. An unpaired machine reads a robot's topics over the LAN only when the robot runs with CERULION_LAN_ACCESS=open.
- At your desk: the CLI inspects, records and verifies the robot's data, and Cerulion MCP gives your coding agent tools for its recordings, your Cerulion Cloud instances and the robots on your account. A captured failure is a recording to inspect. Before re-running it, check anchor.resimmable_reason in the bag's __cerulion/flashback.json attachment.
- Zero-copy, beyond fixed-size messages. Write camera frames, scans and schema-defined variable-length fields directly into shared memory. Other local processes read the same bytes.
- A scheduler that knows your graph. Dependency levels are computed ahead of time, and periodic, data-triggered, synchronized and external nodes run when their inputs are ready. Recorded scheduling, explicit backpressure and deadlines and a virtual clock make that execution testable and deterministic on replay.
- Recordings you can check code against. Standard MCAP, byte-exact output comparisons, per-field tolerances and machine-readable verdicts for CI. Test your changed code against the inputs that actually reached the robot.
- Flashback when you forgot to record. Save the moments before and after a failure from a rolling in-memory history, then inspect the incident or replay its recorded topics.
- Your robots, wherever they are. Sign in to the same account on the robot and on your computer, and your computer finds the robot and reads its topics with no addresses or flags to set. The same single- and multi-process workflow runs on Linux and macOS, on your LAN or across the internet.
- A path from ROS 2. Run an existing stack through rmw_cerulion, attach to one already running, or combine ROS 2 processes and native nodes in the same graph.
- Tools your coding agent can use. The hosted Cerulion MCP endpoint gives an agent tools for your Cerulion Cloud instances, your MCAP recordings and the robots on your account, and Hindsight investigates recordings with evidence tied to topics and timestamps.
Guides: Execution · Multi-process graphs · Networking and remote robots · Remote access
Cerulion works on real hardware, here on a six-axis industrial arm running ROS 2 and MoveIt. The recording shows the arm's discovered topics, its live 3D model driven by its joint states and a controller state plot; a phone video of the real arm, inset in editing, moves in step with the model. All six moves play at real speed, and a fast-forward mark shows where each pause between moves was cut.
Inspect the quickstart graph's topics with the CLI:
cerulion topic list
cerulion topic hz /obstacle_avoidance/laser_scanner/scan
cerulion topic echo /obstacle_avoidance/safety_controller/linear_velocity
Guides: Inspect topics · Remote robots
Flashback keeps a memory-bounded window of recent activity in every running graph, so the moments before a problem are already there when you need them. It is event-based logging: nothing is written to disk until an event fires, and then the window around that event becomes a recording.
It captures on its own when the runtime sees a fault, such as a dying worker process or a stalled topic, and Flashback lists every trigger.
You can also ask for one by hand from another shell on the robot:
cerulion flashback --note "controller stopped late"
Re-execution uses the capture's execution trace and any state anchors its starting point needs. bag info reports whether a capture can be re-executed or only played back. To record an already-running graph instead, use cerulion bag record --run=obstacle_avoidance.
Guides: Flashback · Record and replay · Bags and replay
Cerulion MCP at https://mcp.cerulion.com/mcp/cerulion gives Claude Code, Codex and other MCP clients tools for your Cerulion Cloud instances, analysis of MCAP recordings served over https://, and a list of the robots on your account.
Hindsight at https://mcp.cerulion.com/mcp/hindsight serves the recording-analysis tools on their own, with answers grounded in specific topics and timestamps.
Both sign in with OAuth using your Cerulion account. The hosted endpoints have no local workspace tools, so build, run and inspect nodes and topics with the cerulion CLI.
The hosted endpoints are in early access. Add each one to your client as its own connector:
claude mcp add --transport http cerulion https://mcp.cerulion.com/mcp/cerulion
claude mcp add --transport http hindsight https://mcp.cerulion.com/mcp/hindsight
Hindsight and the hosted MCP server are closed source. Request access.
Guide: Connect an MCP client
The direction is review for robot changes: bring recordings, simulations and behavioral evidence into the review process before a change reaches a robot.
The roadmap is open for use cases and contributions: discuss a feature. Release changes are recorded in the changelog.

Data centers need power – lower income, rural residents pay the price
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:53:54 UTC
04:53:54 KST

Data centers need power – lower income, rural residents pay the price
By Caitlin Hayes, Cornell Chronicle
As the engines of artificial intelligence, large data centers can consume as much power as medium-sized cities, straining grids and prompting expensive upgrades to U.S. energy infrastructure. New research finds that residents, and especially lower-income and rural residents, are disproportionately bearing the costs.
In the study, published Nov. 9 in Nature Energy, Cornell researchers analyzed growth in energy demand and infrastructure projects for four major U.S. power grids from 2020 to 2025 and found that data centers have hiked wholesale energy prices and on average paid only 38% of the infrastructure costs linked to their growth – $3.12 billion of an $8.25 billion bill in 2025. The remaining $5.13 billion, or 62%, fell largely on residents, with the lowest income households seeing extra energy costs up to 28% of their incomes.
“We were trying to answer a very simple question: With the booming of data centers, who pays for that expansion and who benefits?” said Fengqi You, the Roxanne E. and Michael J. Zak Professor in Energy Systems Engineering in the Cornell Duffield College of Engineering. “It’s not just a question of generating enough electricity but of whether the rules for paying for the grid are keeping pace with who is driving new demand.”
The research is the first multi-region, long-term assessment of how data center development impacts infrastructure growth and how costs are distributed across communities. It comes amidst a boom in data center construction.
The researchers found that transmission investment in energy infrastructure projects increased from $2 billion in 2020 to $34 billion in 2025, with data centers driving much of that rise. Debates are ongoing – from local communities to state governments to Congress – about who picks up the bill as growth accelerates; data centers are projected to consume between 8% and 17% of U.S. electricity by 2030, with 70% to 80% of that attributed to AI.
With first author and postdoctoral researcher Cheng Feng, You also looked at data centers’ economic benefits, finding that each gigawatt of data center capacity can create 15,000 jobs across the economy, with an average 2,700 jobs directly associated with the data center (although not necessarily all on-site).
“In a lot of discussions right now, people take one side or the other, completely yes on data centers or completely no,” said You, director of the Cornell AI for Sustainability Initiative. “But there are benefits and costs, and the key issue in the end is how to balance the trade-offs.”
Sharing the cost of power infrastructure
The team analyzed data from 4,000 existing and proposed data centers, as well as power-price records, rate filings from utilities, power-line plans, and federal household and job data. The major grids they studied encompass regions with the largest clusters of data centers, including the southern San Francisco Bay area; north Texas around Dallas-Fort Worth; the Midwest; and the mid-Atlantic region, including northern Virginia, where the biggest concentration of data centers has been built to date.
State incentives for data centers have provided returns on the investments, if inconsistently, the researchers found. Some states, such as California, reaped $180 in worker income for every dollar spent, while Virginia increased income $2 for every dollar invested. The researchers determined that the presence of an existing, qualified workforce was a stronger indicator of high returns than the amount of incentive spending.
Data centers also increased wholesale electricity prices, the amounts utilities pay regional power markets before delivering the electricity to homes. In the most-affected hubs, data center growth raised those prices by nearly 10% or more by 2025. In Virginia, the costs jumped 15% and in California’s southern Bay Area, 20%. In contrast, residents in New York and New England, where data center construction has been limited, have not seen clear price increases due to data center expansion, the researchers found.
“I think that means we’re at a good starting point in New York to think about the next steps,” You said. “Economic benefits, job benefits, investment, fairly allocating the costs – these are the dimensions and trade-offs we need to consider.”
A key problem is how the costs of new infrastructure projects are currently shared: The allocation is often based on previous patterns of energy consumption.
“It’s kind of a chicken-and-egg problem,” You said. “Because if you’re going to build a data center, you need to expand this power infrastructure. Otherwise, there’s no electricity for them to run. But before they build, they’re not using energy. So with this model of relying on past consumption, households end up taking on much of the infrastructure costs.”
Many more rural and lower-income residents saw proportionally higher bills; the share of rural families shouldering a high energy burden – spending more than 6% of their incomes on energy – rose 76% more than among urban residents. The authors cite a national survey finding that rural residents are also less likely to use AI tools like ChatGPT than urban residents.
The impact on energy bills also has a wide geographical reach. For example, residents in New Jersey, Pennsylvania and Ohio help pay for grid upgrades linked to data center growth in Virginia, the researchers said.
“You do not have to live next to a data center to help pay for its power infrastructure,” You said. “The grid connects communities across states, and cost-sharing rules connect them financially.”
Using insights from the data, You and Feng recommend changes to cost-sharing rules and strategies to lower burdens. In all four regions, they found that, if data centers cut back power use during 22 of the region’s peak demand hours each year, they would relieve strain on the grid and minimize the need to expand. Tech companies, which often own multiple data centers in different states and even countries, could shift operations to another region during the peak demand.
“It’s feasible, and the challenge is more about implementation,” You said. “Coordination is needed, but there’s a clear benefit for better coordination.”
States could also charge data centers directly in line with the new demand they add, or institute programs, like a recent law in Minnesota, to direct funds to low-income households to offset higher energy expenses. Tax incentives could also be tied to job creation and increased wages, so those benefits are more ensured.
You and Feng’s recommendations come as states grapple with how to share costs; six states have recently set special terms for very large energy consumers, and in March, seven major technology companies signed the White House Ratepayer Protection Pledge, a commitment to pay for required grid updates.
“This is an issue that connects directly to debates in many states and in Congress,” You said. “We want to provide science-based evidence and concrete data so that all the stakeholders can better understand the picture and weigh their options.”
Funding for the study came from the Eric and Wendy Schmidt AI in Science Postdoctoral Fellowship.

Data centers need power – lower income, rural residents pay the price
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:53:54 UTC
04:53:54 KST

Data centers need power – lower income, rural residents pay the price
By Caitlin Hayes, Cornell Chronicle
As the engines of artificial intelligence, large data centers can consume as much power as medium-sized cities, straining grids and prompting expensive upgrades to U.S. energy infrastructure. New research finds that residents, and especially lower-income and rural residents, are disproportionately bearing the costs.
In the study, published Nov. 9 in Nature Energy, Cornell researchers analyzed growth in energy demand and infrastructure projects for four major U.S. power grids from 2020 to 2025 and found that data centers have hiked wholesale energy prices and on average paid only 38% of the infrastructure costs linked to their growth – $3.12 billion of an $8.25 billion bill in 2025. The remaining $5.13 billion, or 62%, fell largely on residents, with the lowest income households seeing extra energy costs up to 28% of their incomes.
“We were trying to answer a very simple question: With the booming of data centers, who pays for that expansion and who benefits?” said Fengqi You, the Roxanne E. and Michael J. Zak Professor in Energy Systems Engineering in the Cornell Duffield College of Engineering. “It’s not just a question of generating enough electricity but of whether the rules for paying for the grid are keeping pace with who is driving new demand.”
The research is the first multi-region, long-term assessment of how data center development impacts infrastructure growth and how costs are distributed across communities. It comes amidst a boom in data center construction.
The researchers found that transmission investment in energy infrastructure projects increased from $2 billion in 2020 to $34 billion in 2025, with data centers driving much of that rise. Debates are ongoing – from local communities to state governments to Congress – about who picks up the bill as growth accelerates; data centers are projected to consume between 8% and 17% of U.S. electricity by 2030, with 70% to 80% of that attributed to AI.
With first author and postdoctoral researcher Cheng Feng, You also looked at data centers’ economic benefits, finding that each gigawatt of data center capacity can create 15,000 jobs across the economy, with an average 2,700 jobs directly associated with the data center (although not necessarily all on-site).
“In a lot of discussions right now, people take one side or the other, completely yes on data centers or completely no,” said You, director of the Cornell AI for Sustainability Initiative. “But there are benefits and costs, and the key issue in the end is how to balance the trade-offs.”
Sharing the cost of power infrastructure
The team analyzed data from 4,000 existing and proposed data centers, as well as power-price records, rate filings from utilities, power-line plans, and federal household and job data. The major grids they studied encompass regions with the largest clusters of data centers, including the southern San Francisco Bay area; north Texas around Dallas-Fort Worth; the Midwest; and the mid-Atlantic region, including northern Virginia, where the biggest concentration of data centers has been built to date.
State incentives for data centers have provided returns on the investments, if inconsistently, the researchers found. Some states, such as California, reaped $180 in worker income for every dollar spent, while Virginia increased income $2 for every dollar invested. The researchers determined that the presence of an existing, qualified workforce was a stronger indicator of high returns than the amount of incentive spending.
Data centers also increased wholesale electricity prices, the amounts utilities pay regional power markets before delivering the electricity to homes. In the most-affected hubs, data center growth raised those prices by nearly 10% or more by 2025. In Virginia, the costs jumped 15% and in California’s southern Bay Area, 20%. In contrast, residents in New York and New England, where data center construction has been limited, have not seen clear price increases due to data center expansion, the researchers found.
“I think that means we’re at a good starting point in New York to think about the next steps,” You said. “Economic benefits, job benefits, investment, fairly allocating the costs – these are the dimensions and trade-offs we need to consider.”
A key problem is how the costs of new infrastructure projects are currently shared: The allocation is often based on previous patterns of energy consumption.
“It’s kind of a chicken-and-egg problem,” You said. “Because if you’re going to build a data center, you need to expand this power infrastructure. Otherwise, there’s no electricity for them to run. But before they build, they’re not using energy. So with this model of relying on past consumption, households end up taking on much of the infrastructure costs.”
Many more rural and lower-income residents saw proportionally higher bills; the share of rural families shouldering a high energy burden – spending more than 6% of their incomes on energy – rose 76% more than among urban residents. The authors cite a national survey finding that rural residents are also less likely to use AI tools like ChatGPT than urban residents.
The impact on energy bills also has a wide geographical reach. For example, residents in New Jersey, Pennsylvania and Ohio help pay for grid upgrades linked to data center growth in Virginia, the researchers said.
“You do not have to live next to a data center to help pay for its power infrastructure,” You said. “The grid connects communities across states, and cost-sharing rules connect them financially.”
Using insights from the data, You and Feng recommend changes to cost-sharing rules and strategies to lower burdens. In all four regions, they found that, if data centers cut back power use during 22 of the region’s peak demand hours each year, they would relieve strain on the grid and minimize the need to expand. Tech companies, which often own multiple data centers in different states and even countries, could shift operations to another region during the peak demand.
“It’s feasible, and the challenge is more about implementation,” You said. “Coordination is needed, but there’s a clear benefit for better coordination.”
States could also charge data centers directly in line with the new demand they add, or institute programs, like a recent law in Minnesota, to direct funds to low-income households to offset higher energy expenses. Tax incentives could also be tied to job creation and increased wages, so those benefits are more ensured.
You and Feng’s recommendations come as states grapple with how to share costs; six states have recently set special terms for very large energy consumers, and in March, seven major technology companies signed the White House Ratepayer Protection Pledge, a commitment to pay for required grid updates.
“This is an issue that connects directly to debates in many states and in Congress,” You said. “We want to provide science-based evidence and concrete data so that all the stakeholders can better understand the picture and weigh their options.”
Funding for the study came from the Eric and Wendy Schmidt AI in Science Postdoctoral Fellowship.

Adam Allington

Meet the people who want peptides to be made in America
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:53:45 UTC
04:53:45 KST

This is Optimizer, a weekly newsletter sent from Verge senior reviewer Victoria Song that dissects and discusses the latest gizmos and potions that swear they’re going to change your life. We’ll be taking a break next week and will be back on November 25th. Opt in for Optimizer here.
Meet the people who want peptides to be made in America
For some, the answer to a dodgy gray market is to bring the pharmaceutical supply chain back to American soil.
The SS Jeremiah O’Brien is a World War II Liberty ship. The hulking vessel was built in 56 days. It made 11 trips to Normandy, France, to supply the Allied powers in the fight against German U-boats. Last week, it was the site of a black-tie peptide party thrown by a trio of Gen Z venture capitalists.
Roughly 150 people attended the event, where four types of peptide samples were provided to guests — no injections. One was a topical cream with GHK-Cu, while the other three were dissolvable sublingual strips from peptide startup Afterglow. GHK-Cu is a popular skincare ingredient, but it’s unclear how well sublingual peptide strips work. The topic of the night wasn’t just the state of peptides. The venue was a deliberate reference to American industrial prowess — and why the pharmaceutical supply chain ought to be centered in the United States.
The timing couldn’t have been more pointed.
Sourcing is one of the biggest question marks with gray-market peptides — something the US Department of Health and Human Services is trying to address with its recent interim policy decision allowing certain unapproved peptides for compounding.
The decision is a controversial one. FDA career scientists haven’t endorsed it, though to the average person it might seem like they have. In fact, at a public hearing in July, FDA scientists categorically stated that there wasn’t enough evidence proving safety or efficacy for the experimental peptides in question. Some industry experts have cautioned that the HHS’s decision threatens the current clinical trial framework. By circumventing gold-standard clinical trials, it arguably allows telehealth providers and compounding pharmacies to bypass the approval process and create a parallel, dubiously regulated market.
But for the party’s organizers Roxana Grunenwald, Yuva Krishnapillai, and Elia Ahmadi — all three are from Haus Fund, a biotech accelerator — this was an opportunity to make the case for American peptides. Hence the party on a wartime cargo ship, where guests left with stickers and hats emblazoned with “My Peptides Are Made in America.”
Listening to the three speak, they wouldn’t strike you as typical hooah patriots or MAHA activists — though there’s some overlap in their talking points. They’re venture capitalists promoting a certain viewpoint that’s trending in Silicon Valley right now. But it’s not just them making this argument. Hims & Hers — one of the most recognizable telehealth brands right now — is also hopping on the American peptide bandwagon.
The push for American manufacturing is a thorny one. Last year, President Donald Trump’s tariff policies also reignited the American manufacturing argument, though the reality is it’s difficult to return to “made in America” after decades spent shaping the country into a services-based economy.
Pharmaceuticals have also globalized. According to the US Pharmacopeia, 51 percent of all US prescription active pharmaceutical ingredients (API) comes from India and the European Union. China accounts for 10 percent. But when it comes to gray-market peptides, there’s a reason they’re commonly referred to as “Chinese peptides.” The vast majority of these research chemicals sold online come from Chinese manufacturers.
“Right now, Americans are buying consumer peptides from gray-market sellers in China. And that concerns me because American consumers have little recourse if something goes wrong,” Ahmadi said in response to a panel question. “This is not a blanket criticism of China. This is not a criticism of Chinese people. America needs to be able to make its own medicines.”
“I think this is a really good first step in solving the gray market problem,” Krishnapillai tells me, referring to the HHS’s interim decision. While building industrial infrastructure is a lengthy, complicated, and expensive process, he argues that this decision opens the door for compounding pharmacies to build a supply chain that adheres to US consumer safety laws. That in turn could give the FDA more insight into how to more effectively regulate the market. “The FDA [will be] able to see from a bird’s-eye view a lot more clarity, which will allow for structure to be built out and eventually a new path to API classification,” he adds.
“We’re taking the stance that if people will be purchasing these peptides and taking them, sometimes even injecting, we want them to be American peptides,” adds Grunenwald. “We want them to be both compounded, produced, and verified on American soil.”
“The hardware industry — semiconductors, the compute industry, electronics — have all recognized that the reliance on China for critical minerals is a huge issue. That’s very clear. The bio industry has not yet had that same pushback and the same epiphany about our reliance on Chinese APIs. We aim to bring this onshore. It’ll take a lot of money and a lot of time, but it’s not impossible,” says Grunenwald.
For Ahmadi, onshoring peptides back to America is also a matter of regulatory consistency.
“That made-in-America stamp is really only the beginning,” he says. “If we don’t have our own domestic production, we won’t be able to enforce our own regulations. Our markets are always going to be flooded by foreign imports and we won’t be able to have accountability to American consumers. So, we see this as the first step in a larger conversation to have America set the global standard for quality and safety in this new space.”
Broadly speaking, these points have some merit. Several gray-market peptide users I’ve spoken with for an upcoming story stated that the mere act of importing “research drugs” and worrying about customs was a significant source of anxiety. That said, cost is another major reason why my sources tell me they opted to seek Chinese suppliers. It’s too soon to tell, but American-made peptides will likely be more expensive. That’s because an end-to-end US supply chain for these drugs is no easy feat.
Even so, for some, that extra expense is worth it if it means signaling a higher-quality product.
“We purchased an API manufacturing facility in California recently … Our intention is to leverage that capability to provide US-made API, and as a result, US-made product through the whole supply chain,” says Dr. Anant Vinjamoori, chief medical officer at Hims & Hers. The company purchased the facility last year, though it does partner with other companies globally to deliver compounded medications outside the US.
But in this particular case, Vinjamoori is also specifically referring to Peptides Plus, a forthcoming service from Hims & Hers announced in the wake of the HHS interim policy. In a blog announcing Peptides Plus, Hims & Hers CEO Andrew Dudum writes that the company “spent years building the infrastructure we need in our very own U.S.-based peptide manufacturing facility.” At launch, the company plans to provide access to “Sermorelin, Glutathione, B12, NAD+, and L-carnitine, with BPC-157 and TB-500 to follow when allowable.” Notably, some of these substances, like vitamin B12 and NAD+, aren’t peptides at all. Meanwhile, sermorelin and glutathione are already legal. All of these, with the exception of BPC-157 and TB-500, are currently available through legal pathways.
“I think it’s important not just to be able to say, ‘Oh, this is US made,’ but because it gives us direct oversight into the whole process,” says Vinjamoori, who says that he had prescribed peptides to patients in the past before the Biden administration moved certain research peptides to the no-compounding list.
“Even then, there was inconsistency between compounding pharmacies. Everyone knew this,” he says, noting that doctors would individually recommend pharmacies based on what they were prescribing. “That to me, is still not ideal. That’s why we really felt it was worth making this investment — and to be clear, this is not easy.”
When asked about how this might affect Peptide Plus pricing, Vinjamoori demurred, stating that the company would have more to share in “due time.”
There are yet many unknowns with regard to the HHS decision and how it’ll impact the peptide economy at large. The decision is most notably an interim policy. It’s not necessarily a permanent or lasting change. It was also made before the midterm elections, so who can say how the policy winds will shift concerning tariffs and whether the zeal for “made in America” will fizzle as a result.
But for now, it’s more than likely that you’ll start seeing some vendors touting that their peptides are proudly made in America. It’s entirely possible that they might imply that makes their product “safer.” That might be true with regard to sourcing and, to a degree, accountability. But it’s not the whole story. After all, in peptide America, sourcing is only part of the problem.
