No, AI agents did not discover a previously unknown Maya site
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:42:40 UTC
04:42:40 KST

Armed with a team of AI agents, Google researcher Pietro Miotti claimed earlier this week to have discovered a potentially unmapped Maya site in Mexico. There was just one problem: The site had already been discovered and was published in an academic journal last year. The incident, which sparked criticism among archeologists online, underscores a growing tension in science as amateurs wielding increasingly powerful AI tools parachute into specialist fields, often without the expertise needed to understand what they are looking at.
No, AI agents did not discover a previously unknown Maya site
Scientists are getting tired of amateurs parachuting into their fields and making ‘discoveries’ with AI.
Local communities are often already well aware of sites in their area.
Miotti said the idea to use AI to search for archeological sites came to him while traveling in Peru, when he began wondering what historic explorers might do with access to modern technology. He settled on lidar, a laser-based mapping technology that can produce detailed three-dimensional images of the environment, and set a team of 48 AI agents to comb through NASA lidar data for signs of undocumented sites. As “Peru isn’t well covered” by public lidar data, Miotti wrote, he shifted his focus to the Maya region.
The agents flagged numerous promising locations, including “one major village [that] doesn’t seem to have been registered” in existing records. Miotti, who described the research to The Verge as a personal side project done without any Google compute or resources, said he then reached out to Mexico’s National Institute of Anthropology and History (INAH) to check whether the apparent discovery was already known, including through unpublished research. Using the agents to translate his correspondence into Spanish, Miotti said INAH told him it had no record of a registered site at that location.
“It took less than 72 hours to go from a random thought while traveling to an official reply from the Mexican government. This is by far the best time ever to be alive,” Miotti wrote on his blog.
Miotti stressed that he is not an archeologist and emphasized that the finding would need to be verified with fieldwork. Nevertheless, he hailed it as proof that “we’re on the brink of a revolution across every science” and called for the “entire planet” to be mapped with lidar so AI agents could “help us find the rest.” Had he been an archaeologist familiar with the region, however, Miotti might have spotted a problem: The site was already known, and had been documented in an academic journal more than a year earlier.
“I didn’t expect it to get this much attention.”
Archeologists were quick to point out the oversight on social media. In a series of posts on Bluesky, Sarah Parcak, an archeology professor at the University of Alabama at Birmingham, stressed the importance of carefully checking findings against existing research. “This is why we have peer review,” Parcak wrote, adding that just because the Mexican government doesn’t have something in its database doesn’t mean it is unknown or unmapped. Other archeologists echoed her criticism, slamming the project for spreading misinformation and taking credit away from archeologists, who have been using advanced digital tools in their work for decades.
“This was a personal project that I was excited to share,” Miotti told The Verge. “I didn’t expect it to get this much attention.” He added that as a non-archaeologist, he had not realized a site not being registered wasn’t the same as it not having been mapped, adding that expert knowledge “is needed to verify what the agents find.” He also addressed the limits of his AI tools: “My agents and I missed it. Archaeologists rightly don’t publish the coordinates of sites, so checking whether a site is already known means comparing images against figures in papers, not searching a database. That’s much harder for agents, and it’s something these tools need to get better at. When people pointed it out, I updated the post and the blog as soon as I could to credit their work.”
Nevertheless, Miotti said this was an example of how agents could be used to help experts sort through large amounts of data. He said he has been encouraged by the archaeologists and PhD students that he said were reaching out to him “excited about the workflow and wanting to know how it works and whether it could help with their own survey data.”
“Our ‘problem’ is not discovering new sites,” Elizabeth Graham, a professor of Mesoamerican archaeology at University College London, told The Verge. “Most of us working in the Maya area know about tons of sites not explored or excavated or mapped, most of these found on foot,” she said. Graham also critiqued the use of the word “discover,” noting that local communities are often already well aware of sites in their area.
While she credited Miotti with doing a decent job of using AI to map the mounds from lidar data — “the interpretation of lidar data can be tricky” — Graham argued the bigger obstacle is funding for research. “The fact is, there just isn’t the funding to excavate or map sites as there was in the past, so in a way it doesn’t matter how many more are ‘discovered.’”
“Just spotting something does not add much to our knowledge.”
The controversy reflects a broader problem with AI in science, perhaps most visible with OpenAI’s recent advances in mathematics. While AI tools are increasingly capable of producing genuinely impressive results, they don’t always engage with the existing research those findings build upon. The result can be claimed breakthroughs that have, as is the case here, already been made by someone else, or a general failure to credit earlier work. Even when the findings are actually new, they can lack context a specialist would bring. Ultimately, amateur use of AI tools often ends up shifting the burden of establishing what has actually been found and why it matters to actual experts, work that would ordinarily be part of making a scientific discovery in the first place.
Ivan Šprajc, a professor of archaeology at the Research Centre of the Slovenian Academy of Sciences and Arts in Ljubljana and a coauthor of the paper documenting the site, told The Verge he wasn’t particularly impressed by the agents’ work. Archeologists have been using lidar and “other sophisticated remote sensing tools for quite some time,” he said, adding that “just spotting something does not add much to our knowledge.” He told The Verge that while it is commendable that Miotti checked in with INAH, doing so is “not sufficient” as the institution does not have access to all possible information. “The fact that INAH has no official knowledge of a particular cluster of archaeological features does not necessarily mean that it has not been spotted or even explored by archaeologists.”
Šprajc said that he worries the growing use of AI tools by amateurs could overwhelm archeologists with claims of new discoveries. “Just today I have received an email from another AI ‘discoverer,’ so I am a little bit concerned about being flooded by this kind of message,” he said. He also raised another potential risk of amateurs publicizing the locations of archeological sites: attracting looters. “But as long as these findings do not lead to looting, I don’t see a major problem,” he added.

Google’s latest Search upgrade promises better answers to your most complicated questions
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:27:45 UTC
04:27:45 KST

Google Search is getting better at understanding complicated questions, including those with multiple parts, highly specific requests, or confusing typos. The updates are coming via the company’s newly released Gemini 4 Hydrogen model, which Google claims is its “biggest model improvement to Search yet.”
While Google’s frontier models, Gemini 4 Carbon and Gemini 4 Argon, are best at deep reasoning for hard problems, Hydrogen is designed for fast execution across a variety of agentic workflows, or tasks in which an AI carries out multiple steps on its own, the company explained in an announcement.
The idea behind the model’s release is that using the most powerful model isn’t always needed — sometimes a faster, cheaper, and reasonably smart one will do. Google says Hydrogen completes tasks 2.5 times faster than Gemini 3.8 Flash, its previous fast model, but it’s still pretty smart, even beating the more powerful Gemini 4 Argon on one broad intelligence benchmark, the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index, at its default high thinking level.
In Search, Hydrogen brings several improvements, including a better understanding of user intent, the ability to offer more helpful links and visual responses, and better answers across the different languages supported by Google’s AI Mode.
In terms of intent, Google says Hydrogen can handle more nuanced and complicated questions more quickly. This means people can ask their questions in their natural train of thought, including those that have multiple parts or that include highly specific requests. Hydrogen is also better at navigating hard-to-understand typos, Google says. Because of its improvements and better ability to follow instructions and call the right tools, Hydrogen is able to more precisely address each part of a user’s question.
Its advanced reasoning also improves Search’s fan-out technique, which breaks down a single user’s prompt into multiple subqueries. This allows people to discover websites related to the query that they may have otherwise missed, the blog post explains.
The addition of Hydrogen to Search will also mean more images, structured responses, comparison tables, and custom tools will appear as visual responses to users’ queries in AI Mode.
Google also says Hydrogen can work on its own for everyday work in areas like writing code, running tests, or verifying code changes, or it can be paired with Carbon and Argon in workflows that combine several models.
The model offers four thinking levels: low, medium, high (default), and a new, extra-high tier (“xhigh”), all of which are designed to complete tasks faster with fewer output tokens.
On multi-step tasks like deep research, data analysis, and broad codebase navigation, Hydrogen ranks No. 5 on Agent Arena’s benchmarks, the company pointed out.
In addition to Search, Gemini 4 Hydrogen is coming to all tiers of Google’s AI Studio, the Gemini API, the Gemini App, and the paid AI Pro and Ultra subscribers in Google’s Antigravity development platform.
Google says the Hydrogen update is rolling out to all Search users globally.

Nectar launches an AI agent to automate creator discovery and outreach for brands
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:11:00 UTC
04:11:00 KST

Nectar, an AI-powered operating system for marketing, is launching a new AI agent designed to take a lot of the manual work out of influencer marketing, from finding the right creators to contacting them, managing partnerships, and tracking results.
Founded by sisters Misbah and Farah Uraizee, both former Meta employees, Nectar came out of stealth last year and uses AI agents to help brands manage social media, customer conversations, content moderation, creator partnerships, and competitor research.
Announced Thursday, its new “Creator Agent” can search for creators, check their content and brand partnerships, send personalized messages, coordinate collaborations, and monitor how campaigns perform. Instead of having marketing teams spend weeks scrolling through social media, building spreadsheets, and sending individual DMs, Nectar wants its agent to handle much of that work automatically.
Nectar joins other companies introducing agentic AI assistants designed to automate influencer outreach, including Aspire and GRIN. The launch also comes as more brands turn to creators to reach customers. U.S. spending on creator advertising is expected to reach $44 billion in 2026, according to the Interactive Advertising Bureau. As companies work with hundreds or even thousands of creators, keeping track of relationships and measuring results can get pretty difficult.
Nectar says its Creator Agent searches more than 300 million creator profiles to find potential partners, including smaller creators who may not yet have a large following but are gaining traction. The system analyzes videos to understand what creators post about, how their content fits a brand, and whether their previous partnerships are appropriate. It also checks brand safety by reviewing content in context rather than simply searching for specific keywords.
Nectar then creates ranked lists of recommendations and adds new matches as creators become more popular.
Once it finds a potential match, the agent can draft and send messages in a brand’s voice, create custom landing pages to collect creator information, and help manage contracts, product gifting, and shipping details. It also remembers previous conversations and interactions, so brands can keep track of relationships without relying on spreadsheets.
“Brands can share voice guidelines in any format, and Nectar picks up the rest from their history,” CEO Misbah Uraizee tells TechCrunch. “On top of that sits a set of proprietary guardrails that keep every draft on tone and in context. Nectar understands what is being said in a video, reads the tone of the conversation, and knows the difference between a quote post on X and a TikTok comment section. A brand should not sound identical in both, and with Nectar it doesn’t.”
Another feature looks for personal milestones in creators’ lives, such as an engagement, a new baby, or completing a marathon. The agent can then suggest a personalized message or gift, helping brands build relationships that feel less like one-off advertising deals.
Creator Agent is available today globally and covers Instagram creators and TikTokers.
The company plans to make the agent available through its Model Context Protocol (MCP), allowing teams to use it through AI assistants such as Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, and Copilot, as well as their own custom-built agents.
This past May, Nectar raised a $30 million Series A round led by Menlo Ventures and its Anthology Fund, which is a joint initiative launched with Anthropic.

Microsoft Outlook continues to be unavailable for people, businesses, and public services across the globe, leaving people unable to access their email, either through the web or apps. Microsoft Outlook is still down
Microsoft 365 issues have been cutting people off from their email since early Thursday afternoon, and they still aren’t fixed.
When the outage issues were first identified on Thursday, Microsoft acknowledged from its Microsoft 365 Status account that “We’ve determined a portion of infrastructure is experiencing elevated load across multiple regions, which is likely contributing to the connectivity errors.” Microsoft has gone through several variations of update notes since then, providing suprisingly detailed bulletins about the issues and its attempts to fix them. While Microsoft said it initially had problems rebooting servers and being able to monitor their health, its last few updates say that recovery efforts are going well:
We’re seeing early stabilization and capacity improvements as recovery actions continue. To help preserve delayed mail during this event, we’ve extended email timeout to 48 hours.
Recovery actions are ongoing across affected Microsoft 365 infrastructure and progressing in phases. We’re seeing incremental improvement in several workloads, but service health remains variable and capacity has not yet fully returned to baseline.
We’ve made additional progress toward recovery, with actions now completed across portions of affected infrastructure and traffic being gradually restored. We’re seeing improvements in key scenarios, including mailbox access and delayed email processing, though some intermittent issues may continue. Our focus is extending these recovery actions across remaining affected infrastructure while continuing stabilization and adding capacity to improve consistency.
There’s still no ETA for full restoration, but we will continue to monitor this ongoing outage.

Vibes Don't Maintain
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:06:29 UTC
04:06:29 KST

Vibes Don't Maintain
Since ChatGPT took off, people have been coming up to me to say some version of “you people will be out of a job soon.”
Most of them were managers. They knew it was provocative, and they wanted to see how I’d react - partly to test the waters on whether this was a real scenario. What they had in common was that none of them had a clear idea of what software development actually is. Not what it looks like from the outside. What it is.
Last week I got the best version of that conversation I’ve had so far.
A friend of mine owns a restaurant. He told me he’d built an app for it with Claude Code - a web app to manage tasks and processes for his staff. He was proud of it, and he had every right to be. He had never written a line of code and he had a thing on a screen that did what he described.
I had a look. It was nice. Clean layout, sensible flow, data that persisted between page loads so it felt like a working system.
Then I started poking. No responsive layout - it broke on the phones his staff would actually use. No dark mode 🏴☠️. No login, so no notion of who did what. No settings. No translations, in a kitchen where half the team doesn’t speak the language the app was written in. No error states. No handling of the empty case, the duplicate case, the two-people-edit-the-same-thing case. The data “persisted” in a way that would survive a refresh and not much else.
It was a click-dummy. A prototype. A perfectly good proof of concept for a first round of conversations about whether the idea holds up - which is a real and valuable thing to have.
Then he asked me if I could host it for him and do ops.
This is the biggest misunderstanding people have about software, and it has nothing to do with AI.
People see the surface. They see the parts that work. They don’t see the parts that run when something goes wrong, the parts that exist to stop things going wrong, or the parts that have never once executed in production because they’re there for a worst case someone sat down and imagined.
The seatbelt and the airbag in your car have, with luck, never done anything. That doesn’t make them useless. You don’t buckle up because the law says so or because the car beeps at you. You buckle up because you understand that one day you might need it. Software is mostly seatbelts. The happy path - the thing the demo shows - is the smallest part of the codebase.
My friend, understandably, didn’t see that. His read was that the app works 100% as intended, so 90% of the work is done. The rest is hosting. And I shouldn’t overcomplicate it.
Here’s how that version plays out.
The prototype came almost free and almost instantly. He decides the product is done. He might even get it in front of users - his staff start using it for real shifts, real tasks, real schedules.
Then something goes wrong. The database file corrupts. A dependency update breaks something. Someone finds the app on the open internet and has a look around. An edge case fires that nobody wrote down. Or my personal favorite: daylight saving time, and every shift in the schedule is off by an hour.
Everything goes boom.
He doesn’t know what he built. He doesn’t know why it was built the way it was - the reasoning lived in a chat session that’s gone. He doesn’t know what to look for or where to start looking. There is no spec, no test, no document, no commit message that explains the intent of anything.
At that point he has two options. Find someone to fix it and prepare to hand them all his money, because the first week of that engagement is pure archaeology. Or flee the country.
That is what “vibes don’t maintain” means in practice. The vibe got him to the demo. Nothing carried forward from there.
None of this is “don’t use AI.” We use it every day. But two things are true at the same time.
First: AI does not make you a software engineer if your background is baking bread, any more than your oven at home makes you a chef. It makes execution cheap. It does nothing about knowing what to execute, or what to do when the execution fails.
Second: shit in, shit out. If the input is a loose description of what the happy path looks like, the output is a happy path. The AI will not invent the seatbelts for you. It doesn’t know you’ll need them, because you didn’t.
What survives a boom is not the code. It’s the record of what the code was supposed to do - the stories, the specs, the decisions - written down somewhere a human or an agent can read them back. Ours are markdown files in git, kept with bacon-tracker. My friend has a chat history he can’t find anymore.
That record is what you’re paying for. Not the click-dummy.
There’s a second question hiding in his request, and in all those “you’ll be out of a job” conversations: if the tool really can do my job, what exactly was I still for? But that’s for another day.
I called him the next day. I told him I got myself a Thermomix - the one with AI - and was planning to open a restaurant the week after.
He laughed. Then he went quiet for a second.
I hope he got it. But knowing him I think he did not.

Dwarf planet candidate appears which takes roughly 280 years to orbit our sun
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:05:33 UTC
04:05:33 KST

Astronomers Found a Mysterious Object Orbiting Far Beyond Pluto. It Could Be a New Dwarf Planet Hidden in Our Solar System
The dwarf planet candidate, called ED138, appeared in telescope images and takes roughly 280 years to orbit the sun. Astronomers need to determine its size to properly classify it
Matthew Holman, a senior astrophysicist at the Smithsonian Astrophysical Observatory, was sifting through data from the Vera C. Rubin Observatory in Chile when something odd caught his eye. Running a custom-made computer program, he and his colleague Kevin Napier had already spotted more than 2,000 little bright dots in the telescope data, corresponding to newly discovered small objects in the distant solar system. But this one was different: It seemed to be big.
Holman managed his expectations. After all, astronomers in the business of finding distant objects are used to disappointment. “We all have ‘found Mars’ … in terms of a false positive,” Holman tells Smithsonian magazine. They find a bright object that seems really big, then, with a closer look, learn that it’s nothing very remarkable after all. “We’re all really cautious when you see something that looks like it’s large.”
Still, this object grabbed Holman’s attention, so he tried to verify it with Napier and Rubin solar system lead scientist Mario Jurić. They found the celestial body in previous photos of the sky throughout the past decade, which helped them zero in on its orbital path—and the more historical data they found, the more confident they became.
“We know that it’s real,” Holman says. “Every time you look at the predicted location, there it is.” They even found it in photographic plates from 1937, stored in the Harvard plate stacks, he says.
The object, called 2026 ED138, is 46 times farther from the sun than Earth is, and it takes 281.1 years to complete an orbit. Now, the central question is whether it’s big enough to be classified as a new dwarf planet, which would put it among the rare ranks of only five other objects in our solar system.
“This is incredibly exciting,” Paul Byrne, a planetary scientist at the Washington University in St. Louis who was not involved in the discovery, tells National Geographic’s Robin George Andrews. “It heralds a new era of solar system and planetary science.”
Dwarf planets are smaller planets that orbit a star and have a nearly round shape, which happens naturally with objects above a certain size, as their gravitational force pulls evenly on all sides. But dwarf planets are not large enough for their gravity to clear their orbital paths of similar-size debris—the eight planets of our solar system, meanwhile, have achieved that feat. (Pluto’s designation was changed from planet to dwarf planet in 2006 for this reason—its orbit is littered with icy Kuiper Belt objects.)
Quick fact: Dynamic dwarf planet
Ceres, a dwarf planet in the main asteroid belt between Mars and Jupiter, may contain salty water that can percolate onto the surface in some regions.
ED138 is likely big enough to be round and, therefore, a dwarf planet—estimates for its size currently range from 370 miles to 930 miles across. But astronomers will have to confirm that measurement, and from the object’s brightness alone, that’s impossible. A highly reflective, smaller object, perhaps covered in gleaming ice caps, might appear just as bright as a less reflective, duller body that’s much larger.
Astronomers have two ways to get the required data. They could observe the world in infrared light, which reveals the thermal energy that it emits after being warmed by the sun. “From that you can figure out how big it is, because we know approximately what temperature it should have,” Holman says.
Alternatively, astronomers could watch the dwarf planet candidate pass in front of a star. If they know how much light comes from that star and how fast ED138 is moving, they can calculate the body’s size based on how long it obscures the light.
Holman says people are working on making those observations with telescopes such as the Atacama Large Millimeter/submillimeter Array (ALMA). The dwarf planet candidate isn’t extremely easy to see at the moment, he adds, but “in the next few months it will be, and people will try.”
Right now, astronomers recognize five dwarf planets: Pluto, Eris, Makemake and Haumea in the distant Kuiper Belt, and Ceres in the main asteroid belt between Mars and Jupiter. Experts have long suspected that there could be more of these objects, but many of them could have highly elliptical orbits that keep them too far away for us to see most of the time. What we observe is potentially the “tip of the iceberg of a few that are in the close part of their orbit,” Holman says. “There must be a lot that we just haven’t yet detected.”
The discovery also hints at a relatively unexplored area of our solar system—the region that’s in front of the dense, starry core of the Milky Way from our perspective. It’s harder to pick out objects against all the background light in that zone, and ED138 has been traveling through it for the past 25 to 30 years, Holman notes. “There’s a swath on the sky where we really don’t have a good idea of what is there,” he adds.
What’s more, ED138 is in the southern hemisphere’s sky, and a lot of astronomical observations have focused on the northern hemisphere. But Rubin is poised to help change that: The new telescope, which has the world’s largest digital camera and captured its first images last year, is one of several observatories operating out of Chile’s Atacama Desert.
“Rubin is giving us an unprecedented capacity to explore the sky through a combination of speed, depth and precision,” Jurić says in a statement.
“I was surprised that it found such an object so early,” Megan Schwamb, a planetary scientist at Queen’s University Belfast in Northern Ireland and a member of the Rubin solar system operations team, tells CNN’s Jacopo Prisco. “This was not on my bingo card for this year.”
Understanding more about how and where ED138 formed could reveal new details about the early days of the solar system. And its existence offers a clear reminder that surprising objects remain to be found on the fringes of our planetary neighborhood.
“Is there a Mars- or Earth-size or bigger object out there?” Holman says. “That’s not ruled out.”

Microsoft’s Outlook email hit by new outage
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:03:39 UTC
04:03:39 KST

Microsoft is slowly recovering from its daylong outage after much of the company’s systems were hit by a cascading software issue.
The problems, which started Thursday, were initially flagged as affecting the company’s services in the U.S. and the Americas, but quickly spread around the world, as engineers struggled to recover downed servers.
What began as an Outlook email and Teams video conferencing outage soon developed into a full-blown meltdown affecting the company’s cloud services for both consumer and business email, files, and documents. The outage also knocked out the company’s Copilot AI tools and Defender security screening software, and prevented system administrators from accessing their internal Intune dashboards and features. Some users reportedly experienced issues with accessing their cloud-based Windows computers.
Microsoft hasn’t yet said what caused the issues, only saying the outage involved “a large number of servers being unexpectedly unavailable.” The company said it continues to investigate the cause.
The outage affected a broad range of industries, including TechCrunch’s own email. We’ve reached out to Microsoft for comment about the incident.
As of Friday morning on the U.S. east coast, Microsoft’s Outlook email service had begun to recover and some emails have been trickling in. The recovery comes after engineers initially could not find the cause of the issue that was causing servers in Microsoft’s data centers to fail.
Doug Madory, the director of internet analysis at Kentik, a company that monitors internet traffic worldwide, said in a post on Bluesky that traffic was returning to Microsoft’s services after about 18 hours of downtime.
Microsoft said in its latest status update on Friday that there was “no evidence of malicious activity associated with this incident,” and later noted that there was “not related to cyber or AI / agents.” That may be some solace for the millions who went without their email and online services yesterday, but the company is likely to face questions about its inability to swiftly recover from an outage of its own making.
A Microsoft spokesperson did not respond to our questions asking if the company has the technical means to detect malicious activity, such as logs, or if Microsoft has a way to check what happened.
“We’re seeing encouraging recovery progress, with service availability and email delivery performance improving across a growing portion of affected infrastructure.” said Charles Lamanna, Microsoft’s executive vice president for Copilot, agents and platform, in a statement on Friday midday (ET). “Our teams are reviewing what happened and how we can prevent similar issues going forward.”
The last major incident affecting Microsoft services was in September, when a days-long outage hit Microsoft’s cloud services. During that outage, Microsoft blamed a “core authentication configuration” issue that prevented users from accessing the service.
This story was first published on November 12 and has been updated throughout.

Google Pushes Privacy Angle As Gemini-Powered Smartglasses Debut
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-12
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Google Pushes Privacy Angle As Gemini-Powered Smartglasses Debut (arstechnica.com) 29
			
		 	
				Google is launching its first Gemini-powered smart glasses, with Samsung supplying the computing hardware. Given their built-in 12-megapixel camera, Google is emphasizing privacy protections, including a tamper-resistant recording light, automatic face redaction, and restrictions on real-time facial recognition and livestreaming. The company also promises not to use captured photos or videos for AI training or targeted advertising. Ars Technica reports:  Google says the frames all weigh about 41 grams, which is not terrible but is definitely near the top for glasses generally. They will, naturally, weigh much more when you account for lenses. That will also determine the final pricing. So far, we only have the starting prices for these devices, including non-prescription lenses.
The setup here is similar to the Ray-Ban Meta glasses we've seen for a few years. There are no screens in the lenses, but you can interact with the AI system via voice or by activating the integrated camera. The experience should be similar to using Gemini Live on your phone, but it will be a lot easier to fire up the AI. You can say "Hey Gemini" or tap a touch-sensitive area of the frame to start interacting with the robot.
Since this is the same chatbot that operates across the Google ecosystem, it has access to your data and preferences. You can ask questions and get personalized answers, have Google Maps guide you by voice, or just talk with the AI to kill time. The glasses use custom open-ear speakers to limit distractions for those around you. Google notes the directional microphones should not pick up speech from other people, either.
Adding the camera feed is where things get interesting. When the AI can see what you see, it has the potential to do more work on your behalf. Google suggests asking Gemini for recommendations when looking at a bookshelf or glancing at an event poster to have the AI add it to your calendar. The glasses will also support Gemini Guided Vision, which provides conversational audio descriptions for people who are visually impaired.  The new frames are available for preorder today starting at $395.
